```markdown
0001 | +++
0002 | title = "03: The First Surprise"
0003 | date = "2026-08-11T01:19:00+01:00"
0004 | draft = false
0005 | description = "Change one rule number and watch a tiny deterministic system become unexpectedly difficult to predict."
0006 | categories = ["Programming", "Artificial Life"]
0007 | tags = ["Digital Life", "Cellular Automata", "Rule 30", "Emergence", "Complexity"]
0008 | +++
0009 | 
0010 | We ended the last chapter by changing one number.
0011 | 
0012 | ```python
0013 | rule = 22
0014 | ```
0015 | 
0016 | became:
0017 | 
0018 | ```python
0019 | rule = 30
0020 | ```
0021 | 
0022 | Nothing else changed.
0023 | 
0024 | Same world size.
0025 | 
0026 | Same single active cell.
0027 | 
0028 | Same three-cell neighborhood.
0029 | 
0030 | Same synchronous update.
0031 | 
0032 | Same periodic boundary.
0033 | 
0034 | Same implementation.
0035 | 
0036 | Only the eight-bit local rule changed.
0037 | 
0038 | And the spacetime diagram changed dramatically.
0039 | 
0040 | ![Rule 30 from a single active cell](/images/books/digital-life/ch03-rule30-hero.png)
0041 | 
0042 | There is visible structure on the left.
0043 | 
0044 | Irregularity on the right.
0045 | 
0046 | Repeating fragments.
0047 | 
0048 | Regions that look patterned for a while and then break apart.
0049 | 
0050 | And every pixel in that history is determined completely by the previous row.
0051 | 
0052 | No random numbers are involved.
0053 | 
0054 | That is our first real surprise.
0055 | 
0056 | ---
0057 | 
0058 | # Nothing random happened
0059 | 
0060 | Rule 30 does not roll dice.
0061 | 
0062 | Its transition table is fixed:
0063 | 
0064 | ```text
0065 | 111 → 0
0066 | 110 → 0
0067 | 101 → 0
0068 | 100 → 1
0069 | 011 → 1
0070 | 010 → 1
0071 | 001 → 1
0072 | 000 → 0
0073 | ```
0074 | 
0075 | Those outputs give:
0076 | 
0077 | ```text
0078 | 00011110
0079 | ```
0080 | 
0081 | which is:
0082 | 
0083 | ```text
0084 | 30
0085 | ```
0086 | 
0087 | Every future state follows from:
0088 | 
0089 | ```text
0090 | current state
0091 | +
0092 | rule
0093 | ```
0094 | 
0095 | under the experimental setup we defined.
0096 | 
0097 | If we start from exactly the same state again, we get exactly the same history.
0098 | 
0099 | Every time.
0100 | 
0101 | So the irregularity cannot be explained by saying:
0102 | 
0103 | > randomness was injected.
0104 | 
0105 | It was not.
0106 | 
0107 | ---
0108 | 
0109 | # Deterministic does not mean obvious
0110 | 
0111 | This distinction matters.
0112 | 
0113 | A deterministic system means:
0114 | 
0115 | > given the current state, the next state is fixed.
0116 | 
0117 | It does **not** mean:
0118 | 
0119 | > the long-term trajectory is easy to predict without running the system.
0120 | 
0121 | Those are different claims.
0122 | 
0123 | Each individual update is tiny:
0124 | 
0125 | ```python
0126 | next_state = step(state, rule=30)
0127 | ```
0128 | 
0129 | One cell.
0130 | 
0131 | Three inputs.
0132 | 
0133 | One output bit.
0134 | 
0135 | But after repeated updates, a large history unfolds.
0136 | 
0137 | So we need to separate:
0138 | 
0139 | ```text
0140 | local mechanism
0141 | ```
0142 | 
0143 | from:
0144 | 
0145 | ```text
0146 | global consequence
0147 | ```
0148 | 
0149 | Locally, Rule 30 is trivial to inspect.
0150 | 
0151 | Globally, its trajectory is not.
0152 | 
0153 | That distinction is going to matter throughout this book.
0154 | 
0155 | ---
0156 | 
0157 | # Where is the complexity?
0158 | 
0159 | This is a useful question.
0160 | 
0161 | Is the apparent complexity stored in the rule?
0162 | 
0163 | Not in any obvious sense.
0164 | 
0165 | The rule is only eight bits.
0166 | 
0167 | Is it stored in the initial condition?
0168 | 
0169 | We started with one active cell.
0170 | 
0171 | Is it stored inside each cell?
0172 | 
0173 | Each cell contains one bit.
0174 | 
0175 | So the interesting structure seems to arise from:
0176 | 
0177 | ```text
0178 | simple rule
0179 | +
0180 | simple state
0181 | +
0182 | repeated local interaction
0183 | +
0184 | time
0185 | ```
0186 | 
0187 | The structure lives in the unfolding.
0188 | 
0189 | That is why the word:
0190 | 
0191 | ```text
0192 | emergence
0193 | ```
0194 | 
0195 | is tempting.
0196 | 
0197 | But we should still be careful.
0198 | 
0199 | ---
0200 | 
0201 | # Do not use "emergence" as a synonym for surprise
0202 | 
0203 | A weak definition of emergence would be:
0204 | 
0205 | > Something happened that I did not expect.
0206 | 
0207 | That is not enough.
0208 | 
0209 | Surprise depends partly on the observer.
0210 | 
0211 | A better working description is:
0212 | 
0213 | > **A macroscopic pattern or behavior arises from local interactions without being explicitly represented in the individual components.**
0214 | 
0215 | Under that definition, Rule 30 is a useful example.
0216 | 
0217 | The cells do not contain:
0218 | 
0219 | ```text
0220 | triangle
0221 | left edge
0222 | irregular right side
0223 | long-range structure
0224 | ```
0225 | 
0226 | The rule does not contain a stored spacetime diagram.
0227 | 
0228 | Yet those structures appear when the local interactions repeat.
0229 | 
0230 | That supports a modest claim:
0231 | 
0232 | > **Simple local mechanics can generate nontrivial global organization.**
0233 | 
0234 | Not life.
0235 | 
0236 | Not intelligence.
0237 | 
0238 | Not even necessarily deep complexity.
0239 | 
0240 | But enough to keep investigating.
0241 | 
0242 | ---
0243 | 
0244 | # A controlled comparison
0245 | 
0246 | Now compare Rule 22 and Rule 30 directly.
0247 | 
0248 | Same initial condition:
0249 | 
0250 | ```text
0251 | 000000000010000000000
0252 | ```
0253 | 
0254 | Run Rule 22.
0255 | 
0256 | Then run Rule 30.
0257 | 
0258 | ![Rule 22 compared with Rule 30](/images/books/digital-life/ch03-rule22-vs-rule30.png)
0259 | 
0260 | Everything except the local rule remains fixed.
0261 | 
0262 | Conceptually:
0263 | 
0264 | ```text
0265 | Experiment A
0266 | rule = 22
0267 | 
0268 | Experiment B
0269 | rule = 30
0270 | ```
0271 | 
0272 | Same:
0273 | 
0274 | ```text
0275 | initial state
0276 | boundary condition
0277 | world size
0278 | generation count
0279 | update semantics
0280 | ```
0281 | 
0282 | Only the rule changes.
0283 | 
0284 | So under this fixed experimental configuration, the change in trajectory is attributable to the change in rule.
0285 | 
0286 | That sounds obvious.
0287 | 
0288 | Later, when our systems contain hundreds of interacting mechanisms, it will not be obvious at all.
0289 | 
0290 | This is why we are learning the discipline here.
0291 | 
0292 | ---
0293 | 
0294 | # Change one bit in the rule
0295 | 
0296 | Rule 30 is:
0297 | 
0298 | ```text
0299 | 00011110
0300 | ```
0301 | 
0302 | Flip one output bit and we move to a neighboring rule.
0303 | 
0304 | For example:
0305 | 
0306 | ```text
0307 | 00011111
0308 | ```
0309 | 
0310 | or:
0311 | 
0312 | ```text
0313 | 00010110
0314 | ```
0315 | 
0316 | The representation changes only slightly.
0317 | 
0318 | The resulting trajectory may not.
0319 | 
0320 | That creates another question:
0321 | 
0322 | > **How stable is a system's global behavior under small changes to its local mechanism?**
0323 | 
0324 | This is a much better question than:
0325 | 
0326 | > What does Rule 30 do?
0327 | 
0328 | Because eventually we will care about:
0329 | 
0330 | ```text
0331 | mutation
0332 | variation
0333 | robustness
0334 | search
0335 | evolution
0336 | ```
0337 | 
0338 | A tiny change in representation can have a large change in consequence.
0339 | 
0340 | We should remember that.
0341 | 
0342 | ---
0343 | 
0344 | # Change one bit in the world
0345 | 
0346 | Now keep the rule fixed and perturb the initial condition.
0347 | 
0348 | Start with:
0349 | 
0350 | ```text
0351 | 000000000010000000000
0352 | ```
0353 | 
0354 | Then compare:
0355 | 
0356 | ```text
0357 | 000000000011000000000
0358 | ```
0359 | 
0360 | One extra active cell.
0361 | 
0362 | Everything else remains identical.
0363 | 
0364 | Run both under Rule 30.
0365 | 
0366 | ![A one-cell perturbation spreading under Rule 30](/images/books/digital-life/ch03-rule30-perturbation.png)
0367 | 
0368 | At first the difference is tiny.
0369 | 
0370 | Then it spreads.
0371 | 
0372 | One changed cell alters several local neighborhoods.
0373 | 
0374 | Those changed neighborhoods alter later neighborhoods.
0375 | 
0376 | The perturbation propagates.
0377 | 
0378 | Now we have something measurable.
0379 | 
0380 | ---
0381 | 
0382 | # Track the difference, not just the worlds
0383 | 
0384 | Suppose we have:
0385 | 
0386 | ```text
0387 | A
0388 | 00000100000
0389 | 
0390 | B
0391 | 00000110000
0392 | ```
0393 | 
0394 | Their difference is:
0395 | 
0396 | ```text
0397 | 00000010000
0398 | ```
0399 | 
0400 | One cell.
0401 | 
0402 | After one generation, the worlds may differ in several cells.
0403 | 
0404 | Then more.
0405 | 
0406 | Instead of only studying:
0407 | 
0408 | ```text
0409 | world A
0410 | world B
0411 | ```
0412 | 
0413 | we can construct:
0414 | 
0415 | ```text
0416 | difference(A, B)
0417 | ```
0418 | 
0419 | That difference field is useful because it converts:
0420 | 
0421 | > these two runs look different
0422 | 
0423 | into something we can measure.
0424 | 
0425 | We can track:
0426 | 
0427 | ```text
0428 | number of differing cells
0429 | fraction of differing cells
0430 | spatial width of the difference
0431 | rate of divergence
0432 | ```
0433 | 
0434 | This is an important step.
0435 | 
0436 | The animation gives us the hypothesis.
0437 | 
0438 | The difference field gives us an instrument.
0439 | 
0440 | ---
0441 | 
0442 | # Measure the spread
0443 | 
0444 | A simple quantity is the Hamming distance between two binary states.
0445 | 
0446 | For states:
0447 | 
0448 | $$
0449 | A_t
0450 | $$
0451 | 
0452 | and:
0453 | 
0454 | $$
0455 | B_t
0456 | $$
0457 | 
0458 | define:
0459 | 
0460 | $$
0461 | D(t)
0462 | 
0463 | \sum_i
0464 | \mathbf{1}
0465 | \left[
0466 | A_t(i) \neq B_t(i)
0467 | \right]
0468 | $$
0469 | 
0470 | In code:
0471 | 
0472 | ```python
0473 | def difference_count(a, b):
0474 |     return sum(x != y for x, y in zip(a, b))
0475 | ```
0476 | 
0477 | Now run the two initial conditions forward and record:
0478 | 
0479 | ```python
0480 | difference = []
0481 | 
0482 | for _ in range(generations):
0483 |     difference.append(
0484 |         difference_count(state_a, state_b)
0485 |     )
0486 | 
0487 |     state_a = step(state_a, rule=30)
0488 |     state_b = step(state_b, rule=30)
0489 | ```
0490 | 
0491 | Plot that over time.
0492 | 
0493 | ![Measured growth of the perturbation](/images/books/digital-life/ch03-rule30-difference-growth.png)
0494 | 
0495 | Now we are no longer saying:
0496 | 
0497 | > the disturbance seems to spread.
0498 | 
0499 | We are asking:
0500 | 
0501 | > **how much of the world becomes different, and how quickly?**
0502 | 
0503 | That is a better question.
0504 | 
0505 | ---
0506 | 
0507 | # Sensitivity is not yet chaos
0508 | 
0509 | At this point it is tempting to say:
0510 | 
0511 | > Rule 30 is chaotic.
0512 | 
0513 | Maybe.
0514 | 
0515 | But we should not use that word casually.
0516 | 
0517 | In dynamical systems, chaos has technical meanings.
0518 | 
0519 | Likewise:
0520 | 
0521 | ```text
0522 | irregular
0523 | random
0524 | chaotic
0525 | complex
0526 | emergent
0527 | ```
0528 | 
0529 | are not synonyms.
0530 | 
0531 | For this chapter, we can safely say:
0532 | 
0533 | ```text
0534 | Rule 30 is deterministic.
0535 | 
0536 | Its spacetime diagram contains visually irregular regions.
0537 | 
0538 | Small changes to the initial state can spread through later states.
0539 | 
0540 | The divergence can be measured.
0541 | ```
0542 | 
0543 | That is enough.
0544 | 
0545 | If a stronger word requires a stronger definition, we should earn it later.
0546 | 
0547 | ---
0548 | 
0549 | # The first control is not enough
0550 | 
0551 | Suppose our difference count grows rapidly.
0552 | 
0553 | Can we immediately claim that Rule 30 is unusually sensitive?
0554 | 
0555 | No.
0556 | 
0557 | Compared with what?
0558 | 
0559 | We should also run:
0560 | 
0561 | ```text
0562 | Rule 22
0563 | Rule 30
0564 | other neighboring rules
0565 | ```
0566 | 
0567 | with the same perturbation protocol.
0568 | 
0569 | Then we can ask:
0570 | 
0571 | ```text
0572 | Which rules amplify a one-bit perturbation?
0573 | 
0574 | How quickly?
0575 | 
0576 | Does the perturbation saturate?
0577 | 
0578 | Does it disappear?
0579 | 
0580 | Does it remain localized?
0581 | ```
0582 | 
0583 | That is where a visual example becomes a comparative experiment.
0584 | 
0585 | We do not have to complete that whole survey here.
0586 | 
0587 | But the experimental direction is now clear.
0588 | 
0589 | ---
0590 | 
0591 | # The danger of the screenshot
0592 | 
0593 | A single Rule 30 image is seductive.
0594 | 
0595 | But a screenshot is weak evidence.
0596 | 
0597 | I chose:
0598 | 
0599 | ```text
0600 | the rule
0601 | the initial condition
0602 | the world size
0603 | the crop
0604 | the number of generations
0605 | the rendering
0606 | ```
0607 | 
0608 | I could show only the most dramatic part.
0609 | 
0610 | That is exactly the same problem we encountered with Lenia.
0611 | 
0612 | A spectacular example is useful for finding a phenomenon.
0613 | 
0614 | It tells us much less about how general that phenomenon is.
0615 | 
0616 | Eventually we will need to ask:
0617 | 
0618 | ```text
0619 | How often does this happen?
0620 | 
0621 | Across which rules?
0622 | 
0623 | Across which initial conditions?
0624 | 
0625 | How sensitive is it?
0626 | 
0627 | How persistent is it?
0628 | 
0629 | Can we detect it automatically?
0630 | ```
0631 | 
0632 | That is where enumeration and measurement become more important than screenshots.
0633 | 
0634 | ---
0635 | 
0636 | # One system, several observables
0637 | 
0638 | There is another useful lesson here.
0639 | 
0640 | We can look at Rule 30 as:
0641 | 
0642 | ```text
0643 | a whole spatial state at one time
0644 | ```
0645 | 
0646 | or:
0647 | 
0648 | ```text
0649 | a complete spacetime diagram
0650 | ```
0651 | 
0652 | or:
0653 | 
0654 | ```text
0655 | one cell observed across many times
0656 | ```
0657 | 
0658 | or:
0659 | 
0660 | ```text
0661 | a difference field between two runs
0662 | ```
0663 | 
0664 | or:
0665 | 
0666 | ```text
0667 | a trajectory through global state space
0668 | ```
0669 | 
0670 | These are different observables of the same underlying system.
0671 | 
0672 | That matters because what we notice depends strongly on what we measure.
0673 | 
0674 | A system may look simple under one observable and complicated under another.
0675 | 
0676 | This will become important later when we study:
0677 | 
0678 | ```text
0679 | density
0680 | activity
0681 | movement
0682 | causality
0683 | lineage
0684 | repair
0685 | ```
0686 | 
0687 | The system does not come with one privileged measurement.
0688 | 
0689 | We have to choose observables that match the question.
0690 | 
0691 | ---
0692 | 
0693 | # A tiny rule can create a large causal footprint
0694 | 
0695 | There is something almost insulting about how small Rule 30 is.
0696 | 
0697 | Its entire local behavior fits in eight lines:
0698 | 
0699 | ```python
0700 | rule = {
0701 |     (1, 1, 1): 0,
0702 |     (1, 1, 0): 0,
0703 |     (1, 0, 1): 0,
0704 |     (1, 0, 0): 1,
0705 |     (0, 1, 1): 1,
0706 |     (0, 1, 0): 1,
0707 |     (0, 0, 1): 1,
0708 |     (0, 0, 0): 0,
0709 | }
0710 | ```
0711 | 
0712 | There is no hidden controller below it.
0713 | 
0714 | Yet one changed bit can influence a growing region of future states.
0715 | 
0716 | That gives us the first major result of the book:
0717 | 
0718 | > **A globally nontrivial trajectory does not require a globally complicated controller.**
0719 | 
0720 | And now we can add something stronger:
0721 | 
0722 | > **A local perturbation can acquire a large future footprint through repeated interaction.**
0723 | 
0724 | That is more than a pretty pattern.
0725 | 
0726 | It is the beginning of causal structure.
0727 | 
0728 | ---
0729 | 
0730 | # But we still do not have a thing
0731 | 
0732 | Rule 30 gives us:
0733 | 
0734 | ```text
0735 | structure
0736 | propagation
0737 | divergence
0738 | history
0739 | ```
0740 | 
0741 | But does it give us an entity?
0742 | 
0743 | Something we can point to and say:
0744 | 
0745 | > that thing persisted.
0746 | 
0747 | Not obviously.
0748 | 
0749 | The pattern expands.
0750 | 
0751 | Regions change.
0752 | 
0753 | Differences propagate.
0754 | 
0755 | But identifying one localized organization through time is difficult.
0756 | 
0757 | So the next chapter changes the question.
0758 | 
0759 | We need a system where a pattern can appear here:
0760 | 
0761 | ```text
0762 | .##.
0763 | ##..
0764 | ....
0765 | ```
0766 | 
0767 | and later appear somewhere else while remaining recognizably the same organization.
0768 | 
0769 | That takes us to Conway's Game of Life.
0770 | 
0771 | And to a harder question:
0772 | 
0773 | > **When does a pattern become a thing?**
0774 | 
0775 | Next: **When Does a Pattern Become a Thing?**
```

0001 | +++
0002 | title = "04: When Does a Pattern Become a Thing?"

0004 | draft = false
0005 | description = "Use Conway's Game of Life to ask when a recurring localized pattern becomes useful to treat as an entity."
0006 | categories = ["Programming", "Artificial Life"]
0007 | tags = ["Digital Life", "Cellular Automata", "Conway's Game of Life", "Glider", "Emergence", "Pattern Identity"]
0008 | +++
0009 | 
0010 | Rule 30 gave us structure.
0011 | 
0012 | It gave us propagation.
0013 | 
0014 | It gave us sensitivity to perturbation.
0015 | 
0016 | But it did not obviously give us a **thing**.
0017 | 
0018 | The pattern expanded.  
0019 | Regions changed.  
0020 | Differences spread.
0021 | 
0022 | Interesting global behavior appeared.
0023 | 
0024 | But if I pointed at one region and asked:
0025 | 
0026 | > Is that the same object ten generations later?
0027 | 
0028 | the answer was not obvious.
0029 | 
0030 | So let's change systems.
0031 | 
0032 | Not because Conway's Game of Life is more complicated.  
0033 | It isn't.
0034 | 
0035 | But because it gives us something Rule 30 does not give us so cleanly:
0036 | 
0037 | > **a localized pattern that appears to persist while moving through space**
0038 | 
0039 | That creates a harder question.
0040 | 
0041 | Not:
0042 | 
0043 | > Is it alive?
0044 | 
0045 | Much earlier than that:
0046 | 
0047 | > **When does it become useful to treat a pattern as a continuing thing?**
0048 | 
0049 | ---
0050 | 
0051 | ## Start With Five Cells
0052 | 
0053 | Consider this pattern:
0054 | 
0055 | ```text
0056 | .#.
0057 | ..#
0058 | ###
0059 | ```
0060 | 
0061 | Five active cells.  
0062 | Everything else is empty.
0063 | 
0064 | Nothing in those five cells says:
0065 | 
0066 | ```text
0067 | move diagonally
0068 | ```
0069 | 
0070 | There is no velocity.  
0071 | No direction variable.  
0072 | No object controller.  
0073 | No instruction such as:
0074 | 
0075 | ```python
0076 | glider.x += 1
0077 | glider.y += 1
0078 | ```
0079 | 
0080 | And yet if we run Conway's Game of Life, something remarkable happens.
0081 | 
0082 | ![A Game of Life glider moving across a fixed lattice](/images/books/digital-life/ch04-glider.gif)
0083 | 
0084 | A recognizable organization appears to travel diagonally through the grid.
0085 | 
0086 | We normally say:
0087 | 
0088 | > the glider moved.
0089 | 
0090 | But before we accept that sentence, we should ask what actually moved.
0091 | 
0092 | ---
0093 | 
0094 | ## The Cells Did Not Move
0095 | 
0096 | Game of Life cells occupy fixed coordinates.
0097 | 
0098 | A cell does not travel across the lattice.
0099 | 
0100 | Instead, cells become active and inactive according to their neighborhoods.
0101 | 
0102 | So the underlying process is closer to:
0103 | 
0104 | ```text
0105 | activity in one region
0106 |         ↓
0107 | changes nearby neighborhoods
0108 |         ↓
0109 | different cells become active
0110 |         ↓
0111 | earlier cells become inactive
0112 |         ↓
0113 | a similar organization appears nearby
0114 | ```
0115 | 
0116 | ```mermaid
0117 | flowchart LR
0118 |     A[Active cells<br/>in region R] --> B[Change nearby<br/>neighborhoods]
0119 |     B --> C[Different cells<br/>become active]
0120 |     C --> D[Earlier cells<br/>become inactive]
0121 |     D --> E[Similar organization<br/>appears shifted]
0122 | ```
0123 | 
0124 | No material object crosses the grid.  
0125 | The **organization** does.
0126 | 
0127 | This is the same conceptual problem we encountered with Lenia.  
0128 | But Game of Life gives us a much cleaner laboratory.
0129 | 
0130 | ---
0131 | 
0132 | ## The Game of Life Rule
0133 | 
0134 | Game of Life uses a two-dimensional binary lattice.
0135 | 
0136 | Every cell examines its eight immediate neighbors.
0137 | 
0138 | An active cell survives if it has:
0139 | 
0140 | ```text
0141 | 2 or 3
0142 | ```
0143 | 
0144 | active neighbors.
0145 | 
0146 | An inactive cell becomes active if it has:
0147 | 
0148 | ```text
0149 | exactly 3
0150 | ```
0151 | 
0152 | active neighbors.
0153 | 
0154 | Usually written:
0155 | 
0156 | ```text
0157 | B3/S23
0158 | ```
0159 | 
0160 | That is the entire rule.
0161 | 
0162 | There is no special rule for:
0163 | 
0164 | ```text
0165 | glider
0166 | block
0167 | blinker
0168 | spaceship
0169 | ```
0170 | 
0171 | There is only:
0172 | 
0173 | ```text
0174 | local binary state
0175 | +
0176 | eight-cell neighborhood
0177 | +
0178 | B3/S23
0179 | +
0180 | synchronous update
0181 | ```
0182 | 
0183 | The larger structures are descriptions we introduce after observing what the local dynamics produce.
0184 | 
0185 | ---
0186 | 
0187 | ## Build the Rule
0188 | 
0189 | A clear implementation is small:
0190 | 
0191 | ```python
0192 | import numpy as np
0193 | 
0194 | 
0195 | def life_step(state):
0196 |     neighbors = (
0197 |         np.roll(np.roll(state,  1, axis=0),  1, axis=1)
0198 |         + np.roll(state,  1, axis=0)
0199 |         + np.roll(np.roll(state,  1, axis=0), -1, axis=1)
0200 |         + np.roll(state,  1, axis=1)
0201 |         + np.roll(state, -1, axis=1)
0202 |         + np.roll(np.roll(state, -1, axis=0),  1, axis=1)
0203 |         + np.roll(state, -1, axis=0)
0204 |         + np.roll(np.roll(state, -1, axis=0), -1, axis=1)
0205 |     )
0206 | 
0207 |     born = (state == 0) & (neighbors == 3)
0208 |     survive = (state == 1) & ((neighbors == 2) | (neighbors == 3))
0209 | 
0210 |     return (born | survive).astype(np.uint8)
0211 | ```
0212 | 
0213 | Again:
0214 | 
0215 | ```text
0216 | local state
0217 | +
0218 | local interaction
0219 | +
0220 | same rule everywhere
0221 | ```
0222 | 
0223 | No global object model.
0224 | 
0225 | ---
0226 | 
0227 | ## Put a Glider Into the World
0228 | 
0229 | ```python
0230 | state = np.zeros((20, 20), dtype=np.uint8)
0231 | 
0232 | glider = np.array([
0233 |     [0, 1, 0],
0234 |     [0, 0, 1],
0235 |     [1, 1, 1],
0236 | ], dtype=np.uint8)
0237 | 
0238 | state[2:5, 2:5] = glider
0239 | ```
0240 | 
0241 | Now update four times:
0242 | 
0243 | ```python
0244 | for _ in range(4):
0245 |     state = life_step(state)
0246 | ```
0247 | 
0248 | After four generations, the same local configuration returns one cell diagonally away.
0249 | 
0250 | So we can measure:
0251 | 
0252 | ```text
0253 | period = 4 generations
0254 | displacement = (+1, +1)
0255 | ```
0256 | 
0257 | That is already stronger than:
0258 | 
0259 | > it looks like it moved.
0260 | 
0261 | We have a repeatable relationship.
0262 | 
0263 | ---
0264 | 
0265 | ## What Persisted?
0266 | 
0267 | Suppose we label the five active cells at the start:
0268 | 
0269 | ```text
0270 | A
0271 | B
0272 | C
0273 | D
0274 | E
0275 | ```
0276 | 
0277 | Four generations later, the original active coordinates are mostly no longer active.
0278 | 
0279 | The pattern is made from different cells.  
0280 | Its coordinates changed.  
0281 | Its intermediate shape changed.
0282 | 
0283 | Yet we still naturally say:
0284 | 
0285 | > the glider is over there now.
0286 | 
0287 | So what persisted?
0288 | 
0289 | Not:
0290 | 
0291 | ```text
0292 | the same cells
0293 | ```
0294 | 
0295 | Not:
0296 | 
0297 | ```text
0298 | the same coordinates
0299 | ```
0300 | 
0301 | Not even:
0302 | 
0303 | ```text
0304 | the same exact shape at every generation
0305 | ```
0306 | 
0307 | What persisted was something more like:
0308 | 
0309 | ```text
0310 | a recurring sequence of local configurations
0311 | +
0312 | a stable transformation through time
0313 | +
0314 | a fixed displacement
0315 | ```
0316 | 
0317 | That is already a much richer notion of identity than material continuity.
0318 | 
0319 | ---
0320 | 
0321 | ## Material Identity and Organizational Identity
0322 | 
0323 | We can distinguish two rough notions of sameness.
0324 | 
0325 | ### Material identity
0326 | 
0327 | ```text
0328 | same components
0329 | =
0330 | same thing
0331 | ```
0332 | 
0333 | That works well for some physical objects.  
0334 | But it is a poor description of a glider.
0335 | 
0336 | ### Organizational identity
0337 | 
0338 | ```text
0339 | same organization
0340 | through an allowed transformation
0341 | =
0342 | usefully treated as the same thing
0343 | ```
0344 | 
0345 | The glider strongly invites this second description.
0346 | 
0347 | But there is an important qualification.  
0348 | We have not proved:
0349 | 
0350 | > every persistent pattern is an entity.
0351 | 
0352 | We have shown something narrower:
0353 | 
0354 | > **Material continuity is not necessary for a useful operational notion of identity in this system.**
0355 | 
0356 | That is enough.
0357 | 
0358 | ```mermaid
0359 | flowchart TD
0360 |     A[Identity Criteria] --> B[Material Identity]
0361 |     A --> C[Organizational Identity]
0362 |     B --> B1["Same cells<br/>(fails for glider)"]
0363 |     C --> C1["Same configuration<br/>+ transformation"]
0364 |     C1 --> C2["Recurrence after period<br/>+ translation"]
0365 | ```
0366 | 
0367 | ---
0368 | 
0369 | ## Measure the Motion
0370 | 
0371 | We can make this less philosophical.
0372 | 
0373 | Take the active coordinates:
0374 | 
0375 | ```python
0376 | positions = np.argwhere(state == 1)
0377 | ```
0378 | 
0379 | and compute their centroid:
0380 | 
0381 | ```python
0382 | centroid = positions.mean(axis=0)
0383 | ```
0384 | 
0385 | Track that through time.
0386 | 
0387 | ![The measured centroid trajectory of a glider](/images/books/digital-life/ch04-glider-centroid.png)
0388 | 
0389 | Now:
0390 | 
0391 | > the glider moved
0392 | 
0393 | can become:
0394 | 
0395 | > the centroid of this localized pattern changes systematically through time.
0396 | 
0397 | That gives us:
0398 | 
0399 | ```text
0400 | position
0401 | displacement
0402 | velocity
0403 | trajectory
0404 | ```
0405 | 
0406 | as observables.
0407 | 
0408 | The noun:
0409 | 
0410 | ```text
0411 | glider
0412 | ```
0413 | 
0414 | starts becoming experimentally useful because several measurable quantities remain coherent through its transformations.
0415 | 
0416 | ---
0417 | 
0418 | ## Localization Matters
0419 | 
0420 | Why is the glider easier to treat as a thing than Rule 30's expanding history?
0421 | 
0422 | One major reason is:
0423 | 
0424 | > **localization**
0425 | 
0426 | Most of the world is not part of the glider.  
0427 | Activity is concentrated in a bounded region.
0428 | 
0429 | We can measure:
0430 | 
0431 | ```python
0432 | positions = np.argwhere(state == 1)
0433 | 
0434 | minimum = positions.min(axis=0)
0435 | maximum = positions.max(axis=0)
0436 | ```
0437 | 
0438 | That gives us a bounding box.
0439 | 
0440 | We can define properties such as:
0441 | 
0442 | ```text
0443 | area
0444 | active-cell count
0445 | centroid
0446 | velocity
0447 | shape
0448 | orientation
0449 | period
0450 | ```
0451 | 
0452 | Now we have a candidate operational boundary.
0453 | 
0454 | But we should already mark that word:
0455 | 
0456 | > candidate.
0457 | 
0458 | Later we will encounter systems where connected geometry may not be the correct boundary of the continuing organization.
0459 | 
0460 | For now, localization is a useful first approximation.
0461 | 
0462 | ---
0463 | 
0464 | ## A Block Persists Differently
0465 | 
0466 | Consider:
0467 | 
0468 | ```text
0469 | ##
0470 | ##
0471 | ```
0472 | 
0473 | This is a block.
0474 | 
0475 | Under Game of Life it remains unchanged.
0476 | 
0477 | ![Fixed, periodic and translating persistence](/images/books/digital-life/ch04-persistence-types.png)
0478 | 
0479 | It is:
0480 | 
0481 | ```text
0482 | localized
0483 | persistent
0484 | stationary
0485 | ```
0486 | 
0487 | The glider is:
0488 | 
0489 | ```text
0490 | localized
0491 | persistent
0492 | translating
0493 | periodically transforming internally
0494 | ```
0495 | 
0496 | So:
0497 | 
0498 | ```text
0499 | persistence
0500 | ```
0501 | 
0502 | and:
0503 | 
0504 | ```text
0505 | movement
0506 | ```
0507 | 
0508 | are already separate properties.
0509 | 
0510 | ---
0511 | 
0512 | ## A Blinker Persists by Changing
0513 | 
0514 | Now consider:
0515 | 
0516 | ```text
0517 | ###
0518 | ```
0519 | 
0520 | One generation later:
0521 | 
0522 | ```text
0523 | #
0524 | #
0525 | #
0526 | ```
0527 | 
0528 | Then back again.
0529 | 
0530 | This is a blinker.
0531 | 
0532 | Its exact state does not persist.  
0533 | Its behavior does.
0534 | 
0535 | So persistence itself has several forms.
0536 | 
0537 | ---
0538 | 
0539 | ## Three Forms of Persistence
0540 | 
0541 | ### Fixed persistence
0542 | 
0543 | ```text
0544 | state(t + 1) = state(t)
0545 | ```
0546 | 
0547 | Example: block
0548 | 
0549 | ### Periodic persistence
0550 | 
0551 | ```text
0552 | state(t + p) = state(t)
0553 | ```
0554 | 
0555 | for some period `p`.
0556 | 
0557 | Example: blinker (p=2)
0558 | 
0559 | ### Translating persistence
0560 | 
0561 | For some period `p` and spatial translation Δ:
0562 | 
0563 | ```text
0564 | state(t + p) = translate(state(t), Δ)
0565 | ```
0566 | 
0567 | Example: glider (p=4, Δ=(+1,+1))
0568 | 
0569 | ```mermaid
0570 | graph TD
0571 |     Persistence[Persistence Types] --> Fixed[Fixed<br/>state unchanged]
0572 |     Persistence --> Periodic[Periodic<br/>returns after p steps]
0573 |     Persistence --> Translating[Translating<br/>returns shifted after p steps]
0574 |     Fixed --> Block["Example: Block"]
0575 |     Periodic --> Blinker["Example: Blinker (p=2)"]
0576 |     Translating --> Glider["Example: Glider (p=4, Δ=+1,+1)"]
0577 | ```
0578 | 
0579 | That is much better than one vague word.  
0580 | We can now specify what kind of persistence we mean.
0581 | 
0582 | ---
0583 | 
0584 | ## Identity Depends on Which Changes We Ignore
0585 | 
0586 | Suppose the glider at `t = 0` occupies one location.  
0587 | At `t = 4`, it occupies the same local configuration shifted by (+1,+1).
0588 | 
0589 | A raw whole-grid comparison gives:
0590 | 
0591 | ```python
0592 | np.array_equal(state_t0, state_t4)
0593 | ```
0594 | 
0595 | which returns `False`.
0596 | 
0597 | But if we compensate for translation:
0598 | 
0599 | ```python
0600 | shifted = np.roll(
0601 |     np.roll(state_t0, 1, axis=0),
0602 |     1,
0603 |     axis=1,
0604 | )
0605 | ```
0606 | 
0607 | the patterns match.
0608 | 
0609 | So identity depends partly on what transformations we decide are irrelevant.
0610 | 
0611 | Do we care about:
0612 | 
0613 | ```text
0614 | position?
0615 | orientation?
0616 | scale?
0617 | phase?
0618 | exact constituent cells?
0619 | ```
0620 | 
0621 | Different choices imply different identity criteria.
0622 | 
0623 | This is not merely philosophy.  
0624 | It determines the algorithm we use to detect persistent entities.
0625 | 
0626 | ---
0627 | 
0628 | ## Identity Requires Invariants
0629 | 
0630 | One useful way to think about identity is:
0631 | 
0632 | > What survives the transformations we allow?
0633 | 
0634 | For the glider:
0635 | 
0636 | ```text
0637 | position          changes
0638 | active cells      change
0639 | intermediate form changes
0640 | ```
0641 | 
0642 | But after four generations:
0643 | 
0644 | ```text
0645 | local configuration recurs
0646 | +
0647 | displacement is predictable
0648 | ```
0649 | 
0650 | That relationship is invariant.
0651 | 
0652 | So an operational definition might be:
0653 | 
0654 | > **A glider is a localized dynamical pattern whose local state recurs after a fixed period up to spatial translation.**
0655 | 
0656 | That is testable.
0657 | 
0658 | ---
0659 | 
0660 | ## But This Is Only Our First Definition of a Thing
0661 | 
0662 | This is important.
0663 | 
0664 | Right now we are using:
0665 | 
0666 | ```text
0667 | localized connected geometry
0668 | +
0669 | recurrence
0670 | +
0671 | coherent trajectory
0672 | ```
0673 | 
0674 | as evidence that treating something as an entity is useful.
0675 | 
0676 | That works beautifully for a glider.
0677 | 
0678 | It does not follow that this is the universal definition of a digital individual.
0679 | 
0680 | Later we may find:
0681 | 
0682 | ```text
0683 | disconnected components
0684 | ```
0685 | 
0686 | that participate in:
0687 | 
0688 | ```text
0689 | one continuing causal process
0690 | ```
0691 | 
0692 | or:
0693 | 
0694 | ```text
0695 | identical geometries
0696 | ```
0697 | 
0698 | that belong to:
0699 | 
0700 | ```text
0701 | completely different causal histories
0702 | ```
0703 | 
0704 | If that happens, geometry will stop being enough.
0705 | 
0706 | So the glider gives us a **working definition**, not a final ontology.
0707 | 
0708 | That distinction is going to become important.
0709 | 
0710 | ---
0711 | 
0712 | ## The Danger of Naming Things
0713 | 
0714 | Game of Life contains named patterns such as:
0715 | 
0716 | ```text
0717 | block
0718 | blinker
0719 | beacon
0720 | glider
0721 | lightweight spaceship
0722 | pulsar
0723 | gun
0724 | ```
0725 | 
0726 | Names are useful.  
0727 | Instead of listing coordinates, we can say:
0728 | 
0729 | > glider
0730 | 
0731 | and reason at a higher level.
0732 | 
0733 | But naming has a side effect.  
0734 | Once something has a noun, the mind starts treating it as an object automatically.
0735 | 
0736 | The noun does not prove the ontology.
0737 | 
0738 | Calling a pattern:
0739 | 
0740 | ```text
0741 | spaceship
0742 | ```
0743 | 
0744 | does not make it an engineered vehicle.  
0745 | Calling something:
0746 | 
0747 | ```text
0748 | gun
0749 | ```
0750 | 
0751 | does not make it analogous to a physical gun in every meaningful respect.
0752 | 
0753 | We will use names.  
0754 | But we should remember:
0755 | 
0756 | > **Naming compresses description. It does not establish mechanism.**
0757 | 
0758 | ---
0759 | 
0760 | ## Can Two Apparent Things Interact?
0761 | 
0762 | Now put two localized patterns in the same world.
0763 | 
0764 | Depending on their geometry and timing, they may:
0765 | 
0766 | ```text
0767 | pass
0768 | collide
0769 | destroy one another
0770 | produce debris
0771 | produce another recognizable pattern
0772 | ```
0773 | 
0774 | ![Two localized Game of Life patterns interacting](/images/books/digital-life/ch04-collision.gif)
0775 | 
0776 | Now the entity-level description becomes even more useful.
0777 | 
0778 | We can ask:
0779 | 
0780 | ```text
0781 | Did pattern A alter pattern B?
0782 | 
0783 | Did either persist?
0784 | 
0785 | Was a new localized pattern produced?
0786 | 
0787 | Was the collision outcome reproducible?
0788 | 
0789 | Would the same outcome occur if one input were removed?
0790 | ```
0791 | 
0792 | That final question is especially important.  
0793 | It begins moving us from:
0794 | 
0795 | ```text
0796 | pattern description
0797 | ```
0798 | 
0799 | toward:
0800 | 
0801 | ```text
0802 | causal analysis
0803 | ```
0804 | 
0805 | We will need that later.
0806 | 
0807 | ---
0808 | 
0809 | ## Does a Glider Carry Information?
0810 | 
0811 | A glider begins in one region.  
0812 | Later, a correlated structured state exists somewhere else.
0813 | 
0814 | So something about the earlier configuration constrains a later distant configuration.
0815 | 
0816 | Operationally:
0817 | 
0818 | ```text
0819 | localized state
0820 |       ↓
0821 | repeated local transformations
0822 |       ↓
0823 | correlated localized state elsewhere
0824 | ```
0825 | 
0826 | ```mermaid
0827 | flowchart LR
0828 |     A[Localized pattern<br/>at t=0, pos x] --> B[Repeated local<br/>transformations]
0829 |     B --> C[Correlated localized<br/>pattern at t=4, pos x+Δ]
0830 | ```
0831 | 
0832 | This is why gliders can be used as signals in computational constructions inside Game of Life.
0833 | 
0834 | But we should again avoid saying too much.
0835 | 
0836 | We do not need:
0837 | 
0838 | > the glider understands information.
0839 | 
0840 | We only need:
0841 | 
0842 | > **a structured local state can propagate through space while preserving relationships that another mechanism can respond to.**
0843 | 
0844 | That is enough to make it useful as a signal.
0845 | 
0846 | ---
0847 | 
0848 | ## So Is the Glider a Thing?
0849 | 
0850 | Biologically?  
0851 | We have shown nothing close to enough.
0852 | 
0853 | Philosophically?  
0854 | We can argue indefinitely.
0855 | 
0856 | Experimentally?  
0857 | We can be precise.
0858 | 
0859 | The glider is:
0860 | 
0861 | ```text
0862 | localized
0863 | dynamically persistent
0864 | periodic
0865 | translating
0866 | reproducible from the same initial condition
0867 | describable independently of the exact cells currently active
0868 | ```
0869 | 
0870 | That makes it useful to treat the glider as:
0871 | 
0872 | > **a pattern-level entity under a defined persistence criterion**
0873 | 
0874 | Notice the wording.
0875 | 
0876 | Not:
0877 | 
0878 | > the glider is alive.
0879 | 
0880 | Not:
0881 | 
0882 | > the glider is objectively an organism.
0883 | 
0884 | Not even:
0885 | 
0886 | > connected glider geometry is the universal meaning of individuality.
0887 | 
0888 | Only:
0889 | 
0890 | > treating it as a continuing entity is useful because measurable relationships remain coherent across its transformations.
0891 | 
0892 | That is the bounded claim.
0893 | 
0894 | ---
0895 | 
0896 | ## Now We Have Something We Can Hurt
0897 | 
0898 | Rule 30 gave us spreading structure.  
0899 | The glider gives us a localized persistent pattern.
0900 | 
0901 | That means we can finally perform a stronger experiment.
0902 | 
0903 | We can:
0904 | 
0905 | ```text
0906 | remove one cell
0907 | remove several cells
0908 | alter its phase
0909 | collide it with another pattern
0910 | ```
0911 | 
0912 | and ask:
0913 | 
0914 | ```text
0915 | Does the organization continue?
0916 | 
0917 | Does it remain recognizable?
0918 | 
0919 | Does it return toward its previous form?
0920 | 
0921 | Does it become another persistent structure?
0922 | 
0923 | Does it disappear?
0924 | ```
0925 | 
0926 | That leads to three ideas that are easy to confuse:
0927 | 
0928 | ```text
0929 | persistence
0930 | ≠
0931 | robustness
0932 | ≠
0933 | regeneration
0934 | ```
0935 | 
0936 | A pattern surviving forever in an undisturbed world tells us much less than a pattern surviving disturbance.
0937 | 
0938 | So next, we stop admiring it.  
0939 | We damage it.
0940 | 
0941 | Next: **Kill It.**
```


---

## F0007 — `05-kill-it/index.md`

```text

```markdown
0001 | +++
0002 | title = "05: Kill It"
0003 | date = "2026-08-11T01:34:00+01:00"
0004 | draft = false
0005 | description = "Damage persistent digital patterns and separate survival, robustness and regeneration into experimentally distinct claims."
0006 | categories = ["Programming", "Artificial Life"]
0007 | tags = ["Digital Life", "Cellular Automata", "Robustness", "Regeneration", "Perturbation", "Game of Life"]
0008 | +++
0009 | 
0010 | We have been too kind to our systems.
0011 | 
0012 | So far we have:
0013 | 
0014 | - initialized them carefully,
0015 | - placed them in clean environments,
0016 | - watched them evolve,
0017 | - selected examples that behave interestingly,
0018 | - and mostly left them alone.
0019 | 
0020 | That is a dangerous way to understand a mechanism.
0021 | 
0022 | A structure that persists under exactly the conditions that produce it tells us something.
0023 | 
0024 | But not enough.
0025 | 
0026 | If we want to know what is actually maintaining that structure, we need to interfere.
0027 | 
0028 | So let's interfere.
0029 | 
0030 | ---
0031 | 
0032 | ## Start With the Glider
0033 | 
0034 | Our glider:
0035 | 
0036 | ```text
0037 | .#.
0038 | ..#
0039 | ###
0040 | ```
0041 | 
0042 | is a dynamically persistent localized pattern.
0043 | 
0044 | Under undisturbed Game of Life dynamics, it repeats its local configuration every four generations while translating one cell diagonally.
0045 | 
0046 | That gave us a useful operational definition of persistence.
0047 | 
0048 | Now remove one cell.
0049 | 
0050 | For example:
0051 | 
0052 | ```text
0053 | .#.
0054 | ...
0055 | ###
0056 | ```
0057 | 
0058 | One bit changed.  
0059 | Same rule.  
0060 | Same world.  
0061 | Same boundary condition.  
0062 | Same update semantics.
0063 | 
0064 | Only the state has been perturbed.
0065 | 
0066 | Run it.
0067 | 
0068 | ![Survival as damage severity increases](/images/books/digital-life/ch05-damage-survival-curve.png)
0069 | 
0070 | Several outcomes are possible.
0071 | 
0072 | The structure may disappear.  
0073 | It may become another Life pattern.  
0074 | Some activity may survive.  
0075 | Debris may spread.
0076 | 
0077 | But one possibility is especially important:
0078 | 
0079 | > **the original glider may fail to reconstruct itself**
0080 | 
0081 | That immediately separates two claims:
0082 | 
0083 | ```text
0084 | persists when undisturbed
0085 | ```
0086 | 
0087 | from:
0088 | 
0089 | ```text
0090 | returns after disturbance
0091 | ```
0092 | 
0093 | Those are not the same property.
0094 | 
0095 | ---
0096 | 
0097 | ## Three Different Claims
0098 | 
0099 | We need separate words for separate phenomena.
0100 | 
0101 | ### Persistence
0102 | 
0103 | A structure continues under its ordinary dynamics.
0104 | 
0105 | ```text
0106 | undisturbed state
0107 |       ↓
0108 | ordinary dynamics
0109 |       ↓
0110 | organization continues
0111 | ```
0112 | 
0113 | ### Robustness
0114 | 
0115 | A perturbation occurs, but some defined property continues to hold.
0116 | 
0117 | ```text
0118 | organization
0119 |       ↓
0120 | perturbation
0121 |       ↓
0122 | property still holds
0123 | ```
0124 | 
0125 | ### Regeneration
0126 | 
0127 | A perturbation substantially changes the structure, and subsequent dynamics return it toward a defined previous organization.
0128 | 
0129 | ```text
0130 | organization
0131 |       ↓
0132 | damage
0133 |       ↓
0134 | organization disrupted
0135 |       ↓
0136 | continued dynamics
0137 |       ↓
0138 | defined organization returns
0139 | ```
0140 | 
0141 | ```mermaid
0142 | flowchart TD
0143 |     A[Original Organization] --> B[Perturbation]
0144 |     B --> C[Outcome?]
0145 |     C --> D[Property still holds<br/><i>Robustness</i>]
0146 |     C --> E[Organization disrupted]
0147 |     E --> F[Dynamics continue]
0148 |     F --> G[Previous organization returns<br/><i>Regeneration</i>]
0149 |     C --> H[Organization lost<br/><i>No recovery</i>]
0150 | ```
0151 | 
0152 | So:
0153 | 
0154 | ```text
0155 | persistence
0156 | ≠
0157 | robustness
0158 | ≠
0159 | regeneration
0160 | ```
0161 | 
0162 | A system can possess one without possessing the others.  
0163 | This distinction will matter repeatedly.
0164 | 
0165 | ---
0166 | 
0167 | ## A Block Persists Perfectly
0168 | 
0169 | Take the Game of Life block:
0170 | 
0171 | ```text
0172 | ##
0173 | ##
0174 | ```
0175 | 
0176 | Under ordinary Life evolution:
0177 | 
0178 | ```text
0179 | t = 0
0180 | 
0181 | ##
0182 | ##
0183 | 
0184 | t = 1
0185 | 
0186 | ##
0187 | ##
0188 | 
0189 | t = 2
0190 | 
0191 | ##
0192 | ##
0193 | ```
0194 | 
0195 | Perfect persistence.
0196 | 
0197 | Now delete one cell:
0198 | 
0199 | ```text
0200 | #.
0201 | ##
0202 | ```
0203 | 
0204 | Run the dynamics.
0205 | 
0206 | The pattern collapses.
0207 | 
0208 | So the block has excellent:
0209 | 
0210 | ```text
0211 | undisturbed persistence
0212 | ```
0213 | 
0214 | and poor tolerance to this particular intervention.
0215 | 
0216 | The perturbation has exposed something that observation alone did not.
0217 | 
0218 | ---
0219 | 
0220 | ## The Perturbation Is the Experiment
0221 | 
0222 | Before this chapter, many of our experiments looked like:
0223 | 
0224 | ```text
0225 | initial state
0226 |       ↓
0227 | evolution
0228 |       ↓
0229 | observation
0230 | ```
0231 | 
0232 | Now the structure changes:
0233 | 
0234 | ```text
0235 | initial state
0236 |       ↓
0237 | ordinary behavior
0238 |       ↓
0239 | controlled intervention
0240 |       ↓
0241 | response
0242 |       ↓
0243 | measurement
0244 | ```
0245 | 
0246 | The perturbation is not unwanted noise around the experiment.
0247 | 
0248 | > **The perturbation is the experiment.**
0249 | 
0250 | We deliberately change something because the response can reveal which mechanisms matter.
0251 | 
0252 | This is the beginning of causal experimentation.
0253 | 
0254 | ---
0255 | 
0256 | ## Damage Must Be Defined
0257 | 
0258 | “Damage the pattern” sounds intuitive.
0259 | 
0260 | It is useless experimentally until we specify what we changed.
0261 | 
0262 | For a binary cellular automaton, damage could mean:
0263 | 
0264 | ```text
0265 | turn one active cell off
0266 | ```
0267 | 
0268 | or:
0269 | 
0270 | ```text
0271 | flip N randomly selected cells
0272 | ```
0273 | 
0274 | or:
0275 | 
0276 | ```text
0277 | erase everything inside a region
0278 | ```
0279 | 
0280 | or:
0281 | 
0282 | ```text
0283 | replace part of the pattern with random state
0284 | ```
0285 | 
0286 | These are different interventions.
0287 | 
0288 | A reproducible protocol might be:
0289 | 
0290 | ```text
0291 | pattern: glider
0292 | damage time: t = 20
0293 | damage operation: active → inactive
0294 | damage amount: one cell
0295 | damage positions: every active cell in turn
0296 | repetitions: all possible deletions
0297 | observation window: 50 generations
0298 | ```
0299 | 
0300 | Now another person can repeat the experiment.  
0301 | And now we can meaningfully compare systems.
0302 | 
0303 | ---
0304 | 
0305 | ## One Lucky Recovery Proves Very Little
0306 | 
0307 | Imagine a structure containing ten active cells.
0308 | 
0309 | Delete one particular cell.  
0310 | It returns to its previous pattern.
0311 | 
0312 | Interesting.
0313 | 
0314 | Now delete one of the other nine cells.  
0315 | It collapses.
0316 | 
0317 | Then the statement:
0318 | 
0319 | > the system regenerates after damage
0320 | 
0321 | would be far too broad.
0322 | 
0323 | A better experiment systematically tests perturbations.
0324 | 
0325 | For example:
0326 | 
0327 | ```python
0328 | for cell in active_cells:
0329 |     damaged = state.copy()
0330 |     damaged[cell] = 0
0331 | 
0332 |     result = run(damaged)
0333 | 
0334 |     measure_response(result)
0335 | ```
0336 | 
0337 | Now we can ask:
0338 | 
0339 | ```text
0340 | Which perturbations were tolerated?
0341 | 
0342 | Which positions were critical?
0343 | 
0344 | How much organization remained?
0345 | 
0346 | Did the original organization return?
0347 | 
0348 | How long did that take?
0349 | ```
0350 | 
0351 | Instead of one anecdote, we obtain a:
0352 | 
0353 | > **damage-response profile**
0354 | 
0355 | ---
0356 | 
0357 | ## Survival Is Not Recovery
0358 | 
0359 | Suppose we damage a structure and some activity remains.
0360 | 
0361 | That does not mean the structure regenerated.
0362 | 
0363 | Imagine:
0364 | 
0365 | ```text
0366 | before
0367 | 
0368 | .###.
0369 | ##.##
0370 | .###.
0371 | ```
0372 | 
0373 | After damage:
0374 | 
0375 | ```text
0376 | .#...
0377 | ##...
0378 | .....
0379 | ```
0380 | 
0381 | Twenty generations later:
0382 | 
0383 | ```text
0384 | ##...
0385 | ##...
0386 | .....
0387 | ```
0388 | 
0389 | Something survived.  
0390 | But the previous organization did not return.
0391 | 
0392 | So:
0393 | 
0394 | ```text
0395 | nonzero activity
0396 | ```
0397 | 
0398 | is not recovery.
0399 | 
0400 | Neither is:
0401 | 
0402 | ```text
0403 | localized activity
0404 | ```
0405 | 
0406 | To make a regeneration claim, we need a defined relationship between:
0407 | 
0408 | ```text
0409 | organization before perturbation
0410 | ```
0411 | 
0412 | and:
0413 | 
0414 | ```text
0415 | organization after continued evolution
0416 | ```
0417 | 
0418 | ---
0419 | 
0420 | ## Recovery Forces Us to Define Identity
0421 | 
0422 | This connects directly to the previous chapter.
0423 | 
0424 | Suppose our target pattern is `T` and the later pattern is `R`.
0425 | 
0426 | For a stationary discrete pattern, we might compare them directly:
0427 | 
0428 | ```python
0429 | np.array_equal(T, R)
0430 | ```
0431 | 
0432 | But that fails for a glider.  
0433 | A glider could return while shifted in space.
0434 | 
0435 | So perhaps recovery means:
0436 | 
0437 | ```text
0438 | same organization
0439 | up to translation
0440 | ```
0441 | 
0442 | For an oscillator:
0443 | 
0444 | ```text
0445 | same organization
0446 | up to phase
0447 | ```
0448 | 
0449 | For more complicated systems we may need invariance under:
0450 | 
0451 | ```text
0452 | translation
0453 | rotation
0454 | phase
0455 | scale
0456 | internal deformation
0457 | ```
0458 | 
0459 | The moment we ask:
0460 | 
0461 | > Did it regenerate?
0462 | 
0463 | we are forced to answer:
0464 | 
0465 | > **What counts as the same entity?**
0466 | 
0467 | The philosophical problem has become an engineering requirement.
0468 | 
0469 | ---
0470 | 
0471 | ## Exact Equality Is Usually the Wrong Metric
0472 | 
0473 | Suppose we compare two whole worlds using:
0474 | 
0475 | ```python
0476 | def similarity(a, b):
0477 |     return np.mean(a == b)
0478 | ```
0479 | 
0480 | There is an immediate problem.
0481 | 
0482 | If almost every cell is zero, two completely different tiny structures may still score close to 1.0 because the empty background dominates the metric.
0483 | 
0484 | So our measurement must focus on the structure we care about.
0485 | 
0486 | One simple choice is intersection-over-union.
0487 | 
0488 | For active-cell sets A and B:
0489 | 
0490 | $$
0491 | IoU(A,B) = \frac{|A \cap B|}{|A \cup B|}
0492 | $$
0493 | 
0494 | Identical active regions produce 1.0.  
0495 | Disjoint regions produce 0.0.
0496 | 
0497 | In code:
0498 | 
0499 | ```python
0500 | def iou(a, b):
0501 |     intersection = np.logical_and(a, b).sum()
0502 |     union = np.logical_or(a, b).sum()
0503 | 
0504 |     if union == 0:
0505 |         return 1.0
0506 | 
0507 |     return intersection / union
0508 | ```
0509 | 
0510 | For moving structures, we would first align them under the transformations our identity criterion allows.
0511 | 
0512 | Still imperfect.  
0513 | But explicit.
0514 | 
0515 | ---
0516 | 
0517 | ## Metrics Create Claims
0518 | 
0519 | Suppose we define:
0520 | 
0521 | ```text
0522 | recovered =
0523 |     IoU >= 0.90
0524 |     within 50 generations
0525 |     after optimal translation alignment
0526 | ```
0527 | 
0528 | Now we can report:
0529 | 
0530 | > Under this perturbation protocol, the pattern returned to at least 0.90 IoU with its pre-damage morphology within 50 updates after translation alignment.
0531 | 
0532 | That is much uglier than:
0533 | 
0534 | > It healed itself.
0535 | 
0536 | Good.
0537 | 
0538 | The ugly statement tells us what was actually demonstrated.  
0539 | We can always summarize later.  
0540 | But the experiment should preserve the operational claim.
0541 | 
0542 | ---
0543 | 
0544 | ## Robustness Does Not Require Regeneration
0545 | 
0546 | A system may fail to restore its previous shape while preserving something else.
0547 | 
0548 | Imagine a future digital system whose behavior is:
0549 | 
0550 | ```text
0551 | move toward a signal source
0552 | ```
0553 | 
0554 | Damage changes its morphology.  
0555 | Its shape similarity falls dramatically.  
0556 | Yet it continues moving toward the signal.
0557 | 
0558 | Then:
0559 | 
0560 | ```text
0561 | morphology failed
0562 | ```
0563 | 
0564 | while:
0565 | 
0566 | ```text
0567 | capability survived
0568 | ```
0569 | 
0570 | So there are at least two questions:
0571 | 
0572 | ```text
0573 | Did the structure recover?
0574 | ```
0575 | 
0576 | and:
0577 | 
0578 | ```text
0579 | Did the capability survive or recover?
0580 | ```
0581 | 
0582 | These are different measurements.
0583 | 
0584 | And this is a broader lesson:
0585 | 
0586 | > **Robustness always needs an object: robust with respect to what?**
0587 | 
0588 | Shape?  
0589 | Movement?  
0590 | Computation?  
0591 | Replication?  
0592 | Memory?
0593 | 
0594 | A system is not simply “robust.”  
0595 | It is robust with respect to some property under some class of perturbations.
0596 | 
0597 | ---
0598 | 
0599 | ## Build a Damage Curve
0600 | 
0601 | One perturbation gives one data point.
0602 | 
0603 | Increase the intervention systematically.
0604 | 
0605 | For example:
0606 | 
0607 | ```text
0608 | 0% removed
0609 | 5% removed
0610 | 10% removed
0611 | 20% removed
0612 | 40% removed
0613 | 60% removed
0614 | ```
0615 | 
0616 | At each level:
0617 | 
0618 | 1. perturb the pattern,
0619 | 2. evolve it,
0620 | 3. measure survival,
0621 | 4. measure structural similarity,
0622 | 5. repeat across positions or random seeds.
0623 | 
0624 | Then plot:
0625 | 
0626 | ```text
0627 | damage severity
0628 |       ↓
0629 | response
0630 | ```
0631 | 
0632 | ![A Game of Life glider undergoing a controlled perturbation](/images/books/digital-life/ch05-glider-damage.gif)
0633 | 
0634 | Now we can ask whether degradation is:
0635 | 
0636 | ```text
0637 | gradual
0638 | abrupt
0639 | threshold-like
0640 | position-dependent
0641 | ```
0642 | 
0643 | That tells us much more than one impressive animation.
0644 | 
0645 | ---
0646 | 
0647 | ## Damage Location Matters
0648 | 
0649 | Suppose a structure contains one hundred active cells.
0650 | 
0651 | Removing ten cells from one region might do almost nothing.  
0652 | Removing ten elsewhere might destroy the pattern completely.
0653 | 
0654 | So:
0655 | 
0656 | ```text
0657 | amount of damage
0658 | ```
0659 | 
0660 | is not enough.
0661 | 
0662 | We also need:
0663 | 
0664 | ```text
0665 | location of damage
0666 | ```
0667 | 
0668 | This allows us to search for:
0669 | 
0670 | ```text
0671 | fragile regions
0672 | redundant regions
0673 | critical structures
0674 | distributed organization
0675 | ```
0676 | 
0677 | Eventually this becomes a question about where the system's causal organization actually lives.
0678 | 
0679 | Is function concentrated?  
0680 | Distributed?  
0681 | Redundant?  
0682 | Does there even exist a clean spatial location corresponding to the mechanism?
0683 | 
0684 | We should not assume the answer in advance.
0685 | 
0686 | ---
0687 | 
0688 | ## Repeated Perturbation Is a Stronger Test
0689 | 
0690 | Suppose a system survives one intervention.
0691 | 
0692 | Damage it again.  
0693 | Then again.
0694 | 
0695 | ```text
0696 | damage
0697 |   ↓
0698 | response
0699 |   ↓
0700 | damage
0701 |   ↓
0702 | response
0703 |   ↓
0704 | damage
0705 |   ↓
0706 | response
0707 | ```
0708 | 
0709 | A system might survive one perturbation by consuming redundancy that never returns.  
0710 | Another might repeatedly reconstruct the lost organization.
0711 | 
0712 | Those are different mechanisms.
0713 | 
0714 | So repeated intervention lets us distinguish:
0715 | 
0716 | ```text
0717 | one-time tolerance
0718 | ```
0719 | 
0720 | from:
0721 | 
0722 | ```text
0723 | sustained recovery capacity
0724 | ```
0725 | 
0726 | Again, the perturbation reveals structure that clean observation hides.
0727 | 
0728 | ---
0729 | 
0730 | ## Damage Does Not Have to Mean Deleting Cells
0731 | 
0732 | The deeper idea is not “injury.”  
0733 | It is intervention.
0734 | 
0735 | We could alter:
0736 | 
0737 | ```text
0738 | state
0739 | parameters
0740 | environment
0741 | resource access
0742 | boundary conditions
0743 | noise level
0744 | update timing
0745 | other interacting structures
0746 | ```
0747 | 
0748 | The common pattern is:
0749 | 
0750 | ```text
0751 | hold most things fixed
0752 |       ↓
0753 | change one defined condition
0754 |       ↓
0755 | measure the response
0756 | ```
0757 | 
0758 | This matters because a capability becomes much more informative when we know where it fails.
0759 | 
0760 | A system that works only under one carefully selected setup may still be interesting.  
0761 | But the boundary of the claim is narrow.  
0762 | Perturbation helps us find that boundary.
0763 | 
0764 | ---
0765 | 
0766 | ## The Glider Fails the Stronger Test
0767 | 
0768 | Return to the glider.
0769 | 
0770 | It is:
0771 | 
0772 | ```text
0773 | localized
0774 | periodic
0775 | translating
0776 | dynamically persistent
0777 | ```
0778 | 
0779 | But perturb the wrong cell and its characteristic trajectory disappears.
0780 | 
0781 | That does not make the glider less interesting.  
0782 | It makes our description more precise.
0783 | 
0784 | We can say:
0785 | 
0786 | > **The glider exhibits translating dynamic persistence under undisturbed Game of Life dynamics.**
0787 | 
0788 | We should not infer:
0789 | 
0790 | > The glider exhibits robust self-maintenance under damage.
0791 | 
0792 | Those are different achievements.  
0793 | And now we have an experiment that separates them.
0794 | 
0795 | ---
0796 | 
0797 | ## Could Local Dynamics Regenerate Structure?
0798 | 
0799 | Now the stronger question becomes interesting.
0800 | 
0801 | Can we build a system where:
0802 | 
0803 | ```text
0804 | organized state
0805 |       ↓
0806 | perturbation
0807 |       ↓
0808 | organization disrupted
0809 |       ↓
0810 | local dynamics continue
0811 |       ↓
0812 | organization returns
0813 | ```
0814 | 
0815 | without a central controller simply storing the desired image and repainting it?
0816 | 
0817 | That would be a more interesting mechanism.
0818 | 
0819 | If we eventually encounter or construct such a system, the protocol is already waiting for it:
0820 | 
0821 | ```text
0822 | How much perturbation?
0823 | 
0824 | Where?
0825 | 
0826 | How many trials?
0827 | 
0828 | How similar was the recovered organization?
0829 | 
0830 | How long did recovery take?
0831 | 
0832 | What baseline are we comparing against?
0833 | 
0834 | Does recovery generalize to perturbations not used to construct the system?
0835 | ```
0836 | 
0837 | The measurement rules come before the spectacular GIF.
0838 | 
0839 | ---
0840 | 
0841 | ## Intervention Is Becoming Part of Our Method
0842 | 
0843 | We started the book with:
0844 | 
0845 | ```text
0846 | state
0847 | +
0848 | local interaction
0849 | +
0850 | time
0851 | ```
0852 | 
0853 | Then we added observation.  
0854 | Now we have added intervention.
0855 | 
0856 | Our laboratory increasingly looks like:
0857 | 
0858 | ```mermaid
0859 | flowchart TD
0860 |     A[System] --> B[Observation]
0861 |     B --> C[Hypothesis]
0862 |     C --> D[Controlled Intervention]
0863 |     D --> E[Response]
0864 |     E --> F[Measurement]
0865 |     F --> G[Bounded Claim]
0866 |     G -.->|refine| C
0867 | ```
0868 | 
0869 | This is a major step.
0870 | 
0871 | We are no longer merely asking:
0872 | 
0873 | > What does the system do?
0874 | 
0875 | We can ask:
0876 | 
0877 | > **What makes the system able to do it?**
0878 | 
0879 | ---
0880 | 
0881 | ## Attack the Capability
0882 | 
0883 | This gives us a general rule for the rest of the book.
0884 | 
0885 | If a system appears to possess:
0886 | 
0887 | ```text
0888 | memory
0889 | persistence
0890 | regeneration
0891 | adaptation
0892 | inheritance
0893 | coordination
0894 | ```
0895 | 
0896 | do not only observe the condition in which the capability appears.  
0897 | Intervene on the mechanism that supposedly supports it.
0898 | 
0899 | For memory:
0900 | 
0901 | ```text
0902 | remove or corrupt candidate stored state
0903 | ```
0904 | 
0905 | For regeneration:
0906 | 
0907 | ```text
0908 | damage the organization
0909 | ```
0910 | 
0911 | For adaptation:
0912 | 
0913 | ```text
0914 | change the environment
0915 | ```
0916 | 
0917 | For inheritance:
0918 | 
0919 | ```text
0920 | compare successors with and without inherited information
0921 | ```
0922 | 
0923 | For coordination:
0924 | 
0925 | ```text
0926 | break communication or alter neighbors
0927 | ```
0928 | 
0929 | Then ask whether the capability survives.
0930 | 
0931 | That moves us from association toward mechanism.
0932 | 
0933 | ---
0934 | 
0935 | ## And Now Reproduction Enters — Carefully
0936 | 
0937 | Damage raises another possibility.
0938 | 
0939 | Instead of requiring one organization to persist indefinitely, a system might produce another organization resembling itself.
0940 | 
0941 | That would move persistence from:
0942 | 
0943 | ```text
0944 | one continuing instance
0945 | ```
0946 | 
0947 | to something like:
0948 | 
0949 | ```text
0950 | information or organization
0951 | continuing across instances
0952 | ```
0953 | 
0954 | Biology uses this strategy extensively.
0955 | 
0956 | But we should not therefore assume that digital life must.
0957 | 
0958 | A digital system might:
0959 | 
0960 | ```text
0961 | persist indefinitely
0962 | repair itself
0963 | grow continuously
0964 | fork
0965 | copy
0966 | checkpoint
0967 | restore
0968 | merge
0969 | ```
0970 | 
0971 | Reproduction is only one possible mechanism among several.
0972 | 
0973 | So the next chapter is not asking:
0974 | 
0975 | > What must life do next?
0976 | 
0977 | It is asking a narrower experimental question:
0978 | 
0979 | > **What changes when one persistent pattern can produce another?**
0980 | 
0981 | That question brings new traps.
0982 | 
0983 | A duplicate is not automatically offspring.  
0984 | Similarity is not automatically causal reproduction.  
0985 | Variation is not automatically inheritance.  
0986 | Mutation is not automatically evolution.
0987 | 
0988 | Before we can use any of those words, we need mechanisms and tests.
0989 | 
0990 | So next we ask:
0991 | 
0992 | > **Can It Make Another One?**
```


---
