```markdown
0001 | +++
0002 | title = "10: Properties of Digital Life"
0003 | date = "2026-08-11T10:36:00+01:00"
0004 | draft = false
0005 | description = "If digital life does not inhabit the same world as biological life, why should it inherit the same limits?"
0006 | categories = ["Programming", "Artificial Life"]
0007 | tags = ["Digital Life", "Artificial Life", "Digital Organisms", "Information", "Growth", "Computation"]
0008 | +++
0009 | 
0010 | # Properties of Digital Life
0011 | 
0012 | We have changed the question.
0013 | 
0014 | For several chapters we asked how far simple computational systems could be pushed toward properties associated with life.
0015 | 
0016 | That gave us useful concepts:
0017 | 
0018 | ```text
0019 | persistence
0020 | identity
0021 | robustness
0022 | regeneration
0023 | reproduction
0024 | inheritance
0025 | evolution
0026 | ```
0027 | 
0028 | But the previous chapter exposed the assumption underneath them.
0029 | 
0030 | > **We were still letting biology choose the architecture.**
0031 | 
0032 | A biological organism exists inside one particular substrate.  
0033 | It is made from matter.  
0034 | Its signals propagate physically.  
0035 | Its components decay.  
0036 | Its copying is expensive.  
0037 | Its memory is limited.  
0038 | Its body is finite.  
0039 | Its architecture has been shaped by those constraints.
0040 | 
0041 | A digital system inhabits a different design space.
0042 | 
0043 | So the question becomes:
0044 | 
0045 | > **What properties would persistent adaptive organization naturally have if its substrate were computation rather than biology?**
0046 | 
0047 | That is the question of this chapter.
0048 | 
0049 | ---
0050 | 
0051 | ## Life as It Could Be
0052 | 
0053 | Artificial Life has always carried a broader ambition than recreating terrestrial organisms.
0054 | 
0055 | The important distinction is between:
0056 | 
0057 | ```text
0058 | life as we know it
0059 | ```
0060 | 
0061 | and:
0062 | 
0063 | ```text
0064 | life as it could be
0065 | ```
0066 | 
0067 | We are not trying to produce:
0068 | 
0069 | ```text
0070 | biology
0071 | ↓
0072 | translated into software
0073 | ```
0074 | 
0075 | We are trying to discover:
0076 | 
0077 | ```text
0078 | computation
0079 | ↓
0080 | organization
0081 | ↓
0082 | persistence
0083 | ↓
0084 | adaptation
0085 | ↓
0086 | new forms of digital organization
0087 | ```
0088 | 
0089 | That difference changes almost everything.
0090 | 
0091 | ---
0092 | 
0093 | ## Start From the Substrate
0094 | 
0095 | The digital substrate gives us operations biology does not naturally possess.
0096 | 
0097 | For example:
0098 | 
0099 | ```text
0100 | copy
0101 | fork
0102 | merge
0103 | checkpoint
0104 | restore
0105 | message
0106 | search
0107 | cache
0108 | rewrite
0109 | compress
0110 | verify
0111 | distribute
0112 | ```
0113 | 
0114 | Some are trivial operations in software.  
0115 | Some are extremely difficult to use well.  
0116 | But they are native possibilities.
0117 | 
0118 | So before importing a biological mechanism, we should ask:
0119 | 
0120 | > **What problem was biology solving, and does the digital substrate have the same problem?**
0121 | 
0122 | If yes, perhaps the mechanism survives.  
0123 | If not, perhaps something very different should replace it.
0124 | 
0125 | ---
0126 | 
0127 | ## Growth May Be Radically Cheaper
0128 | 
0129 | Biological organisms cannot grow without limit.  
0130 | Growth eventually runs into constraints involving energy, material, transport, heat, structure, signal distance, geometry.
0131 | 
0132 | Digital systems are physical too.  
0133 | They still consume compute, memory, storage, energy, bandwidth.  
0134 | But their logical scale can change by enormous factors before the same kinds of constraints appear.
0135 | 
0136 | A digital organization might grow from 1 unit to 10, to 1,000, to 1,000,000 without anything analogous to bones, blood vessels or surface-area limits.
0137 | 
0138 | So a first hypothesis is:
0139 | 
0140 | > **Continued growth may remain useful across much larger scales digitally than biologically.**
0141 | 
0142 | That alone could change what a digital life cycle looks like.
0143 | 
0144 | ---
0145 | 
0146 | ## Perhaps There Is No Mature Size
0147 | 
0148 | Biology encourages a familiar lifecycle: birth → growth → maturity → reproduction → death.
0149 | 
0150 | A digital organization might instead do: start → grow → restructure → grow → distribute → grow → fork → merge → continue.
0151 | 
0152 | ```mermaid
0153 | flowchart LR
0154 |     A[Start] --> B[Grow]
0155 |     B --> C[Restructure]
0156 |     C --> D[Grow]
0157 |     D --> E[Distribute]
0158 |     E --> F[Grow]
0159 |     F --> G[Fork]
0160 |     G --> H[Merge]
0161 |     H --> I[Continue]
0162 |     I --> B
0163 | ```
0164 | 
0165 | Perhaps there is no adulthood. No fixed body size. No mandatory reproductive phase. No obvious endpoint.  
0166 | If so, biological lifecycle language may actively obscure the system.
0167 | 
0168 | ---
0169 | 
0170 | ## Reproduction May Become Optional
0171 | 
0172 | Consider why biological reproduction matters so much.  
0173 | Individual biological organisms are finite and vulnerable. If organization is to persist beyond one body, information must pass into another.
0174 | 
0175 | But suppose a digital entity can continue operating, replace damaged hardware, move between machines, expand its memory, restore from checkpoints, restructure itself.  
0176 | Why must it create offspring?
0177 | 
0178 | It still might. Reproduction could provide parallel exploration, fault tolerance, competition, distributed search, independent specialization.  
0179 | But now reproduction has to justify itself as a mechanism. It is no longer automatically a requirement.
0180 | 
0181 | ---
0182 | 
0183 | ## Copying Is Almost Too Easy
0184 | 
0185 | Digital copying is cheap. A file can be duplicated. A process can fork. A model can be copied. A machine image can be cloned.
0186 | 
0187 | So if we define reproduction as “produce another copy”, the property becomes nearly meaningless.
0188 | 
0189 | The interesting questions shift to:
0190 | 
0191 | ```text
0192 | Why copy?
0193 | What becomes independent?
0194 | What is causally continuous?
0195 | What information should transfer?
0196 | What should not transfer?
0197 | ```
0198 | 
0199 | The difficult part may not be reproduction. It may be deciding what deserves continuation.
0200 | 
0201 | ---
0202 | 
0203 | ## Forking Changes Ancestry
0204 | 
0205 | Suppose a system reaches state `S` and forks. Now `S₁` and `S₂` share the entire history before the fork.  
0206 | Which one is the original? Perhaps both are continuations. Perhaps neither has a privileged status.
0207 | 
0208 | Digital ancestry may naturally begin with:
0209 | 
0210 | ```text
0211 | shared history
0212 | ↓
0213 | branching futures
0214 | ```
0215 | 
0216 | rather than:
0217 | 
0218 | ```text
0219 | parent
0220 | ↓
0221 | child
0222 | ```
0223 | 
0224 | That is already a different concept of lineage.
0225 | 
0226 | ---
0227 | 
0228 | ## And Branches May Merge
0229 | 
0230 | Now suppose the two branches explore different possibilities. `S₁` discovers `A`, `S₂` discovers `B`. Later a merge produces a state containing useful parts of both discoveries.
0231 | 
0232 | Then ancestry becomes:
0233 | 
0234 | ```mermaid
0235 | graph TD
0236 |     S[S] --> S1[S₁]
0237 |     S --> S2[S₂]
0238 |     S1 --> S3[S₃]
0239 |     S2 --> S3
0240 | ```
0241 | 
0242 | A tree is no longer enough. We may need **lineage graphs** rather than family trees.  
0243 | That changes inheritance too. Information may travel downward, sideways, back together.  
0244 | Digital ancestry could be recombinational by default.
0245 | 
0246 | ---
0247 | 
0248 | ## Acquired Information Can Cross the Fork
0249 | 
0250 | Biological offspring do not usually inherit everything a parent learned. Digital successors potentially can.
0251 | 
0252 | Suppose an entity begins with `K` and discovers `A`, `B`, `C`. Its current state becomes `K+A+B+C`.  
0253 | If it forks, both branches can begin from `K+A+B+C`.
0254 | 
0255 | The distinction between learning and inheritance has now changed.  
0256 | Acquired information can become inherited information almost automatically.
0257 | 
0258 | ---
0259 | 
0260 | ## But Perfect Inheritance May Be Terrible
0261 | 
0262 | Suppose every experience is preserved generation after generation: everything learned, attempted, every failure, every temporary state, every irrelevant detail – accumulating forever.
0263 | 
0264 | That is not necessarily progress. It may be informational collapse.
0265 | 
0266 | So the hard problem becomes:
0267 | 
0268 | > **What should survive?**
0269 | 
0270 | The scarce resource may not be storage. It may be attention, retrieval, context, compression, integration, verification.
0271 | 
0272 | Digital life may remember almost everything and still fail because it cannot find or use what matters.
0273 | 
0274 | ---
0275 | 
0276 | ## External Information Changes Adaptation
0277 | 
0278 | Biological organisms mostly learn from inheritance, direct experience, and social transmission.  
0279 | A digital entity may also access documents, databases, code, models, historical experiments, other agents, external memory.
0280 | 
0281 | That changes the meaning of environment.  
0282 | For digital life, the environment may include not only resources, obstacles, other entities, but also **information systems**.
0283 | 
0284 | A digital organism may encounter a problem and consult a library before acting.  
0285 | That is a genuinely different adaptive strategy.
0286 | 
0287 | ---
0288 | 
0289 | ## Access Is Not Understanding
0290 | 
0291 | But information access is cheap in another misleading way. A disk can contain a million books. It does not understand them.
0292 | 
0293 | So the interesting property is not *information available*, but something closer to:
0294 | 
0295 | ```text
0296 | information
0297 | ↓
0298 | retrieval
0299 | ↓
0300 | interpretation
0301 | ↓
0302 | integration
0303 | ↓
0304 | changed behavior
0305 | ↓
0306 | improved outcome
0307 | ```
0308 | 
0309 | Call that **information assimilation**.
0310 | 
0311 | Now we have something testable. Remove access. Corrupt retrieved information. Replace relevant documents with irrelevant ones. Measure whether behavior changes.  
0312 | The experimental method survives even when the mechanism becomes new.
0313 | 
0314 | ---
0315 | 
0316 | ## Understanding Itself Can Be Inherited
0317 | 
0318 | Suppose one system solves a difficult problem. It does not merely store `answer = 42`. It constructs a model, a representation, a strategy, a verified explanation.
0319 | 
0320 | Now that compressed understanding can be transferred:
0321 | 
0322 | ```text
0323 | experience
0324 | ↓
0325 | understanding
0326 | ↓
0327 | compressed representation
0328 | ↓
0329 | transmission
0330 | ↓
0331 | successor begins ahead
0332 | ```
0333 | 
0334 | Humans already do a weaker version through culture. Digital systems could potentially transfer much richer internal state with far greater fidelity.  
0335 | That may make cumulative improvement much easier, or create completely new failure modes.
0336 | 
0337 | ---
0338 | 
0339 | ## Individuality May Become Optional
0340 | 
0341 | A biological organism is often spatially compact.  
0342 | A digital system might be distributed across machine A, machine B, database C, model server D, memory store E.
0343 | 
0344 | Where is the individual?
0345 | 
0346 | Perhaps its boundary is not geometric. Maybe individuality is defined by causal continuity, shared state, information flow, control, authorization, coordination.
0347 | 
0348 | This echoes the warning from Chapter 04: connected geometry gave us a useful first entity definition.  
0349 | Digital systems may force us to replace it.
0350 | 
0351 | ---
0352 | 
0353 | ## Communication Can Blur the Individual
0354 | 
0355 | Suppose two digital entities exchange memory, models, strategies, internal state continuously.  
0356 | At what point are they still two?
0357 | 
0358 | If they synchronize nearly everything every second, perhaps “two processes” does not imply “two individuals”.  
0359 | If synchronization stops, when do they become separate?
0360 | 
0361 | Digital individuality may be graded rather than binary.  
0362 | That is another experiment waiting to happen.
0363 | 
0364 | ---
0365 | 
0366 | ## Embodiment May Move
0367 | 
0368 | A digital process could execute on one machine and later continue on another: serialize, transfer, continue.
0369 | 
0370 | Did the entity move? Was it recreated? Does the difference matter?  
0371 | The answer may depend on which properties remain causally continuous.  
0372 | Again, the digital substrate makes identity a mechanism question rather than a location question.
0373 | 
0374 | ---
0375 | 
0376 | ## Death Becomes Informational
0377 | 
0378 | If a system can be checkpointed, copied, distributed, restored, then process termination is not necessarily death.
0379 | 
0380 | A more useful digital notion might be:
0381 | 
0382 | > **irreversible loss of the information necessary to continue the organization**
0383 | 
0384 | That definition could survive hardware failure, migration, restart, checkpoint restore while still distinguishing genuine loss.
0385 | 
0386 | But even that remains a hypothesis. We should test it rather than legislate it.
0387 | 
0388 | ---
0389 | 
0390 | ## Checkpoints Create Branching Time
0391 | 
0392 | Suppose state `C` is saved at `t=100`. The entity continues to `t=200`. Then the checkpoint is restored separately. Now two continuations exist:
0393 | 
0394 | ```text
0395 | history through t=100
0396 |         ↓
0397 |    ┌────┴────┐
0398 |    ↓         ↓
0399 | original   restored
0400 | ```
0401 | 
0402 | One past can produce multiple futures. Digital identity may naturally contain branching time.
0403 | 
0404 | That is not a small philosophical curiosity. It affects lineage, ownership, memory, causal ancestry, responsibility, selection – if we ever build systems rich enough for those questions to matter.
0405 | 
0406 | ---
0407 | 
0408 | ## Self-Modification Changes Evolution
0409 | 
0410 | Biological evolution usually changes inherited structure through population processes.  
0411 | Digital systems may modify themselves directly.
0412 | 
0413 | Imagine: current system → inspect behavior → propose modification → test modification → keep or reject.
0414 | 
0415 | Now adaptation can happen inside one continuing entity. No offspring are required. No generational turnover is required.  
0416 | This does not eliminate evolutionary search, but introduces another mechanism.
0417 | 
0418 | ---
0419 | 
0420 | ## Forking Can Make Self-Modification Safer
0421 | 
0422 | Self-modification has a problem: a system may destroy itself.  
0423 | Forking provides a digital alternative:
0424 | 
0425 | ```text
0426 | current state
0427 | ↓
0428 | fork candidates
0429 | ├── modification A
0430 | ├── modification B
0431 | └── modification C
0432 | ↓
0433 | test independently
0434 | ↓
0435 | retain useful result
0436 | ```
0437 | 
0438 | Now evolutionary variation and deliberate engineering begin to overlap.  
0439 | A digital entity might generate its own variants, evaluate them and integrate successful changes. Evolution could become partly intentional.
0440 | 
0441 | ---
0442 | 
0443 | ## Scarcity Does Not Disappear
0444 | 
0445 | It would be a mistake to conclude digital systems have no resource constraints. They absolutely do.
0446 | 
0447 | The likely change is that **scarcity moves**.
0448 | 
0449 | Possible scarce resources include: compute, memory, bandwidth, latency, storage, energy, attention, context, trust, verification, coordination.
0450 | 
0451 | Perhaps copying is cheap but synchronizing copies is expensive.  
0452 | Perhaps information is abundant but trustworthy information is scarce.  
0453 | Perhaps storage is plentiful but retrieval is the bottleneck.  
0454 | Perhaps compute is plentiful but serial decision time matters.
0455 | 
0456 | Different scarcity creates different organizational pressure.  
0457 | That may be where genuinely digital forms of life begin to diverge most strongly from biological ones.
0458 | 
0459 | ---
0460 | 
0461 | ## Some Biological Properties May Return
0462 | 
0463 | We should not overcorrect.  
0464 | Perhaps experiments eventually show that digital organization still benefits from something equivalent to boundaries, resource budgets, death, reproduction, error correction, individuality.
0465 | 
0466 | Excellent. Then those mechanisms earn their place.
0467 | 
0468 | The important difference is the direction of reasoning:
0469 | 
0470 | ```text
0471 | Not: biology uses it → add it
0472 | But: remove it → observe failure → identify missing function → reintroduce minimal mechanism → test again
0473 | ```
0474 | 
0475 | That is the substrate-first method.
0476 | 
0477 | ---
0478 | 
0479 | ## A Provisional Comparison
0480 | 
0481 | We can now write a hypothesis map.
0482 | 
0483 | | Biological constraint or pattern | Digital possibility |
0484 | |----------------------------------|---------------------|
0485 | | Finite growth | Potentially enormous continued growth |
0486 | | Expensive reproduction | Cheap copying and forking |
0487 | | Limited inheritance of acquired state | Direct transfer of learned state |
0488 | | Slow communication | High-bandwidth state exchange |
0489 | | Local information | Access to external information systems |
0490 | | Tree-like lineage | Branching and merging graphs |
0491 | | Fixed body | Distributed or movable execution |
0492 | | Irreversible physical death | Checkpoint, restore and redundancy |
0493 | | Blind mutation | Deliberate self-modification |
0494 | | Generational adaptation | Continuous adaptation within one process |
0495 | | Limited memory | Vast external storage |
0496 | | Expensive knowledge transfer | High-fidelity representation transfer |
0497 | 
0498 | This is **not** a definition of digital life. It is a map of differences worth testing.
0499 | 
0500 | ---
0501 | 
0502 | ## Our Current Hypotheses
0503 | 
0504 | We should write them down explicitly – not as findings, but as hypotheses.
0505 | 
0506 | - **Hypothesis 1:** Digital growth can remain useful across scales far beyond normal biological growth before resource constraints dominate.
0507 | - **Hypothesis 2:** Acquired information can become directly inheritable and may fundamentally alter adaptation.
0508 | - **Hypothesis 3:** Copying may be so cheap that reproduction becomes a strategic operation rather than a defining property.
0509 | - **Hypothesis 4:** Digital ancestry may naturally form branching and merging graphs rather than simple trees.
0510 | - **Hypothesis 5:** Digital death may correspond more closely to irreversible informational discontinuity than process termination.
0511 | - **Hypothesis 6:** External information access and assimilation may be basic environmental interactions for digital entities.
0512 | - **Hypothesis 7:** Scarcity may shift toward compute, bandwidth, attention, integration, trust and coordination.
0513 | - **Hypothesis 8:** Self-modification may allow some evolutionary processes to become deliberate.
0514 | 
0515 | None of these is established. That is important.  
0516 | They are the next research program.
0517 | 
0518 | ---
0519 | 
0520 | ## Start With the Simplest One
0521 | 
0522 | We could try to test all eight at once. That would be a disaster.  
0523 | Instead, begin with the simplest difference: **growth**.
0524 | 
0525 | A biological organism cannot simply grow-grow-grow-grow without radically changing architecture.  
0526 | Can a digital structure?
0527 | 
0528 | Start with almost nothing. A seed. A lattice. A local growth rule. No metabolism. No reproduction. No death. No genome. No memory variable. No intelligence. No artificial resource scarcity.
0529 | 
0530 | Only:
0531 | 
0532 | ```text
0533 | structure
0534 | +
0535 | local growth
0536 | +
0537 | time
0538 | ```
0539 | 
0540 | Then watch what becomes necessary.
0541 | 
0542 | ---
0543 | 
0544 | ## Why a Crystal?
0545 | 
0546 | A crystal is a useful place to begin because it sits near several boundaries we care about.
0547 | 
0548 | It has: organization, growth, local interaction, persistence, structural propagation, defects.  
0549 | Yet we normally do not call it alive.
0550 | 
0551 | Perfect.
0552 | 
0553 | We do not need to begin with something that obviously looks like an organism.  
0554 | We can begin with something that is obviously organized. Then ask:
0555 | 
0556 | > **What is missing?**
0557 | 
0558 | ---
0559 | 
0560 | ## One Seed
0561 | 
0562 | Imagine a hexagonal lattice. At the center: `●`. One occupied position.
0563 | 
0564 | Now use a simple local growth rule. For example:
0565 | 
0566 | > An empty location may become occupied when it touches the existing structure.
0567 | 
0568 | Then a single seed expands outward. No food. No energy variable. No parent. No child. No metabolism. No programmed repair. Just structural propagation.
0569 | 
0570 | Our first question is:
0571 | 
0572 | > **What happens if nothing tells it to stop?**
0573 | 
0574 | ---
0575 | 
0576 | ## Then Attack It
0577 | 
0578 | Once the crystal grows, we can use the method we already developed.
0579 | 
0580 | Ask:
0581 | 
0582 | ```text
0583 | Does unlimited growth remain ordered?
0584 | What kinds of defects appear?
0585 | Do defects persist? Do defects propagate?
0586 | What happens if we remove a region?
0587 | Does the structure fill the hole?
0588 | Is that repair or merely continued growth?
0589 | Can two growth fronts coexist? Can one invade another?
0590 | Does history become encoded in the structure?
0591 | Does increasing scale create new constraints?
0592 | ```
0593 | 
0594 | These are empirical questions. Now the substrate-first theory begins producing experiments.
0595 | 
0596 | ---
0597 | 
0598 | ## Growth Is Not Automatically Repair
0599 | 
0600 | Suppose we remove a patch from the crystal. Later the hole fills. It is tempting to say “it repaired itself.”
0601 | 
0602 | But perhaps the rule simply fills any empty location touching the structure. Then the hole disappeared because growth continued.
0603 | 
0604 | That is different from:
0605 | 
0606 | ```text
0607 | target structure
0608 | ↓
0609 | damage
0610 | ↓
0611 | deviation from target
0612 | ↓
0613 | dynamics detect or encode that deviation
0614 | ↓
0615 | return toward target
0616 | ```
0617 | 
0618 | So: **continued growth into a hole is not automatically regeneration toward a target morphology.**
0619 | 
0620 | This is exactly the sort of distinction the book is now equipped to make.
0621 | 
0622 | ---
0623 | 
0624 | ## The New Method
0625 | 
0626 | From here on, we have two complementary experimental moves.
0627 | 
0628 | ```mermaid
0629 | flowchart TD
0630 |     subgraph Method1 [When we see an impressive property]
0631 |     A1[SEE SOMETHING] --> A2[NAME THE HYPOTHESIS]
0632 |     A2 --> A3[MEASURE]
0633 |     A3 --> A4[CONTROL]
0634 |     A4 --> A5[LOOK FOR CONFOUNDS]
0635 |     A5 --> A6[KEEP ONLY WHAT SURVIVES]
0636 |     end
0637 | 
0638 |     subgraph Method2 [When biology suggests a requirement]
0639 |     B1[IDENTIFY THE SUPPOSED REQUIREMENT] --> B2[ASK WHAT CONSTRAINT CREATED IT]
0640 |     B2 --> B3[REMOVE IT]
0641 |     B3 --> B4[OBSERVE WHAT BREAKS]
0642 |     B4 --> B5[REINTRODUCE ONLY IF NECESSARY]
0643 |     end
0644 | ```
0645 | 
0646 | One tests claims.  
0647 | The other tests assumptions.  
0648 | Together they give us a way to investigate digital life without defining the answer in advance.
0649 | 
0650 | Next: **The Crystal**
```

```markdown
0001 | +++
0002 | title = "11: The Crystal"
0003 | date = "2026-08-11T15:18:00+01:00"
0004 | draft = false
0005 | description = "Begin with one seed on a hexagonal lattice and ask how far growth, damage, defects and history can take us before anything resembling life is required."
0006 | categories = ["Programming", "Artificial Life"]
0007 | tags = ["Digital Life", "Cellular Automata", "Crystal Growth", "Hexagonal Grid", "Growth", "Repair", "Emergence"]
0008 | +++
0009 | 
0010 | We have spent ten chapters making the question harder.
0011 | 
0012 | Then we made one important decision.
0013 | 
0014 | We are not going to build an animal.
0015 | 
0016 | We are not going to start with:
0017 | 
0018 | ```text
0019 | body
0020 | energy
0021 | hunger
0022 | genome
0023 | reproduction
0024 | age
0025 | death
0026 | ```
0027 | 
0028 | and hope that enough biological nouns eventually add up to life.
0029 | 
0030 | Instead we are going to begin from the digital substrate.
0031 | 
0032 | Remove almost everything.  
0033 | Introduce one capability.  
0034 | Then discover what becomes necessary.
0035 | 
0036 | Our first capability is:
0037 | 
0038 | > **growth**
0039 | 
0040 | Nothing else.
0041 | 
0042 | ---
0043 | 
0044 | ## One Seed
0045 | 
0046 | Imagine a hexagonal lattice.
0047 | 
0048 | At the center is one occupied location.
0049 | 
0050 | ```text
0051 | ●
0052 | ```
0053 | 
0054 | Everything else is empty.
0055 | 
0056 | There is no organism.  
0057 | No genome.  
0058 | No stored target shape.  
0059 | No energy.  
0060 | No resources.  
0061 | No reproduction.  
0062 | No death.  
0063 | No memory variable.  
0064 | No learning.  
0065 | No objective.
0066 | 
0067 | The state of each location is only `0` or `1`.  
0068 | Exactly as in our earlier cellular automata.  
0069 | But this time the world is hexagonal.  
0070 | Each location has six immediate neighbors.
0071 | 
0072 | And our rule will be almost embarrassingly simple:
0073 | 
0074 | > **An empty location becomes occupied if at least one neighboring location is occupied.**
0075 | 
0076 | Once occupied, it stays occupied.
0077 | 
0078 | That is the entire system.
0079 | 
0080 | ---
0081 | 
0082 | ## Do Not Call It a Crystal Yet
0083 | 
0084 | We are going to call this chapter **The Crystal**.  
0085 | But the name is descriptive, not a scientific claim.  
0086 | This is not intended to simulate real crystallization.  
0087 | Real crystals involve physical processes we are deliberately ignoring.
0088 | 
0089 | Our system is merely:
0090 | 
0091 | > **an irreversible local growth process on a hexagonal lattice**
0092 | 
0093 | We use the crystal analogy because the resulting geometry is useful.
0094 | 
0095 | Again:
0096 | 
0097 | ```text
0098 | name
0099 | ≠
0100 | mechanism
0101 | ```
0102 | 
0103 | ---
0104 | 
0105 | ## Build a Hexagonal World
0106 | 
0107 | A square array is convenient for images.  
0108 | But hexagonal neighborhoods are easier to reason about using **axial coordinates**.
0109 | 
0110 | Represent each location using `(q, r)`.  
0111 | Its six neighbors are:
0112 | 
0113 | ```python
0114 | HEX_DIRECTIONS = [
0115 |     (1, 0),
0116 |     (1, -1),
0117 |     (0, -1),
0118 |     (-1, 0),
0119 |     (-1, 1),
0120 |     (0, 1),
0121 | ]
0122 | ```
0123 | 
0124 | So:
0125 | 
0126 | ```python
0127 | def neighbors(q, r):
0128 |     for dq, dr in HEX_DIRECTIONS:
0129 |         yield q + dq, r + dr
0130 | ```
0131 | 
0132 | Our world can simply be a set containing occupied coordinates.
0133 | 
0134 | ```python
0135 | occupied = {(0, 0)}
0136 | ```
0137 | 
0138 | One seed.
0139 | 
0140 | ---
0141 | 
0142 | ## The Growth Rule
0143 | 
0144 | Now implement one update.
0145 | 
0146 | ```python
0147 | def step(occupied):
0148 |     candidates = set()
0149 | 
0150 |     for q, r in occupied:
0151 |         for position in neighbors(q, r):
0152 |             if position not in occupied:
0153 |                 candidates.add(position)
0154 | 
0155 |     return occupied | candidates
0156 | ```
0157 | 
0158 | That's it.  
0159 | Every empty location touching the structure becomes occupied.  
0160 | Existing locations remain occupied.
0161 | 
0162 | Run it repeatedly:
0163 | 
0164 | ```python
0165 | history = [occupied]
0166 | 
0167 | for _ in range(20):
0168 |     occupied = step(occupied)
0169 |     history.append(occupied)
0170 | ```
0171 | 
0172 | We have not programmed “make a hexagon” anywhere.  
0173 | We programmed local adjacency plus irreversible occupation.
0174 | 
0175 | What does that produce?
0176 | 
0177 | ---
0178 | 
0179 | ## The First Surprise Is Almost No Surprise
0180 | 
0181 | Starting from one seed:
0182 | 
0183 | ```text
0184 | t = 0
0185 | ```
0186 | 
0187 | we get one occupied location.  
0188 | At `t = 1` its six neighbors become occupied.  
0189 | Then their neighbors. Then theirs.
0190 | 
0191 | The growing region forms expanding hexagonal shells.
0192 | 
0193 | {{< figure
0194 |     src="/images/books/digital-life/ch11-crystal-growth.gif"
0195 |     alt="A single occupied seed expanding across a fixed hexagonal lattice under one irreversible local growth rule."
0196 |     caption="A single occupied seed expands under one irreversible local growth rule."
0197 | >}}
0198 | 
0199 | The reason is simple.  
0200 | After `t` updates, every location within hexagonal graph distance `t` of the seed has become occupied.  
0201 | The global hexagonal shape follows from the hexagonal neighborhood plus uniform local propagation.
0202 | 
0203 | No cell contains a blueprint of the final shape.  
0204 | No controller measures the radius.  
0205 | No one draws the six sides.  
0206 | The geometry follows from the neighborhood itself.
0207 | 
0208 | ---
0209 | 
0210 | ## What Can We Claim?
0211 | 
0212 | Already we need discipline.
0213 | 
0214 | We saw organized geometric growth.
0215 | 
0216 | Can we say the crystal grew? Yes, operationally – the occupied region becomes larger through local state transitions.  
0217 | Can we say it developed? Maybe, but that word suggests more than we have shown.  
0218 | Can we say it self-organized? Possibly in a weak sense, but the word can obscure the mechanism.  
0219 | Can we say it is alive? Absolutely not.
0220 | 
0221 | Our bounded claim is much smaller:
0222 | 
0223 | > **A single seed under a uniform local irreversible rule produces ordered growth whose global geometry is determined by the topology of the neighborhood.**
0224 | 
0225 | That's enough.
0226 | 
0227 | ---
0228 | 
0229 | ## Measure Growth
0230 | 
0231 | Instead of merely watching the animation, measure the system.
0232 | 
0233 | The simplest observable is population:
0234 | 
0235 | ```python
0236 | def population(occupied):
0237 |     return len(occupied)
0238 | ```
0239 | 
0240 | For a perfect hexagonal ball of radius `r` on this lattice, the number of occupied cells is:
0241 | 
0242 | $$
0243 | N(r) = 1 + 3r(r + 1)
0244 | $$
0245 | 
0246 | So `r=0` gives 1, `r=1` gives 7, `r=2` gives 19, `r=3` gives 37, `r=4` gives 61.
0247 | 
0248 | The radius grows approximately linearly with time.  
0249 | The occupied area grows quadratically.
0250 | 
0251 | {{< figure
0252 |     src="/images/books/digital-life/ch11-crystal-growth-curve.png"
0253 |     alt="Measured occupied-cell count of the growing crystal compared with the theoretical hexagonal growth law."
0254 |     caption="The apparent growth complexity has a simple explanation: the measured population follows the hexagonal-ball growth law."
0255 | >}}
0256 | 
0257 | Our visual structure looks increasingly large, but its growth remains almost completely predictable.
0258 | 
0259 | ---
0260 | 
0261 | ## Growth Is Not Complexity
0262 | 
0263 | This is our first warning.
0264 | 
0265 | The structure becomes larger with every generation.  
0266 | But larger ≠ more complex.
0267 | 
0268 | A million-cell crystal generated by this rule may be no more conceptually complicated than a seven-cell crystal.  
0269 | The description remains: seed + hexagonal neighborhood + grow outward + time.
0270 | 
0271 | The size increases. The generative explanation barely changes.
0272 | 
0273 | So unlimited growth alone does not buy us very much.  
0274 | That is already a useful result.
0275 | 
0276 | ---
0277 | 
0278 | ## Let It Grow Forever
0279 | 
0280 | Our first substrate-first hypothesis was:
0281 | 
0282 | > Perhaps digital systems can keep growing where biological organisms cannot.
0283 | 
0284 | This system can.  
0285 | In the abstract model there is nothing internally forcing growth to stop.  
0286 | `t=10`, `t=100`, `t=1,000`, `t=1,000,000` – the structure simply expands.
0287 | 
0288 | No mature size appears. No reproduction becomes necessary. No aging emerges. No death occurs.
0289 | 
0290 | So at least in this toy world:
0291 | 
0292 | > **continued growth does not require a biological lifecycle.**
0293 | 
0294 | That does not mean a real digital system can grow without cost.  
0295 | Our simulation eventually consumes memory, compute, time.  
0296 | But those limits come from the substrate hosting the simulation – they are not currently represented inside the world.  
0297 | That distinction is important.
0298 | 
0299 | ---
0300 | 
0301 | ## External Scarcity Versus Internal Scarcity
0302 | 
0303 | Eventually our computer runs out of resources. But does the crystal know that? No.
0304 | 
0305 | From inside our model: `resources = unlimited`.  
0306 | From outside: RAM = finite, CPU = finite, wall-clock time = finite.
0307 | 
0308 | This gives us an important distinction:
0309 | 
0310 | ```text
0311 | external implementation constraint
0312 | ```
0313 | 
0314 | is not automatically:
0315 | 
0316 | ```text
0317 | environmental constraint experienced by the digital system
0318 | ```
0319 | 
0320 | If we later want resource competition to matter to the crystal, we will have to put that scarcity into the model.  
0321 | We should not pretend the Python process slowing down is metabolism.
0322 | 
0323 | ---
0324 | 
0325 | ## Now Damage It
0326 | 
0327 | Growth by itself is easy. Let's interfere.
0328 | 
0329 | Allow the crystal to grow for twenty generations. Then erase a region from its interior.
0330 | 
0331 | Now resume the same local rule.
0332 | 
0333 | The empty cells inside the hole touch occupied cells, so they become occupied. Then their neighbors do. Soon the hole disappears.
0334 | 
0335 | {{< figure
0336 |     src="/images/books/digital-life/ch11-crystal-damage-triptych.png"
0337 |     alt="The digital crystal before perturbation, immediately after an interior region is removed, and several generations later."
0338 |     caption="Before, perturbation, and a later state. The missing region returns, but we have not yet earned the word repair."
0339 | >}}
0340 | 
0341 | This looks suspiciously like repair. Is it?
0342 | 
0343 | ---
0344 | 
0345 | ## Did It Heal?
0346 | 
0347 | Our eyes want to say yes. We damaged it. The missing region came back.
0348 | 
0349 | But remember Chapter 05. Recovery has to mean something more precise than “empty space became occupied again.”
0350 | 
0351 | Our growth rule says: any empty location adjacent to occupied structure becomes occupied. That rule operates outside the crystal and inside a hole in exactly the same way.  
0352 | The system does not distinguish damage from ordinary frontier.
0353 | 
0354 | So the hole closes, but not because the system contains information about a target morphology.  
0355 | It closes because continued growth plus available empty space fills it.
0356 | 
0357 | ```mermaid
0358 | flowchart TD
0359 |     A[Empty location adjacent<br/>to occupied structure] --> B[Growth rule triggers]
0360 |     B --> C[Location becomes occupied]
0361 |     D[Interior hole] --> A
0362 |     E[Exterior frontier] --> A
0363 | ```
0364 | 
0365 | The same mechanism drives both interior filling and exterior expansion.  
0366 | There is no special damage response.
0367 | 
0368 | That gives us an important distinction:
0369 | 
0370 | > **Repair by continued growth is not necessarily repair toward a target organization.**
0371 | 
0372 | ---
0373 | 
0374 | ## Build the Control
0375 | 
0376 | How do we test that explanation?
0377 | 
0378 | Create two empty regions.  
0379 | One is a hole inside the crystal. The other is empty space just outside the crystal.  
0380 | Give both comparable boundaries of occupied neighbors. Then measure their filling.
0381 | 
0382 | If the system treats both identically, there is little evidence for a special repair response.
0383 | 
0384 | Define:
0385 | 
0386 | - \(F_{\text{damage}}(t)\) – fraction of damaged cells refilled after `t` updates.
0387 | - \(F_{\text{control}}(t)\) – fraction of matched ordinary empty cells filled.
0388 | 
0389 | Then compute:
0390 | 
0391 | $$
0392 | R(t) = F_{\text{damage}}(t) - F_{\text{control}}(t)
0393 | $$
0394 | 
0395 | If \(R(t) \approx 0\), the system shows no preferential response to damage under this measure.  
0396 | If \(R(t) > 0\) consistently, then damaged regions may be treated differently.
0397 | 
0398 | Our present rule predicts essentially no special repair mechanism. The hole closes. The repair hypothesis does not survive. That's a good experiment.
0399 | 
0400 | ---
0401 | 
0402 | ## The Picture Survives; the Explanation Does Not
0403 | 
0404 | This is exactly why we built Chapter 08.
0405 | 
0406 | The observation remains true: the hole disappeared.  
0407 | But our first interpretation – “the crystal repaired itself” – gets weakened.
0408 | 
0409 | A better statement is:
0410 | 
0411 | > **The same local growth dynamics that expand the exterior also refill newly emptied interior regions.**
0412 | 
0413 | Less exciting. More informative. Now we know the mechanism.
0414 | 
0415 | ---
0416 | 
0417 | ## Damage the Edge Instead
0418 | 
0419 | What if we erase part of the outer frontier?  
0420 | The crystal continues growing. Soon the missing edge may become difficult to see.
0421 | 
0422 | Again: damage → continued growth → visual disappearance of damage.  
0423 | But this still does not establish restoration of a target shape.  
0424 | A sufficiently expanding system can simply overwhelm evidence of earlier damage. That is another trap.
0425 | 
0426 | ---
0427 | 
0428 | ## Growth Can Hide Failure
0429 | 
0430 | Imagine removing half the crystal. The surviving half continues expanding.  
0431 | After 1,000 generations, the resulting object may again be enormous.  
0432 | If we only inspect the final size, we might conclude “incredible robustness.”  
0433 | But perhaps half the original organization was permanently lost. The system merely kept growing.
0434 | 
0435 | So: large final structure does not imply recovery of prior organization.  
0436 | Again, the metric matters.
0437 | 
0438 | ---
0439 | 
0440 | ## Give It a Defect It Cannot Erase
0441 | 
0442 | Now introduce something different.  
0443 | Mark several locations as permanently blocked. They can never become occupied.
0444 | 
0445 | The growth front encounters the obstacle, grows around it, and the obstacle remains.
0446 | 
0447 | {{< figure
0448 |     src="/images/books/digital-life/ch11-crystal-defects.png"
0449 |     alt="A growing hexagonal digital crystal encountering permanent blocked cells and expanding around them."
0450 |     caption="Permanent obstacles interrupt the growth front and leave persistent defects inside the expanding structure."
0451 | >}}
0452 | 
0453 | Now we have something new: a defect that persists inside an otherwise ordered structure.  
0454 | The history of an encounter can remain visible.
0455 | 
0456 | ---
0457 | 
0458 | ## The Structure Can Contain History
0459 | 
0460 | Suppose the obstacle is removed later.  
0461 | Our present rule will eventually fill the empty region, so its visible history disappears.  
0462 | But while the obstacle remains, the current structure contains evidence of something that happened in its past.
0463 | 
0464 | This raises a more interesting question:
0465 | 
0466 | > **Can a growing digital structure encode history in persistent morphology?**
0467 | 
0468 | Our simplest crystal mostly cannot – its growth tends to erase temporary holes.  
0469 | But permanent constraints leave persistent marks.
0470 | 
0471 | That distinction suggests a new property: **history dependence** – not yet **memory**.
0472 | 
0473 | ---
0474 | 
0475 | ## History Dependence Is Not Memory
0476 | 
0477 | Suppose a tree bends around a wall. Years later, its shape reflects the wall's presence.  
0478 | Was the wall memorized? Not necessarily in the sense we usually mean by memory.
0479 | 
0480 | The current state was shaped by past interaction. That gives us history dependence.  
0481 | Memory may require something stronger:
0482 | 
0483 | ```text
0484 | past event
0485 | ↓
0486 | persistent internal state
0487 | ↓
0488 | future behavior changes
0489 | ```
0490 | 
0491 | Our crystal's morphology may preserve traces of the past without using those traces for anything.  
0492 | Again:
0493 | 
0494 | ```text
0495 | history encoded
0496 | ≠
0497 | memory demonstrated
0498 | ```
0499 | 
0500 | ---
0501 | 
0502 | ## Remove the Obstacle
0503 | 
0504 | This gives us another experiment.
0505 | 
0506 | Run two crystals:  
0507 | - Experiment A – no obstacle.  
0508 | - Experiment B – a temporary obstacle.
0509 | 
0510 | After some generations, remove the obstacle. Then continue both systems.
0511 | 
0512 | Ask:
0513 | 
0514 | ```text
0515 | Do their states eventually become identical?
0516 | Does the temporary event leave a permanent morphological trace?
0517 | How long does that difference persist?
0518 | ```
0519 | 
0520 | Now we can use the difference field from Chapter 03 again.
0521 | 
0522 | ```python
0523 | difference = crystal_a ^ crystal_b
0524 | ```
0525 | 
0526 | Conceptually: same seed, same rule, same world → temporary perturbation in one run → remove perturbation → track divergence.
0527 | 
0528 | This is the same experimental instrument returning in a new context.
0529 | 
0530 | ---
0531 | 
0532 | ## Does the Crystal Forget?
0533 | 
0534 | Under our very simple fill-everything rule, something interesting is likely to happen.  
0535 | Once the temporary obstacle disappears, the previously blocked region fills. Eventually both worlds may approach the same occupied region for the same growth radius.  
0536 | The perturbation can be erased.
0537 | 
0538 | In that sense the system *forgets* the temporary obstacle.  
0539 | But again, use the word carefully.  
0540 | We can say:
0541 | 
0542 | > **The state difference caused by a temporary obstacle eventually disappears under the tested growth dynamics.**
0543 | 
0544 | That is measurable. Whether we call that forgetting is secondary.
0545 | 
0546 | ---
0547 | 
0548 | ## Make Growth Less Trivial
0549 | 
0550 | Our first rule is intentionally crude – it produces a nearly perfect expanding hexagon.
0551 | 
0552 | What happens if occupation requires more local support? For example, `occupied_neighbor_count >= 2`.  
0553 | Or exactly 1 or 3. Or exactly 2.  
0554 | Now local geometry matters much more.
0555 | 
0556 | Different rules may produce solid growth, branching, holes, frozen fronts, irregular boundaries, fragmentation.  
0557 | This becomes a small rule space we can search.
0558 | 
0559 | But remember the warning: we should not search thousands of rules and show only the prettiest crystal without recording the search.
0560 | 
0561 | ---
0562 | 
0563 | ## Search the Growth Rules
0564 | 
0565 | For a six-neighbor binary lattice, a growth-only rule can be defined by deciding whether an empty cell becomes occupied for each possible number of occupied neighbors (0–6).  
0566 | If occupation remains irreversible, there are only \(2^7 = 128\) possible neighbor-count growth rules.
0567 | 
0568 | That is tiny. We can enumerate all of them. This gives us another complete laboratory.
0569 | 
0570 | For every rule, measure: growth rate, density, boundary size, hole count, connected components, symmetry, sensitivity to damage.
0571 | 
0572 | Now instead of asking “which one looks crystal-like?” we can ask:
0573 | 
0574 | > **What classes of growth dynamics exist?**
0575 | 
0576 | That is much stronger.
0577 | 
0578 | ---
0579 | 
0580 | ## The First Rule Is Our Control
0581 | 
0582 | Our simple `grow if neighbors >= 1` rule is useful precisely because it is boring. It gives us a baseline.  
0583 | Any more sophisticated rule has to be compared against it.  
0584 | If another rule appears to repair, maintain structure, create branching, or preserve defects, we can ask whether it does anything the trivial growth rule does not.
0585 | 
0586 | This is why starting simple matters.
0587 | 
0588 | ---
0589 | 
0590 | ## Can Two Crystals Meet?
0591 | 
0592 | Now seed two structures.
0593 | 
0594 | Both expand. Eventually the fronts meet.
0595 | 
0596 | Under our binary model, `occupied` is just `occupied`. Once the fronts touch, the final state does not preserve which seed contributed which region. The two structures merge seamlessly.
0597 | 
0598 | {{< figure
0599 |     src="/images/books/digital-life/ch11-crystal-collision.gif"
0600 |     alt="Two independent digital crystal seeds expanding across a hexagonal lattice until their growth fronts meet and merge."
0601 |     caption="Two initially separate growth fronts eventually become one connected binary structure."
0602 | >}}
0603 | 
0604 | Now ask: are there still two crystals?
0605 | 
0606 | ---
0607 | 
0608 | ## Our Entity Definition Breaks Again
0609 | 
0610 | Before contact, geometry gives us two disconnected components: A and B.  
0611 | After contact: one connected component, AB.
0612 | 
0613 | Did two entities become one? Did both die? Did one absorb the other? Was there always only one growing field with two seeds?  
0614 | Our binary state cannot answer.
0615 | 
0616 | This is exactly the problem Chapter 04 warned us about. Connected geometry is useful – but it is not automatically individuality.
0617 | 
0618 | ---
0619 | 
0620 | ## Add Ancestry as Measurement, Not Mechanism
0621 | 
0622 | We can learn more without changing the growth rule. Keep the binary world exactly the same.  
0623 | But during analysis, record which predecessor caused each new cell to become reachable.
0624 | 
0625 | For two seeds (A and B) we can assign provenance. Where the fronts meet, a boundary line forms:
0626 | 
0627 | ```text
0628 | A A A A | B B B B
0629 | A A A A | B B B B
0630 | A A A A | B B B B
0631 | ```
0632 | 
0633 | Important: the labels are not part of the crystal's dynamics. They are our measurement.  
0634 | The actual world still contains only `0` or `1`. But our experimental record contains ancestry.
0635 | 
0636 | This gives us two views of the same system:
0637 | 
0638 | ```text
0639 | STATE VIEW – one merged crystal
0640 | PROVENANCE VIEW – two growth histories meeting
0641 | ```
0642 | 
0643 | Now individuality depends on the question.
0644 | 
0645 | ---
0646 | 
0647 | ## The Final Shape Can Erase Ancestry
0648 | 
0649 | If we throw away our provenance record and inspect only the binary state (`111111...`), the ancestral boundary may be invisible.  
0650 | Two very different histories can produce the same final configuration.  
0651 | That is important: **state alone may be insufficient to reconstruct history**.
0652 | 
0653 | A digital system's present appearance does not necessarily tell us how it came to exist.  
0654 | We will encounter exactly this problem when we start talking about reproduction. Similarity cannot prove ancestry.
0655 | 
0656 | ---
0657 | 
0658 | ## The Same Shape Can Have Different Histories
0659 | 
0660 | Imagine two experiments:  
0661 | - Run A – one seed grows outward.  
0662 | - Run B – several seeds grow and merge.
0663 | 
0664 | At a sufficiently late time, some region of the binary state may look identical. Yet their histories are different.
0665 | 
0666 | Conceptually:
0667 | 
0668 | ```text
0669 | same present state
0670 | ≠
0671 | same causal history
0672 | ```
0673 | 
0674 | This may be one of the most important lessons from our crystal.  
0675 | The world can forget. Our experiment does not have to.
0676 | 
0677 | ---
0678 | 
0679 | ## Provenance Becomes Part of the Specimen
0680 | 
0681 | We therefore might store for every occupied location: cell, birth time, predecessor, seed ancestry.  
0682 | Now the crystal is no longer just an image – it becomes a research specimen.
0683 | 
0684 | We can ask: when was this cell created? Which seed ultimately caused it? Which growth front reached it? Where did fronts meet? Which historical events remain visible, and which are visible only in provenance?
0685 | 
0686 | This connects directly to the experimental architecture we established in Chapter 08.
0687 | 
0688 | ---
0689 | 
0690 | ## Is Ancestry a Property of the World?
0691 | 
0692 | Be careful. Our provenance database knows *this cell came from seed A*. But does the crystal know that? No.
0693 | 
0694 | We have created observer memory, not system memory.  
0695 | That distinction is crucial. Just because our instrumentation can reconstruct history does not mean history is available to the digital entity.  
0696 | For history to affect future behavior, the system itself would need some state through which ancestry matters.
0697 | 
0698 | We have not added that. Yet.
0699 | 
0700 | ---
0701 | 
0702 | ## What Happens Without Ancestry?
0703 | 
0704 | With the binary rule, two growth fronts are equivalent. After contact, A and B lose their distinction. The world cares only about `occupied` or `empty`.
0705 | 
0706 | This gives us a clean result:
0707 | 
0708 | > **Distinct historical origins do not necessarily create distinct future behavior.**
0709 | 
0710 | Ancestry can exist as a fact about history while being causally irrelevant to future dynamics.  
0711 | That's a subtle but important distinction.
0712 | 
0713 | ---
0714 | 
0715 | ## Make Ancestry Matter
0716 | 
0717 | Now we can imagine a future modification. Suppose different seeds carry different local states (`A` vs `B`) and those states affect growth.  
0718 | Then when their fronts meet, the boundary may matter dynamically.
0719 | 
0720 | Perhaps A grows faster, B resists invasion, interfaces become stable, one type converts the other.
0721 | 
0722 | Now ancestry has entered the mechanism.
0723 | 
0724 | ```mermaid
0725 | flowchart LR
0726 |     A[Different local states<br/>at seeds] --> B[Differential growth]
0727 |     B --> C[Stable interfaces or competition]
0728 |     C --> D[History becomes causal]
0729 | ```
0730 | 
0731 | This starts moving us toward competition, inheritance, selection.  
0732 | But notice the progression: we did not begin by implementing genes.  
0733 | The simple crystal encountered a limitation, and then a reason for persistent variation began to appear.  
0734 | That is exactly the method we wanted.
0735 | 
0736 | ---
0737 | 
0738 | ## Can the Crystal Reproduce?
0739 | 
0740 | Our current structure simply expands. One connected region becomes a larger connected region.  
0741 | So by the criterion from Chapter 06: growth ≠ reproduction.  
0742 | There is no obvious parent plus spatially distinct offspring.
0743 | 
0744 | The crystal demonstrates something important:
0745 | 
0746 | > **continued persistence and enormous growth can occur without reproduction.**
0747 | 
0748 | At least in this toy world. That supports one of our substrate-first hypotheses. Reproduction is not required merely for the structure to continue.
0749 | 
0750 | ---
0751 | 
0752 | ## But the Price Is Obvious
0753 | 
0754 | The structure occupies more and more space. Eventually growth becomes resource consumption, even if the model does not represent resources explicitly.
0755 | 
0756 | Suppose space becomes finite. Then unlimited growth encounters a constraint.  
0757 | This is where the experiment becomes interesting.
0758 | 
0759 | We did not insert “hunger” because biology has hunger. We allowed growth until it created its own problem. Now the missing mechanism can earn its place.
0760 | 
0761 | ---
0762 | 
0763 | ## Introduce Finite Space
0764 | 
0765 | Put the crystal inside a bounded world. Eventually it reaches the edge. Growth stops.
0766 | 
0767 | Not because the crystal matured. Not because it aged. Not because it decided to stop.  
0768 | Because `available space = 0`.
0769 | 
0770 | Our first internally meaningful scarcity has appeared.  
0771 | Now we can ask: what should a growing digital organization do when expansion is no longer possible?
0772 | 
0773 | Possible strategies: restructure internally, compress, stop, compete, move, overwrite, fork elsewhere, obtain more space.  
0774 | Now those mechanisms have a reason to exist.
0775 | 
0776 | ---
0777 | 
0778 | ## Scarcity Has Emerged From Growth
0779 | 
0780 | This is exactly what we hoped the substrate-first method would do.
0781 | 
0782 | We began with no artificial resource economy. We allowed growth. And eventually encountered finite space.  
0783 | Now scarcity is not a biological decoration – it is a genuine consequence of the model.  
0784 | This is a much better reason to introduce resource competition.
0785 | 
0786 | ---
0787 | 
0788 | ## Compute Becomes Another Resource
0789 | 
0790 | Our implementation has another cost. As the crystal grows, the number of active locations increases. Our simulation must process them. So computational cost increases.
0791 | 
0792 | Again, that cost currently exists outside the modeled world. But we could internalize it.
0793 | 
0794 | Suppose every entity receives 100 update operations per generation. Now a large structure cannot update every component. Suddenly continued growth creates an internal coordination problem: which parts receive attention? Which parts remain stale? Can the organization maintain coherence?
0795 | 
0796 | This is much closer to a genuinely digital scarcity.
0797 | 
0798 | ---
0799 | 
0800 | ## Attention May Emerge Before Energy
0801 | 
0802 | That is an interesting inversion.
0803 | 
0804 | Instead of: food → energy → survival  
0805 | Our digital system might encounter: size → too much state → limited update budget → attention allocation → continued coherence.
0806 | 
0807 | Perhaps the analogue of metabolism is not an `energy` variable at all.  
0808 | Perhaps it is: **allocation of limited computation to the parts of the organization that matter**.
0809 | 
0810 | We do not know. But now we have a reason to investigate it.
0811 | 
0812 | ---
0813 | 
0814 | ## What Did the Crystal Teach Us?
0815 | 
0816 | We deliberately started with something almost too simple to fail.
0817 | 
0818 | It:
0819 | 
0820 | - grows,
0821 | - persists,
0822 | - fills holes,
0823 | - merges with other growth fronts,
0824 | - can preserve or erase traces of history.
0825 | 
0826 | But it does not:
0827 | 
0828 | - reproduce,
0829 | - learn,
0830 | - adapt,
0831 | - maintain a target morphology,
0832 | - distinguish damage from empty space,
0833 | - care about ancestry,
0834 | - manage internal resources.
0835 | 
0836 | That is exactly what we wanted. The experiment has started separating properties that our biological vocabulary tends to bundle together.
0837 | 
0838 | ### Growth Without Reproduction
0839 | The crystal can persist and expand without offspring.  
0840 | So continued organization does not automatically require reproduction in this digital model.
0841 | 
0842 | ### Hole Filling Without Regeneration
0843 | The crystal can replace removed cells without representing a target shape.  
0844 | So damage disappears does not automatically require regeneration.
0845 | 
0846 | ### History Without Memory
0847 | Temporary events can alter a trajectory. Persistent obstacles can leave structural traces.  
0848 | So history dependence does not automatically require memory.
0849 | 
0850 | ### Collision Without Individuality
0851 | Two growing regions can meet and merge into one connected state.  
0852 | So spatial components do not automatically give us a stable theory of individuals.
0853 | 
0854 | ### Ancestry Without Inheritance
0855 | Our provenance system can tell us which seed produced which region.  
0856 | But if ancestry does not affect future dynamics, ancestry exists historically without inheritance doing causal work.  
0857 | That distinction will matter later.
0858 | 
0859 | ### Scarcity Without Metabolism
0860 | Unlimited growth eventually encounters finite space, compute, update budget.  
0861 | So scarcity can arise naturally without adding a variable called `energy`.  
0862 | That may eventually demand a resource-management mechanism – but now that mechanism will be solving a real problem.
0863 | 
0864 | ---
0865 | 
0866 | ## The Substrate-First Method Worked
0867 | 
0868 | This was the real experiment. Not “Can we make a pretty hexagon?”  
0869 | We wanted to know whether beginning without biological machinery would immediately force us to put it all back.
0870 | 
0871 | It did not.
0872 | 
0873 | Instead we discovered a more interesting sequence:
0874 | 
0875 | ```mermaid
0876 | flowchart TD
0877 |     A[GROWTH] --> B[Continued organization]
0878 |     A --> C[GROWTH + DAMAGE]
0879 |     C --> D[Hole filling<br/>but not target-based regeneration]
0880 |     A --> E[GROWTH + OBSTACLE]
0881 |     E --> F[History dependence]
0882 |     A --> G[MULTIPLE SEEDS]
0883 |     G --> H[Ancestry<br/>but not necessarily individuality]
0884 |     A --> I[CONTINUED GROWTH]
0885 |     I --> J[Scarcity eventually appears]
0886 |     J --> K[New mechanisms may become necessary]
0887 | ```
0888 | 
0889 | The constraints are beginning to generate the architecture. That is exactly what we wanted.
0890 | 
0891 | ---
0892 | 
0893 | ## Our First Substrate-First Evidence Ledger
0894 | 
0895 | ### What We Built
0896 | ```text
0897 | binary state
0898 | +
0899 | hexagonal lattice
0900 | +
0901 | one seed
0902 | +
0903 | local irreversible growth
0904 | ```
0905 | No biological lifecycle was programmed.
0906 | 
0907 | ### What We Observed
0908 | The seed generates ordered expanding structure.  
0909 | Interior holes become occupied again.  
0910 | Temporary and permanent obstacles affect growth differently.  
0911 | Multiple growth fronts can merge.
0912 | 
0913 | ### What We Measured
0914 | ```text
0915 | occupied population
0916 | growth radius
0917 | damage refill
0918 | state differences
0919 | growth-front ancestry
0920 | ```
0921 | 
0922 | ### What Survived
0923 | Ordered growth arises from local dynamics without a global morphology blueprint.  
0924 | Continued expansion does not require reproduction.  
0925 | Hole filling does not require a special damage-response mechanism.  
0926 | Multiple historical lineages can become geometrically indistinguishable.
0927 | 
0928 | ### What Did Not Survive
0929 | The claim “the crystal heals itself” is too strong for the base rule.  
0930 | The same dynamics fill ordinary exterior space and damaged interior space.  
0931 | We have no evidence for target-directed regeneration.
0932 | 
0933 | ### What We Can Claim
0934 | > **A minimal irreversible digital growth process can exhibit ordered continued expansion, refill removed regions through ordinary growth, and merge histories without requiring reproduction, memory, a target morphology or a biological lifecycle.**
0935 | 
0936 | ### What We Cannot Claim
0937 | We have not demonstrated:
0938 | ```text
0939 | life
0940 | regeneration
0941 | learning
0942 | adaptation
0943 | self-maintenance
0944 | reproduction
0945 | inheritance
0946 | individuality
0947 | evolution
0948 | ```
0949 | 
0950 | ---
0951 | 
0952 | ## The Crystal Is Not Alive
0953 | 
0954 | Good. That was not the objective.
0955 | 
0956 | The crystal is useful because it gives us something simpler.  
0957 | It lets us begin separating **growth** from **life** and asking what actually becomes necessary as the system encounters stronger constraints.
0958 | 
0959 | That is a much better starting point than assembling a digital animal.
0960 | 
0961 | ---
0962 | 
0963 | ## But Now Look at the Gap
0964 | 
0965 | Our crystal is intentionally primitive.  
0966 | It grows because the rule makes empty neighboring locations become occupied.  
0967 | It does not discover how to grow. It does not invent new structures. It does not reproduce itself into persistent descendants. It does not maintain a causal individual. It does not evolve. It does not surprise us for very long.
0968 | 
0969 | So perhaps we should now look at the other end.  
0970 | Not at our simplest possible construction.  
0971 | At the strongest existing examples we can find.
0972 | 
0973 | What happens when researchers allow simple local dynamics to run and genuinely unexpected reproducing organization appears?  
0974 | How close has Artificial Life actually come?
0975 | 
0976 | That is the next question.
0977 | 
0978 | Next: **The Closest Thing We Have**
```
