```markdown
0001 | +++
0002 | title = "12: The Closest Thing We Have"
0003 | date = "2026-08-11T10:43:00+01:00"
0004 | draft = false
0005 | description = "We examine Outlier, an unusually minimal artificial-life system in which causally verified self-replicating structures emerge from local cellular-automaton dynamics."
0006 | categories = ["Programming", "Artificial Life"]
0007 | tags = ["Digital Life", "Artificial Life", "Outlier", "Cellular Automata", "Self-Replication", "Causality"]
0008 | +++
0009 | 
0010 | Before building anything more complicated, we should steal everything we can.
0011 | 
0012 | Not code.
0013 | 
0014 | Ideas.  
0015 | Experiments.  
0016 | Failures.  
0017 | Mechanisms.
0018 | 
0019 | So let's ask an obvious question:
0020 | 
0021 | > **How far has computation already gone without us deliberately building the organism?**
0022 | 
0023 | There is no objective answer.
0024 | 
0025 | Different Artificial Life systems demonstrate different properties.
0026 | 
0027 | Evoloops demonstrated Darwinian evolution of self-reproducing structures inside a deterministic cellular automaton.[1]
0028 | 
0029 | Flow-Lenia produces spatially localized continuous structures with complex behaviour, conserves mass, and allows parameters governing different pattern dynamics to become localized inside the world itself. Researchers have measured emergent evolutionary activity in the resulting system.[2]
0030 | 
0031 | Genelife attaches inheritable genomes to cellular dynamics and has demonstrated continuing genetic and spatial innovation, while its authors explicitly distinguish this from the stronger functional innovation characteristic of biological evolution.[3]
0032 | 
0033 | Other computational systems have shown that self-replicating programs can emerge from simple interactions without an explicit fitness landscape.[4]
0034 | 
0035 | All of those matter.
0036 | 
0037 | But for what we are trying to understand, one system is particularly useful.
0038 | 
0039 | It is called:
0040 | 
0041 | ## Outlier
0042 | 
0043 | And initially it looks almost absurdly small.
0044 | 
0045 | ---
0046 | 
0047 | ## This Chapter Is an Excursion
0048 | 
0049 | Before going further, we need to be precise about what role Outlier plays in this book.
0050 | 
0051 | Chapter 11 gave us a deliberately simple system:
0052 | 
0053 | ```text
0054 | one seed
0055 | +
0056 | one local growth rule
0057 | +
0058 | one lattice
0059 | +
0060 | time
0061 | ```
0062 | 
0063 | We built that system ourselves.
0064 | 
0065 | We knew exactly why every mechanism was there.
0066 | 
0067 | Outlier is different.
0068 | 
0069 | It is an independently discovered cellular automaton in which surprisingly complicated structures appeared before anyone had decided what those structures should be.
0070 | 
0071 | So:
0072 | 
0073 | > **Outlier is not the next version of our crystal.**
0074 | 
0075 | We are not replacing our controlled growth model with Outlier.
0076 | 
0077 | We are leaving our laboratory temporarily and looking at a frontier system.
0078 | 
0079 | Our questions are:
0080 | 
0081 | ```text
0082 | What has simple digital physics already produced?
0083 | 
0084 | Which biological-looking interpretations survive
0085 | when causality is reconstructed?
0086 | 
0087 | Which ideas are worth carrying back
0088 | into our controlled experiments?
0089 | 
0090 | Which ideas should be discarded?
0091 | ```
0092 | 
0093 | That distinction will become important.
0094 | 
0095 | After Outlier, we will return to systems of our own construction.
0096 | 
0097 | But we should first learn everything this one can teach us.
0098 | 
0099 | ---
0100 | 
0101 | ## Two States
0102 | 
0103 | The universe contains cells.
0104 | 
0105 | Every cell is either:
0106 | 
0107 | ```text
0108 | 0
0109 | ```
0110 | 
0111 | or:
0112 | 
0113 | ```text
0114 | 1
0115 | ```
0116 | 
0117 | Dead and alive are convenient names, but unnecessary ones.
0118 | 
0119 | We could equally call them:
0120 | 
0121 | ```text
0122 | OFF
0123 | ON
0124 | ```
0125 | 
0126 | Each cell examines a `3 × 3` Moore neighborhood:
0127 | 
0128 | ```text
0129 | a b c
0130 | d e f
0131 | g h i
0132 | ```
0133 | 
0134 | including itself.
0135 | 
0136 | Nine bits means:
0137 | 
0138 | $$
0139 | 2^9 = 512
0140 | $$
0141 | 
0142 | possible neighborhood configurations.
0143 | 
0144 | For every configuration, the rule says:
0145 | 
0146 | ```text
0147 | next state = 0 or 1
0148 | ```
0149 | 
0150 | That is the universe.
0151 | 
0152 | There is no built-in:
0153 | 
0154 | ```text
0155 | organism
0156 | energy
0157 | genome
0158 | reproduction API
0159 | individual
0160 | fitness function
0161 | ```
0162 | 
0163 | Just:
0164 | 
0165 | ```text
0166 | binary state
0167 | +
0168 | local neighborhood
0169 | +
0170 | transition rule
0171 | +
0172 | time
0173 | ```
0174 | 
0175 | The published Outlier rule is rotationally symmetric.
0176 | 
0177 | Its complete transition table contains:
0178 | 
0179 | ```text
0180 | 512 neighborhood cases
0181 | 220 live outputs
0182 | 292 dead outputs
0183 | ```
0184 | 
0185 | That is all the local physics.
0186 | 
0187 | ---
0188 | 
0189 | ## Nobody Designed the Organism
0190 | 
0191 | This distinction matters.
0192 | 
0193 | Outlier was discovered during an automated search through cellular-automaton rules intended to find dynamics conducive to open-ended evolution.[5]
0194 | 
0195 | The search did not begin by constructing:
0196 | 
0197 | ```python
0198 | class Organism:
0199 |     ...
0200 | 
0201 | class Reproduction:
0202 |     ...
0203 | ```
0204 | 
0205 | Nor was there a hand-designed structure whose reproduction mechanism had been carefully engineered.
0206 | 
0207 | Instead the search found:
0208 | 
0209 | > **a rule for the universe**
0210 | 
0211 | and structures appeared inside that universe.
0212 | 
0213 | ![The published Outlier cellular automaton evolving from its tiny seed](/images/books/digital-life/ch10-outlier-growth.gif)
0214 | 
0215 | Conceptually:
0216 | 
0217 | ```mermaid
0218 | flowchart LR
0219 |     subgraph Designed
0220 |         A1["Programmer designs organism"] --> A2["Designs reproduction mechanism"]
0221 |         A2 --> A3["Organism reproduces"]
0222 |     end
0223 | 
0224 |     subgraph Outlier
0225 |         B1["Search discovers local physics"] --> B2["Local physics runs"]
0226 |         B2 --> B3["Structures emerge"]
0227 |         B3 --> B4["Some structures replicate"]
0228 |     end
0229 | ```
0230 | 
0231 | The second route does not establish life.
0232 | 
0233 | But it removes an enormous amount of cargo cult.
0234 | 
0235 | ---
0236 | 
0237 | ## Start Almost From Nothing
0238 | 
0239 | The published rule can produce rich behaviour from sparse random initial conditions.
0240 | 
0241 | Small shape-changing clusters appear.
0242 | 
0243 | Some clusters produce additional clusters.
0244 | 
0245 | Some periodically duplicate.
0246 | 
0247 | Multiple smaller structures can assemble into larger formations.
0248 | 
0249 | Those formations can themselves replicate.
0250 | 
0251 | Collections of them can eventually form the boundary of a still larger expanding complex.[5]
0252 | 
0253 | So the published system contains organization at multiple scales:
0254 | 
0255 | ```text
0256 | cells
0257 |   ↓
0258 | clusters
0259 |   ↓
0260 | replicating formations
0261 |   ↓
0262 | larger expanding complex
0263 | ```
0264 | 
0265 | Replication appears at more than one scale.
0266 | 
0267 | ![Outlier at successive generations from the same initial seed](/images/books/digital-life/ch10-outlier-snapshots.png)
0268 | 
0269 | That is already strange.
0270 | 
0271 | But we have spent enough of this book learning not to stop at strange.
0272 | 
0273 | ---
0274 | 
0275 | ## There Was Still a Problem
0276 | 
0277 | Suppose we see:
0278 | 
0279 | ```text
0280 | A
0281 | ```
0282 | 
0283 | and later:
0284 | 
0285 | ```text
0286 | A     A
0287 | ```
0288 | 
0289 | It is tempting to say:
0290 | 
0291 | > A reproduced.
0292 | 
0293 | But perhaps the first `A` did not cause the second one.
0294 | 
0295 | Perhaps the local dynamics simply created another similar-looking configuration nearby.
0296 | 
0297 | Those are completely different claims.
0298 | 
0299 | ```text
0300 | visual recurrence
0301 | ≠
0302 | causal reproduction
0303 | ```
0304 | 
0305 | So in 2026, Arend Hintze and Clifford Bohm returned to Outlier with a much stronger question.
0306 | 
0307 | They reconstructed **causal ancestry**.[6]
0308 | 
0309 | ---
0310 | 
0311 | ## Who Caused Whom?
0312 | 
0313 | The method asks something close to:
0314 | 
0315 | > Which cells in the previous state were necessary for this later cell to appear?
0316 | 
0317 | At cell level:
0318 | 
0319 | ```text
0320 | previous cells
0321 |      ↓
0322 | causal contribution
0323 |      ↓
0324 | new cell
0325 | ```
0326 | 
0327 | Those dependencies can then be aggregated into relationships between larger structures:
0328 | 
0329 | ```text
0330 | cluster A
0331 |     ↓
0332 | cluster B
0333 |     ↓
0334 | cluster C
0335 | ```
0336 | 
0337 | Now the question is no longer merely:
0338 | 
0339 | ```text
0340 | does this look like that?
0341 | ```
0342 | 
0343 | It becomes:
0344 | 
0345 | ```text
0346 | did this organization
0347 | causally contribute to
0348 | the existence of that organization?
0349 | ```
0350 | 
0351 | That gives us an ancestry graph.
0352 | 
0353 | The 2026 analysis found branching causal reproduction in Outlier: earlier structures could causally produce multiple later structures, which could themselves participate in continuing lineages.[6]
0354 | 
0355 | That is a substantially stronger claim than visual similarity.
0356 | 
0357 | ```mermaid
0358 | flowchart TD
0359 |     subgraph Visual
0360 |         V1["Pattern at t"] -. "looks similar to" .-> V2["Pattern at t+1"]
0361 |     end
0362 | 
0363 |     subgraph Causal
0364 |         C1["Cells at t"] -->|"necessary contribution"| C2["Cells at t+1"]
0365 |         C2 --> C3["Aggregate causal relationships"]
0366 |         C3 --> C4["Earlier structure causally contributes to later structure"]
0367 |     end
0368 | ```
0369 | 
0370 | The distinction will matter enormously in the next chapter.
0371 | 
0372 | ---
0373 | 
0374 | ## 433 Copies
0375 | 
0376 | The 2026 experiment used a much larger system than the small examples we will run later.
0377 | 
0378 | It ran Outlier on:
0379 | 
0380 | ```text
0381 | grid       1024 × 1024
0382 | boundary   periodic
0383 | duration   20,000 updates
0384 | ```
0385 | 
0386 | The resulting causal analysis contained tens of millions of cluster instances and causal relationships.[6]
0387 | 
0388 | Consider the original seed cluster, called `c0`.
0389 | 
0390 | Within the first 10,000 updates, the researchers identified:
0391 | 
0392 | ```text
0393 | 433
0394 | ```
0395 | 
0396 | copies causally descending from that original seed.[6]
0397 | 
0398 | But those descendants did not indefinitely continue the same `c0` lineage.
0399 | 
0400 | So we immediately learn something useful:
0401 | 
0402 | ```text
0403 | replication
0404 | ≠
0405 | successful long-term lineage
0406 | ```
0407 | 
0408 | Producing another instance is not the same as founding a persistent dynasty.
0409 | 
0410 | ---
0411 | 
0412 | ## Then They Found Better Replicators
0413 | 
0414 | Other structures generated branching, multi-generation causal lineages.
0415 | 
0416 | One cluster type, `c2`, became particularly useful for tracing reproduction through the causal graph.[6]
0417 | 
0418 | The surrounding environment also mattered.
0419 | 
0420 | Replicators produced:
0421 | 
0422 | ```text
0423 | debris
0424 | collisions
0425 | fragments
0426 | recombinations
0427 | ```
0428 | 
0429 | and some later replicating structures arose through those interactions.
0430 | 
0431 | So the system is not simply doing:
0432 | 
0433 | ```text
0434 | copy parent
0435 | ```
0436 | 
0437 | It is closer to:
0438 | 
0439 | ```text
0440 | replicating process
0441 |         ↓
0442 | interaction
0443 |         ↓
0444 | fragments
0445 |         ↓
0446 | collisions
0447 |         ↓
0448 | recombination
0449 |         ↓
0450 | new structures
0451 | ```
0452 | 
0453 | That is much more interesting than simple geometric copying.
0454 | 
0455 | But we should still be careful about what words we attach to it.
0456 | 
0457 | ---
0458 | 
0459 | ## A Replicator May Not Be Connected
0460 | 
0461 | One of the most provocative findings from the causal analysis is that a self-replicating organization does not necessarily have to correspond to one compact connected cluster.
0462 | 
0463 | Some causally reproducing structures consisted of multiple spatially separated components whose combined causal dynamics participated in reproduction.[6]
0464 | 
0465 | The authors describe this in terms of distributed, multi-component selfhood.
0466 | 
0467 | For this book, we will keep the narrower result.
0468 | 
0469 | > **Causal self-replication can be distributed across multiple spatial components.**
0470 | 
0471 | That does **not** yet establish that those components constitute one natural individual.
0472 | 
0473 | Those are different claims.
0474 | 
0475 | A system like:
0476 | 
0477 | ```text
0478 |      A
0479 |     / \
0480 |    B   C
0481 |     \ /
0482 |      D
0483 | ```
0484 | 
0485 | might form one causally reproducing organization even though its visible components are spatially separated.
0486 | 
0487 | So connected geometry is not sufficient as a universal definition of a replicator.
0488 | 
0489 | But we should not jump from:
0490 | 
0491 | ```text
0492 | distributed causal reproduction
0493 | ```
0494 | 
0495 | to:
0496 | 
0497 | ```text
0498 | one distributed individual
0499 | ```
0500 | 
0501 | without another experiment.
0502 | 
0503 | That distinction is exactly the kind of thing this book is supposed to preserve.
0504 | 
0505 | ---
0506 | 
0507 | ## This Is Why Outlier Is Useful to Us
0508 | 
0509 | Outlier has an unusually useful combination of properties.
0510 | 
0511 | Its underlying world is:
0512 | 
0513 | ```text
0514 | binary
0515 | local
0516 | deterministic
0517 | spatial
0518 | small-rule
0519 | reproducible
0520 | ```
0521 | 
0522 | Yet the published experiments report:
0523 | 
0524 | ```text
0525 | emergent structures
0526 | hierarchical organization
0527 | causal self-replication
0528 | multiple generations
0529 | interaction
0530 | recombination
0531 | multi-component reproduction
0532 | ```
0533 | 
0534 | The 2026 authors argue for a stronger interpretation involving distributed selfhood.[6]
0535 | 
0536 | We are not required to adopt that interpretation.
0537 | 
0538 | What matters to us is that causal analysis lets us separate several questions that animations otherwise collapse together:
0539 | 
0540 | ```text
0541 | Did another pattern appear?
0542 | 
0543 | Was it similar?
0544 | 
0545 | Did the earlier structure cause it?
0546 | 
0547 | Did a lineage continue?
0548 | 
0549 | Were several components jointly necessary?
0550 | 
0551 | Does that causal organization deserve to be called one individual?
0552 | ```
0553 | 
0554 | Those are six different questions.
0555 | 
0556 | That makes Outlier an extraordinary reference system for our method.
0557 | 
0558 | ---
0559 | 
0560 | ## But Outlier Is Not Life
0561 | 
0562 | Nothing above establishes that Outlier is alive.
0563 | 
0564 | We have not established:
0565 | 
0566 | ```text
0567 | learning
0568 | understanding
0569 | self-maintenance
0570 | general-purpose adaptation
0571 | deliberate self-modification
0572 | knowledge assimilation
0573 | knowledge transfer
0574 | cumulative capability acquisition
0575 | open-ended functional improvement
0576 | ```
0577 | 
0578 | Nor does causal self-replication automatically establish any of those things.
0579 | 
0580 | The supported result is narrower:
0581 | 
0582 | > **Very simple digital physics can support emergent structures for which causal analysis identifies genuine, branching and sometimes multi-component self-replication.**
0583 | 
0584 | That is already remarkable.
0585 | 
0586 | It does not need embellishment.
0587 | 
0588 | ---
0589 | 
0590 | ## What About Flow-Lenia?
0591 | 
0592 | It is tempting to choose Flow-Lenia as our main reference system instead.
0593 | 
0594 | Visually, Flow-Lenia is much closer to what we intuitively imagine as an organism.
0595 | 
0596 | Its mass-conserving continuous dynamics can produce localized structures with complicated behaviour, and parameters governing those structures can themselves become localized within the simulated world. This permits multiple kinds of patterns to coexist and interact under locally different dynamics.[2]
0597 | 
0598 | Researchers have also measured evolutionary activity in Flow-Lenia.[2]
0599 | 
0600 | For studying:
0601 | 
0602 | ```text
0603 | continuous morphology
0604 | movement
0605 | mass flow
0606 | localized parameters
0607 | multispecies interactions
0608 | ```
0609 | 
0610 | Flow-Lenia may be the richer substrate.
0611 | 
0612 | But Outlier has an enormous advantage for what we want to do next.
0613 | 
0614 | We can almost completely expose its mechanism.
0615 | 
0616 | There are only:
0617 | 
0618 | ```text
0619 | two cell states
0620 | 512 neighborhood cases
0621 | one deterministic transition table
0622 | ```
0623 | 
0624 | No neural network.
0625 | 
0626 | No hidden controller.
0627 | 
0628 | No floating-point organism representation.
0629 | 
0630 | That makes it an unusually good object to reproduce and attack.
0631 | 
0632 | ---
0633 | 
0634 | ## Let's Implement It
0635 | 
0636 | The exact rule is published.
0637 | 
0638 | The Outlier paper provides the complete 512-entry rule encoded as a standard `MAP` string.[5]
0639 | 
0640 | It also provides a tiny `3 × 3` seed that reproduces the published seed dynamics.
0641 | 
0642 | The rule is:
0643 | 
0644 | ```text
0645 | ERETQB4eHWkQ7xD4eYZosBQZFixOBHmtFeehExrKVhURLRAqGxeIlSO1JYZP6DRi69rop7TQCkvWTIag7kAS8g
0646 | ```
0647 | 
0648 | The seed is:
0649 | 
0650 | ```text
0651 | .1.
0652 | 111
0653 | ..1
0654 | ```
0655 | 
0656 | That is enough to begin.
0657 | 
0658 | ---
0659 | 
0660 | ## Decode the Rule
0661 | 
0662 | A Moore neighborhood has nine cells.
0663 | 
0664 | We assign them these binary weights:
0665 | 
0666 | ```text
0667 | 256 128  64
0668 |  32  16   8
0669 |   4   2   1
0670 | ```
0671 | 
0672 | A binary `3 × 3` neighborhood therefore maps to an integer from `0` through `511`.
0673 | 
0674 | For example:
0675 | 
0676 | ```text
0677 | 1 0 0
0678 | 0 1 0
0679 | 0 0 1
0680 | ```
0681 | 
0682 | becomes:
0683 | 
0684 | ```text
0685 | 256 + 16 + 1 = 273
0686 | ```
0687 | 
0688 | The corresponding entry in the 512-entry transition table determines the next state of the center cell.
0689 | 
0690 | The published MAP string contains those rule bits encoded in Base64.
0691 | 
0692 | ```python
0693 | import base64
0694 | import numpy as np
0695 | 
0696 | 
0697 | OUTLIER_MAP = (
0698 |     "ERETQB4eHWkQ7xD4eYZosBQZFixOBHmtFeehExrKVhURLRAq"
0699 |     "GxeIlSO1JYZP6DRi69rop7TQCkvWTIag7kAS8g"
0700 | )
0701 | 
0702 | 
0703 | def decode_map_rule(encoded: str) -> np.ndarray:
0704 |     """Decode the published 512-bit MAP rule."""
0705 |     padding = "=" * ((4 - len(encoded) % 4) % 4)
0706 |     raw = base64.b64decode(encoded + padding)
0707 | 
0708 |     bits = np.unpackbits(
0709 |         np.frombuffer(raw, dtype=np.uint8)
0710 |     )
0711 | 
0712 |     if len(bits) < 512:
0713 |         raise ValueError(
0714 |             "MAP rule contains fewer than 512 bits"
0715 |         )
0716 | 
0717 |     return bits[:512].astype(np.uint8)
0718 | 
0719 | 
0720 | RULE = decode_map_rule(OUTLIER_MAP)
0721 | ```
0722 | 
0723 | At this point it is tempting to continue.
0724 | 
0725 | We should not.
0726 | 
0727 | Everything that follows depends on us having decoded the correct universe.
0728 | 
0729 | So before we trust the simulation, we test the decoder.
0730 | 
0731 | ---
0732 | 
0733 | ## Verify Before We Trust It
0734 | 
0735 | The paper gives us two properties we can check immediately.
0736 | 
0737 | The rule should contain:
0738 | 
0739 | ```text
0740 | 512 outputs
0741 | 220 live outputs
0742 | ```
0743 | 
0744 | So:
0745 | 
0746 | ```python
0747 | assert RULE.shape == (512,)
0748 | assert int(RULE.sum()) == 220
0749 | ```
0750 | 
0751 | If either assertion fails, we stop.
0752 | 
0753 | But we can do better.
0754 | 
0755 | The published rule is rotationally symmetric.[5]
0756 | 
0757 | That means rotating a neighborhood through a quarter turn must not change the corresponding rule output.
0758 | 
0759 | We can test every possible neighborhood.
0760 | 
0761 | ```python
0762 | def index_to_grid(index: int) -> np.ndarray:
0763 |     bits = np.array(
0764 |         [
0765 |             (index >> shift) & 1
0766 |             for shift in range(8, -1, -1)
0767 |         ],
0768 |         dtype=np.uint8,
0769 |     )
0770 |     return bits.reshape(3, 3)
0771 | 
0772 | 
0773 | def grid_to_index(grid: np.ndarray) -> int:
0774 |     value = 0
0775 | 
0776 |     for bit in grid.reshape(-1):
0777 |         value = (value << 1) | int(bit)
0778 | 
0779 |     return value
0780 | 
0781 | 
0782 | def verify_rotational_symmetry(
0783 |     rule: np.ndarray,
0784 | ) -> None:
0785 |     for index in range(512):
0786 |         grid = index_to_grid(index)
0787 |         expected = int(rule[index])
0788 | 
0789 |         rotated = grid.copy()
0790 | 
0791 |         for _ in range(3):
0792 |             rotated = np.rot90(rotated)
0793 | 
0794 |             rotated_index = grid_to_index(
0795 |                 rotated
0796 |             )
0797 | 
0798 |             assert (
0799 |                 int(rule[rotated_index])
0800 |                 == expected
0801 |             ), (
0802 |                 index,
0803 |                 rotated_index,
0804 |                 expected,
0805 |                 int(rule[rotated_index]),
0806 |             )
0807 | 
0808 | 
0809 | verify_rotational_symmetry(RULE)
0810 | ```
0811 | 
0812 | If that completes successfully, we have checked:
0813 | 
0814 | ```text
0815 | decoded entries             512
0816 | live outputs                220
0817 | quarter-turn symmetry       all 512 neighborhoods
0818 | ```
0819 | 
0820 | This is a tiny piece of verification.
0821 | 
0822 | It is also load-bearing.
0823 | 
0824 | > **If our MAP decoder or bit ordering is wrong, every later experiment would be about a different cellular automaton.**
0825 | 
0826 | The visual output might still look fascinating.
0827 | 
0828 | It would simply be irrelevant.
0829 | 
0830 | ---
0831 | 
0832 | ## One Update
0833 | 
0834 | Now we can implement one step of the universe.
0835 | 
0836 | ```python
0837 | def outlier_step(
0838 |     state: np.ndarray,
0839 |     rule: np.ndarray,
0840 | ) -> np.ndarray:
0841 | 
0842 |     nw = np.roll(
0843 |         np.roll(state, 1, axis=0),
0844 |         1,
0845 |         axis=1,
0846 |     )
0847 | 
0848 |     north = np.roll(state, 1, axis=0)
0849 | 
0850 |     ne = np.roll(
0851 |         np.roll(state, 1, axis=0),
0852 |         -1,
0853 |         axis=1,
0854 |     )
0855 | 
0856 |     west = np.roll(state, 1, axis=1)
0857 | 
0858 |     centre = state
0859 | 
0860 |     east = np.roll(state, -1, axis=1)
0861 | 
0862 |     sw = np.roll(
0863 |         np.roll(state, -1, axis=0),
0864 |         1,
0865 |         axis=1,
0866 |     )
0867 | 
0868 |     south = np.roll(state, -1, axis=0)
0869 | 
0870 |     se = np.roll(
0871 |         np.roll(state, -1, axis=0),
0872 |         -1,
0873 |         axis=1,
0874 |     )
0875 | 
0876 |     neighborhood = (
0877 |           (nw.astype(np.uint16) << 8)
0878 |         | (north.astype(np.uint16) << 7)
0879 |         | (ne.astype(np.uint16) << 6)
0880 |         | (west.astype(np.uint16) << 5)
0881 |         | (centre.astype(np.uint16) << 4)
0882 |         | (east.astype(np.uint16) << 3)
0883 |         | (sw.astype(np.uint16) << 2)
0884 |         | (south.astype(np.uint16) << 1)
0885 |         |  se.astype(np.uint16)
0886 |     )
0887 | 
0888 |     return rule[neighborhood]
0889 | ```
0890 | 
0891 | Look at what is missing.
0892 | 
0893 | There is no:
0894 | 
0895 | ```python
0896 | replicate()
0897 | ```
0898 | 
0899 | No:
0900 | 
0901 | ```python
0902 | organism()
0903 | ```
0904 | 
0905 | No:
0906 | 
0907 | ```python
0908 | find_child()
0909 | ```
0910 | 
0911 | No:
0912 | 
0913 | ```python
0914 | evolve()
0915 | ```
0916 | 
0917 | Only the transition rule.
0918 | 
0919 | ---
0920 | 
0921 | ## Add the Published Seed
0922 | 
0923 | The seed is:
0924 | 
0925 | ```python
0926 | SEED = np.array(
0927 |     [
0928 |         [0, 1, 0],
0929 |         [1, 1, 1],
0930 |         [0, 0, 1],
0931 |     ],
0932 |     dtype=np.uint8,
0933 | )
0934 | ```
0935 | 
0936 | Place it in the center of a periodic world.
0937 | 
0938 | ```python
0939 | def make_world(
0940 |     size: int = 512,
0941 | ) -> np.ndarray:
0942 | 
0943 |     world = np.zeros(
0944 |         (size, size),
0945 |         dtype=np.uint8,
0946 |     )
0947 | 
0948 |     row = size // 2 - 1
0949 |     col = size // 2 - 1
0950 | 
0951 |     world[
0952 |         row:row + 3,
0953 |         col:col + 3,
0954 |     ] = SEED
0955 | 
0956 |     return world
0957 | ```
0958 | 
0959 | And run:
0960 | 
0961 | ```python
0962 | RULE = decode_map_rule(OUTLIER_MAP)
0963 | 
0964 | assert RULE.shape == (512,)
0965 | assert int(RULE.sum()) == 220
0966 | 
0967 | verify_rotational_symmetry(RULE)
0968 | 
0969 | world = make_world(512)
0970 | 
0971 | for tick in range(5000):
0972 |     world = outlier_step(
0973 |         world,
0974 |         RULE,
0975 |     )
0976 | 
0977 |     if tick % 100 == 0:
0978 |         print(
0979 |             tick,
0980 |             int(world.sum()),
0981 |         )
0982 | ```
0983 | 
0984 | That gives us a compact implementation of the published local physics.
0985 | 
0986 | But there is an important distinction we must make before interpreting anything it produces.
0987 | 
0988 | ---
0989 | 
0990 | ## Our Implementation Is Not Yet the Full Published Experiment
0991 | 
0992 | The code above reproduces:
0993 | 
0994 | ```text
0995 | the published transition rule
0996 | +
0997 | the published seed
0998 | +
0999 | periodic local dynamics
1000 | ```
1001 | 
1002 | That does **not** mean every experiment we run is equivalent to the published experiments.
1003 | 
1004 | The 2026 causal study used:
1005 | 
1006 | ```text
1007 | grid       1024 × 1024
1008 | boundary   periodic
1009 | duration   20,000 updates
1010 | ```
1011 | 
1012 | Our teaching implementation may use smaller worlds or shorter runs.
1013 | 
1014 | That difference is not cosmetic.
1015 | 
1016 | The earlier Outlier work reports a strong scale effect: sparse random worlds smaller than roughly `512 × 512` did not produce the larger replicating formations seen in the principal experiments.[5]
1017 | 
1018 | So we establish a rule now:
1019 | 
1020 | > **A result obtained from a smaller or shorter Outlier run is a result about that run. It must not automatically be generalized to the full published Outlier regime.**
1021 | 
1022 | This becomes particularly important in the next chapter.
1023 | 
1024 | If we run:
1025 | 
1026 | ```text
1027 | 512 × 512
1028 | for
1029 | 1,600 generations
1030 | ```
1031 | 
1032 | then our claims apply to:
1033 | 
1034 | ```text
1035 | the structures observable
1036 | in that 512 × 512
1037 | 1,600-generation experiment
1038 | ```
1039 | 
1040 | They do not automatically apply to:
1041 | 
1042 | ```text
1043 | 1024 × 1024
1044 | 20,000-generation
1045 | hierarchical Outlier dynamics
1046 | ```
1047 | 
1048 | Scale is part of the experimental condition.
1049 | 
1050 | ---
1051 | 
1052 | ## Something Important Has Already Changed
1053 | 
1054 | Remember our crystal from Chapter 11.
1055 | 
1056 | It grew because we explicitly chose a growth rule.
1057 | 
1058 | Outlier is different.
1059 | 
1060 | We did not define:
1061 | 
1062 | ```text
1063 | parent
1064 | offspring
1065 | family
1066 | replicator
1067 | formation
1068 | ```
1069 | 
1070 | inside its transition law.
1071 | 
1072 | Those are descriptions applied after larger organizations appeared.
1073 | 
1074 | That makes Outlier useful for a very particular purpose.
1075 | 
1076 | It allows us to ask:
1077 | 
1078 | > **When does our interpretation outrun the mechanism?**
1079 | 
1080 | That is exactly what the next chapter will test.
1081 | 
1082 | ---
1083 | 
1084 | ## What We Should Steal
1085 | 
1086 | Outlier gives us several ideas worth carrying forward.
1087 | 
1088 | ### 1. Do not design the organism
1089 | 
1090 | We can design or discover local physics and allow candidate structures to arise within it.
1091 | 
1092 | ### 2. Do not assume one scale
1093 | 
1094 | Interesting organization may exist simultaneously at:
1095 | 
1096 | ```text
1097 | cell
1098 | cluster
1099 | formation
1100 | larger structure
1101 | ```
1102 | 
1103 | without one scale automatically being privileged.
1104 | 
1105 | ### 3. Do not equate connected geometry with causal organization
1106 | 
1107 | A causally reproducing structure may involve spatially separated components.
1108 | 
1109 | That does not establish individuality.
1110 | 
1111 | It tells us only that connectedness is not enough.
1112 | 
1113 | ### 4. Track causation
1114 | 
1115 | Visual resemblance is not sufficient evidence for:
1116 | 
1117 | ```text
1118 | reproduction
1119 | inheritance
1120 | influence
1121 | ancestry
1122 | ```
1123 | 
1124 | Whenever possible, reconstruct the dependencies.
1125 | 
1126 | ### 5. Let interactions matter
1127 | 
1128 | Collision, recombination and environmental interference can generate novelty without us injecting a mutation operator from outside.
1129 | 
1130 | Those are useful lessons.
1131 | 
1132 | They are not a specification for our next system.
1133 | 
1134 | ---
1135 | 
1136 | ## What We Should Not Steal Yet
1137 | 
1138 | Outlier also tempts us into vocabulary we have not earned.
1139 | 
1140 | Words such as:
1141 | 
1142 | ```text
1143 | organism
1144 | individual
1145 | family
1146 | offspring
1147 | self
1148 | collective
1149 | flocking
1150 | ```
1151 | 
1152 | are extremely seductive once an animation starts moving.
1153 | 
1154 | So we are not going to carry those words forward unquestioned.
1155 | 
1156 | In particular, causal ancestry creates an obvious hypothesis:
1157 | 
1158 | > Structures that share recent causal ancestry may behave as one continuing organization.
1159 | 
1160 | Perhaps.
1161 | 
1162 | But perhaps what looks like family behaviour is merely:
1163 | 
1164 | ```text
1165 | spatial proximity
1166 | shared local flow
1167 | common environmental disturbance
1168 | ```
1169 | 
1170 | Perhaps connected clusters are the wrong units.
1171 | 
1172 | Perhaps causal families are the wrong units.
1173 | 
1174 | Perhaps there is no unique natural unit at all.
1175 | 
1176 | Those are experimental questions.
1177 | 
1178 | ---
1179 | 
1180 | ## Outlier Is a Reference Case, Not Our Destination
1181 | 
1182 | Outlier does not know anything about:
1183 | 
1184 | ```text
1185 | the internet
1186 | external knowledge
1187 | its own rule
1188 | experiments
1189 | checkpoints
1190 | forking
1191 | merging
1192 | deliberate modification
1193 | ```
1194 | 
1195 | But that is not a criticism.
1196 | 
1197 | Those capabilities are irrelevant to what Outlier has already demonstrated.
1198 | 
1199 | For us, Outlier establishes a lower reference point:
1200 | 
1201 | > **How much complicated causal organization can appear before anything resembling intelligence is required?**
1202 | 
1203 | That is enormously useful.
1204 | 
1205 | But we are not going to take Outlier and start bolting capabilities onto it.
1206 | 
1207 | That would simply create another cargo cult.
1208 | 
1209 | Instead:
1210 | 
1211 | ```text
1212 | OUTLIER
1213 | ↓
1214 | observe
1215 | ↓
1216 | form interpretation
1217 | ↓
1218 | attack interpretation
1219 | ↓
1220 | keep only what survives
1221 | ↓
1222 | return to our controlled laboratory
1223 | ```
1224 | 
1225 | That is the bridge to the rest of the book.
1226 | 
1227 | ---
1228 | 
1229 | ## Evidence Ledger
1230 | 
1231 | At this point we can separate what is established from what remains open.
1232 | 
1233 | | Claim                                                                                  | Status                            | Evidence                         |
1234 | | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | --------------------------------- | -------------------------------- |
1235 | | The published MAP decodes to 512 binary outputs                                        | **SUPPORTED**                     | exhaustive decode                |
1236 | | The decoded rule contains 220 live outputs                                             | **SUPPORTED**                     | direct count                     |
1237 | | The decoded rule is invariant under quarter-turn rotations                             | **SUPPORTED**                     | exhaustive 512-neighborhood test |
1238 | | Outlier produces self-replicating organization under published experimental conditions | **SUPPORTED FROM PUBLISHED WORK** | Yang 2025                        |
1239 | | Causal ancestry identifies genuine branching replication                               | **SUPPORTED FROM PUBLISHED WORK** | Hintze & Bohm 2026               |
1240 | | Causal reproduction can span multiple spatial components                               | **SUPPORTED FROM PUBLISHED WORK** | Hintze & Bohm 2026               |
1241 | | Multi-component reproduction establishes one natural individual                        | **UNTESTED HERE**                 | requires an additional criterion |
1242 | | Shared ancestry causes coordinated motion                                              | **UNTESTED HERE**                 | next chapter                     |
1243 | | Our smaller teaching run reproduces the entire published Outlier regime                | **NOT CLAIMED**                   | scale and duration differ        |
1244 | | Outlier is alive                                                                       | **NOT CLAIMED**                   | evidence insufficient            |
1245 | 
1246 | This ledger matters.
1247 | 
1248 | The most interesting entries may eventually be the ones that fail.
1249 | 
1250 | ---
1251 | 
1252 | ## What Is the Closest Thing We Have?
1253 | 
1254 | So is Outlier digital life?
1255 | 
1256 | We will not make that claim.
1257 | 
1258 | A narrower statement survives:
1259 | 
1260 | > **Outlier is a striking demonstration that extremely compact digital physics can support emergent, hierarchical self-replication, and that causal reconstruction can identify reproduction distributed across multiple spatial components.**
1261 | 
1262 | The entire local universe is specified by:
1263 | 
1264 | ```text
1265 | 512 bits of transition law
1266 | +
1267 | initial state
1268 | +
1269 | space
1270 | +
1271 | time
1272 | ```
1273 | 
1274 | From that, unexpectedly complicated causal organization can appear.
1275 | 
1276 | But the animation gives us more interpretations than the evidence has earned.
1277 | 
1278 | So before returning to our own Digital Crystal, we are going to do something more important.
1279 | 
1280 | We are going to attack Outlier.
1281 | 
1282 | We will ask:
1283 | 
1284 | ```text
1285 | Is recurrence really reproduction?
1286 | 
1287 | Can we reconstruct causal descendants?
1288 | 
1289 | Does shared ancestry predict shared behaviour?
1290 | 
1291 | Does the apparent family effect survive
1292 | a better control?
1293 | 
1294 | What happens when proximity is separated
1295 | from ancestry?
1296 | 
1297 | And what can our finite replication
1298 | actually tell us about the larger system?
1299 | ```
1300 | 
1301 | Outlier is not the organism from which our later Digital Crystal descends.
1302 | 
1303 | It is the **hostile reference case** that teaches us which interpretations survive.
1304 | 
1305 | We are not going to copy it.
1306 | 
1307 | We are going to try to prove ourselves wrong about it.
1308 | 
1309 | Next:
1310 | 
1311 | **Is It Really Reproducing?**
1312 | 
1313 | ---
1314 | 
1315 | ## References
1316 | 
1317 | **[1]** Sayama, H. & Nehaniv, C. L. *Self-Reproduction and Evolution in Cellular Automata: 25 Years After Evoloops.* Artificial Life 31(1), 81–95 (2025).
1318 | 
1319 | **[2]** Plantec, E., Hamon, G., Etcheverry, M., Chan, B. W-C., Oudeyer, P-Y. & Moulin-Frier, C. *Flow-Lenia: Emergent Evolutionary Dynamics in Mass Conservative Continuous Cellular Automata.* Artificial Life 31(2), 228–250 (2025). doi:10.1162/artl_a_00471
1320 | 
1321 | **[3]** Packard, N. H. & McCaskill, J. S. *Open-Endedness in Genelife.* Artificial Life 30(3), 356–389 (2024). doi:10.1162/artl_a_00426
1322 | 
1323 | **[4]** Agüera y Arcas, B. et al. *Computational Life: How Well-formed, Self-replicating Programs Emerge from Simple Interaction.* arXiv:2406.19108 (2024).
1324 | 
1325 | **[5]** Yang, B. *Emergence of Self-Replicating Hierarchical Structures in a Binary Cellular Automaton.* Artificial Life 31(1), 96–105 (2025). doi:10.1162/artl_a_00449
1326 | 
1327 | **[6]** Hintze, A. & Bohm, C. *Rethinking self-replication: detecting distributed selfhood in the Outlier cellular automaton.* npj Complexity 3, 11 (2026).
```
