```markdown
0001 | +++
0002 | date = '2026-08-11T12:48:00+01:00'
0003 | draft = false
0004 | title = '13: Is It Really Reproducing?'
0005 | categories = ['Programming', 'Artificial Life']
0006 | tags = ['Digital Life', 'Artificial Life', 'Cellular Automata', 'Outlier', 'Causality', 'Collective Motion', 'Experiments']
0007 | series = ['Digital Life From First Principles']
0008 | +++
0009 | 
0010 | Something strange happened while we were looking at Outlier.
0011 | 
0012 | We had started with a question about reproduction.
0013 | 
0014 | Then I watched the simulation.
0015 | 
0016 | And I thought:
0017 | 
0018 | > That looks like flocking.
0019 | 
0020 | That is exactly the kind of observation that can get us into trouble.
0021 | 
0022 | Humans are extraordinarily good at seeing things in motion and assigning meaning to them.
0023 | 
0024 | We see:
0025 | 
0026 | ```text
0027 | a shape
0028 | another shape
0029 | movement
0030 | coordination
0031 | ```
0032 | 
0033 | and very quickly turn that into:
0034 | 
0035 | ```text
0036 | organisms
0037 | offspring
0038 | groups
0039 | flocks
0040 | ```
0041 | 
0042 | But this book has one rule that matters more than almost any other:
0043 | 
0044 | ```text
0045 | appearance
0046 | is not
0047 | evidence
0048 | ```
0049 | 
0050 | So instead of calling it flocking, we stopped.
0051 | 
0052 | And measured it.
0053 | 
0054 | That led us into a much deeper investigation than I expected.
0055 | 
0056 | ---
0057 | 
0058 | ## The Problem With Watching Artificial Life
0059 | 
0060 | Here is the Outlier simulation again.
0061 | 
0062 | {{< figure src="/images/books/digital-life/ch10-outlier-growth.gif" caption="The published Outlier cellular automaton running from its small initial seed." >}}
0063 | 
0064 | It is very difficult to watch something like this without inventing nouns.
0065 | 
0066 | You start saying:
0067 | 
0068 | ```text
0069 | that thing moved
0070 | that thing split
0071 | those things are travelling together
0072 | that one produced another one
0073 | ```
0074 | 
0075 | But every noun contains a hypothesis.
0076 | 
0077 | What is a **thing**?
0078 | What counts as the same structure at a later time?
0079 | What makes one structure the parent of another?
0080 | What makes two nearby structures part of the same causal process rather than merely close together?
0081 | 
0082 | These are not philosophical decorations.
0083 | 
0084 | They are experimental questions.
0085 | 
0086 | ---
0087 | 
0088 | ## First Question: Is It Really Reproducing?
0089 | 
0090 | In the previous chapter we introduced Outlier because it gives us something unusually valuable.
0091 | 
0092 | It is extremely simple at the substrate level.
0093 | 
0094 | The world is a binary cellular automaton.
0095 | Each cell sees a `3 × 3` neighborhood.
0096 | That neighborhood gives us one of `512` possible local configurations.
0097 | A fixed rule determines whether the centre cell will be alive or dead on the next step.
0098 | 
0099 | There is no class called `Organism`.
0100 | There is no method called `reproduce()`.
0101 | There is no explicit genome object.
0102 | 
0103 | And yet large structures appear.
0104 | 
0105 | Some structures recur.
0106 | 
0107 | Some appear to produce more structures.
0108 | 
0109 | That makes Outlier ideal for our purposes.
0110 | 
0111 | If reproduction occurs here, it cannot be because the programmer wrote:
0112 | 
0113 | ```python
0114 | if organism.ready:
0115 |     organism.reproduce()
0116 | ```
0117 | 
0118 | Something more interesting must be happening.
0119 | 
0120 | ---
0121 | 
0122 | ### Similarity Is Not Reproduction
0123 | 
0124 | Suppose we see this:
0125 | 
0126 | ```text
0127 | time t
0128 | 
0129 |     A
0130 | 
0131 | time t + 500
0132 | 
0133 |     A     A
0134 | ```
0135 | 
0136 | It is tempting to say:
0137 | 
0138 | > A reproduced.
0139 | 
0140 | But that observation alone does not establish reproduction.
0141 | 
0142 | Perhaps both copies were produced independently by the surrounding environment.
0143 | 
0144 | Perhaps the second structure would have appeared even if the first one had never existed.
0145 | 
0146 | Perhaps what we are calling two structures are actually parts of a larger repeating process.
0147 | 
0148 | So reproduction is not merely a question of similarity.
0149 | 
0150 | It is a **causal claim**.
0151 | 
0152 | We want something closer to:
0153 | 
0154 | ```text
0155 | parent existed
0156 | ↓
0157 | parent participated causally in a process
0158 | ↓
0159 | later candidate appeared
0160 | ↓
0161 | without the relevant earlier structure,
0162 | the later structure would not have appeared in the same way
0163 | ```
0164 | 
0165 | That is a much stronger statement.
0166 | 
0167 | ---
0168 | 
0169 | ## Counterfactual Causality
0170 | 
0171 | Because Outlier is a cellular automaton, we have an unusually clean opportunity.
0172 | 
0173 | For every live cell at time `t + 1`, we know exactly which `3 × 3` neighborhood produced it.
0174 | 
0175 | So we can ask:
0176 | 
0177 | > Which live cells in the preceding neighborhood were actually necessary for this cell to become alive?
0178 | 
0179 | Our simplified test works like this.
0180 | 
0181 | Take a live child cell.
0182 | 
0183 | Then, one at a time, remove each live predecessor from its local neighborhood.
0184 | 
0185 | Re-evaluate the cellular automaton rule.
0186 | 
0187 | If removing a predecessor changes the child from `alive` to `dead`, then that predecessor was positively necessary under this counterfactual test.
0188 | 
0189 | ```mermaid
0190 | flowchart TD
0191 |     A["Child cell is alive"] --> B["Identify live predecessors in 3×3 neighborhood"]
0192 |     B --> C["Remove one predecessor at a time"]
0193 |     C --> D["Recompute rule on modified neighborhood"]
0194 |     D --> E{"Child becomes dead?"}
0195 |     E -- Yes --> F["Record causal dependency: predecessor → child"]
0196 |     E -- No --> G["Predecessor not necessary"]
0197 | ```
0198 | 
0199 | This is not a complete theory of causality.
0200 | 
0201 | But it gives us something much stronger than visual resemblance.
0202 | 
0203 | It gives us a causal graph.
0204 | 
0205 | ---
0206 | 
0207 | ### From Cells to Structures
0208 | 
0209 | Cell-level causality produces a huge amount of information.
0210 | 
0211 | So we aggregate cells into connected clusters.
0212 | 
0213 | Then causal dependencies between cells become causal relationships between clusters.
0214 | 
0215 | The result looks approximately like:
0216 | 
0217 | ```text
0218 | cluster at t
0219 |      ↓
0220 | cluster at t+1
0221 |      ↓
0222 | cluster at t+2
0223 |      ↓
0224 | ...
0225 | ```
0226 | 
0227 | with branching where one earlier organization contributes to multiple later structures.
0228 | 
0229 | For our `512 × 512` experiment over `1,600` generations we found:
0230 | 
0231 | ```text
0232 | 138,891 clusters
0233 | 196,466 causal edges
0234 | ```
0235 | 
0236 | That is already a useful warning.
0237 | 
0238 | The visual animation looks as though it contains a collection of fairly obvious moving objects.
0239 | 
0240 | The causal graph tells us that underneath that appearance is a very large network of dependencies.
0241 | 
0242 | ---
0243 | 
0244 | ## Scope of This Experiment
0245 | 
0246 | Before going further, we need to pin down exactly what this chapter can and cannot establish.
0247 | 
0248 | Our run is:
0249 | 
0250 | ```text
0251 | grid          512 × 512
0252 | generations   1,600
0253 | boundary      periodic
0254 | ```
0255 | 
0256 | The published causal Outlier study used a substantially larger regime:
0257 | 
0258 | ```text
0259 | grid          1024 × 1024
0260 | updates       20,000
0261 | boundary      periodic
0262 | ```
0263 | 
0264 | That difference matters.
0265 | 
0266 | Outlier is scale-sensitive.
0267 | 
0268 | Larger and later hierarchical formations may not be represented in a shorter `512 × 512` run.
0269 | 
0270 | So every result in this chapter should be read as:
0271 | 
0272 | > **a result about the structures observable in our 512 × 512, 1,600-generation reproduction of Outlier.**
0273 | 
0274 | We must not automatically generalize a null result here to the larger published regime.
0275 | 
0276 | This limitation will matter again near the end of the chapter.
0277 | 
0278 | ---
0279 | 
0280 | ## Finding c2 Again
0281 | 
0282 | The published Outlier seed starts as a tiny configuration:
0283 | 
0284 | ```text
0285 | .1.
0286 | 111
0287 | ..1
0288 | ```
0289 | 
0290 | After two updates it produces a small structure we call `c2`.
0291 | 
0292 | In our simulation that structure had:
0293 | 
0294 | ```text
0295 | area          6
0296 | bounding box  3 × 3
0297 | ```
0298 | 
0299 | Instead of searching for arbitrary repeating shapes, we derived the actual `c2` signature directly from the known initial seed.
0300 | 
0301 | Then we searched the entire run for later occurrences of that structure, allowing translation and rotation.
0302 | 
0303 | We found:
0304 | 
0305 | ```text
0306 | 144 c2 occurrences
0307 | ```
0308 | 
0309 | between:
0310 | 
0311 | ```text
0312 | t = 2
0313 | and
0314 | t = 1598
0315 | ```
0316 | 
0317 | But recurrence still does not prove reproduction.
0318 | 
0319 | So we combined recurrence with the causal graph.
0320 | 
0321 | ---
0322 | 
0323 | ### A Causal Family Tree
0324 | 
0325 | For every `c2`, we searched forward through the causal graph for later `c2` structures reachable through that causal history.
0326 | 
0327 | The original `c2` at `t = 2` produced a branching causal structure with four later `c2` descendants.
0328 | 
0329 | The complete return graph contained:
0330 | 
0331 | ```text
0332 | 99 visible c2 return edges
0333 | ```
0334 | 
0335 | The full graph was too complicated to use as an illustration, so the figure shows a deliberately pruned family.
0336 | 
0337 | {{< figure src="/images/books/digital-life/ch10-outlier-causal-lineage.png" caption="A readable subset of the Outlier c2 causal family tree. The visual is pruned; the analysis uses the full causal graph." >}}
0338 | 
0339 | This is much stronger evidence than:
0340 | 
0341 | > I saw one shape and later saw several similar shapes.
0342 | 
0343 | We can now say something closer to:
0344 | 
0345 | > **Later occurrences of the c2 structure are reachable through measurable causal ancestry originating in earlier c2 structures.**
0346 | 
0347 | That is the kind of evidence we need before using words such as reproduction.
0348 | 
0349 | ---
0350 | 
0351 | ## Causal Reproduction Survives in This Run
0352 | 
0353 | Within our `512 × 512`, `1,600`-generation reproduction, the evidence for `c2` reproduction is not merely geometric recurrence.
0354 | 
0355 | It is:
0356 | 
0357 | ```text
0358 | structural recurrence
0359 | +
0360 | counterfactual causal ancestry
0361 | +
0362 | branching lineage
0363 | ```
0364 | 
0365 | That distinction matters.
0366 | 
0367 | So the narrow claim survives:
0368 | 
0369 | > **In our run, recurring c2 structures participate in a branching causal return graph.**
0370 | 
0371 | That does not establish life.
0372 | 
0373 | It does not establish a natural individual.
0374 | 
0375 | It does establish something much stronger than visual copying.
0376 | 
0377 | ---
0378 | 
0379 | ## Then I Saw the Flock
0380 | 
0381 | While looking at the same simulation, another visual pattern became difficult to ignore.
0382 | 
0383 | Groups of structures appeared to move together.
0384 | 
0385 | Not merely outward.
0386 | 
0387 | Together.
0388 | 
0389 | It looked remarkably like **flocking**.
0390 | 
0391 | But we had just spent several chapters warning ourselves against doing exactly this.
0392 | 
0393 | So we made the visual impression into a hypothesis.
0394 | 
0395 | ---
0396 | 
0397 | ### What Would Flocking Mean?
0398 | 
0399 | We did not begin by trying to reproduce every formal definition from swarm biology or active-matter physics.
0400 | 
0401 | We started with a much narrower question:
0402 | 
0403 | > Do nearby persistent moving structures travel in unusually similar directions?
0404 | 
0405 | That can be measured.
0406 | 
0407 | First we needed persistent motion tracks.
0408 | 
0409 | Using the causal graph, we followed plausible continuations of clusters through time.
0410 | 
0411 | The run produced:
0412 | 
0413 | ```text
0414 | 13,635 persistent motion tracks
0415 | ```
0416 | 
0417 | with minimum length:
0418 | 
0419 | ```text
0420 | 8 generations
0421 | ```
0422 | 
0423 | Across those tracks we obtained:
0424 | 
0425 | ```text
0426 | 633,808 motion observations
0427 | ```
0428 | 
0429 | Each observation gave us:
0430 | 
0431 | ```text
0432 | position
0433 | velocity
0434 | time
0435 | causal identity
0436 | ```
0437 | 
0438 | That was enough to perform a first test.
0439 | 
0440 | ---
0441 | 
0442 | ### Measuring Directional Alignment
0443 | 
0444 | For two velocity vectors (v_i) and (v_j), we normalize them and take their dot product:
0445 | 
0446 | $$
0447 | A_{ij}
0448 | ======
0449 | 
0450 | \frac{v_i}{|v_i|}
0451 | \cdot
0452 | \frac{v_j}{|v_j|}
0453 | $$
0454 | 
0455 | The result lies between `-1` and `+1`:
0456 | 
0457 | ```text
0458 | +1  = same direction
0459 |  0  = no directional agreement
0460 | -1  = opposite directions
0461 | ```
0462 | 
0463 | Then we compare simultaneously moving structures at different spatial separations.
0464 | 
0465 | For performance, we do not compare every structure with every other structure.
0466 | 
0467 | We use a spatial index and examine nearby pairs.
0468 | 
0469 | That is also a better experiment.
0470 | 
0471 | Structures on opposite sides of the universe are not particularly useful for measuring local collective motion.
0472 | 
0473 | ---
0474 | 
0475 | ## The First Result
0476 | 
0477 | The first experiment produced a striking result.
0478 | 
0479 | At short distances, observed velocity alignment was approximately:
0480 | 
0481 | ```text
0482 | 0.74
0483 | ```
0484 | 
0485 | while a velocity-shuffled control was much lower.
0486 | 
0487 | {{< figure src="/images/books/digital-life/ch10-outlier-flocking-test.png" caption="Nearby persistent structures exhibit much stronger directional alignment than a shuffled velocity control." >}}
0488 | 
0489 | So the visual observation was not completely imaginary.
0490 | 
0491 | There really was measurable short-range motion coherence.
0492 | 
0493 | But this still did not prove flocking.
0494 | 
0495 | We had immediately created another problem.
0496 | 
0497 | ---
0498 | 
0499 | ## Maybe Everything Is Simply Moving Outward
0500 | 
0501 | Outlier develops as an expanding structure.
0502 | 
0503 | Suppose nearby clusters sit on the same expanding front.
0504 | 
0505 | They might move in similar directions simply because both are being carried outward.
0506 | 
0507 | Imagine two pieces of debris on the same expanding circular wave.
0508 | 
0509 | They could have very similar velocity vectors without interacting with one another at all.
0510 | 
0511 | So we needed another control.
0512 | 
0513 | ---
0514 | 
0515 | ### Removing Radial Expansion
0516 | 
0517 | For each position (x_i), we calculate its radial direction relative to the centre:
0518 | 
0519 | $$
0520 | r_i
0521 | ===
0522 | 
0523 | \frac{x_i-c}{|x_i-c|}
0524 | $$
0525 | 
0526 | Then decompose its velocity into:
0527 | 
0528 | ```text
0529 | radial motion
0530 | +
0531 | non-radial motion
0532 | ```
0533 | 
0534 | and remove the radial component.
0535 | 
0536 | If the apparent flocking was really just expansion, the alignment should collapse.
0537 | 
0538 | Instead we found:
0539 | 
0540 | ```text
0541 | raw short-range alignment        = 0.7373
0542 | radial-subtracted alignment      = 0.7427
0543 | shuffled residual control        = 0.1933
0544 | ```
0545 | 
0546 | The alignment did not disappear.
0547 | 
0548 | It barely changed.
0549 | 
0550 | {{< figure src="/images/books/digital-life/ch11-outlier-radial-family-flocking.png" caption="Short-range motion coherence survives subtraction of the global radial expansion field." >}}
0551 | 
0552 | So we could rule out one simple explanation:
0553 | 
0554 | > **The observed coherence is not explained merely by every structure moving radially away from the original seed.**
0555 | 
0556 | That made the result more interesting.
0557 | 
0558 | And then the causal analysis gave us another possibility.
0559 | 
0560 | ---
0561 | 
0562 | ## Shared Causal History
0563 | 
0564 | Several spatially disconnected clusters can participate in the same causal organization.
0565 | 
0566 | So what looks like:
0567 | 
0568 | ```text
0569 | thing A
0570 | thing B
0571 | thing C
0572 | ```
0573 | 
0574 | might actually be:
0575 | 
0576 | ```text
0577 | component A
0578 | component B
0579 | component C
0580 |         ↓
0581 | one distributed causal process
0582 | ```
0583 | 
0584 | That does **not** establish that the components form one natural individual.
0585 | 
0586 | It gives us a narrower hypothesis:
0587 | 
0588 | > **Does shared causal organization explain the apparent coordinated motion?**
0589 | 
0590 | So we asked:
0591 | 
0592 | > Do structures belonging to the same causal lineage move more coherently?
0593 | 
0594 | ---
0595 | 
0596 | ### Giving Structures Causal Families
0597 | 
0598 | Our first family definition was too narrow.
0599 | 
0600 | It started with four branches descending from one early `c2`.
0601 | 
0602 | That produced strong same-family alignment:
0603 | 
0604 | ```text
0605 | 0.768
0606 | ```
0607 | 
0608 | but no useful different-family comparison.
0609 | 
0610 | So we strengthened the definition.
0611 | 
0612 | Every cluster was assigned its **most recent identifiable c2 ancestor**.
0613 | 
0614 | If a cluster itself was `c2`, it began a new family.
0615 | 
0616 | Otherwise we propagated ancestry through the causal graph.
0617 | 
0618 | If equally close causal paths disagreed, we marked the assignment ambiguous rather than inventing an answer.
0619 | 
0620 | The coverage was remarkable.
0621 | 
0622 | Among:
0623 | 
0624 | ```text
0625 | 138,891 clusters
0626 | ```
0627 | 
0628 | we assigned a recent `c2` ancestor to:
0629 | 
0630 | ```text
0631 | 138,132
0632 | ```
0633 | 
0634 | Only:
0635 | 
0636 | ```text
0637 | 10 ambiguous
0638 | 749 unassigned
0639 | ```
0640 | 
0641 | Among our motion observations:
0642 | 
0643 | ```text
0644 | 633,696 / 633,808
0645 | ```
0646 | 
0647 | received a recent-`c2` family label.
0648 | 
0649 | {{< figure src="/images/books/digital-life/ch11-outlier-family-coverage.png" caption="Almost every tracked moving structure in this single-seed experiment can be associated with a recent c2 causal ancestor." >}}
0650 | 
0651 | That tells us something important about this experiment.
0652 | 
0653 | Almost everything we are tracking belongs to a branching causal history.
0654 | 
0655 | But causal history alone does not tell us whether that history explains current motion.
0656 | 
0657 | ---
0658 | 
0659 | ## A Very Exciting Result
0660 | 
0661 | When we compared nearby structures after subtracting a local background flow, we initially found:
0662 | 
0663 | ```text
0664 | same recent-c2 family         = 0.828
0665 | different recent-c2 family    = -0.349
0666 | ```
0667 | 
0668 | That looked spectacular.
0669 | 
0670 | Perhaps causal relatives really were moving together.
0671 | 
0672 | Perhaps different families were even moving against one another.
0673 | 
0674 | But there was a problem.
0675 | 
0676 | The control itself could create the negative result.
0677 | 
0678 | ---
0679 | 
0680 | ### Our Experiment Was Wrong
0681 | 
0682 | This is worth slowing down for.
0683 | 
0684 | To estimate the local environmental flow around object `A`, we averaged nearby velocity vectors after excluding structures from `A`'s own family.
0685 | 
0686 | When comparing two different families `A` and `B`, `B` could therefore contribute to the estimate of `A`'s background — and `A` could contribute to the estimate of `B`'s background.
0687 | 
0688 | In the simplest case:
0689 | 
0690 | $$
0691 | r_A \approx v_A-v_B
0692 | $$
0693 | 
0694 | and:
0695 | 
0696 | $$
0697 | r_B \approx v_B-v_A
0698 | $$
0699 | 
0700 | which means:
0701 | 
0702 | $$
0703 | r_B \approx -r_A
0704 | $$
0705 | 
0706 | We had accidentally built an anti-correlation into the estimator.
0707 | 
0708 | So the `-0.349` result was not trustworthy.
0709 | 
0710 | ```mermaid
0711 | flowchart TD
0712 |     A["Object A velocity"] --> B["Background estimate for A uses B velocity"]
0713 |     B --> C["Residual A ≈ vA - vB"]
0714 |     D["Object B velocity"] --> E["Background estimate for B uses A velocity"]
0715 |     E --> F["Residual B ≈ vB - vA"]
0716 |     C --> G["Residuals artificially anti-correlated"]
0717 |     F --> G
0718 | ```
0719 | 
0720 | This is exactly why experiments need controls.
0721 | 
0722 | And why controls themselves need criticism.
0723 | 
0724 | ---
0725 | 
0726 | ### Pair-Excluded Background Flow
0727 | 
0728 | We fixed the problem.
0729 | 
0730 | When testing a pair from families `α` and `β`, we estimated local background motion while excluding **both** families from both estimates.
0731 | 
0732 | Now neither member of the tested pair could create the other's residual.
0733 | 
0734 | Using this stronger control we obtained:
0735 | 
0736 | ```text
0737 | Same recent c2 ancestor          0.746
0738 | Very close c2 ancestry           0.101
0739 | Close c2 ancestry                0.032
0740 | Distant c2 ancestry              0.135
0741 | Very distant c2 ancestry         0.081
0742 | ```
0743 | 
0744 | {{< figure src="/images/books/digital-life/ch11-outlier-relatedness-coherence.png" caption="Motion coherence is extremely high for structures sharing the same recent c2 ancestor, while more distant genealogical relationships show much weaker coherence." >}}
0745 | 
0746 | This again looked spectacular.
0747 | 
0748 | The apparent gap between:
0749 | 
0750 | ```text
0751 | same recent c2 ancestor    0.746
0752 | very close ancestry        0.101
0753 | ```
0754 | 
0755 | was:
0756 | 
0757 | ```text
0758 | 0.645
0759 | ```
0760 | 
0761 | That looked like strong evidence that recent causal ancestry predicted dynamical organization.
0762 | 
0763 | But there was another problem.
0764 | 
0765 | ---
0766 | 
0767 | ## The Four-and-a-Half-Cell Problem
0768 | 
0769 | The structures sharing the same recent `c2` ancestor were also extremely close together.
0770 | 
0771 | Their mean separation was only about:
0772 | 
0773 | ```text
0774 | 4.5 cells
0775 | ```
0776 | 
0777 | Other causal groups were typically tens of cells apart.
0778 | 
0779 | So we had confounded:
0780 | 
0781 | ```text
0782 | causal relatedness
0783 | ```
0784 | 
0785 | with:
0786 | 
0787 | ```text
0788 | spatial proximity
0789 | ```
0790 | 
0791 | Perhaps structures sharing a family move together because they are parts of the same tiny local formation.
0792 | 
0793 | Perhaps any two structures that close together would show similar motion.
0794 | 
0795 | Once again, the exciting interpretation had outrun the evidence.
0796 | 
0797 | So we performed another experiment.
0798 | 
0799 | ---
0800 | 
0801 | ## Distance-Matched Causal Coherence
0802 | 
0803 | We constructed a matched dataset.
0804 | 
0805 | For a same-family pair, we searched for different-family comparisons occurring under approximately the same conditions.
0806 | 
0807 | We matched on:
0808 | 
0809 | ```text
0810 | simulation time
0811 | spatial distance
0812 | local density
0813 | ```
0814 | 
0815 | and continued to use the stronger pair-excluded background-flow correction.
0816 | 
0817 | The question was now very precise:
0818 | 
0819 | > At similar times, at similar distances, and in similar local environments, do members of the same c2 causal family move more coherently than members of different families?
0820 | 
0821 | The underlying pair dataset contained:
0822 | 
0823 | ```text
0824 | 2,617,077 usable pair records
0825 | ```
0826 | 
0827 | The original matching analysis constructed approximately:
0828 | 
0829 | ```text
0830 | 65,000 matched pairs per group
0831 | ```
0832 | 
0833 | across hundreds of matched strata.
0834 | 
0835 | At first, the result appeared straightforward.
0836 | 
0837 | ---
0838 | 
0839 | ## The Apparent Effect Collapsed
0840 | 
0841 | After matching distance, simulation time and local density, the same-family and different-family means became almost identical.
0842 | 
0843 | The earlier analysis gave approximately:
0844 | 
0845 | ```text
0846 | same-family         0.1515
0847 | different-family    0.1588
0848 | difference         -0.0073
0849 | ```
0850 | 
0851 | The matched-stratum bootstrap interval crossed zero.
0852 | 
0853 | That was already strong evidence that the dramatic `0.645` apparent family gap had largely been a spatial-context effect.
0854 | 
0855 | But there was still one more problem.
0856 | 
0857 | The matched analysis itself assumed that same-family and different-family pairs were actually available for comparison across the distance range.
0858 | 
0859 | That assumption needed to be tested.
0860 | 
0861 | ---
0862 | 
0863 | ## Common Support
0864 | 
0865 | Matching cannot create comparison data where none exists.
0866 | 
0867 | If almost all same-family pairs occur at one distance and almost all different-family pairs occur somewhere else, a global matched estimate can hide a region in which the effect is not actually identifiable.
0868 | 
0869 | So we preserved all:
0870 | 
0871 | ```text
0872 | 2,617,077
0873 | ```
0874 | 
0875 | pair records and measured the spatial overlap directly.
0876 | 
0877 | The original Chapter 13 distance bins were:
0878 | 
0879 | ```text
0880 | 0–4
0881 | 4–8
0882 | 8–12
0883 | 12–16
0884 | 16–24
0885 | 24–32
0886 | 32–48
0887 | 48–64
0888 | 64–96
0889 | ```
0890 | 
0891 | For the primary analysis we declared a simple operational support rule:
0892 | 
0893 | > **A distance bin must contain at least 100 same-family and 100 different-family raw pair records.**
0894 | 
0895 | The largest contiguous region satisfying that condition was:
0896 | 
0897 | ```text
0898 | [4, 64) cells
0899 | ```
0900 | 
0901 | This immediately changed the interpretation.
0902 | 
0903 | The shortest-distance regime:
0904 | 
0905 | ```text
0906 | 0–4 cells
0907 | ```
0908 | 
0909 | was **outside** the primary common-support region.
0910 | 
0911 | So we cannot claim that matching has identified the ancestry effect there.
0912 | 
0913 | That part of the experiment remains unresolved.
0914 | 
0915 | {{< figure src="/images/books/digital-life/ch13-outlier-distance-overlap.png" caption="Same-family and different-family distance distributions. The shaded region marks the primary common-support interval from 4 to 64 cells." >}}
0916 | 
0917 | ---
0918 | 
0919 | ## Inside the Region We Can Actually Compare
0920 | 
0921 | Within the primary common-support interval:
0922 | 
0923 | ```text
0924 | 4–64 cells
0925 | ```
0926 | 
0927 | the raw descriptive data still showed an apparent family advantage:
0928 | 
0929 | ```text
0930 | same-family mean         0.1732
0931 | different-family mean    0.1166
0932 | raw difference          +0.0566
0933 | ```
0934 | 
0935 | That is important.
0936 | 
0937 | Even after restricting the distance range, the unbalanced raw data still looks as though same-family pairs move more coherently.
0938 | 
0939 | Then we apply the original matching procedure again.
0940 | 
0941 | Exact matching uses:
0942 | 
0943 | ```text
0944 | time bin
0945 | distance bin
0946 | density bin
0947 | ```
0948 | 
0949 | and takes equal numbers of same-family and different-family pairs within each matched stratum.
0950 | 
0951 | The result becomes:
0952 | 
0953 | ```text
0954 | matched same-family mean        +0.150823
0955 | matched different-family mean   +0.157890
0956 | matched pooled effect           -0.007067
0957 | ```
0958 | 
0959 | Across:
0960 | 
0961 | ```text
0962 | 64,948 matched pairs per group
0963 | 659 matched strata
0964 | ```
0965 | 
0966 | the equal-stratum effect was:
0967 | 
0968 | ```text
0969 | -0.026463
0970 | ```
0971 | 
0972 | with bootstrap interval:
0973 | 
0974 | ```text
0975 | [-0.066450, +0.012172]
0976 | ```
0977 | 
0978 | {{< figure src="/images/books/digital-life/ch11-outlier-distance-matched-coherence.png" caption="Inside the matched comparison, same-family and different-family motion coherence are essentially indistinguishable." >}}
0979 | 
0980 | This is the important result.
0981 | 
0982 | ---
0983 | 
0984 | ## How Much of the Original Effect Could Still Be Real?
0985 | 
0986 | The original apparent family gap was:
0987 | 
0988 | ```text
0989 | 0.746 - 0.101 = 0.645
0990 | ```
0991 | 
0992 | Inside common support, the upper end of the bootstrap interval is:
0993 | 
0994 | ```text
0995 | +0.012172
0996 | ```
0997 | 
0998 | So the largest positive ancestry effect compatible with that interval is approximately:
0999 | 
1000 | ```text
1001 | 0.012172 / 0.645
1002 | ≈
1003 | 1.9%
1004 | ```
1005 | 
1006 | of the original apparent gap.
1007 | 
1008 | That gives us a much stronger statement than:
1009 | 
1010 | > the confidence interval crosses zero.
1011 | 
1012 | We can now say:
1013 | 
1014 | > **Within the 4–64 cell region where the comparison has adequate empirical support, the data rule out anything remotely resembling the original apparent family effect. The upper bootstrap bound is only about 1.9% of the original 0.645 gap.**
1015 | 
1016 | That is a real negative result.
1017 | 
1018 | And it is much more informative than simply saying the effect disappeared.
1019 | 
1020 | ---
1021 | 
1022 | ## The Result Is Not an Artifact of One Support Threshold
1023 | 
1024 | The common-support criterion itself was also tested.
1025 | 
1026 | We repeated the analysis using minimum counts of:
1027 | 
1028 | ```text
1029 | 50
1030 | 100
1031 | 250
1032 | 500
1033 | ```
1034 | 
1035 | same-family and different-family records per distance bin.
1036 | 
1037 | At threshold `50`, common support expanded to:
1038 | 
1039 | ```text
1040 | 4–96 cells
1041 | ```
1042 | 
1043 | The matched pooled effect remained:
1044 | 
1045 | ```text
1046 | -0.0058
1047 | ```
1048 | 
1049 | and the equal-stratum effect remained slightly negative:
1050 | 
1051 | ```text
1052 | -0.0182
1053 | ```
1054 | 
1055 | At thresholds:
1056 | 
1057 | ```text
1058 | 100
1059 | 250
1060 | 500
1061 | ```
1062 | 
1063 | the support interval remained:
1064 | 
1065 | ```text
1066 | 4–64 cells
1067 | ```
1068 | 
1069 | and the matched result remained:
1070 | 
1071 | ```text
1072 | matched pooled effect     -0.0071
1073 | equal-stratum effect      -0.0265
1074 | upper bootstrap bound     +0.0122
1075 | ```
1076 | 
1077 | So the conclusion does not qualitatively depend on one arbitrary minimum-count threshold.
1078 | 
1079 | That increases our confidence in the bounded result.
1080 | 
1081 | ---
1082 | 
1083 | ## But the 0–4 Cell Regime Is Still Open
1084 | 
1085 | We should be equally precise about what the analysis did **not** resolve.
1086 | 
1087 | The structures sharing a recent `c2` ancestor are concentrated at extremely short range.
1088 | 
1089 | The `0–4` cell bin lies outside the declared primary common-support region.
1090 | 
1091 | That means:
1092 | 
1093 | ```text
1094 | same-family pairs
1095 | are common there
1096 | 
1097 | different-family controls
1098 | are too sparse for the same comparison
1099 | ```
1100 | 
1101 | So we cannot say:
1102 | 
1103 | > ancestry has no effect at 0–4 cells.
1104 | 
1105 | Nor can we say:
1106 | 
1107 | > ancestry causes the effect at 0–4 cells.
1108 | 
1109 | The correct status is:
1110 | 
1111 | ```text
1112 | UNRESOLVED
1113 | ```
1114 | 
1115 | That is not a weakness in the result.
1116 | 
1117 | It is the correct description of what the data can identify.
1118 | 
1119 | ---
1120 | 
1121 | ## So Was It Flocking?
1122 | 
1123 | Not on the evidence we currently have.
1124 | 
1125 | But the original observation was not worthless.
1126 | 
1127 | We learned several things.
1128 | 
1129 | 1. **Outlier exhibits strong short-range velocity coherence.**
1130 |    That is measurable.
1131 | 
1132 | 2. **The coherence is not explained by simple global radial expansion.**
1133 | 
1134 | 3. **Shared c2 ancestry initially appears to predict much stronger coherence.**
1135 | 
1136 | 4. **That dramatic ancestry signal collapses after controlling spatial distance, simulation time and local density over the region where same-family and different-family pairs have adequate overlap.**
1137 | 
1138 | 5. **The 0–4 cell regime remains unresolved because suitable different-family controls are too sparse there.**
1139 | 
1140 | So the bounded claim is:
1141 | 
1142 | > **Within the 4–64 cell region where same-family and different-family pairs have adequate common support, shared recent c2 ancestry contributes no detectable additional motion coherence after matching spatial distance, simulation time and local density. The upper bootstrap bound is only about 1.9% of the original apparent 0.645 family gap. The shortest 0–4 cell regime remains unresolved.**
1143 | 
1144 | That is not as exciting as:
1145 | 
1146 | > We discovered flocking.
1147 | 
1148 | It is much better.
1149 | 
1150 | Because we now have some idea what the evidence actually supports.
1151 | 
1152 | ---
1153 | 
1154 | ### What Might the Motion Be?
1155 | 
1156 | Our best current interpretation is not:
1157 | 
1158 | ```text
1159 | causal relatives
1160 | recognize one another
1161 | and flock
1162 | ```
1163 | 
1164 | It is closer to:
1165 | 
1166 | ```text
1167 | local geometry
1168 | +
1169 | local cellular dynamics
1170 | +
1171 | spatial organization
1172 | ↓
1173 | coherent motion
1174 | ```
1175 | 
1176 | That still matters.
1177 | 
1178 | Remember what Outlier is.
1179 | 
1180 | There are no explicit moving objects.
1181 | 
1182 | No velocity variable.
1183 | 
1184 | No steering force.
1185 | 
1186 | No alignment rule.
1187 | 
1188 | No flocking controller.
1189 | 
1190 | At the substrate level there are only:
1191 | 
1192 | ```text
1193 | binary cells
1194 | local neighborhoods
1195 | one deterministic update rule
1196 | ```
1197 | 
1198 | Yet from those rules emerge structures with measurable coherent local motion.
1199 | 
1200 | That is already remarkable.
1201 | 
1202 | We simply should not give that phenomenon a stronger name than our measurements justify.
1203 | 
1204 | ---
1205 | 
1206 | **## What Survived the Hypothesis?**
1207 | 
1208 | The strongest interpretation in this chapter did not survive.
1209 | 
1210 | We began with:
1211 | 
1212 | ```text
1213 | nearby structures move coherently
1214 | ↓
1215 | shared c2 ancestry may explain that coherence
1216 | ```
1217 | 
1218 | Inside the region where same-family and different-family pairs can actually be compared, that explanation failed.
1219 | 
1220 | The matched pooled effect was approximately:
1221 | 
1222 | ```text
1223 | -0.0071
1224 | ```
1225 | 
1226 | and the upper bootstrap bound on a positive ancestry effect was only about:
1227 | 
1228 | ```text
1229 | +0.0122
1230 | ```
1231 | 
1232 | roughly `1.9%` of the original apparent `0.645` family gap.
1233 | 
1234 | So the ancestry explanation does not survive over the `4–64` cell common-support interval.
1235 | 
1236 | But the observation that motivated it does.
1237 | 
1238 | **### The surviving observation**
1239 | 
1240 | Persistent moving structures exhibit strong short-range velocity coherence.
1241 | 
1242 | The first measurements gave approximately:
1243 | 
1244 | ```text
1245 | raw short-range alignment        0.7373
1246 | radial-subtracted alignment      0.7427
1247 | shuffled residual control        0.1933
1248 | ```
1249 | 
1250 | Removing the global radial expansion field did not remove the effect.
1251 | 
1252 | So the chapter leaves us with two separate statements:
1253 | 
1254 | ```text
1255 | SHORT-RANGE MOTION COHERENCE
1256 | MEASURED
1257 | 
1258 | ANCESTRY EXPLAINS THAT COHERENCE
1259 | FAILED OVER COMMON SUPPORT
1260 | ```
1261 | 
1262 | Those are not contradictory.
1263 | 
1264 | The experiment killed an explanation without killing the phenomenon.
1265 | 
1266 | **### Phenomenon record**
1267 | 
1268 | **Phenomenon:** Local coherent motion
1269 | 
1270 | **Status:** **MEASURED**
1271 | 
1272 | **Current bounded description:**
1273 | 
1274 | > Persistent structures in this Outlier run exhibit strong short-range directional coherence that survives subtraction of the global radial expansion field.
1275 | 
1276 | **Best current mechanistic description:**
1277 | 
1278 | ```text
1279 | local geometry
1280 | +
1281 | local cellular dynamics
1282 | +
1283 | spatial organization
1284 | ↓
1285 | coherent motion
1286 | ```
1287 | 
1288 | This description does not require:
1289 | 
1290 | ```text
1291 | flocking
1292 | social interaction
1293 | ancestry recognition
1294 | a natural individual
1295 | ```
1296 | 
1297 | **### Open cross-chapter hypothesis**
1298 | 
1299 | There is a broader possibility that should remain separate from the measured result:
1300 | 
1301 | > **Some of the local coherence may be part of a spatially propagating dynamical field rather than the motion of independent object-like structures.**
1302 | 
1303 | For now this is only an **OPEN HYPOTHESIS**.
1304 | 
1305 | The present chapter did not measure:
1306 | 
1307 | ```text
1308 | phase propagation
1309 | lag versus distance
1310 | propagation velocity
1311 | dispersion relation
1312 | travelling-wave structure
1313 | ```
1314 | 
1315 | So we should not call the phenomenon a wave.
1316 | 
1317 | A future audit can test the literal signature using the existing motion data:
1318 | 
1319 | ```text
1320 | activity at x,t
1321 | ↓ lag
1322 | activity at x+d,t+τ
1323 | ```
1324 | 
1325 | If the correlation peak moves systematically with distance, then a propagation velocity becomes measurable.
1326 | 
1327 | If it does not, the propagating-field interpretation should be rejected.
1328 | 
1329 | **### Important unresolved region**
1330 | 
1331 | The shortest-distance regime remains special.
1332 | 
1333 | ```text
1334 | 0–4 cells
1335 | ```
1336 | 
1337 | lies outside the declared common-support interval because suitable different-family controls are too sparse.
1338 | 
1339 | Therefore:
1340 | 
1341 | ```text
1342 | ANCESTRY EFFECT AT 0–4 CELLS
1343 | UNRESOLVED
1344 | ```
1345 | 
1346 | We should neither promote nor dismiss it.
1347 | 
1348 | **### What this phenomenon does not establish**
1349 | 
1350 | This surviving motion phenomenon does **not** establish:
1351 | 
1352 | - classical flocking,
1353 | - interaction between natural individuals,
1354 | - a shared wave mechanism,
1355 | - ancestry-dependent coordination,
1356 | - organism-like units,
1357 | - or life.
1358 | 
1359 | It establishes something narrower and, for this project, more useful:
1360 | 
1361 | > **Simple local cellular dynamics can generate strongly coherent short-range motion even after one of the most tempting biological explanations for that motion has been removed.**
1362 | 
1363 | That phenomenon now belongs in the project-wide phenomenon record independently of the chapter's ancestry hypothesis.
1364 | 
1365 | 
1366 | ---
1367 | 
1368 | ## The Most Important Result May Be Methodological
1369 | 
1370 | This investigation began with five words:
1371 | 
1372 | > That looks like flocking.
1373 | 
1374 | If we had stopped there, we would have had a nice animation and a bad claim.
1375 | 
1376 | Instead:
1377 | 
1378 | ```mermaid
1379 | flowchart TD
1380 |     A["Visual impression: That looks like flocking"] --> B["Operational definition"]
1381 |     B --> C["Tracking"]
1382 |     C --> D["Velocity measurement"]
1383 |     D --> E["Shuffled control"]
1384 |     E --> F["Radial-flow control"]
1385 |     F --> G["Causal-family hypothesis"]
1386 |     G --> H["Local-flow control"]
1387 |     H --> I["Discover estimator bug"]
1388 |     I --> J["Pair-excluded control"]
1389 |     J --> K["Discover spatial confound"]
1390 |     K --> L["Distance/time/density matching"]
1391 |     L --> M["Audit common support"]
1392 |     M --> N["Bound positive effect"]
1393 |     N --> O["Keep unresolved region unresolved"]
1394 | ```
1395 | 
1396 | This is exactly the process we need if we are going to talk seriously about digital life.
1397 | 
1398 | Interesting pictures are where questions begin.
1399 | 
1400 | Not where claims end.
1401 | 
1402 | ---
1403 | 
1404 | ## The Database Changed How We Could Work
1405 | 
1406 | There was another practical lesson.
1407 | 
1408 | Our simulation produced:
1409 | 
1410 | ```text
1411 | 138,891 clusters
1412 | 196,466 causal edges
1413 | 633,808 motion observations
1414 | 2,617,077 usable pair records
1415 | ```
1416 | 
1417 | Initially we recomputed expensive quantities every time we asked a new question.
1418 | 
1419 | That quickly became absurd.
1420 | 
1421 | So we created a SQLite database.
1422 | 
1423 | The expensive simulation became a reusable experimental specimen.
1424 | 
1425 | Then we improved the evidence architecture again.
1426 | 
1427 | The derived pair-level comparison dataset is now also preserved:
1428 | 
1429 | ```text
1430 | ch13_pair_datasets
1431 | ch13_pair_records
1432 | ```
1433 | 
1434 | So the pipeline becomes:
1435 | 
1436 | ```mermaid
1437 | flowchart LR
1438 |     A["Outlier run"] --> B["SQLite specimen"]
1439 |     B --> C["clusters"]
1440 |     B --> D["causal edges"]
1441 |     B --> E["c2 occurrences"]
1442 |     B --> F["motion observations"]
1443 |     C & D & E & F --> G["pair-level evidence"]
1444 |     G --> H["distance matching"]
1445 |     G --> I["common-support analysis"]
1446 |     G --> J["sensitivity analysis"]
1447 |     H & I & J --> K["bounded claim"]
1448 | ```
1449 | 
1450 | This matters beyond performance.
1451 | 
1452 | It changes how we investigate these systems.
1453 | 
1454 | The simulation becomes something closer to an experimental specimen.
1455 | 
1456 | We can ask multiple questions of the same run while preserving exactly which data each conclusion came from.
1457 | 
1458 | ---
1459 | 
1460 | ## Causal Reproduction Still Survives
1461 | 
1462 | The flocking hypothesis weakened.
1463 | 
1464 | The reproduction result did not disappear with it.
1465 | 
1466 | We still observed:
1467 | 
1468 | ```text
1469 | 144 c2 occurrences
1470 | ```
1471 | 
1472 | and constructed causal paths between recurring `c2` structures.
1473 | 
1474 | The original `c2` at `t = 2` lies at the root of a branching causal return structure.
1475 | 
1476 | So the evidence for reproduction is not:
1477 | 
1478 | > it looks like one object became several
1479 | 
1480 | It is:
1481 | 
1482 | ```text
1483 | structural recurrence
1484 | +
1485 | causal ancestry
1486 | +
1487 | branching lineage
1488 | ```
1489 | 
1490 | That distinction matters.
1491 | 
1492 | The motion investigation failed to give us a natural social unit.
1493 | 
1494 | But it did not undo the causal reproduction result.
1495 | 
1496 | ---
1497 | 
1498 | ## Causal Organization Is Not Yet Individuality
1499 | 
1500 | Outlier has shown us something important.
1501 | 
1502 | Visible connectedness and causal organization do not necessarily coincide.
1503 | 
1504 | Two disconnected clusters may participate in the same causal process.
1505 | 
1506 | Two visually similar structures may not share the causal relationship we assume.
1507 | 
1508 | Several nearby structures may move coherently because of local dynamics rather than because they form a flock.
1509 | 
1510 | But none of that yet gives us a natural definition of an individual.
1511 | 
1512 | So the surviving conclusion is narrower:
1513 | 
1514 | > **Geometry alone is not sufficient to tell us how to partition the system into meaningful causal organizations.**
1515 | 
1516 | That is enough for this book.
1517 | 
1518 | Questions about individuality can wait until we have earned them.
1519 | 
1520 | ---
1521 | 
1522 | ## What We Did Not Prove
1523 | 
1524 | We should be explicit.
1525 | 
1526 | We have **not** shown that Outlier is alive.
1527 | 
1528 | We have not shown classical flocking.
1529 | 
1530 | We have not shown that `c2` is the uniquely correct unit of organization.
1531 | 
1532 | We have not shown that our counterfactual causal test captures every meaningful dependency.
1533 | 
1534 | We have not shown that connected clusters correspond to organisms.
1535 | 
1536 | We have not shown that ancestry has no effect in the `0–4` cell regime.
1537 | 
1538 | We have not shown that our `512 × 512`, `1,600`-generation result generalizes to the larger `1024 × 1024`, `20,000`-update published regime.
1539 | 
1540 | And we have not shown open-ended evolution.
1541 | 
1542 | What we have shown is narrower.
1543 | 
1544 | ---
1545 | 
1546 | ## Evidence Ledger
1547 | 
1548 | | Claim                                                             | Status                                       | Evidence / limitation                        |
1549 | | ----------------------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------- |
1550 | | Later `c2` occurrences recur in our run                           | **SUPPORTED**                                | 144 occurrences from `t=2` to `t=1598`       |
1551 | | Recurring `c2` structures participate in causal ancestry          | **SUPPORTED**                                | counterfactual causal graph                  |
1552 | | The `c2` return graph branches                                    | **SUPPORTED**                                | 99 visible return edges                      |
1553 | | Persistent structures exhibit short-range velocity coherence      | **SUPPORTED**                                | observed alignment ~0.74 vs shuffled control |
1554 | | Global radial expansion explains the coherence                    | **FAILED**                                   | radial-subtracted alignment remains ~0.7427  |
1555 | | Same-family ancestry produces the original dramatic coherence gap | **FAILED OVER COMMON SUPPORT**               | matched pooled effect `-0.0071`              |
1556 | | Positive ancestry effect larger than `+0.0122` inside 4–64 cells  | **NOT SUPPORTED BY THIS BOOTSTRAP INTERVAL** | upper 95% bound                              |
1557 | | `0–4` cell ancestry effect                                        | **UNRESOLVED**                               | inadequate different-family overlap          |
1558 | | Observed motion constitutes flocking                              | **NOT ESTABLISHED**                          | interaction / unit criteria not demonstrated |
1559 | | Causal family defines a natural individual                        | **UNTESTED**                                 | ancestry is not individuation                |
1560 | | Result generalizes to full 1024² × 20,000 Outlier regime          | **UNTESTED**                                 | current run is smaller and shorter           |
1561 | | Outlier is alive                                                  | **NOT CLAIMED**                              | evidence insufficient                        |
1562 | 
1563 | ---
1564 | 
1565 | ## Bounded Claims
1566 | 
1567 | From this chapter we can reasonably claim:
1568 | 
1569 | 1. **Causal recurrence** — Later `c2` structures can be connected to earlier `c2` structures through measured counterfactual dependencies in our reproduction of Outlier.
1570 | 
1571 | 2. **Branching lineage** — The resulting causal return graph contains branching ancestry rather than mere isolated recurrence.
1572 | 
1573 | 3. **Local motion coherence** — Persistent moving structures exhibit strong short-range directional alignment relative to a shuffled-velocity control.
1574 | 
1575 | 4. **Not merely radial expansion** — Removing the global radial component does not eliminate that short-range coherence.
1576 | 
1577 | 5. **The dramatic family effect does not survive control over the identifiable region** — Within `4–64` cells, after matching spatial distance, simulation time and local density, the same-family motion advantage is approximately zero.
1578 | 
1579 | 6. **A strong quantitative bound** — The upper bootstrap bound on a positive ancestry effect inside common support is approximately `+0.0122`, only about `1.9%` of the original apparent `0.645` family gap.
1580 | 
1581 | 7. **The shortest-distance regime remains unresolved** — The `0–4` cell region lacks adequate different-family common support under the declared criterion.
1582 | 
1583 | That is where the evidence currently stops.
1584 | 
1585 | ---
1586 | 
1587 | ## And That Is Enough
1588 | 
1589 | There is a temptation in artificial life to treat every surprising pattern as a sign that we are almost there.
1590 | 
1591 | Almost alive.
1592 | 
1593 | Almost intelligent.
1594 | 
1595 | Almost social.
1596 | 
1597 | Almost evolutionary.
1598 | 
1599 | But that is backwards.
1600 | 
1601 | The interesting work begins when we stop rewarding the system for looking familiar.
1602 | 
1603 | Outlier gave us something that looked like reproduction.
1604 | 
1605 | So we asked whether the apparent descendants were causally connected.
1606 | 
1607 | Then it gave us something that looked like flocking.
1608 | 
1609 | So we measured the motion.
1610 | 
1611 | Then we tried to destroy our own explanation.
1612 | 
1613 | Again.
1614 | 
1615 | And again.
1616 | 
1617 | The most impressive result in the experiment was not the apparent family effect.
1618 | 
1619 | It was watching that effect collapse as the controls became good enough.
1620 | 
1621 | That is the method.
1622 | 
1623 | ```text
1624 | SEE SOMETHING
1625 | ↓
1626 | NAME THE HYPOTHESIS
1627 | ↓
1628 | DEFINE THE MEASUREMENT
1629 | ↓
1630 | BUILD THE CONTROL
1631 | ↓
1632 | LOOK FOR THE CONFOUND
1633 | ↓
1634 | BUILD A BETTER CONTROL
1635 | ↓
1636 | CHECK WHETHER THE COMPARISON IS IDENTIFIABLE
1637 | ↓
1638 | KEEP UNRESOLVED REGIONS UNRESOLVED
1639 | ↓
1640 | BOUND WHAT SURVIVES
1641 | ```
1642 | 
1643 | Digital life will not be discovered by finding the right metaphor.
1644 | 
1645 | It will be discovered by finding which properties survive this process.
1646 | 
1647 | ---
1648 | 
1649 | ## The Deeper Question
1650 | 
1651 | Outlier has now forced us to distinguish several ideas that initially looked like one:
1652 | 
1653 | ```text
1654 | shape
1655 | causal continuity
1656 | reproduction
1657 | collective motion
1658 | organization
1659 | ```
1660 | 
1661 | They are not the same thing.
1662 | 
1663 | A shape can recur without reproducing.
1664 | 
1665 | A structure can reproduce without giving us an obvious biological body.
1666 | 
1667 | Several disconnected structures can participate in one causal process.
1668 | 
1669 | Nearby structures can move together without being a flock.
1670 | 
1671 | Genealogical relatedness does not necessarily determine present dynamical organization.
1672 | 
1673 | And sometimes the most interesting part of an experiment is not the effect we find.
1674 | 
1675 | It is discovering where the effect cannot actually be identified.
1676 | 
1677 | So we leave Outlier with two things.
1678 | 
1679 | First:
1680 | 
1681 | > **Causal reproduction survives.**
1682 | 
1683 | Second:
1684 | 
1685 | > **Our strongest interpretation of the coordinated motion does not.**
1686 | 
1687 | That is exactly what an external reference system is for.
1688 | 
1689 | We have attacked the interpretation.
1690 | 
1691 | Now we can return to our controlled laboratory and carry forward only what survived.
```
