```markdown
0001 | +++
0002 | title = "14: The Digital Crystal"
0003 | date = "2026-08-11T20:36:00+01:00"
0004 | draft = false
0005 | description = "Can a local computational growth process turn an external signal into persistent morphology? We build a Digital Crystal, hide the signal that formed it, and test what the final structure actually preserves."
0006 | categories = ["Programming", "Artificial Life"]
0007 | tags = ["Digital Life", "Digital Crystal", "Cellular Automata", "Morphology", "Information", "Simulation", "Experiment"]
0008 | series = ["Digital Life From First Principles"]
0009 | +++
0010 | 
0011 | We are leaving Outlier.
0012 | 
0013 | Not because it failed.
0014 | 
0015 | Quite the opposite.
0016 | 
0017 | Outlier did exactly what we needed an external reference system to do.
0018 | 
0019 | It showed us that very simple digital physics can support structures that recur, move, interact and participate in measurable causal lineages.
0020 | 
0021 | Then we attacked our own interpretations.
0022 | 
0023 | Some survived.
0024 | 
0025 | Some did not.
0026 | 
0027 | The causal reproduction result survived.
0028 | 
0029 | One of our strongest explanations for the apparent coordinated motion did not.
0030 | 
0031 | That is useful.
0032 | 
0033 | But Outlier is not the next version of our crystal.
0034 | 
0035 | We are not going to copy it.
0036 | 
0037 | We are not going to take its structures and start bolting biological capabilities onto them.
0038 | 
0039 | We are going back to the laboratory.
0040 | 
0041 | There is one idea worth carrying with us:
0042 | 
0043 | ```text
0044 | local computation
0045 | ↓
0046 | repeated interaction
0047 | ↓
0048 | characteristic larger-scale structure
0049 | ```
0050 | 
0051 | That idea is much smaller than an organism.
0052 | 
0053 | It is much smaller than reproduction.
0054 | 
0055 | It does not require individuality.
0056 | 
0057 | And it brings us back to something we encountered earlier.
0058 | 
0059 | The crystal.
0060 | 
0061 | ---
0062 | 
0063 | ## Back to the Laboratory
0064 | 
0065 | In Chapter 11 we built an intentionally trivial growth system.
0066 | 
0067 | One seed.
0068 | 
0069 | A hexagonal lattice.
0070 | 
0071 | A local rule.
0072 | 
0073 | Occupied cells remained occupied.
0074 | 
0075 | Empty cells could become occupied through local contact.
0076 | 
0077 | No target morphology.
0078 | 
0079 | No stored radius.
0080 | 
0081 | No global shape.
0082 | 
0083 | And yet a characteristic large-scale structure appeared.
0084 | 
0085 | That experiment established something small but important:
0086 | 
0087 | > **A repeated local computational process can generate persistent global structure without containing an explicit representation of that structure.**
0088 | 
0089 | Then we left our laboratory.
0090 | 
0091 | Outlier was an excursion into a system we did not design.
0092 | 
0093 | Now we return.
0094 | 
0095 | But we are not simply continuing the Chapter 11 model unchanged.
0096 | 
0097 | We are going to ask it a new question.
0098 | 
0099 | > Can the environment leave a measurable mark on the structure that grows inside it?
0100 | 
0101 | That requires a new experimental model.
0102 | 
0103 | ---
0104 | 
0105 | ## What Changed Since Chapter 11?
0106 | 
0107 | This matters enough to state explicitly.
0108 | 
0109 | Chapter 14 does **not** use exactly the same growth rule as Chapter 11.
0110 | 
0111 | The Chapter 11 crystal was deliberately minimal:
0112 | 
0113 | ```text
0114 | CHAPTER 11
0115 | 
0116 | binary occupancy
0117 | +
0118 | hexagonal neighborhood
0119 | +
0120 | irreversible growth
0121 | +
0122 | deterministic local attachment
0123 | ```
0124 | 
0125 | Its purpose was to ask what continued structured growth requires.
0126 | 
0127 | The model in this chapter adds several mechanisms:
0128 | 
0129 | ```text
0130 | CHAPTER 14
0131 | 
0132 | binary occupancy
0133 | +
0134 | hexagonal neighborhood
0135 | +
0136 | irreversible growth
0137 | +
0138 | stochastic attachment
0139 | +
0140 | environmental forcing
0141 | +
0142 | fixed lattice anisotropy
0143 | +
0144 | crowding penalty
0145 | ```
0146 | 
0147 | Why introduce them?
0148 | 
0149 | Because our new question is different.
0150 | 
0151 | Chapter 11 asked:
0152 | 
0153 | > Can simple local growth generate characteristic persistent form?
0154 | 
0155 | Chapter 14 asks:
0156 | 
0157 | > Can changing external conditions influence that growth strongly enough to leave a persistent measurable signature?
0158 | 
0159 | To ask that question, the environment needs some way to modulate local attachment.
0160 | 
0161 | Stochastic attachment gives the forcing room to influence which locally available events actually happen.
0162 | 
0163 | Anisotropy gives the lattice persistent directional structure.
0164 | 
0165 | Crowding prevents attachment probability from increasing without bound simply because a location has many occupied neighbours.
0166 | 
0167 | These mechanisms are introduced for this experiment.
0168 | 
0169 | They are not discoveries from Chapter 11.
0170 | 
0171 | And this has an important consequence:
0172 | 
0173 | > **Results from the Chapter 11 crystal do not automatically transfer to Digital Crystal v1.**
0174 | 
0175 | For example, Chapter 11's hole-filling experiment does not prove that this stochastic model has the same damage response.
0176 | 
0177 | This is a new model.
0178 | 
0179 | It must earn its own claims.
0180 | 
0181 | ---
0182 | 
0183 | ## We Gave It a Name
0184 | 
0185 | We gave it a name.
0186 | 
0187 | That was the easy part.
0188 | 
0189 | ```text
0190 | DIGITAL CRYSTAL
0191 | ```
0192 | 
0193 | The harder question is whether the name means anything.
0194 | 
0195 | A digital crystal cannot mean:
0196 | 
0197 | > a thing that looks like quartz
0198 | 
0199 | Nor can it mean:
0200 | 
0201 | > anything arranged on a hexagonal grid
0202 | 
0203 | Those would be visual definitions.
0204 | 
0205 | And we already know what happens when we allow appearance to substitute for mechanism.
0206 | 
0207 | So we need a computational definition.
0208 | 
0209 | A physical crystal acquires structure through repeated local interactions during formation.
0210 | 
0211 | The large-scale structure follows from those interactions.
0212 | 
0213 | Let us try the analogous idea digitally.
0214 | 
0215 | Our first working definition is:
0216 | 
0217 | > **A Digital Crystal is a local computational growth process in which characteristics of an external input become expressed as persistent, measurable morphology.**
0218 | 
0219 | That is deliberately narrow.
0220 | 
0221 | It says nothing about:
0222 | 
0223 | ```text
0224 | life
0225 | memory
0226 | learning
0227 | adaptation
0228 | reproduction
0229 | intelligence
0230 | agency
0231 | ```
0232 | 
0233 | Good.
0234 | 
0235 | We have a definition that gives us an experiment.
0236 | 
0237 | ---
0238 | 
0239 | ## Give the Crystal an Environment
0240 | 
0241 | Our earlier crystal could be written approximately as:
0242 | 
0243 | $$
0244 | C_{t+1}=G(C_t)
0245 | $$
0246 | 
0247 | where (C_t) is the current crystal and (G) is the local growth rule.
0248 | 
0249 | Now we introduce an environmental forcing signal (E_t):
0250 | 
0251 | $$
0252 | C_{t+1}=G(C_t,E_t)
0253 | $$
0254 | 
0255 | The signal does not draw the crystal.
0256 | 
0257 | It does not say:
0258 | 
0259 | ```python
0260 | if signal == "sine":
0261 |     draw_sine_shape()
0262 | ```
0263 | 
0264 | That would merely be a strange plotting library.
0265 | 
0266 | The same growth mechanism must operate under every source.
0267 | 
0268 | The external signal only changes the conditions under which individual local attachment events occur.
0269 | 
0270 | ```mermaid
0271 | flowchart LR
0272 |     E["Environmental signal E(t)"] --> G["Local growth dynamics G(C,E)"]
0273 |     C["Current crystal state C(t)"] --> G
0274 |     G --> C2["Next crystal state C(t+1)"]
0275 |     C2 --> M["Final morphology"]
0276 | ```
0277 | 
0278 | Different input.
0279 | 
0280 | Same growth rule.
0281 | 
0282 | Different result?
0283 | 
0284 | That is our first question.
0285 | 
0286 | ---
0287 | 
0288 | ## The Local Rule
0289 | 
0290 | We retain the axial hexagonal lattice.
0291 | 
0292 | Every cell has six immediate neighbors.
0293 | 
0294 | ```python
0295 | HEX_DIRECTIONS = (
0296 |     (1, 0),
0297 |     (1, -1),
0298 |     (0, -1),
0299 |     (-1, 0),
0300 |     (-1, 1),
0301 |     (0, 1),
0302 | )
0303 | ```
0304 | 
0305 | Growth remains local.
0306 | 
0307 | For every candidate frontier cell, attachment depends on several quantities:
0308 | 
0309 | ```text
0310 | occupied-neighbour count
0311 | fixed lattice anisotropy
0312 | current environmental signal
0313 | local crowding
0314 | stochastic attachment
0315 | ```
0316 | 
0317 | There is no target morphology.
0318 | 
0319 | No global drawing routine.
0320 | 
0321 | No stored answer describing what the crystal should become.
0322 | 
0323 | The signal only affects attachment probability.
0324 | 
0325 | One convenient form is:
0326 | 
0327 | $$
0328 | P(\text{attach})
0329 | ================
0330 | 
0331 | \sigma(
0332 | a
0333 | +
0334 | bn
0335 | +
0336 | cE_t
0337 | +
0338 | dA
0339 | --
0340 | 
0341 | q
0342 | )
0343 | $$
0344 | 
0345 | where:
0346 | - (n) is occupied-neighbor count,
0347 | - (E_t) is the current signal,
0348 | - (A) is a fixed local anisotropy term,
0349 | - (q) is a crowding penalty,
0350 | - (\sigma) maps the result into a probability.
0351 | 
0352 | For Digital Crystal v1 the growth parameters are frozen during the experiment.
0353 | 
0354 | The important experimental constraint is:
0355 | 
0356 | > **The growth mechanism remains fixed while the source changes.**
0357 | 
0358 | Otherwise source recovery would tell us nothing.
0359 | 
0360 | ---
0361 | 
0362 | ## First Make Sure It Still Grows
0363 | 
0364 | Before giving the system interesting signals, give it the least interesting signal possible:
0365 | 
0366 | $$
0367 | E(t)=0
0368 | $$
0369 | 
0370 | Constant.
0371 | 
0372 | No oscillation.
0373 | 
0374 | No changing environment.
0375 | 
0376 | The generalized model should still produce a growing structure.
0377 | 
0378 | It did.
0379 | 
0380 | In the full baseline run the crystal reached approximately:
0381 | 
0382 | ```text
0383 | occupied cells        5,924
0384 | maximum hex radius       44
0385 | boundary edges          552
0386 | ```
0387 | 
0388 | {{< figure
0389 | src="/images/books/digital-life/ch14-01-baseline.png"
0390 | alt="Baseline Digital Crystal grown under a constant zero-valued environmental signal, shown alongside its input signal and population growth."
0391 | caption="Digital Crystal v1 produces a growing structure under constant forcing. This establishes only that the generalized model grows."
0392 | 
0393 | >}}
0394 | 
0395 | This proves very little.
0396 | 
0397 | It does **not** prove that Chapter 11's properties survived.
0398 | 
0399 | It does not establish robustness.
0400 | 
0401 | It does not establish anything resembling life.
0402 | 
0403 | It merely establishes that our generalized model remains a growth process.
0404 | 
0405 | That is enough to continue.
0406 | 
0407 | ---
0408 | 
0409 | ## Six Environments
0410 | 
0411 | Now give exactly the same growth rule six different types of forcing:
0412 | 
0413 | ```text
0414 | constant
0415 | sine
0416 | square
0417 | sawtooth
0418 | white noise
0419 | random walk
0420 | ```
0421 | 
0422 | The individual source instances vary.
0423 | 
0424 | Periods vary.
0425 | 
0426 | Phases vary.
0427 | 
0428 | Noise realizations vary.
0429 | 
0430 | Random walks vary.
0431 | 
0432 | At each growth step the crystal receives only the scalar value currently presented to it.
0433 | 
0434 | For one run:
0435 | 
0436 | $$
0437 | E_t=\sin(\omega t+\phi)
0438 | $$
0439 | 
0440 | Another receives a square wave.
0441 | 
0442 | Another receives white noise.
0443 | 
0444 | Another receives a random walk.
0445 | 
0446 | {{< figure
0447 | src="/images/books/digital-life/ch14-02-source-signals.png"
0448 | alt="Example environmental signals used to drive Digital Crystal growth: constant, sine, square, sawtooth, white noise and random walk."
0449 | caption="Six forcing-process families. Digital Crystal v1 receives only the current scalar environmental value."
0450 | 
0451 | >}}
0452 | 
0453 | Then we let the crystals grow.
0454 | 
0455 | Same model.
0456 | 
0457 | Different environment.
0458 | 
0459 | {{< figure
0460 | src="/images/books/digital-life/ch14-02-source-gallery.png"
0461 | alt="Six Digital Crystals grown using the same local rule but driven by constant, sine, square, sawtooth, white-noise and random-walk signals."
0462 | caption="Same local growth mechanism, different forcing processes. The morphologies appear different, but appearance is only the start of the experiment."
0463 | 
0464 | >}}
0465 | 
0466 | And immediately we encounter the problem that has followed us through this entire book.
0467 | 
0468 | They look different.
0469 | 
0470 | So what?
0471 | 
0472 | ---
0473 | 
0474 | ## Pretty Pictures Are Cheap
0475 | 
0476 | It would be very easy to stop here.
0477 | 
0478 | We could write:
0479 | 
0480 | > Look. Different signals generate different crystals.
0481 | 
0482 | And the chapter would make an attractive demonstration.
0483 | 
0484 | But thirteen chapters have taught us not to do that.
0485 | 
0486 | A picture can suggest a hypothesis.
0487 | 
0488 | It cannot establish the explanation.
0489 | 
0490 | Perhaps one random seed generated a larger structure by chance.
0491 | 
0492 | Perhaps square waves merely produce a larger mean attachment probability.
0493 | 
0494 | Perhaps our eyes are categorizing noise.
0495 | 
0496 | Perhaps the source classes differ in some trivial statistic that becomes encoded as total area.
0497 | 
0498 | We need populations.
0499 | 
0500 | Not examples.
0501 | 
0502 | ---
0503 | 
0504 | ## Six Hundred Crystals
0505 | 
0506 | For the main source-family experiment we generated:
0507 | 
0508 | ```text
0509 | 100 constant
0510 | 100 sine
0511 | 100 square
0512 | 100 sawtooth
0513 | 100 white noise
0514 | 100 random walk
0515 | ```
0516 | 
0517 | A total of:
0518 | 
0519 | ```text
0520 | 600 crystals
0521 | ```
0522 | 
0523 | Each experiment uses:
0524 | 
0525 | ```text
0526 | same Digital Crystal rule
0527 | same experimental horizon
0528 | same measurement system
0529 | different source instance
0530 | different stochastic growth realization
0531 | ```
0532 | 
0533 | Now we stop looking at individual pictures.
0534 | 
0535 | We measure them.
0536 | 
0537 | ```mermaid
0538 | flowchart TD
0539 |     S1["Constant ×100"] --> G["Same fixed growth rule"]
0540 |     S2["Sine ×100"] --> G
0541 |     S3["Square ×100"] --> G
0542 |     S4["Sawtooth ×100"] --> G
0543 |     S5["White noise ×100"] --> G
0544 |     S6["Random walk ×100"] --> G
0545 |     G --> M["Morphology: 42 measured features"]
0546 |     M --> H["Hide source labels"]
0547 |     H --> C["Train on one subset"]
0548 |     C --> T["Test on unseen crystals"]
0549 | ```
0550 | 
0551 | ---
0552 | 
0553 | ## Measure the Morphology
0554 | 
0555 | For every final crystal we extracted 42 morphological measurements.
0556 | 
0557 | They included quantities such as:
0558 | 
0559 | ```text
0560 | area
0561 | perimeter
0562 | maximum radius
0563 | compactness
0564 | boundary roughness
0565 | bounding-box aspect
0566 | centroid displacement
0567 | radial distribution
0568 | angular distribution
0569 | six-fold angular structure
0570 | boundary-radius variation
0571 | ```
0572 | 
0573 | The source signal itself is **not** included.
0574 | 
0575 | The classifier gets:
0576 | 
0577 | ```text
0578 | the final measured crystal
0579 | ```
0580 | 
0581 | not:
0582 | 
0583 | ```text
0584 | the final crystal
0585 | +
0586 | the answer
0587 | ```
0588 | 
0589 | {{< figure
0590 | src="/images/books/digital-life/ch14-05-morphology-metrics.png"
0591 | alt="Distributions of several measured Digital Crystal morphology features across the six source-process families."
0592 | caption="Some measured morphological characteristics differ systematically across forcing-process families."
0593 | 
0594 | >}}
0595 | 
0596 | Already this is more useful than looking at the gallery.
0597 | 
0598 | There are measurable differences.
0599 | 
0600 | But measurable differences still do not tell us how much source information survives.
0601 | 
0602 | ---
0603 | 
0604 | ## Hide the Source
0605 | 
0606 | Imagine I hand you one finished crystal.
0607 | 
0608 | I do not tell you whether it grew under:
0609 | 
0610 | ```text
0611 | sine
0612 | square
0613 | noise
0614 | random walk
0615 | ...
0616 | ```
0617 | 
0618 | Can the forcing family be recovered from morphology alone?
0619 | 
0620 | There are six classes.
0621 | 
0622 | Random guessing succeeds at:
0623 | 
0624 | $$
0625 | \frac{1}{6}\approx16.7%
0626 | $$
0627 | 
0628 | That gives us a clean baseline.
0629 | 
0630 | Train on one part of the crystal population.
0631 | 
0632 | Test on crystals the model has never seen.
0633 | 
0634 | ---
0635 | 
0636 | ## Can We Recover the Source?
0637 | 
0638 | The held-out result was:
0639 | 
0640 | ```text
0641 | chance                 16.7%
0642 | 
0643 | random forest           52.2%
0644 | logistic regression     53.9%
0645 | ```
0646 | 
0647 | {{< figure
0648 | src="/images/books/digital-life/ch14-06-source-accuracy.png"
0649 | alt="Held-out classification accuracy for recovering the source process from Digital Crystal morphology compared with the six-way chance baseline."
0650 | caption="Source-process family can be recovered from held-out Digital Crystal morphology substantially above the 16.7% chance baseline."
0651 | 
0652 | >}}
0653 | 
0654 | Two very different classifier families produce broadly similar performance.
0655 | 
0656 | That matters.
0657 | 
0658 | We are not relying on one unusual model discovering one unusual boundary.
0659 | 
0660 | The confusion matrix shows that the retained information is uneven.
0661 | 
0662 | {{< figure
0663 | src="/images/books/digital-life/ch14-06-source-confusion.png"
0664 | alt="Confusion matrix showing predictions of source-process family from final Digital Crystal morphology."
0665 | caption="Source recovery is substantial but incomplete. Some forcing families leave more distinctive morphological signatures than others."
0666 | 
0667 | >}}
0668 | 
0669 | Now we have a result worth keeping.
0670 | 
0671 | Not:
0672 | 
0673 | > The crystal remembers its history.
0674 | 
0675 | Not:
0676 | 
0677 | > The crystal understands the environment.
0678 | 
0679 | Something much smaller:
0680 | 
0681 | > **The final morphology contains information that makes forcing-process family recoverable substantially above chance.**
0682 | 
0683 | The relationship is:
0684 | 
0685 | ```text
0686 | source process
0687 | ↓
0688 | local stochastic growth
0689 | ↓
0690 | persistent morphology
0691 | ↓
0692 | source family partly recoverable
0693 | ```
0694 | 
0695 | That is evidence for the Digital Crystal definition.
0696 | 
0697 | ---
0698 | 
0699 | ## But What Exactly Was Recovered?
0700 | 
0701 | This is where the experiment becomes interesting.
0702 | 
0703 | Consider sine and square forcing.
0704 | 
0705 | They differ in temporal ordering.
0706 | 
0707 | But they also differ in:
0708 | 
0709 | ```text
0710 | value distribution
0711 | time spent near extrema
0712 | autocorrelation
0713 | transition structure
0714 | possibly other simple statistics
0715 | ```
0716 | 
0717 | So perhaps the classifier is not recovering the temporal process.
0718 | 
0719 | Perhaps it is recovering some broad statistical property of the values experienced during growth.
0720 | 
0721 | That would still be a result.
0722 | 
0723 | But it would be a different result.
0724 | 
0725 | So now we try to destroy the stronger interpretation.
0726 | 
0727 | ---
0728 | 
0729 | ## The Same-Mean Control
0730 | 
0731 | The varying signals were normalized to approximately the same mean.
0732 | 
0733 | The constant control remained zero.
0734 | 
0735 | Yet morphology-class centroids still differed substantially.
0736 | 
0737 | Standardized distances from the constant population were approximately:
0738 | 
0739 | ```text
0740 | sine          4.45
0741 | square       13.82
0742 | saw           2.44
0743 | white noise   2.40
0744 | random walk   1.40
0745 | ```
0746 | 
0747 | So mean forcing alone cannot explain the source-family result.
0748 | 
0749 | Variation matters.
0750 | 
0751 | But this still does not establish that **temporal order** matters.
0752 | 
0753 | ---
0754 | 
0755 | ## Destroy Time
0756 | 
0757 | Take a source sequence:
0758 | 
0759 | ```text
0760 | 0.7
0761 | 0.2
0762 | -0.3
0763 | 0.9
0764 | -0.8
0765 | ...
0766 | ```
0767 | 
0768 | Shuffle it:
0769 | 
0770 | ```text
0771 | -0.3
0772 | 0.9
0773 | 0.7
0774 | -0.8
0775 | 0.2
0776 | ...
0777 | ```
0778 | 
0779 | The shuffled signal preserves:
0780 | 
0781 | ```text
0782 | same values
0783 | same mean
0784 | same variance
0785 | same minimum
0786 | same maximum
0787 | same histogram
0788 | ```
0789 | 
0790 | But its temporal ordering is destroyed.
0791 | 
0792 | So now the hypothesis becomes much cleaner.
0793 | 
0794 | If the final crystal retains recoverable information about chronology, crystals grown from the ordered and shuffled sequences should be distinguishable.
0795 | 
0796 | ```mermaid
0797 | flowchart LR
0798 |     O["Ordered signal"] --> G1["Same growth rule"]
0799 |     S["Shuffled signal\<br/>same values"] --> G2["Same growth rule"]
0800 |     G1 --> M1["Final morphology"]
0801 |     G2 --> M2["Final morphology"]
0802 |     M1 --> C["Can morphology reveal order condition?"]
0803 |     M2 --> C
0804 | ```
0805 | 
0806 | ---
0807 | 
0808 | ## Ordered or Shuffled?
0809 | 
0810 | Binary chance is:
0811 | 
0812 | ```text
0813 | 50%
0814 | ```
0815 | 
0816 | The result:
0817 | 
0818 | ```text
0819 | chance                 50.0%
0820 | 
0821 | random forest           51.3%
0822 | logistic regression     51.7%
0823 | ```
0824 | 
0825 | {{< figure
0826 | src="/images/books/digital-life/ch14-07-order-accuracy.png"
0827 | alt="Classification accuracy for distinguishing crystals grown from ordered signals versus shuffled versions containing the same signal values."
0828 | caption="Ordered-versus-shuffled source histories cannot be recovered above chance in this experiment."
0829 | 
0830 | >}}
0831 | 
0832 | The confusion matrix tells the same story.
0833 | 
0834 | {{< figure
0835 | src="/images/books/digital-life/ch14-07-order-confusion.png"
0836 | alt="Confusion matrix for classifying ordered versus shuffled signal histories from final Digital Crystal morphology."
0837 | caption="The morphology provides no useful separation between ordered and shuffled histories under this protocol."
0838 | 
0839 | >}}
0840 | 
0841 | Under this experiment, neither classifier recovered temporal order above roughly:
0842 | 
0843 | ```text
0844 | 52%
0845 | ```
0846 | 
0847 | That is not evidence of useful sequence recovery.
0848 | 
0849 | And we need to be precise about what this means.
0850 | 
0851 | It does **not** prove that no conceivable measurement could ever recover any temporal information from this model.
0852 | 
0853 | It does establish:
0854 | 
0855 | > **Our morphology representation and classifiers do not recover ordered-versus-shuffled history above chance under this protocol.**
0856 | 
0857 | That is already enough to kill the stronger claim we were drifting toward.
0858 | 
0859 | ---
0860 | 
0861 | ## Maybe the Control Is Still Too Weak
0862 | 
0863 | There is another problem.
0864 | 
0865 | A sine wave and a square wave do not merely differ in ordering.
0866 | 
0867 | Their distributions differ.
0868 | 
0869 | So perhaps the successful source-family classifier is relying primarily on distributional differences.
0870 | 
0871 | We can remove even that possibility.
0872 | 
0873 | Construct a single value multiset:
0874 | 
0875 | $$
0876 | V={v_1,v_2,\ldots,v_{72}}
0877 | $$
0878 | 
0879 | Now every source receives **exactly the same values**.
0880 | 
0881 | Not approximately the same distribution.
0882 | 
0883 | Not merely the same mean and variance.
0884 | 
0885 | Exactly the same multiset.
0886 | 
0887 | Only the temporal arrangement changes.
0888 | 
0889 | ---
0890 | 
0891 | ## Six Ways to Arrange the Same World
0892 | 
0893 | Using one fixed set of values, create several temporal organizations:
0894 | 
0895 | ```text
0896 | RANDOM
0897 | random permutation
0898 | 
0899 | BLOCK
0900 | low values grouped, then high values grouped
0901 | 
0902 | ALTERNATING
0903 | low, high, low, high...
0904 | 
0905 | SMOOTH
0906 | neighboring values change gradually
0907 | 
0908 | BURST
0909 | quiet periods interrupted by concentrated excursions
0910 | 
0911 | PERIODIC
0912 | values arranged into a repeating temporal motif
0913 | ```
0914 | 
0915 | Every source contains exactly the same samples.
0916 | 
0917 | {{< figure
0918 | src="/images/books/digital-life/ch14-09-matched-temporal-signals.png"
0919 | alt="Six temporal organizations built from exactly the same set of input values: random, block, alternating, smooth, burst and periodic."
0920 | caption="Exactly the same values, reorganized in time. Distribution is held fixed; temporal organization changes."
0921 | 
0922 | >}}
0923 | 
0924 | Then grow crystals again.
0925 | 
0926 | Frozen model.
0927 | 
0928 | No parameter tuning.
0929 | 
0930 | No attempt to rescue the hypothesis.
0931 | 
0932 | {{< figure
0933 | src="/images/books/digital-life/ch14-09-matched-temporal-crystals.png"
0934 | alt="Digital Crystals grown from six differently ordered signals containing exactly the same input values."
0935 | caption="The temporal forcing histories differ radically, but their final morphologies are much harder to distinguish."
0936 | 
0937 | >}}
0938 | 
0939 | There is another important safeguard.
0940 | 
0941 | All temporal arrangements created from one value set remain together during train/test splitting.
0942 | 
0943 | The classifier cannot train on one ordering of value set A and then be tested on another ordering of that same value set.
0944 | 
0945 | Held-out value sets are genuinely unseen.
0946 | 
0947 | That prevents a subtle leakage path.
0948 | 
0949 | ---
0950 | 
0951 | ## The Stronger Temporal Experiment Fails Too
0952 | 
0953 | When the value multiset is held exactly constant, temporal organization is not recoverable above chance under the tested classifiers.
0954 | 
0955 | This is the sharper negative result.
0956 | 
0957 | The positive source-family result is therefore **not evidence that Digital Crystal v1 is a chronological recorder**.
0958 | 
0959 | Whatever source information survives is much more compatible with:
0960 | 
0961 | ```text
0962 | distributional characteristics
0963 | +
0964 | aggregate forcing statistics
0965 | +
0966 | interaction with stochastic growth
0967 | ```
0968 | 
0969 | than with:
0970 | 
0971 | ```text
0972 | recoverable temporal sequence
0973 | ```
0974 | 
0975 | The crystal had a state.
0976 | 
0977 | It did not have a recoverable history.
0978 | 
0979 | ---
0980 | 
0981 | ## A Crystal Is Not a Tape Recorder
0982 | 
0983 | This is where the result becomes better than the idea we started with.
0984 | 
0985 | We initially imagined:
0986 | 
0987 | ```text
0988 | environmental history
0989 | ↓
0990 | crystal
0991 | ↓
0992 | history written into morphology
0993 | ```
0994 | 
0995 | That was too strong.
0996 | 
0997 | What the experiment supports is closer to:
0998 | 
0999 | ```text
1000 | environmental statistics
1001 | ↓
1002 | local dynamics
1003 | ↓
1004 | persistent morphology
1005 | ```
1006 | 
1007 | The exact sequence is largely lost under the measurements and classifiers we tested.
1008 | 
1009 | Some broader characteristics survive.
1010 | 
1011 | That is strangely crystal-like.
1012 | 
1013 | Inspect a physical crystal and its structure may reveal something about the conditions under which it formed.
1014 | 
1015 | It does not necessarily contain a frame-by-frame movie of formation.
1016 | 
1017 | Our Digital Crystal appears closer to that.
1018 | 
1019 | A morphological statistic.
1020 | 
1021 | Not a tape recorder.
1022 | 
1023 | ---
1024 | 
1025 | ## Robustness
1026 | 
1027 | There is still another danger.
1028 | 
1029 | Perhaps source recovery occurs only at one carefully selected environmental forcing strength.
1030 | 
1031 | So we varied the forcing strength while leaving the rest of the local growth mechanism unchanged.
1032 | 
1033 | Held-out random-forest source-classification accuracy was:
1034 | 
1035 | ```text
1036 | forcing         accuracy
1037 | 
1038 | 0.75             34.1%
1039 | 0.85             43.2%
1040 | 0.95             50.0%
1041 | 1.00             52.3%
1042 | 1.05             52.3%
1043 | 1.15             63.6%
1044 | 1.25             43.2%
1045 | ```
1046 | 
1047 | Chance remains:
1048 | 
1049 | ```text
1050 | 16.7%
1051 | ```
1052 | 
1053 | {{< figure
1054 | src="/images/books/digital-life/ch14-08-robustness.png"
1055 | alt="Held-out source classification accuracy across several Digital Crystal forcing strengths."
1056 | caption="Source-family recovery remains above chance across the tested forcing-strength sweep, although the small sweep is noisy and should not be interpreted as a smooth response curve."
1057 | 
1058 | >}}
1059 | 
1060 | We need to be disciplined here.
1061 | 
1062 | The sweep is noisy.
1063 | 
1064 | There is no clean monotonic trend.
1065 | 
1066 | `1.15` happens to perform much better than `1.25`.
1067 | 
1068 | So we should **not** say:
1069 | 
1070 | > Increasing forcing predictably increases recoverability.
1071 | 
1072 | We have not shown that.
1073 | 
1074 | The surviving claim is narrower:
1075 | 
1076 | > **Source-family information remains recoverable above chance at every tested forcing strength in this sweep.**
1077 | 
1078 | That suggests the phenomenon is not confined to exactly one parameter value.
1079 | 
1080 | It does **not** establish a scaling law.
1081 | 
1082 | It does not tell us what the optimal forcing strength is.
1083 | 
1084 | And this experiment is too small to infer the shape of the response curve.
1085 | 
1086 | That is enough.
1087 | 
1088 | ---
1089 | 
1090 | ## What Have We Actually Built?
1091 | 
1092 | We began with a metaphor:
1093 | 
1094 | ```text
1095 | DIGITAL CRYSTAL
1096 | ```
1097 | 
1098 | Then gave it a provisional operational definition:
1099 | 
1100 | > **A local computational growth process in which characteristics of an external input become expressed as persistent, measurable morphology.**
1101 | 
1102 | That definition survives.
1103 | 
1104 | A stronger version does not.
1105 | 
1106 | We cannot say:
1107 | 
1108 | > The crystal records its temporal history.
1109 | 
1110 | We tried.
1111 | 
1112 | The evidence did not support it.
1113 | 
1114 | That failure makes the definition cleaner.
1115 | 
1116 | ---
1117 | 
1118 | ## A Better Computational Definition
1119 | 
1120 | Let (C_t) denote the complete crystal state.
1121 | 
1122 | Let (E_t) denote environmental forcing.
1123 | 
1124 | Let (G) denote the fixed local stochastic growth dynamics.
1125 | 
1126 | Then:
1127 | 
1128 | $$
1129 | C_{t+1}=G(C_t,E_t)
1130 | $$
1131 | 
1132 | At experimental horizon (T), morphology is measured through:
1133 | 
1134 | $$
1135 | M_T=\Phi(C_T)
1136 | $$
1137 | 
1138 | where (\Phi) extracts the morphological measurements.
1139 | 
1140 | Our source-family experiment establishes:
1141 | 
1142 | $$
1143 | M_T
1144 | \rightarrow
1145 | \text{recoverable information about source family}
1146 | $$
1147 | 
1148 | substantially above chance.
1149 | 
1150 | But the matched-value temporal experiment does **not** establish:
1151 | 
1152 | $$
1153 | M_T
1154 | \rightarrow
1155 | \text{recoverable temporal ordering}
1156 | $$
1157 | 
1158 | So our current evidence supports:
1159 | 
1160 | ```text
1161 | SOURCE CHARACTERISTICS
1162 |        ↓
1163 | LOCAL DYNAMICS
1164 |        ↓
1165 | MORPHOLOGY
1166 |        ↓
1167 | PARTIAL SOURCE RECOVERY
1168 | ```
1169 | 
1170 | but not:
1171 | 
1172 | ```text
1173 | COMPLETE TEMPORAL HISTORY
1174 |        ↓
1175 | MORPHOLOGY
1176 |        ↓
1177 | HISTORY RECOVERABLE
1178 | ```
1179 | 
1180 | That distinction matters enormously.
1181 | 
1182 | Because it tells us what the crystal is missing.
1183 | 
1184 | ---
1185 | 
1186 | ## A Picture Made by a Process
1187 | 
1188 | There is also a practical implication.
1189 | 
1190 | A Digital Crystal can function as a generative visualization.
1191 | 
1192 | Feed it:
1193 | 
1194 | ```text
1195 | software metrics
1196 | network traffic
1197 | model activity
1198 | market data
1199 | music
1200 | weather
1201 | sensor data
1202 | mathematical functions
1203 | ```
1204 | 
1205 | and let those signals influence formation.
1206 | 
1207 | A conventional graph usually performs something like:
1208 | 
1209 | ```text
1210 | value
1211 | ↓
1212 | coordinate
1213 | ↓
1214 | pixel
1215 | ```
1216 | 
1217 | The Digital Crystal instead performs:
1218 | 
1219 | ```text
1220 | value
1221 | ↓
1222 | local attachment conditions
1223 | ↓
1224 | many stochastic local interactions
1225 | ↓
1226 | persistent morphology
1227 | ```
1228 | 
1229 | The result is not directly drawn from the signal.
1230 | 
1231 | It is **grown under its influence**.
1232 | 
1233 | That may make it useful as art.
1234 | 
1235 | It may make it useful as telemetry.
1236 | 
1237 | It may make it useful as a regime visualization.
1238 | 
1239 | But those are possible applications.
1240 | 
1241 | They are not the scientific claim of this chapter.
1242 | 
1243 | ---
1244 | 
1245 | ## Could a Software System Grow a Crystal?
1246 | 
1247 | Imagine a running service emits measurements every few seconds:
1248 | 
1249 | ```text
1250 | latency
1251 | errors
1252 | test state
1253 | queue depth
1254 | memory pressure
1255 | ```
1256 | 
1257 | Instead of drawing another conventional dashboard, allow those signals to influence a Digital Crystal.
1258 | 
1259 | At the end of the day we have:
1260 | 
1261 | > **the day's crystal**
1262 | 
1263 | Days with different statistical regimes may produce different morphologies.
1264 | 
1265 | A healthy system may produce a different morphological distribution from a noisy or persistently degraded one.
1266 | 
1267 | We now have experimental reason to think this kind of mapping is plausible.
1268 | 
1269 | But we also know an important limitation.
1270 | 
1271 | Suppose two days contain approximately the same set of values in different temporal order.
1272 | 
1273 | Digital Crystal v1 may not distinguish them.
1274 | 
1275 | That is not an inconvenience to hide.
1276 | 
1277 | It is a property of the model we have measured.
1278 | 
1279 | ---
1280 | 
1281 | ## The Crystal Has State
1282 | 
1283 | There is a subtle point here.
1284 | 
1285 | The crystal obviously has state.
1286 | 
1287 | At time (t), (C_t) depends on earlier attachment events.
1288 | 
1289 | A cell added at step 10 may still exist at step 70.
1290 | 
1291 | Past events contributed causally to the present.
1292 | 
1293 | But that does not imply that the present is a useful record of the past.
1294 | 
1295 | This experiment gives us a distinction we are going to need:
1296 | 
1297 | > **Past contributed to present does not imply present preserves the order of the past.**
1298 | 
1299 | Those are different properties.
1300 | 
1301 | We now have evidence separating them.
1302 | 
1303 | ---
1304 | 
1305 | ## State Is Not History
1306 | 
1307 | Consider two sequences:
1308 | 
1309 | ```text
1310 | A B C D
1311 | ```
1312 | 
1313 | and:
1314 | 
1315 | ```text
1316 | D B A C
1317 | ```
1318 | 
1319 | Both can influence a process.
1320 | 
1321 | Both can alter its final state.
1322 | 
1323 | But suppose the final state contains no recoverable information allowing us to distinguish which ordering occurred.
1324 | 
1325 | Then the process accumulated consequences without retaining recoverable chronology.
1326 | 
1327 | That is approximately where Digital Crystal v1 stands.
1328 | 
1329 | It has a **state**.
1330 | 
1331 | We have not yet demonstrated a **history**.
1332 | 
1333 | ```mermaid
1334 | flowchart LR
1335 |     P["Past events"] -->|causal influence| S["Current state"]
1336 |     S --> Q{"Can temporal order\<br/>be reconstructed?"}
1337 |     Q -- Yes --> H["History retained"]
1338 |     Q -- No --> N["State without demonstrated history"]
1339 | ```
1340 | 
1341 | That gives us the next experimental problem.
1342 | 
1343 | ---
1344 | 
1345 | ## Evidence Ledger
1346 | 
1347 | ### SUPPORTED — Source Information in Morphology
1348 | 
1349 | Across 600 crystals, forcing-process family could be recovered from morphology substantially above six-way chance:
1350 | 
1351 | ```text
1352 | random forest           52.2%
1353 | logistic regression     53.9%
1354 | chance                   16.7%
1355 | ```
1356 | 
1357 | So we can support:
1358 | 
1359 | > **A fixed local computational growth process can transform differences in forcing environment into persistent morphological differences containing recoverable source-family information.**
1360 | 
1361 | ---
1362 | 
1363 | ### SUPPORTED — Mean Alone Does Not Explain the Effect
1364 | 
1365 | The varying source processes were approximately mean-normalized, while their morphology populations remained measurably distinct.
1366 | 
1367 | So:
1368 | 
1369 | > **The source-family result is not explained solely by average forcing value.**
1370 | 
1371 | ---
1372 | 
1373 | ### SUPPORTED — Effect Is Not Confined to One Tested Forcing Strength
1374 | 
1375 | Source-family recovery remained above chance at every tested value in the seven-point forcing-strength sweep.
1376 | 
1377 | But the sweep is noisy.
1378 | 
1379 | So we can claim:
1380 | 
1381 | > **The effect survives moderate parameter variation in the tested sweep.**
1382 | 
1383 | We cannot claim:
1384 | 
1385 | ```text
1386 | a monotonic relationship
1387 | an optimum forcing strength
1388 | a scaling law
1389 | ```
1390 | 
1391 | ---
1392 | 
1393 | ### FAILED — Final Morphology Recovers Temporal Order
1394 | 
1395 | Ordered-versus-shuffled classification gave:
1396 | 
1397 | ```text
1398 | random forest           51.3%
1399 | logistic regression     51.7%
1400 | chance                   50.0%
1401 | ```
1402 | 
1403 | A stronger experiment in which every condition contained exactly the same input multiset also failed to establish recoverable temporal organization.
1404 | 
1405 | So:
1406 | 
1407 | > **We do not find evidence that final morphology preserves temporal order in a form recoverable by the tested feature representation and classifiers.**
1408 | 
1409 | ---
1410 | 
1411 | ### NOT ESTABLISHED — History
1412 | 
1413 | The crystal's present state depends causally on previous events.
1414 | 
1415 | That alone does not establish a recoverable history.
1416 | 
1417 | So:
1418 | 
1419 | ```text
1420 | state                 SUPPORTED
1421 | recoverable chronology NOT SUPPORTED
1422 | history               NOT YET ESTABLISHED
1423 | ```
1424 | 
1425 | ---
1426 | 
1427 | ### NOT CLAIMED
1428 | 
1429 | We cannot claim that the crystal:
1430 | 
1431 | ```text
1432 | learns
1433 | adapts
1434 | understands
1435 | has goals
1436 | has memory in the strong sense
1437 | reproduces
1438 | is an organism
1439 | is alive
1440 | ```
1441 | 
1442 | Nor can we claim that Digital Crystal v1 inherits every property of the Chapter 11 crystal.
1443 | 
1444 | Different model.
1445 | 
1446 | Different experiment.
1447 | 
1448 | Different claims.
1449 | 
1450 | ---
1451 | 
1452 | ## What Survived?
1453 | 
1454 | At the beginning of this chapter we proposed:
1455 | 
1456 | > **A Digital Crystal is a local computational growth process in which characteristics of an external input become expressed as persistent, measurable morphology.**
1457 | 
1458 | That survives.
1459 | 
1460 | The experiment supports:
1461 | 
1462 | ```text
1463 | environment
1464 | ↓
1465 | local computation
1466 | ↓
1467 | persistent form
1468 | ↓
1469 | some information about environment remains
1470 | ```
1471 | 
1472 | The stronger idea:
1473 | 
1474 | ```text
1475 | environmental sequence
1476 | ↓
1477 | persistent form
1478 | ↓
1479 | chronology recoverable
1480 | ```
1481 | 
1482 | did not survive.
1483 | 
1484 | That distinction is now part of the definition.
1485 | 
1486 | ---
1487 | 
1488 | ## What Survived the Hypothesis?
1489 | 
1490 | The stronger hypothesis in this chapter failed.
1491 | 
1492 | We began with an idea close to:
1493 | 
1494 | ```text
1495 | environmental history
1496 | ↓
1497 | growth
1498 | ↓
1499 | final morphology
1500 | ↓
1501 | history recoverable
1502 | ```
1503 | 
1504 | That was too strong.
1505 | 
1506 | The source-family experiment succeeded:
1507 | 
1508 | ```text
1509 | random forest          52.2%
1510 | logistic regression    53.9%
1511 | chance                  16.7%
1512 | ```
1513 | 
1514 | So the final morphology retained substantial information about the **kind of forcing process** under which the crystal formed.
1515 | 
1516 | But when temporal order was isolated, the signal disappeared.
1517 | 
1518 | For ordered-versus-shuffled histories:
1519 | 
1520 | ```text
1521 | random forest          51.3%
1522 | logistic regression    51.7%
1523 | chance                  50.0%
1524 | ```
1525 | 
1526 | And when every condition received exactly the same value multiset and only temporal organization changed, the stronger temporal experiment failed as well.
1527 | 
1528 | So Chapter 14 leaves us with a sharper distinction:
1529 | 
1530 | ```text
1531 | COARSE SOURCE CHARACTERISTICS
1532 | RECOVERABLE
1533 | 
1534 | EXACT TEMPORAL ORDER
1535 | NOT RECOVERED
1536 | ```
1537 | 
1538 | The failed chronology claim does not erase the source-family result.
1539 | 
1540 | It tells us what kind of information the substrate preferentially preserves.
1541 | 
1542 | ### Phenomenon record
1543 | 
1544 | **Phenomenon:** Lossy history integration
1545 | 
1546 | **Status:** **SUPPORTED**
1547 | 
1548 | **Current bounded description:**
1549 | 
1550 | > Digital Crystal v1 transforms external forcing into persistent morphology that preserves enough information for source-family recovery substantially above chance, while the tested final-state representation does not preserve temporal order in a recoverable form.
1551 | 
1552 | The simplest mechanistic picture is:
1553 | 
1554 | ```text
1555 | external forcing
1556 | ↓
1557 | many irreversible local attachment events
1558 | ↓
1559 | aggregate structural bias
1560 | ↓
1561 | persistent morphology
1562 | ```
1563 | 
1564 | The process integrates consequences of the past.
1565 | 
1566 | But integration is lossy.
1567 | 
1568 | It appears to preserve some broad statistics of the forcing process more readily than exact chronology.
1569 | 
1570 | That gives us a provisional cross-chapter principle:
1571 | 
1572 | > **Irreversible growth can preserve coarse historical structure while discarding fine temporal identity.**
1573 | 
1574 | We should not yet treat this as a universal law of Digital Crystals.
1575 | 
1576 | For now it is a substrate-level principle supported by this chapter and available for comparison with later experiments.
1577 | 
1578 | ### Information hierarchy exposed by this chapter
1579 | 
1580 | Chapter 14 separates several different levels of historical information:
1581 | 
1582 | ```text
1583 | past affected present
1584 |         SUPPORTED
1585 | 
1586 | source family recoverable
1587 |         SUPPORTED
1588 | 
1589 | mean alone explains source effect
1590 |         FAILED
1591 | 
1592 | exact temporal order recoverable
1593 |         NOT SUPPORTED
1594 | 
1595 | complete chronology retained
1596 |         NOT ESTABLISHED
1597 | ```
1598 | 
1599 | That hierarchy matters.
1600 | 
1601 | A process can be history-dependent without being a history recorder.
1602 | 
1603 | A final state can contain information about past conditions without preserving the sequence in which those conditions occurred.
1604 | 
1605 | So:
1606 | 
1607 | ```text
1608 | HISTORY-DEPENDENT
1609 | ≠
1610 | HISTORY-READABLE
1611 | ```
1612 | 
1613 | and:
1614 | 
1615 | ```text
1616 | STATE
1617 | ≠
1618 | CHRONOLOGY
1619 | ```
1620 | 
1621 | ### What this phenomenon does not establish
1622 | 
1623 | The surviving phenomenon does **not** establish:
1624 | 
1625 | - memory in the strong sense,
1626 | - learning,
1627 | - adaptation,
1628 | - interpretation of the environment,
1629 | - a stored event log,
1630 | - sender identity,
1631 | - exact chronology,
1632 | - or life.
1633 | 
1634 | It establishes something narrower:
1635 | 
1636 | > **The substrate behaves as a lossy integrator of environmental history: broad characteristics of forcing can survive in morphology even when exact temporal ordering does not.**
1637 | 
1638 | This phenomenon should now be tracked independently of the chapter's failed chronology hypothesis.
1639 | 
1640 | ---
1641 | 
1642 | ## The Useful Failure
1643 | 
1644 | This may be the most important result in the chapter.
1645 | 
1646 | We wanted to ask the crystal:
1647 | 
1648 | > What happened to you?
1649 | 
1650 | The crystal could answer something weaker:
1651 | 
1652 | > I can tell you something about the kind of conditions under which I formed.
1653 | 
1654 | Then we asked:
1655 | 
1656 | > Fine. What happened first?
1657 | 
1658 | And under the experiments we performed, the answer was effectively:
1659 | 
1660 | > I don't know.
1661 | 
1662 | That is not the end of the Digital Crystal.
1663 | 
1664 | It identifies the next missing capability.
1665 | 
1666 | The crystal has a present.
1667 | 
1668 | It does not yet have a usable past.
1669 | 
1670 | So perhaps the next step is not to make it intelligent.
1671 | 
1672 | Not to give it goals.
1673 | 
1674 | Not to give it reproduction.
1675 | 
1676 | Not to give it learning.
1677 | 
1678 | Something much smaller.
1679 | 
1680 | Give it a way to **keep what happened**.
1681 | 
1682 | Not interpret it.
1683 | 
1684 | Not learn from it.
1685 | 
1686 | Not decide what it means.
1687 | 
1688 | Just preserve enough information that the past can still be distinguished from another possible past.
1689 | 
1690 | Because before a process can do anything with history, there must be some history left.
1691 | 
1692 | ---
1693 | 
1694 | ## The Digital Crystal
1695 | 
1696 | We began this chapter with a name.
1697 | 
1698 | Now the name has earned a little more weight.
1699 | 
1700 | A Digital Crystal is not a crystalline picture.
1701 | 
1702 | It is not a cellular automaton merely because it grows on a lattice.
1703 | 
1704 | It is not a primitive animal.
1705 | 
1706 | It is not our attempt to recreate biological life.
1707 | 
1708 | For now:
1709 | 
1710 | > **A Digital Crystal is a local computational growth process in which characteristics of an external input become expressed as persistent, measurable morphology.**
1711 | 
1712 | The definition is small.
1713 | 
1714 | That is intentional.
1715 | 
1716 | The experiments support it.
1717 | 
1718 | The stronger claims failed.
1719 | 
1720 | ```mermaid
1721 | flowchart TD
1722 |     A["ENVIRONMENTAL INPUT"] --> B["FIXED LOCAL DYNAMICS"]
1723 |     B --> C["DIGITAL CRYSTAL"]
1724 |     C --> D["PERSISTENT MORPHOLOGY"]
1725 |     D --> E["SOURCE-FAMILY INFORMATION RECOVERABLE"]
1726 |     D --> F["TEMPORAL ORDER NOT RECOVERED"]
1727 | ```
1728 | 
1729 | The crystal can tell us something about the world that formed it.
1730 | 
1731 | It cannot yet tell us what happened first.
1732 | 
1733 | **So next, we give the crystal a past.**
```
