```markdown
0001 | +++
0002 | title = "15: The Crystal Gets a Past"
0003 | date = "2026-08-11T21:52:00+01:00"
0004 | draft = false
0005 | description = "A Digital Crystal can preserve source statistics in morphology but not temporal order. We add state, checkpointing, event history, replay and branching to discover what a recoverable digital past actually requires."
0006 | categories = ["Programming", "Artificial Life"]
0007 | tags = ["Digital Life", "Digital Crystal", "State", "History", "Checkpointing", "Replay", "Counterfactuals"]
0008 | series = ["Digital Life From First Principles"]
0009 | +++
0010 | 
0011 | At the end of the last chapter, our Digital Crystal could tell us something about the world that formed it.
0012 | 
0013 | But not enough.
0014 | 
0015 | Different forcing processes produced different morphologies.
0016 | 
0017 | We could hide the source and recover its family substantially better than chance.
0018 | 
0019 | Then we asked a harder question:
0020 | 
0021 | > What happened first?
0022 | 
0023 | We took exactly the same environmental values and rearranged them in time.
0024 | 
0025 | Smooth.
0026 | 
0027 | Bursting.
0028 | 
0029 | Periodic.
0030 | 
0031 | Alternating.
0032 | 
0033 | Random.
0034 | 
0035 | The final morphology could not reliably tell them apart.
0036 | 
0037 | ```text
0038 | SAME VALUES
0039 | +
0040 | DIFFERENT ORDER
0041 | ↓
0042 | NO RECOVERABLE TEMPORAL SIGNATURE
0043 | ```
0044 | 
0045 | The crystal had accumulated a state.
0046 | 
0047 | It had not preserved a usable history.
0048 | 
0049 | That gives us the next experiment.
0050 | 
0051 | Not intelligence.
0052 | 
0053 | Not learning.
0054 | 
0055 | Not adaptation.
0056 | 
0057 | Something much smaller.
0058 | 
0059 | > **What must a digital process preserve if we want its past to survive?**
0060 | 
0061 | ---
0062 | 
0063 | ## The Present Is Not the Past
0064 | 
0065 | Our Digital Crystal already has a present state.
0066 | 
0067 | At any moment, (C_t) contains the cells that currently exist.
0068 | 
0069 | Those cells are consequences of earlier growth.
0070 | 
0071 | So the past clearly mattered.
0072 | 
0073 | But Chapter 14 taught us an important distinction:
0074 | 
0075 | > **Past contributed to present does not imply present contains a recoverable record of the past.**
0076 | 
0077 | A footprint exists because someone walked there.
0078 | 
0079 | The footprint is not the walk.
0080 | 
0081 | A crater exists because something struck the ground.
0082 | 
0083 | The crater is not the complete trajectory of the object that made it.
0084 | 
0085 | Our crystal is similar.
0086 | 
0087 | Its current shape contains consequences.
0088 | 
0089 | That does not mean it contains the sequence that produced them.
0090 | 
0091 | So we need to separate two ideas:
0092 | 
0093 | ```text
0094 | STATE
0095 | and
0096 | HISTORY
0097 | ```
0098 | 
0099 | ---
0100 | 
0101 | ## State
0102 | 
0103 | We begin with an operational definition:
0104 | 
0105 | > **A state representation is sufficient if it contains enough information to continue the process faithfully from here.**
0106 | 
0107 | That wording matters.
0108 | 
0109 | We are **not** claiming that we already know the mathematically smallest possible state.
0110 | 
0111 | We are asking whether a particular stored representation is sufficient.
0112 | 
0113 | Perhaps the picture itself is enough.
0114 | 
0115 | Perhaps:
0116 | 
0117 | ```text
0118 | occupied cells
0119 | ```
0120 | 
0121 | is the complete state.
0122 | 
0123 | We can test that.
0124 | 
0125 | Digital Crystal v1 is stochastic.
0126 | 
0127 | Its continuation depends on random attachment decisions.
0128 | 
0129 | Its continuation also depends on where the process currently is in the environmental input.
0130 | 
0131 | So a candidate checkpoint might contain:
0132 | 
0133 | ```text
0134 | occupied cells
0135 | birth-time metadata
0136 | current timestep
0137 | current signal position
0138 | random-number-generator state
0139 | model parameters
0140 | ```
0141 | 
0142 | But we should not declare all of those necessary merely because they sound plausible.
0143 | 
0144 | We should remove them.
0145 | 
0146 | One at a time.
0147 | 
0148 | ---
0149 | 
0150 | ## Run the Crystal Continuously
0151 | 
0152 | We take frozen Digital Crystal v1 from Chapter 14.
0153 | 
0154 | The local growth rule does not change.
0155 | 
0156 | The experiment runs for:
0157 | 
0158 | ```text
0159 | 96 steps
0160 | ```
0161 | 
0162 | with a checkpoint halfway through:
0163 | 
0164 | ```text
0165 | t = 48
0166 | ```
0167 | 
0168 | In the full v3 experiment:
0169 | 
0170 | ```text
0171 | population at checkpoint    2,702
0172 | final population            9,574
0173 | ```
0174 | 
0175 | The final morphology receives a hash:
0176 | 
0177 | ```text
0178 | bc8e6d8c9783431f1459bf17
0179 | ```
0180 | 
0181 | and the full process state receives another:
0182 | 
0183 | ```text
0184 | bd33c9aa0a510803be6ce6bf
0185 | ```
0186 | 
0187 | {{< figure
0188 | src="/images/books/digital-life/ch15-01-reference-and-checkpoint.png"
0189 | alt="The Chapter 15 Digital Crystal input signal with a checkpoint at step 48, together with the crystal at the checkpoint and its final state at step 96."
0190 | caption="The continuous reference trajectory. The midpoint checkpoint will be restored, damaged and replayed in later experiments."
0191 | 
0192 | >}}
0193 | 
0194 | Now we can demand something much stronger than visual similarity.
0195 | 
0196 | If the checkpoint is sufficient, then restoring it should produce:
0197 | 
0198 | > **exactly the same future.**
0199 | 
0200 | ---
0201 | 
0202 | ## Before We Could Save the State, We Found a Hidden One
0203 | 
0204 | Our first implementation failed this experiment.
0205 | 
0206 | The checkpoint appeared complete.
0207 | 
0208 | We saved:
0209 | 
0210 | ```text
0211 | occupied cells
0212 | birth metadata
0213 | timestep
0214 | signal cursor
0215 | RNG state
0216 | ```
0217 | 
0218 | Then we restored it.
0219 | 
0220 | The future changed.
0221 | 
0222 | At first that looked like evidence that something important was missing from our checkpoint.
0223 | 
0224 | But another result contradicted that interpretation.
0225 | 
0226 | When we reconstructed the same checkpoint **without passing through serialization**, continuation was exact.
0227 | 
0228 | So something was happening at the implementation boundary.
0229 | 
0230 | The culprit was surprisingly mundane.
0231 | 
0232 | Candidate attachment sites were held in a Python `set`.
0233 | 
0234 | The growth loop effectively did:
0235 | 
0236 | ```python
0237 | for cell in frontier:
0238 |     ...
0239 |     rng.random()
0240 | ```
0241 | 
0242 | A set has no scientific ordering.
0243 | 
0244 | Its internal iteration order is an implementation detail.
0245 | 
0246 | Two sets can contain exactly the same cells but iterate them in different orders after reconstruction.
0247 | 
0248 | That meant:
0249 | 
0250 | ```text
0251 | same mathematical cells
0252 | +
0253 | same RNG state
0254 | +
0255 | same signal
0256 | ```
0257 | 
0258 | could still become:
0259 | 
0260 | ```text
0261 | RNG draw #1 → different candidate
0262 | RNG draw #2 → different candidate
0263 | ...
0264 | ```
0265 | 
0266 | The simulation had accidentally acquired an undeclared state variable:
0267 | 
0268 | ```text
0269 | PYTHON CONTAINER LAYOUT
0270 | ```
0271 | 
0272 | That is not a property of the Digital Crystal.
0273 | 
0274 | It is a property of the implementation.
0275 | 
0276 | So we removed it.
0277 | 
0278 | The growth rule now consumes stochastic decisions in a canonical order:
0279 | 
0280 | ```python
0281 | for cell in sorted(frontier):
0282 | ```
0283 | 
0284 | Then we reran the experiment.
0285 | 
0286 | ---
0287 | 
0288 | ## State Belonging to the Model vs State Belonging to the Program
0289 | 
0290 | Before trusting checkpointing again, we added a stronger invariant.
0291 | 
0292 | Take a checkpoint.
0293 | 
0294 | Serialize and deserialize:
0295 | 
0296 | ```text
0297 | occupied cells
0298 | birth metadata
0299 | RNG state
0300 | ```
0301 | 
0302 | into fresh Python objects.
0303 | 
0304 | Then compare the original state and reconstructed state.
0305 | 
0306 | They must produce:
0307 | 
0308 | ```text
0309 | same one-step continuation
0310 | and
0311 | same complete remaining continuation
0312 | ```
0313 | 
0314 | The v3 reproducibility invariant passed:
0315 | 
0316 | ```text
0317 | occupied state equal                   True
0318 | birth metadata equal                   True
0319 | signal cursor equal                    True
0320 | RNG state equal                        True
0321 | process hash equal                     True
0322 | 
0323 | one-step continuation exact            True
0324 | full remaining horizon exact           True
0325 | remaining steps checked                48
0326 | ```
0327 | 
0328 | The implementation no longer depends on accidental set layout.
0329 | 
0330 | That gives us a methodological rule worth keeping:
0331 | 
0332 | > **An experimental model must distinguish state belonging to the phenomenon from state accidentally belonging to its implementation.**
0333 | 
0334 | Only after that did checkpointing become a meaningful experiment.
0335 | 
0336 | ---
0337 | 
0338 | ## Stop It
0339 | 
0340 | At step `48` we serialize the operating state.
0341 | 
0342 | Not a screenshot.
0343 | 
0344 | Not a rendered image.
0345 | 
0346 | The actual continuation representation.
0347 | 
0348 | Then we terminate that runtime state.
0349 | 
0350 | Load the checkpoint from SQLite.
0351 | 
0352 | Continue.
0353 | 
0354 | If our checkpoint representation is sufficient, the restored process should not merely resemble the uninterrupted one.
0355 | 
0356 | It should become:
0357 | 
0358 | ```text
0359 | the same cells
0360 | the same attachment decisions
0361 | the same population trajectory
0362 | the same final process state
0363 | ```
0364 | 
0365 | ---
0366 | 
0367 | ## Save, Restore, Continue
0368 | 
0369 | After the reproducibility fix:
0370 | 
0371 | ```text
0372 | exact final morphology            True
0373 | exact final process state         True
0374 | population trajectory identical   True
0375 | attachment trajectory identical   True
0376 | symmetric-difference cells        0
0377 | ```
0378 | 
0379 | The morphology hashes match:
0380 | 
0381 | ```text
0382 | REFERENCE   bc8e6d8c9783431f1459bf17
0383 | RESTORED    bc8e6d8c9783431f1459bf17
0384 | ```
0385 | 
0386 | The complete process hashes match too.
0387 | 
0388 | {{< figure
0389 | src="/images/books/digital-life/ch15-02-exact-restore.png"
0390 | alt="The final continuously run Digital Crystal beside the final crystal produced after checkpoint, SQLite restore and continuation."
0391 | caption="After canonicalizing stochastic candidate traversal, continuous execution and checkpoint → restore → continue produce the exact same trajectory."
0392 | 
0393 | >}}
0394 | 
0395 | And this was not a one-run accident.
0396 | 
0397 | We repeated the checkpoint experiment across:
0398 | 
0399 | ```text
0400 | 30 independent runs
0401 | ```
0402 | 
0403 | Result:
0404 | 
0405 | ```text
0406 | 30 / 30 exact
0407 | ```
0408 | 
0409 | So we have earned the first major claim:
0410 | 
0411 | > **The stored Digital Crystal checkpoint representation is sufficient for exact continuation of the stochastic process.**
0412 | 
0413 | Notice what we have **not** claimed.
0414 | 
0415 | We have not proven that this is the smallest possible state representation.
0416 | 
0417 | That question remains open.
0418 | 
0419 | ---
0420 | 
0421 | ## What Actually Matters for Continuation?
0422 | 
0423 | Now we deliberately damage the checkpoint.
0424 | 
0425 | But this time the experiments are carefully isolated.
0426 | 
0427 | Every variant receives exactly:
0428 | 
0429 | ```text
0430 | 48 continuation updates
0431 | ```
0432 | 
0433 | So altering the environmental cursor does not accidentally alter the length of the experiment.
0434 | 
0435 | ---
0436 | 
0437 | ## Remove the Random State
0438 | 
0439 | First:
0440 | 
0441 | ```text
0442 | same morphology
0443 | same birth metadata
0444 | same timestep
0445 | same signal position
0446 | different RNG continuation state
0447 | ```
0448 | 
0449 | The final result becomes:
0450 | 
0451 | ```text
0452 | final population                 9,550
0453 | symmetric-difference cells          28
0454 | normalized difference          0.002924
0455 | ```
0456 | 
0457 | The difference is small.
0458 | 
0459 | But it is not zero.
0460 | 
0461 | Exact continuation fails.
0462 | 
0463 | So:
0464 | 
0465 | > **RNG continuation state matters for exact continuation of Digital Crystal v1.**
0466 | 
0467 | That is not philosophical speculation.
0468 | 
0469 | We removed it.
0470 | 
0471 | The future changed.
0472 | 
0473 | ---
0474 | 
0475 | ## Move the Environmental Cursor
0476 | 
0477 | Next:
0478 | 
0479 | ```text
0480 | same morphology
0481 | same RNG state
0482 | same timestep
0483 | same continuation horizon
0484 | ```
0485 | 
0486 | but move the signal cursor:
0487 | 
0488 | ```text
0489 | 48 → 45
0490 | ```
0491 | 
0492 | Both conditions still execute exactly `48` future updates.
0493 | 
0494 | The result:
0495 | 
0496 | ```text
0497 | final population                 9,549
0498 | symmetric-difference cells          27
0499 | normalized difference          0.002820
0500 | ```
0501 | 
0502 | So we can now say:
0503 | 
0504 | > **Where the process currently sits in the environmental sequence affects exact continuation.**
0505 | 
0506 | The environment is not merely historical context.
0507 | 
0508 | Its current position is part of the continuation conditions.
0509 | 
0510 | ---
0511 | 
0512 | ## Remove the Birth Times
0513 | 
0514 | Now we perform a cleaner test.
0515 | 
0516 | Preserve:
0517 | 
0518 | ```text
0519 | occupied cells
0520 | RNG state
0521 | signal cursor
0522 | timestep
0523 | ```
0524 | 
0525 | but replace the birth-time metadata.
0526 | 
0527 | What happens?
0528 | 
0529 | ```text
0530 | final population                 9,574
0531 | symmetric-difference cells           0
0532 | normalized difference          0.000000
0533 | ```
0534 | 
0535 | The final occupied set is exact.
0536 | 
0537 | Only the birth-time metadata differs.
0538 | 
0539 | That gives us an extremely useful distinction.
0540 | 
0541 | Under the current Digital Crystal v1 growth rule:
0542 | 
0543 | > **Birth times are part of the historical record, but they are not required for the same geometric continuation.**
0544 | 
0545 | This is the first place where state and history begin to separate experimentally.
0546 | 
0547 | Birth time tells us:
0548 | 
0549 | ```text
0550 | how the current structure formed
0551 | ```
0552 | 
0553 | but the growth mechanism does not consult it when deciding the next attachment.
0554 | 
0555 | So birth time can matter to history without mattering to future growth.
0556 | 
0557 | ---
0558 | 
0559 | ## Save Only the Morphology
0560 | 
0561 | Now perform the brutal test.
0562 | 
0563 | Preserve only the visible occupied structure.
0564 | 
0565 | Reconstruct other process details incorrectly.
0566 | 
0567 | Continue.
0568 | 
0569 | The result:
0570 | 
0571 | ```text
0572 | final population                 9,548
0573 | symmetric-difference cells          30
0574 | normalized difference          0.003133
0575 | ```
0576 | 
0577 | The visible shape at the checkpoint was identical.
0578 | 
0579 | The future was not.
0580 | 
0581 | {{< figure
0582 | src="/images/books/digital-life/ch15-03-state-omission.png"
0583 | alt="Comparison of final-state divergence after restoring the full checkpoint, changing RNG state, shifting the signal cursor, changing birth metadata, or restoring morphology only."
0584 | caption="Visible morphology is insufficient for exact continuation. RNG state and environmental sequence position affect future growth, while birth-time metadata does not affect the occupied-set continuation under Digital Crystal v1."
0585 | 
0586 | >}}
0587 | 
0588 | This gives us another version of a lesson that has followed us through the book:
0589 | 
0590 | > **Appearance is not state.**
0591 | 
0592 | Two systems can look identical and still have different futures.
0593 | 
0594 | ---
0595 | 
0596 | ## What We Have Actually Identified
0597 | 
0598 | We need to be careful here.
0599 | 
0600 | We have not discovered:
0601 | 
0602 | > the unique minimal state of Digital Crystal v1.
0603 | 
0604 | We have discovered something narrower.
0605 | 
0606 | The experiments establish:
0607 | 
0608 | ```text
0609 | FULL CHECKPOINT
0610 | → sufficient
0611 | 
0612 | VISIBLE MORPHOLOGY ALONE
0613 | → insufficient
0614 | 
0615 | RNG CONTINUATION STATE
0616 | → causally relevant to exact continuation
0617 | 
0618 | SIGNAL POSITION
0619 | → causally relevant to exact continuation
0620 | 
0621 | BIRTH-TIME METADATA
0622 | → not required for geometric continuation
0623 | ```
0624 | 
0625 | That is enough.
0626 | 
0627 | The useful operational idea is:
0628 | 
0629 | > **State is whatever information the future actually needs.**
0630 | 
0631 | Not whatever information happens to exist in our data structures.
0632 | 
0633 | And not whatever information sounds philosophically important.
0634 | 
0635 | ---
0636 | 
0637 | ## Now Give It History
0638 | 
0639 | Checkpointing solves one problem:
0640 | 
0641 | > Where are we now, and what do we need to continue?
0642 | 
0643 | It does not answer:
0644 | 
0645 | > How did we get here?
0646 | 
0647 | For that, we create an explicit event history.
0648 | 
0649 | At every growth step we preserve:
0650 | 
0651 | ```text
0652 | step
0653 | input value
0654 | cells added
0655 | population
0656 | resulting morphology hash
0657 | ```
0658 | 
0659 | So the history becomes:
0660 | 
0661 | ```text
0662 | STEP 1
0663 | input = ...
0664 | added = [...]
0665 | state hash = ...
0666 | 
0667 | STEP 2
0668 | input = ...
0669 | added = [...]
0670 | state hash = ...
0671 | 
0672 | STEP 3
0673 | ...
0674 | ```
0675 | 
0676 | This is not memory in any cognitive sense.
0677 | 
0678 | The crystal does not inspect this record.
0679 | 
0680 | It does not learn from it.
0681 | 
0682 | It does not use it to make decisions.
0683 | 
0684 | It is an explicit formation history.
0685 | 
0686 | Now we ask whether the record is sufficient to reconstruct what happened.
0687 | 
0688 | ---
0689 | 
0690 | ## Replay the Process
0691 | 
0692 | There are two different ways to replay a past.
0693 | 
0694 | The distinction matters.
0695 | 
0696 | ---
0697 | 
0698 | ## Procedural Replay
0699 | 
0700 | First:
0701 | 
0702 | ```text
0703 | same initial seed
0704 | +
0705 | same environmental signal
0706 | +
0707 | same frozen growth rule
0708 | +
0709 | same stochastic initialization
0710 | ↓
0711 | run again
0712 | ```
0713 | 
0714 | The result:
0715 | 
0716 | ```text
0717 | exact final morphology     True
0718 | exact final process state  True
0719 | ```
0720 | 
0721 | That establishes reproducibility of the computational procedure.
0722 | 
0723 | But it does not yet establish that the **stored event history** is sufficient.
0724 | 
0725 | So we perform a stronger test.
0726 | 
0727 | ---
0728 | 
0729 | ## Replay the Events
0730 | 
0731 | This time we do **not** rerun the stochastic growth rule.
0732 | 
0733 | Instead, take the recorded event stream.
0734 | 
0735 | At every step:
0736 | 
0737 | ```text
0738 | read cells that appeared
0739 | ↓
0740 | apply those additions
0741 | ↓
0742 | recompute morphology hash
0743 | ↓
0744 | compare with original recorded hash
0745 | ```
0746 | 
0747 | There are:
0748 | 
0749 | ```text
0750 | 96 recorded steps
0751 | ```
0752 | 
0753 | Result:
0754 | 
0755 | ```text
0756 | 96 / 96 morphology hashes match
0757 | ```
0758 | 
0759 | Final morphology:
0760 | 
0761 | ```text
0762 | exact
0763 | ```
0764 | 
0765 | {{< figure
0766 | src="/images/books/digital-life/ch15-04-history-replay.png"
0767 | alt="Step-by-step comparison showing that every morphology hash produced by replaying the Digital Crystal event log matches the recorded original trajectory."
0768 | caption="The event history reconstructs the recorded morphology trajectory exactly: 96 matching hashes out of 96."
0769 | 
0770 | >}}
0771 | 
0772 | Now we have earned a second claim:
0773 | 
0774 | > **The explicit event history is sufficient to reconstruct the exact recorded morphology trajectory.**
0775 | 
0776 | Not the hidden historical RNG state.
0777 | 
0778 | Not every internal implementation detail.
0779 | 
0780 | The formation trajectory.
0781 | 
0782 | That is the correct scope.
0783 | 
0784 | ---
0785 | 
0786 | ## State Is Not History
0787 | 
0788 | We can now make the distinction operational.
0789 | 
0790 | A checkpoint and an event history both describe the same process.
0791 | 
0792 | But they answer different questions.
0793 | 
0794 | ```text
0795 | STATE
0796 | =
0797 | enough information for faithful continuation
0798 | 
0799 | HISTORY
0800 | =
0801 | enough information to reconstruct how the present morphology was formed
0802 | ```
0803 | 
0804 | A checkpoint can continue forward exactly.
0805 | 
0806 | An event log can reconstruct backward through the morphology trajectory.
0807 | 
0808 | But those capabilities are not interchangeable.
0809 | 
0810 | The history reconstructed from cell additions does **not** restore the historical RNG state.
0811 | 
0812 | And if we take reconstructed geometry without the correct stochastic continuation state, exact future continuation fails.
0813 | 
0814 | In the full experiment:
0815 | 
0816 | ```text
0817 | history reconstructs checkpoint morphology    True
0818 | checkpoint continues exactly                  True
0819 | 
0820 | history-derived geometry without RNG
0821 | continues exactly                             False
0822 | ```
0823 | 
0824 | The final difference in that last condition was:
0825 | 
0826 | ```text
0827 | 0.003133
0828 | ```
0829 | 
0830 | So:
0831 | 
0832 | ```text
0833 | CHECKPOINT
0834 | → FUTURE
0835 | 
0836 | EVENT HISTORY
0837 | → PAST MORPHOLOGY
0838 | ```
0839 | 
0840 | They overlap.
0841 | 
0842 | But they are different mechanisms.
0843 | 
0844 | ---
0845 | 
0846 | ## The Digital Answer Was Not Biological
0847 | 
0848 | There is something important about how we solved this.
0849 | 
0850 | We could have tried to build a special region inside the crystal whose geometry somehow encoded its entire formation history.
0851 | 
0852 | A biological analogy might tempt us toward:
0853 | 
0854 | ```text
0855 | memory organ
0856 | genome-like store
0857 | special history structure
0858 | ```
0859 | 
0860 | But computation gives us another possibility.
0861 | 
0862 | ```text
0863 | serialization
0864 | checkpoint
0865 | event log
0866 | replay
0867 | branch
0868 | ```
0869 | 
0870 | These are native computational affordances.
0871 | 
0872 | We do not need to imitate a biological storage mechanism if the substrate gives us a different way to preserve the same capability.
0873 | 
0874 | This is exactly the rule we established earlier:
0875 | 
0876 | > **Do not import a biological mechanism unless the digital substrate actually requires it.**
0877 | 
0878 | The crystal's past does not need to look biological.
0879 | 
0880 | It only needs to be recoverable.
0881 | 
0882 | ---
0883 | 
0884 | ## A Digital Advantage
0885 | 
0886 | Biological systems generally do not offer us cheap, exact, executable copies of an earlier complete physical state.
0887 | 
0888 | Digital systems often can.
0889 | 
0890 | Our checkpoint is executable.
0891 | 
0892 | That changes what a saved past can be.
0893 | 
0894 | It is not merely evidence that something happened.
0895 | 
0896 | It can become:
0897 | 
0898 | ```text
0899 | an experimental starting point
0900 | ```
0901 | 
0902 | from which alternative futures are generated.
0903 | 
0904 | ---
0905 | 
0906 | ## Restore the Same Past Twice
0907 | 
0908 | Take the exact checkpoint at step `48`.
0909 | 
0910 | Restore it twice.
0911 | 
0912 | Both copies begin with:
0913 | 
0914 | ```text
0915 | same occupied cells
0916 | same timestep
0917 | same signal cursor
0918 | same RNG state
0919 | same continuation state
0920 | ```
0921 | 
0922 | Nothing differs.
0923 | 
0924 | Then deliberately provide different future environments.
0925 | 
0926 | ```text
0927 |               ┌── FUTURE A
0928 | SAVED PAST ───┤
0929 |               └── FUTURE B
0930 | ```
0931 | 
0932 | The resulting morphologies diverge.
0933 | 
0934 | In one illustrative branch:
0935 | 
0936 | ```text
0937 | final symmetric difference    35 cells
0938 | normalized difference         0.003655
0939 | ```
0940 | 
0941 | {{< figure
0942 | src="/images/books/digital-life/ch15-06-counterfactual-branches.png"
0943 | alt="One saved Digital Crystal checkpoint shown beside two different final morphologies generated from different prescribed future forcing."
0944 | caption="One exact checkpoint serves as a shared executable starting condition for two controlled future environments."
0945 | 
0946 | >}}
0947 | 
0948 | And we can watch that divergence develop.
0949 | 
0950 | {{< figure
0951 | src="/images/books/digital-life/ch15-06-counterfactual-divergence.png"
0952 | alt="A line plot showing morphology divergence through time between two Digital Crystal futures starting from the same checkpoint."
0953 | caption="Alternative prescribed futures starting from the same saved state generate measurable divergence."
0954 | 
0955 | >}}
0956 | 
0957 | So one capability is clearly established:
0958 | 
0959 | > **A complete digital checkpoint can act as an executable counterfactual branch point.**
0960 | 
0961 | But there is a trap here.
0962 | 
0963 | ---
0964 | 
0965 | ## Different Futures Always Diverge — But Why?
0966 | 
0967 | Suppose two continuations end differently.
0968 | 
0969 | It is tempting to say:
0970 | 
0971 | > The different environments caused the divergence.
0972 | 
0973 | But Digital Crystal v1 is stochastic.
0974 | 
0975 | Different random continuation states also produce different futures.
0976 | 
0977 | So the number:
0978 | 
0979 | ```text
0980 | 35 different cells
0981 | ```
0982 | 
0983 | means nothing by itself.
0984 | 
0985 | We need a null.
0986 | 
0987 | ---
0988 | 
0989 | ## The Stochastic Null
0990 | 
0991 | We compare two experiments.
0992 | 
0993 | ### Environmental treatment
0994 | 
0995 | ```text
0996 | same checkpoint
0997 | same RNG state
0998 | different future forcing
0999 | ```
1000 | 
1001 | ### Stochastic null
1002 | 
1003 | ```text
1004 | same checkpoint
1005 | same future forcing
1006 | different valid RNG states
1007 | ```
1008 | 
1009 | We run:
1010 | 
1011 | ```text
1012 | 60 replicates
1013 | ```
1014 | 
1015 | for each condition.
1016 | 
1017 | Now compare final normalized morphology divergence.
1018 | 
1019 | ---
1020 | 
1021 | ## The Environment Did Not Win
1022 | 
1023 | Different future environments produced:
1024 | 
1025 | ```text
1026 | mean divergence      0.003815
1027 | median               0.003655
1028 | ```
1029 | 
1030 | The stochastic null produced:
1031 | 
1032 | ```text
1033 | mean divergence      0.005290
1034 | median               0.004812
1035 | ```
1036 | 
1037 | So:
1038 | 
1039 | ```text
1040 | treatment - null mean    -0.001475
1041 | ```
1042 | 
1043 | The environmental treatment was not larger.
1044 | 
1045 | The pairwise superiority probability was:
1046 | 
1047 | ```text
1048 | 0.1975
1049 | ```
1050 | 
1051 | and the treatment median did not exceed the stochastic-null 95th percentile.
1052 | 
1053 | {{< figure
1054 | src="/images/books/digital-life/ch15-06-counterfactual-null.png"
1055 | alt="Comparison of final Digital Crystal divergence under different future environments versus divergence produced by ordinary stochastic continuation variation."
1056 | caption="Changing future forcing produces alternative futures, but the resulting divergence is not larger than ordinary stochastic continuation divergence under this experiment."
1057 | 
1058 | >}}
1059 | 
1060 | This kills a stronger interpretation.
1061 | 
1062 | We cannot say:
1063 | 
1064 | > **Environmental differences dominate future divergence.**
1065 | 
1066 | They did not.
1067 | 
1068 | In fact, under this protocol ordinary stochastic variation produced somewhat greater divergence on average.
1069 | 
1070 | That leaves a smaller, cleaner result:
1071 | 
1072 | > **The checkpoint is an executable branch point, but branchability does not imply that one chosen environmental manipulation dominates the system's intrinsic stochastic variation.**
1073 | 
1074 | That is exactly why the null matters.
1075 | 
1076 | ---
1077 | 
1078 | ## This Is Not Reproduction
1079 | 
1080 | We should resist that word.
1081 | 
1082 | We can restore the same checkpoint many times.
1083 | 
1084 | That resembles copying.
1085 | 
1086 | But the copying is performed by our experimental infrastructure.
1087 | 
1088 | Nothing here establishes autonomous reproduction.
1089 | 
1090 | The useful property is narrower:
1091 | 
1092 | > **Digital state can be duplicated as an exact starting condition for controlled counterfactual experiments.**
1093 | 
1094 | That is already powerful enough.
1095 | 
1096 | ---
1097 | 
1098 | ## The Crystal Now Has a Recoverable Past
1099 | 
1100 | Chapter 14 ended with:
1101 | 
1102 | ```text
1103 | PRESENT MORPHOLOGY
1104 | → SOME SOURCE INFORMATION SURVIVES
1105 | 
1106 | TEMPORAL ORDER
1107 | → NOT RECOVERABLE
1108 | ```
1109 | 
1110 | We did not solve that by forcing more temporal information into morphology.
1111 | 
1112 | We introduced a separate historical mechanism.
1113 | 
1114 | Now we have:
1115 | 
1116 | ```text
1117 | CURRENT PROCESS STATE
1118 | +
1119 | EVENT HISTORY
1120 | ```
1121 | 
1122 | which provides two complementary capabilities:
1123 | 
1124 | ```text
1125 | STATE
1126 | → CONTINUE
1127 | 
1128 | HISTORY
1129 | → RECONSTRUCT
1130 | ```
1131 | 
1132 | Together:
1133 | 
1134 | ```text
1135 | PAST
1136 | ↓
1137 | PRESENT
1138 | ↓
1139 | POSSIBLE FUTURES
1140 | ```
1141 | 
1142 | But those arrows mean different things.
1143 | 
1144 | The past can be reconstructed.
1145 | 
1146 | The present can be resumed.
1147 | 
1148 | The future can be branched.
1149 | 
1150 | That is something Digital Crystal v1 did not possess in Chapter 14.
1151 | 
1152 | ---
1153 | 
1154 | ## A Past Without Memory
1155 | 
1156 | It is tempting to call this memory.
1157 | 
1158 | I do not think we have earned that yet.
1159 | 
1160 | We have built:
1161 | 
1162 | ```text
1163 | storage
1164 | checkpointing
1165 | history
1166 | replay
1167 | restore
1168 | branching
1169 | ```
1170 | 
1171 | But the crystal itself does not inspect the record.
1172 | 
1173 | It does not ask:
1174 | 
1175 | > What happened before?
1176 | 
1177 | It does not change its behavior because of an earlier event stored in the log.
1178 | 
1179 | The history exists.
1180 | 
1181 | The process does not yet consult it.
1182 | 
1183 | So for now:
1184 | 
1185 | > **The Digital Crystal has a recoverable past.**
1186 | 
1187 | Not memory.
1188 | 
1189 | Not learning.
1190 | 
1191 | A past.
1192 | 
1193 | That is enough.
1194 | 
1195 | ---
1196 | 
1197 | ## What Survived the Hypothesis?
1198 | 
1199 | The chapter began with a simple problem:
1200 | 
1201 | ```text
1202 | the crystal has a present
1203 | but
1204 | the present does not preserve chronology
1205 | ```
1206 | 
1207 | Chapter 14 had already shown that final morphology contains some information about the conditions under which the crystal formed, but not enough to reconstruct temporal ordering.
1208 | 
1209 | Chapter 15 asked a different question:
1210 | 
1211 | > What information must survive if the process is to continue exactly, and what information must survive if its formation history is to be reconstructed?
1212 | 
1213 | The answer turned out to contain two distinct mechanisms.
1214 | 
1215 | ### The checkpoint phenomenon
1216 | 
1217 | A complete checkpoint can be serialized, restored and continued exactly.
1218 | 
1219 | Across the validation runs:
1220 | 
1221 | ```text
1222 | 30 / 30 exact restores
1223 | ```
1224 | 
1225 | The restored process reproduced:
1226 | 
1227 | ```text
1228 | the same cells
1229 | the same attachment decisions
1230 | the same population trajectory
1231 | the same final process state
1232 | ```
1233 | 
1234 | But visible morphology alone was not sufficient.
1235 | 
1236 | The same occupied structure could lead to a different future when hidden continuation variables differed.
1237 | 
1238 | So:
1239 | 
1240 | ```text
1241 | VISIBLE FORM
1242 | ≠
1243 | EXECUTABLE STATE
1244 | ```
1245 | 
1246 | This is not merely an implementation lesson.
1247 | 
1248 | It tells us that the future of the Digital Crystal depends on information that is not necessarily visible in the current morphology.
1249 | 
1250 | ### Phenomenon record
1251 | 
1252 | **Phenomenon:** Executable hidden state
1253 | 
1254 | **Status:** **SUPPORTED**
1255 | 
1256 | **Current bounded description:**
1257 | 
1258 | > Digital Crystal v1 requires continuation-relevant state beyond visible morphology for exact future replay. A complete checkpoint is sufficient for exact continuation, while morphology alone is not.
1259 | 
1260 | The experimentally identified continuation-relevant components include:
1261 | 
1262 | ```text
1263 | occupied structure
1264 | RNG continuation state
1265 | environmental sequence position
1266 | ```
1267 | 
1268 | while birth-time metadata behaves differently:
1269 | 
1270 | ```text
1271 | birth time
1272 | → historical information
1273 | 
1274 | birth time
1275 | → not required for same occupied-set continuation
1276 | ```
1277 | 
1278 | That gives us another important distinction:
1279 | 
1280 | ```text
1281 | HISTORICAL INFORMATION
1282 | ≠
1283 | CAUSALLY ACTIVE CONTINUATION STATE
1284 | ```
1285 | 
1286 | ### State and history are different computational objects
1287 | 
1288 | The event-log experiment exposes the other half of the chapter.
1289 | 
1290 | The stored history reconstructs:
1291 | 
1292 | ```text
1293 | 96 / 96 morphology states exactly
1294 | ```
1295 | 
1296 | but that event history does not restore the historical RNG state required for exact stochastic continuation.
1297 | 
1298 | So the two representations answer different questions:
1299 | 
1300 | ```text
1301 | CHECKPOINT
1302 | → what is required to continue from here?
1303 | 
1304 | EVENT HISTORY
1305 | → how did the present morphology arise?
1306 | ```
1307 | 
1308 | or more compactly:
1309 | 
1310 | ```text
1311 | STATE
1312 | → FUTURE
1313 | 
1314 | HISTORY
1315 | → PAST
1316 | ```
1317 | 
1318 | They overlap, but they are not interchangeable.
1319 | 
1320 | This gives us a second project-wide distinction:
1321 | 
1322 | > **A process can preserve enough information to reconstruct its past without preserving the continuation state required to regenerate its exact future from that reconstruction alone.**
1323 | 
1324 | ### The implementation failure revealed a deeper rule
1325 | 
1326 | The accidental dependence on Python `set` iteration order matters here too.
1327 | 
1328 | Before canonicalization, the simulation contained an undeclared continuation variable:
1329 | 
1330 | ```text
1331 | Python container traversal order
1332 | ```
1333 | 
1334 | Two mathematically equivalent reconstructed states could consume the same random draws in different orders and diverge.
1335 | 
1336 | That was not Digital Crystal physics.
1337 | 
1338 | It was implementation state leaking into the experiment.
1339 | 
1340 | Once frontier traversal was canonicalized, exact restore became reproducible.
1341 | 
1342 | This gives us a methodological phenomenon worth carrying across the project:
1343 | 
1344 | > **Scientific state must be separated from implementation state.**
1345 | 
1346 | If hidden program details influence outcomes, they must either be promoted into the declared model or eliminated.
1347 | 
1348 | ### Connection to the Lossy-History Principle
1349 | 
1350 | Chapter 15 also sharpens the result from Chapter 14.
1351 | 
1352 | Chapter 14 showed:
1353 | 
1354 | ```text
1355 | coarse source characteristics
1356 | survive in morphology
1357 | 
1358 | exact temporal order
1359 | does not
1360 | ```
1361 | 
1362 | Chapter 15 shows that this loss is not inevitable to the digital substrate as a whole.
1363 | 
1364 | The chronology can be preserved perfectly if we use an explicit event history.
1365 | 
1366 | So the stronger cross-chapter picture becomes:
1367 | 
1368 | ```text
1369 | MORPHOLOGY
1370 | → lossy historical integration
1371 | 
1372 | EVENT LOG
1373 | → exact formation-history reconstruction
1374 | 
1375 | CHECKPOINT
1376 | → exact continuation
1377 | ```
1378 | 
1379 | That matters because it tells us that different representations preserve different slices of the past.
1380 | 
1381 | The substrate gives us several native computational affordances:
1382 | 
1383 | ```text
1384 | serialization
1385 | checkpointing
1386 | event logging
1387 | replay
1388 | branching
1389 | ```
1390 | 
1391 | We do not need to force all historical information into visible form.
1392 | 
1393 | ### Counterfactual branchability survives, dominance does not
1394 | 
1395 | The checkpoint also provides an executable branch point.
1396 | 
1397 | One exact saved state can be restored into multiple controlled futures.
1398 | 
1399 | That property survives.
1400 | 
1401 | But the stronger environmental-divergence interpretation does not.
1402 | 
1403 | Different prescribed future environments produced measurable divergence, yet that divergence did not exceed ordinary stochastic continuation variation under the frozen comparison.
1404 | 
1405 | So:
1406 | 
1407 | ```text
1408 | EXECUTABLE BRANCHABILITY
1409 | SUPPORTED
1410 | 
1411 | ENVIRONMENTAL MANIPULATION
1412 | DOMINATES STOCHASTIC DIVERGENCE
1413 | NOT SUPPORTED
1414 | ```
1415 | 
1416 | Again, the failed interpretation does not erase the underlying capability.
1417 | 
1418 | ### What this phenomenon does not establish
1419 | 
1420 | The surviving phenomena do **not** establish:
1421 | 
1422 | - cognitive memory,
1423 | - learning,
1424 | - adaptation,
1425 | - autonomous reproduction,
1426 | - a mathematically minimal state representation,
1427 | - a biological genome analogue,
1428 | - or life.
1429 | 
1430 | They establish something narrower and more computationally native:
1431 | 
1432 | > **A Digital Crystal can possess hidden executable state sufficient for exact continuation, an explicit history sufficient for exact morphology reconstruction, and a checkpoint that functions as a reusable counterfactual branch point. These are distinct capabilities carried by distinct representations.**
1433 | 
1434 | This phenomenon should now be tracked independently of the chapter's stronger historical or environmental interpretations.
1435 | 
1436 | ---
1437 | 
1438 | ## Evidence Ledger
1439 | 
1440 | | Claim                                                             | Status            | Evidence                                              |
1441 | | ----------------------------------------------------------------- | ----------------- | ----------------------------------------------------- |
1442 | | Canonicalized model survives state serialization/reconstruction   | **SUPPORTED**     | one-step and 48-step invariant pass                   |
1443 | | Complete checkpoint resumes exact trajectory                      | **SUPPORTED**     | identical process and morphology hashes               |
1444 | | Exact restore generalizes across tested seeds                     | **SUPPORTED**     | `30/30` exact                                         |
1445 | | Visible morphology alone is sufficient continuation state         | **FAILED**        | 30-cell divergence                                    |
1446 | | RNG state matters for exact continuation                          | **SUPPORTED**     | 28-cell divergence                                    |
1447 | | Signal cursor matters at fixed horizon                            | **SUPPORTED**     | 27-cell divergence                                    |
1448 | | Birth-time metadata affects future occupied-set growth            | **FAILED**        | 0 differing occupied cells                            |
1449 | | Birth times function as historical metadata in v1                 | **SUPPORTED**     | history differs while geometric continuation does not |
1450 | | Event log reconstructs morphology trajectory                      | **SUPPORTED**     | `96/96` trajectory hashes                             |
1451 | | Event log reconstructs historical RNG state                       | **NOT SUPPORTED** | additions alone do not contain RNG history            |
1452 | | State and history are operationally distinct                      | **SUPPORTED**     | one continues; one reconstructs                       |
1453 | | Checkpoint is an executable branch point                          | **SUPPORTED**     | controlled alternative continuations                  |
1454 | | Different forcing produces divergence beyond stochastic variation | **NOT SUPPORTED** | treatment mean `<` stochastic-null mean               |
1455 | | Checkpoint representation is mathematically minimal               | **UNTESTED**      | only sufficiency/ablations tested                     |
1456 | | Stored history is cognitive memory                                | **NOT CLAIMED**   | process does not consult history                      |
1457 | | Crystal learns or adapts from history                             | **NOT CLAIMED**   | no such mechanism                                     |
1458 | | Crystal is alive                                                  | **NOT CLAIMED**   | evidence insufficient                                 |
1459 | 
1460 | ---
1461 | 
1462 | ## Bounded Claims
1463 | 
1464 | From this chapter we can reasonably claim:
1465 | 
1466 | 1. **Exact continuation** — A complete Digital Crystal v1 checkpoint can be serialized, restored and continued without altering the stochastic trajectory.
1467 | 
1468 | 2. **Reproducibility across runs** — Exact restore succeeded in `30/30` independent validation runs.
1469 | 
1470 | 3. **Morphology is not complete state** — Identical visible structure can produce a different future if relevant hidden continuation variables differ.
1471 | 
1472 | 4. **RNG state matters** — Stochastic continuation state affects exact future growth.
1473 | 
1474 | 5. **Environmental position matters** — Signal position affects exact continuation even when the number of future updates is held fixed.
1475 | 
1476 | 6. **Birth time is history, not growth state in v1** — Changing birth metadata alone does not alter the future occupied-cell trajectory.
1477 | 
1478 | 7. **History can reconstruct morphology** — The explicit event log reconstructs the recorded formation trajectory exactly across `96/96` states.
1479 | 
1480 | 8. **State and history are operationally distinct** — The checkpoint supports continuation; the event log supports reconstruction.
1481 | 
1482 | 9. **A checkpoint is an executable counterfactual branch point** — One saved state can be reused to generate controlled alternative continuations.
1483 | 
1484 | 10. **Environmental branching did not exceed stochastic divergence** — Under the tested protocol, changing future forcing did not produce more divergence than ordinary stochastic continuation variation.
1485 | 
1486 | That is where the evidence stops.
1487 | 
1488 | ---
1489 | 
1490 | ## Something Else Has Happened
1491 | 
1492 | There is one more consequence.
1493 | 
1494 | Our history is made from events.
1495 | 
1496 | Until now those events stay inside the experimental record.
1497 | 
1498 | ```text
1499 | CRYSTAL
1500 | ↓
1501 | EVENT
1502 | ↓
1503 | HISTORY
1504 | ```
1505 | 
1506 | But an event does not have to remain internal.
1507 | 
1508 | One process can emit one.
1509 | 
1510 | Another process can receive it.
1511 | 
1512 | The event does not need to contain language.
1513 | 
1514 | It does not need semantics.
1515 | 
1516 | It may contain almost nothing.
1517 | 
1518 | ```text
1519 | one bit
1520 | a pulse
1521 | a number
1522 | ```
1523 | 
1524 | And if one process can change the conditions experienced by another, then the environment no longer needs to come entirely from outside the system.
1525 | 
1526 | Something generated inside one process can become part of another process's world.
1527 | 
1528 | ```text
1529 | CRYSTAL A
1530 | ↓
1531 | EVENT
1532 | ↓
1533 | CRYSTAL B
1534 | ```
1535 | 
1536 | But Chapter 13 taught us to be careful with correlation.
1537 | 
1538 | Chapter 14 taught us that state is not history.
1539 | 
1540 | And this chapter taught us that a branch can diverge without the environment being the dominant cause.
1541 | 
1542 | So we should not call the next thing communication merely because we connect two systems.
1543 | 
1544 | The next question has to be smaller:
1545 | 
1546 | > **Can an event emitted by one Digital Crystal reliably change another?**
1547 | 
1548 | The crystal has a past.
1549 | 
1550 | **Next, we let the crystals hear each other.**
```

```markdown
0001 | +++
0002 | title = "16: Before There Are Messages"
0003 | date = "2026-08-12T02:38:00+01:00"
0004 | draft = false
0005 | description = "A Digital Crystal can causally alter another through a one-bit event, but causal transmission is not yet sender-specific signalling."
0006 | categories = ["Programming", "Artificial Life"]
0007 | tags = ["Digital Life", "Digital Crystal", "Signalling", "Causality", "Information", "Experiments"]
0008 | series = ["Digital Life From First Principles"]
0009 | +++
0010 | 
0011 | In the last chapter, the crystal acquired a past.  
0012 | Not memory in the biological sense.  
0013 | Not recollection.  
0014 | Not understanding.  
0015 | A recoverable past.
0016 | 
0017 | We learned that a complete checkpoint could continue the process exactly, that an event history could reconstruct the route by which the present morphology had formed, and that the same checkpoint could be used as a branch point for controlled alternative futures.
0018 | 
0019 | That gave us something new:
0020 | 
0021 | - A present that could be resumed.
0022 | - A past that could be reconstructed.
0023 | - A future that could be forked.
0024 | 
0025 | The next temptation was obvious.  
0026 | If one Digital Crystal can preserve state and history, perhaps two crystals can communicate.
0027 | 
0028 | That word is dangerous.  
0029 | “Communication” arrives carrying far more assumptions than we have earned.
0030 | 
0031 | A sender.  
0032 | A receiver.  
0033 | A message.  
0034 | A channel.  
0035 | Meaning.  
0036 | Perhaps even intention.
0037 | 
0038 | We have established none of those things.  
0039 | So we begin with something smaller.  
0040 | Not a message.  
0041 | A pulse.
0042 | 
0043 | > **Before there are messages, there are events that can alter another process.**
0044 | 
0045 | That is the whole experiment.  
0046 | Can one Digital Crystal produce a one‑bit event that changes another Digital Crystal?  
0047 | If the answer is no, there is no reason to discuss communication.  
0048 | If the answer is yes, we still have a long way to go.
0049 | 
0050 | ---
0051 | 
0052 | ## The Smallest Possible Channel
0053 | 
0054 | We keep the Digital Crystal itself unchanged.  
0055 | Its growth rule remains the frozen Digital Crystal v1 model from the previous chapters.  
0056 | A crystal grows locally across a hexagonal lattice. Its attachment probability depends on its local neighbourhood and on an external scalar input.
0057 | 
0058 | We add only one new mechanism: a sender crystal can emit a bit.
0059 | 
0060 | The bit is either `0` or `1`.
0061 | 
0062 | The receiver does not receive a sentence.  
0063 | It does not receive a symbol.  
0064 | It does not receive an identifier saying who sent the event.
0065 | 
0066 | The bit simply perturbs the scalar environmental input used by the receiver’s existing growth rule.
0067 | 
0068 | ```mermaid
0069 | flowchart LR
0070 |     S[Sender Growth] --> E[Endogenous Event\<br/>one bit]
0071 |     E --> R[Receiver Forcing\<br/>perturbation]
0072 |     R --> P[Receiver Attachment\<br/>probabilities change]
0073 |     P --> M[Receiver Morphology\<br/>may diverge]
0074 | ```
0075 | 
0076 | There is no separate oscillator.  
0077 | There is no artificial coordination layer.  
0078 | The pulse reaches the Digital Crystal itself.
0079 | 
0080 | That distinction matters because an earlier version of this experiment had done something weaker: it coupled auxiliary oscillators and looked for synchronization. That could have produced an interesting dynamical system while leaving the crystal’s actual growth almost untouched.
0081 | 
0082 | This experiment asks a harder question:
0083 | 
0084 | > **Does the bit reach the thing we are actually studying?**
0085 | 
0086 | ---
0087 | 
0088 | ## The Sender Does Not Fire on a Clock
0089 | 
0090 | A trivial signalling system would be easy to construct.
0091 | 
0092 | We could write:
0093 | 
0094 | ```python
0095 | if step % 10 == 0:
0096 |     send(1)
0097 | ```
0098 | 
0099 | Then every receiver could respond to a programmer‑supplied metronome.  
0100 | That would establish almost nothing.
0101 | 
0102 | Instead, the pulse must come from the sender’s own dynamics.  
0103 | At each step we count how many new cells the sender attaches.  
0104 | We compare that value with its recent attachment history.  
0105 | When current growth is unusually high relative to that recent history, the sender emits a one‑bit pulse.
0106 | 
0107 | The rule is deliberately simple.  
0108 | The pulse means only:
0109 | 
0110 | > an endogenous growth event occurred.
0111 | 
0112 | It does not mean danger.  
0113 | It does not mean food.  
0114 | It does not mean move left.  
0115 | It does not even mean “I grew.”  
0116 | Those would already be semantic claims.
0117 | 
0118 | At this stage, `1` is merely a detectable event generated by the sender’s own changing state.
0119 | 
0120 | ---
0121 | 
0122 | ## A Problem We Discovered Before the Real Experiment
0123 | 
0124 | The first versions of this experiment produced a misleading endpoint.  
0125 | The crystals eventually filled almost the entire hard‑radius region available to them.  
0126 | Once that happened, very different trajectories converged on the same final shape: a filled hexagonal disk.
0127 | 
0128 | The experiment had not discovered convergence.  
0129 | The boundary condition had erased the differences.
0130 | 
0131 | This is exactly the kind of failure the method of this book is supposed to catch.  
0132 | A final picture can become uninformative even while the process that produced it was different.
0133 | 
0134 | So the experiment now contains a saturation guard.  
0135 | For a hexagonal disk of radius (r), the maximum number of cells is:
0136 | 
0137 | $$
0138 | N(r) = 1 + 3r(r+1)
0139 | $$
0140 | 
0141 | The full experiment uses radius 56, so the hard maximum is 9,577 cells.  
0142 | We predeclare that endpoint morphology becomes invalid once a crystal reaches 85% of that capacity.
0143 | 
0144 | The experiment therefore does not blindly run for a fixed number of steps.  
0145 | Instead it finds the longest safe interval before the boundary starts dominating the result.
0146 | 
0147 | For the full sender/receiver run, 90 steps were requested.  
0148 | The common safe horizon was 76.
0149 | 
0150 | At step 76 the sender occupied 7,723 cells (~80.6% of capacity). The receiver occupied 8,026 cells (~83.8%).  
0151 | Step 77 would have crossed the guard.  
0152 | So we stop at 76.
0153 | 
0154 | That is not an inconvenience. It is part of the experiment.
0155 | 
0156 | > **The requested horizon is not necessarily the experimentally valid horizon.**
0157 | 
0158 | ---
0159 | 
0160 | ## The First Real Test: One Bit Versus No Bit
0161 | 
0162 | Before asking whether a sender’s pulse stream matters, we need to know whether a single received bit matters at all.
0163 | 
0164 | Chapter 15 gave us exactly the tool we need: checkpointing.
0165 | 
0166 | We take one receiver state and fork it.  
0167 | Both futures begin with the same morphology, birth‑time metadata, RNG state, environmental forcing, timestep, and future horizon.
0168 | 
0169 | Then we change exactly one thing: one branch receives a bit; the other does not.
0170 | 
0171 | ```mermaid
0172 | flowchart TD
0173 |     CK[Checkpoint\<br/>identical receiver state] --> BIT1[BIT = 1]
0174 |     CK --> BIT0[BIT = 0]
0175 |     BIT1 --> FUT_A[Future A]
0176 |     BIT0 --> FUT_B[Future B]
0177 |     FUT_A & FUT_B --> COMP[Compare final morphology]
0178 | ```
0179 | 
0180 | This is the strongest experiment in the chapter because it removes almost every obvious alternative explanation.  
0181 | If the two futures differ, the difference was caused by the intervention.
0182 | 
0183 | In the full run we repeated this 120 times.
0184 | 
0185 | The result was not subtle.  
0186 | In **95.8%** of interventions, the final morphologies differed.  
0187 | The mean normalized final difference was **0.1633**, with an average symmetric difference of about **158 cells**.  
0188 | The maximum observed capacity fraction in these experiments was only about 13.5%, so saturation played no role.
0189 | 
0190 | The bit did not always produce a difference; five of the 120 interventions ended with no morphology difference under the measured horizon.  
0191 | That matters.
0192 | 
0193 | The correct claim is not “every received bit necessarily changes the receiver.”  
0194 | The claim is:
0195 | 
0196 | > Under this protocol, changing one received bit while holding receiver state, stochastic state and environmental forcing fixed usually altered the receiver’s subsequent morphology.
0197 | 
0198 | That is enough to establish a primitive causal channel.
0199 | 
0200 | ---
0201 | 
0202 | ## The Pulse Has an Immediate Effect
0203 | 
0204 | We can also measure the receiver’s attachment response after the intervention.  
0205 | Across the 120 full‑profile replicates, the mean attachment difference between the bit and no‑bit branches began:
0206 | 
0207 | ```text
0208 | +1.81
0209 |  0.00
0210 | -0.34
0211 | -0.08
0212 | -0.30
0213 | -0.32
0214 | -0.39
0215 | -0.35
0216 | -0.61
0217 | -0.26
0218 | -0.25
0219 | -0.75
0220 | ```
0221 | 
0222 | The largest average effect occurs immediately.  
0223 | That is useful, but we should not turn it into a latency law.  
0224 | This is a finite‑horizon impulse response; the downstream differences depend on the crystal’s subsequent stochastic and morphological evolution.
0225 | 
0226 | We have shown that the perturbation enters the growth process and can persist through later divergence.  
0227 | We have not measured channel capacity.  
0228 | We have not identified a universal response time.  
0229 | We have not discovered a communication protocol.
0230 | 
0231 | ---
0232 | 
0233 | ## Transmission Is Not Signalling
0234 | 
0235 | At this point it would be easy to overclaim.  
0236 | One crystal emits a bit. Another crystal changes. Therefore they communicate.
0237 | 
0238 | No.
0239 | 
0240 | All we have established is causal transmission.  
0241 | A light switch also changes a circuit.  
0242 | A voltage spike changes a transistor.  
0243 | A falling rock changes whatever it hits.
0244 | 
0245 | Causal influence is necessary for signalling. It is not sufficient.
0246 | 
0247 | To move beyond transmission we need to ask a more difficult question:
0248 | 
0249 | > Does the actual sender‑generated event stream matter, or would almost any similarly structured pulse stream produce the same receiver response?
0250 | 
0251 | That question requires controls.
0252 | 
0253 | ---
0254 | 
0255 | ## Destroy the Timing
0256 | 
0257 | The first control keeps the same number of bits but shuffles when they occur.  
0258 | If exact sender timing matters, the real stream should outperform the shuffled stream.
0259 | 
0260 | It does.
0261 | 
0262 | Across 60 full‑profile replicates, the mean peak message‑to‑growth correlation for the real stream exceeded the shuffled stream by about **0.294**.  
0263 | The probability that a randomly selected real replicate exceeded a randomly selected shuffled replicate was about **0.980**.
0264 | 
0265 | So timing structure matters.  
0266 | That is already more than generic bit injection.
0267 | 
0268 | ---
0269 | 
0270 | ## Replace the Sender With Randomness
0271 | 
0272 | Next we preserve the number of pulses but place them at random times.
0273 | 
0274 | Again the real sender wins strongly.  
0275 | The real‑minus‑random difference was about **0.270**, with pairwise superiority around **0.977**.
0276 | 
0277 | So the receiver is not merely responding to message count; a burst pattern matters.
0278 | 
0279 | But this is still not enough.  
0280 | The strongest control is not randomness. It is another sender.
0281 | 
0282 | ---
0283 | 
0284 | ## The Unrelated Sender
0285 | 
0286 | We now generate a pulse stream from another Digital Crystal of the same type.  
0287 | It has its own independent environment and its own independent growth trajectory.  
0288 | Then we force its pulse count to match the real sender exactly.
0289 | 
0290 | This removes a simple explanation: perhaps the real sender looked better only because it emitted more or fewer pulses.
0291 | 
0292 | Now the receiver sees either the actual sender stream or a same‑class unrelated sender stream with the same pulse count.
0293 | 
0294 | If the actual sender carries something specific, it should win.  
0295 | It does not.
0296 | 
0297 | The real‑minus‑unrelated difference was **-0.015**.  
0298 | Pairwise superiority was **0.457**.
0299 | 
0300 | The unrelated sender was, if anything, fractionally ahead under this metric. That difference is tiny.  
0301 | The important fact is that the receiver cannot distinguish the actual sender from another crystal producing the same general class of pulse activity.
0302 | 
0303 | This is the result that stops us using the word “communication” casually.
0304 | 
0305 | > **The receiver is sensitive to a kind of event stream, but not to which same‑class crystal produced it.**
0306 | 
0307 | ---
0308 | 
0309 | ## Preserve the Pulse Intervals, Destroy Their Order
0310 | 
0311 | We can make the control harder still.  
0312 | Take the real sender’s pulse stream. Measure every gap between pulses.
0313 | 
0314 | Suppose the gaps were: `1, 1, 2, 1, 4, 1, 1, 3, …`
0315 | 
0316 | Now preserve that exact multiset of intervals but permute their order.  
0317 | The surrogate has the same number of pulses, the same collection of inter‑pulse intervals, the same coarse burst statistics — but a different chronology.
0318 | 
0319 | If the exact sequence carries important information, the real stream should outperform this surrogate.  
0320 | It does not.
0321 | 
0322 | The real‑minus‑surrogate difference was only **0.010**.  
0323 | Pairwise superiority was **0.473**.
0324 | 
0325 | Again, effectively no advantage.
0326 | 
0327 | This suggests that much of the receiver’s response is driven by coarse temporal structure rather than a uniquely informative chronology.  
0328 | That is not yet a theorem, but it is the strongest interpretation supported by this experiment.
0329 | 
0330 | > **The receiver appears to respond to the form of the pulse stream rather than the identity of its source.**
0331 | 
0332 | ```mermaid
0333 | flowchart TD
0334 |     subgraph Experiments
0335 |     A[Bit vs No Bit] --> B[Timing Shuffled]
0336 |     B --> C[Rate-Matched Random]
0337 |     C --> D[Unrelated Same-Class Sender]
0338 |     D --> E[Interval-Preserving Surrogate]
0339 |     end
0340 |     B & C --> F[Timing matters]
0341 |     D --> G[Identity of sender does not matter\<br/>under tested controls]
0342 |     E --> G
0343 |     F -.->|necessary| H[Causal Transmission Established]
0344 |     G -.->|missing| I[Sender-Specific Signalling Not Supported]
0345 | ```
0346 | 
0347 | ---
0348 | 
0349 | ## The Chain
0350 | 
0351 | Once we had a causal one‑bit channel, we tried a simple extension.  
0352 | Six crystals in a line:
0353 | 
0354 | ```mermaid
0355 | flowchart LR
0356 |     C1[C1] --> C2[C2] --> C3[C3] --> C4[C4] --> C5[C5] --> C6[C6]
0357 | ```
0358 | 
0359 | Each crystal evolves independently. A pulse from one becomes an input to the next. If that input changes the receiver enough, the receiver’s own dynamics may later generate another pulse.
0360 | 
0361 | At first glance the result looks impressive. The full run produced source‑to‑node pulse correlations of approximately:
0362 | 
0363 | ```text
0364 | C1  1.000
0365 | C2  0.537
0366 | C3  0.477
0367 | C4  0.462
0368 | C5  0.462
0369 | C6  0.458
0370 | ```
0371 | 
0372 | It would be easy to draw a picture of a signal propagating down the chain.  
0373 | So we destroy the topology.
0374 | 
0375 | The shuffled‑edge control produced:
0376 | 
0377 | ```text
0378 | C1  1.000
0379 | C2  0.498
0380 | C3  0.461
0381 | C4  0.478
0382 | C5  0.471
0383 | C6  0.461
0384 | ```
0385 | 
0386 | The mean absolute real‑versus‑shuffled difference by distance was only about **0.0164**.
0387 | 
0388 | So the chain does not establish topology‑specific propagation.  
0389 | The high correlations are real observations. The interpretation “information travelled down this particular chain” is not supported.
0390 | 
0391 | ---
0392 | 
0393 | ## The Board
0394 | 
0395 | Finally we built a 6×6 Crystal Board.  
0396 | Thirty‑six independent Digital Crystals, each connected locally to neighbouring crystals through one‑bit events.
0397 | 
0398 | ```mermaid
0399 | graph TD
0400 |     subgraph 6x6 Board
0401 |     A1---A2---A3---A4---A5---A6
0402 |     |    |    |    |    |    |
0403 |     B1---B2---B3---B4---B5---B6
0404 |     |    |    |    |    |    |
0405 |     C1---C2---C3---C4---C5---C6
0406 |     |    |    |    |    |    |
0407 |     D1---D2---D3---D4---D5---D6
0408 |     |    |    |    |    |    |
0409 |     E1---E2---E3---E4---E5---E6
0410 |     |    |    |    |    |    |
0411 |     F1---F2---F3---F4---F5---F6
0412 |     end
0413 | ```
0414 | 
0415 | No target. No game. No shape to construct. No collective objective. Just local event transmission.
0416 | 
0417 | Again, real and shuffled topologies were run from matched conditions.  
0418 | Again, both were stopped at a common safe horizon before saturation (requested horizon 90 steps, average safe horizon ~73 steps).
0419 | 
0420 | The real topology did not produce a meaningful advantage over shuffled topology.  
0421 | The final real‑minus‑shuffled neighbour‑correlation difference was only about **0.0048**.
0422 | 
0423 | So the board does not yet contain evidence of organized local signalling.  
0424 | We have created connectivity. We have not created coordination.
0425 | 
0426 | ---
0427 | 
0428 | ## What Survived
0429 | 
0430 | Chapter 16 began with a word we did not deserve: communication.  
0431 | After the experiments, most of that word has been stripped away. What survives is smaller and more precise.
0432 | 
0433 | - A one‑bit event can enter another Digital Crystal’s actual growth dynamics and alter its future morphology.
0434 | - Real pulse timing matters relative to shuffled or random timing.
0435 | - But the actual sender does not yet matter.
0436 | - An unrelated same‑class sender performs essentially as well.
0437 | - An interval‑preserving surrogate performs essentially as well.
0438 | - Local chain structure does not outperform shuffled structure convincingly.
0439 | - The 6×6 board does not outperform shuffled topology convincingly.
0440 | 
0441 | So the result is not:
0442 | 
0443 | ```text
0444 | THE CRYSTALS COMMUNICATE
0445 | ```
0446 | 
0447 | It is:
0448 | 
0449 | ```text
0450 | CAUSAL TRANSMISSION IS SUPPORTED
0451 | while
0452 | SENDER-SPECIFIC SIGNALLING IS NOT SUPPORTED
0453 | ```
0454 | 
0455 | ---
0456 | 
0457 | ## What Survived the Hypothesis?
0458 | 
0459 | The strongest interpretation in this chapter did not survive.
0460 | 
0461 | We began with the possibility that one Digital Crystal might do something meaningfully like communicate with another.
0462 | 
0463 | The first intervention gave us a real causal result:
0464 | 
0465 | ```text
0466 | one received bit
0467 | ↓
0468 | receiver growth dynamics change
0469 | ↓
0470 | future morphology often diverges
0471 | ```
0472 | 
0473 | Across 120 paired checkpoint interventions, `95.8%` produced a morphology difference.
0474 | 
0475 | That established something important:
0476 | 
0477 | ```text
0478 | CAUSAL TRANSMISSION
0479 | SUPPORTED
0480 | ```
0481 | 
0482 | But transmission was not the whole hypothesis.
0483 | 
0484 | The harder controls asked whether something specific about the actual sender survived into the receiver.
0485 | 
0486 | That is where the stronger interpretation failed.
0487 | 
0488 | ### The hierarchy exposed by the controls
0489 | 
0490 | The control ladder produced a very clear information hierarchy:
0491 | 
0492 | ```text
0493 | BIT VERSUS NO BIT
0494 |         strong effect
0495 | 
0496 | REAL TIMING VERSUS SHUFFLED
0497 |         real wins
0498 | 
0499 | REAL TIMING VERSUS RATE-MATCHED RANDOM
0500 |         real wins
0501 | 
0502 | REAL SENDER VERSUS COUNT-MATCHED
0503 | UNRELATED SAME-CLASS SENDER
0504 |         no meaningful advantage
0505 | 
0506 | REAL CHRONOLOGY VERSUS
0507 | IPI-PRESERVING SURROGATE
0508 |         no meaningful advantage
0509 | ```
0510 | 
0511 | So the receiver is sensitive to more than pulse count.
0512 | 
0513 | Some temporal structure matters.
0514 | 
0515 | But the information that survives is not fine enough, under these controls, to identify the sender or the exact chronology of its pulse intervals.
0516 | 
0517 | That gives us a much sharper distinction:
0518 | 
0519 | ```text
0520 | COARSE TEMPORAL STRUCTURE
0521 | MATTERS
0522 | 
0523 | SENDER IDENTITY
0524 | NOT RECOVERED
0525 | 
0526 | EXACT INTERVAL ORDER
0527 | NOT RECOVERED
0528 | ```
0529 | 
0530 | ### Phenomenon record
0531 | 
0532 | **Phenomenon:** Lossy transmission of temporal structure
0533 | 
0534 | **Status:** **SUPPORTED**
0535 | 
0536 | **Current bounded description:**
0537 | 
0538 | > A one-bit event can causally alter another Digital Crystal, and receiver response depends on coarse timing structure, but the tested channel does not preserve enough information for sender-specific discrimination or exact interval-order discrimination.
0539 | 
0540 | This is not merely a communication failure.
0541 | 
0542 | It tells us something about the substrate's information resolution.
0543 | 
0544 | The receiver appears sensitive to broad temporal form such as:
0545 | 
0546 | ```text
0547 | pulse density
0548 | burstiness
0549 | interval distribution
0550 | coarse timing regime
0551 | ```
0552 | 
0553 | while being comparatively insensitive to:
0554 | 
0555 | ```text
0556 | which same-class sender produced the stream
0557 | exact ordering of the same interval collection
0558 | ```
0559 | 
0560 | That pattern connects directly to Chapter 14.
0561 | 
0562 | There, final morphology preserved enough information to recover forcing-process family while exact temporal ordering failed.
0563 | 
0564 | Here, receiver response distinguishes real timing from shuffled or rate-random controls, but does not distinguish the actual sender from an unrelated same-class sender or the exact chronology from an interval-preserving surrogate.
0565 | 
0566 | Together they support a provisional cross-chapter principle:
0567 | 
0568 | > **Digital Crystal dynamics preserve coarse process structure more readily than exact chronology or source identity.**
0569 | 
0570 | This is the **Lossy-History Principle** appearing in a different experimental role.
0571 | 
0572 | Chapter 14 observed it in final morphology.
0573 | 
0574 | Chapter 16 observes it during causal transmission.
0575 | 
0576 | ### Transmission is not identity preservation
0577 | 
0578 | The distinction can be written as:
0579 | 
0580 | ```text
0581 | CAUSE
0582 | ≠
0583 | IDENTITY
0584 | 
0585 | TIMING EFFECT
0586 | ≠
0587 | CHRONOLOGY RECOVERY
0588 | 
0589 | CHANNEL
0590 | ≠
0591 | MESSAGE
0592 | 
0593 | RECEIVER RESPONSE
0594 | ≠
0595 | SENDER-SPECIFIC SIGNALLING
0596 | ```
0597 | 
0598 | The pulse absolutely reaches the receiver.
0599 | 
0600 | The receiver absolutely changes.
0601 | 
0602 | But what survives transmission is coarse.
0603 | 
0604 | The channel carries causal influence without yet carrying enough discriminating structure to make the source operationally identifiable.
0605 | 
0606 | ### The topology experiments reinforce the same lesson
0607 | 
0608 | The chain and board experiments initially looked like stronger communication results.
0609 | 
0610 | But their topology-specific interpretations failed.
0611 | 
0612 | The six-crystal chain produced high correlations, yet shuffled edges produced nearly the same pattern.
0613 | 
0614 | The 6×6 board also failed to outperform shuffled topology meaningfully.
0615 | 
0616 | So again:
0617 | 
0618 | ```text
0619 | OBSERVATION
0620 | high correlation / local influence
0621 | 
0622 | STRONG INTERPRETATION
0623 | information propagated specifically through
0624 | the intended communication topology
0625 | 
0626 | STATUS
0627 | FAILED
0628 | ```
0629 | 
0630 | The measured relationships survive.
0631 | 
0632 | The topology-specific explanation does not.
0633 | 
0634 | ### What this phenomenon does not establish
0635 | 
0636 | The surviving phenomenon does **not** establish:
0637 | 
0638 | - semantic communication,
0639 | - sender recognition,
0640 | - channel capacity,
0641 | - message identity,
0642 | - coordinated behaviour,
0643 | - topology-specific information propagation,
0644 | - intention,
0645 | - agency,
0646 | - or life.
0647 | 
0648 | It establishes something narrower:
0649 | 
0650 | > **Digital Crystal dynamics can transmit causal perturbations and preserve coarse temporal structure while discarding finer information about exact chronology and source identity under the tested controls.**
0651 | 
0652 | That phenomenon now belongs in the project-wide phenomenon record independently of the chapter's failed sender-specific signalling hypothesis.
0653 | 
0654 | ---
0655 | 
0656 | ## Evidence Ledger
0657 | 
0658 | | Claim | Status | Evidence |
0659 | |---|---|---|
0660 | | A received one‑bit event can alter receiver morphology | **SUPPORTED** | 120 paired checkpoint interventions; 95.8% produced morphology divergence |
0661 | | The pulse reaches the actual Digital Crystal growth process | **SUPPORTED** | Intervention directly changes receiver forcing used by frozen growth rule |
0662 | | Real pulse timing differs from shuffled timing in receiver response | **SUPPORTED** | Real strongly outperformed shuffled control |
0663 | | Real pulse timing differs from rate‑matched random timing | **SUPPORTED** | Real strongly outperformed random timing |
0664 | | The actual sender matters more than another same‑class sender | **FAILED** | Count‑matched unrelated sender performed essentially the same |
0665 | | Exact chronology matters beyond the same inter‑pulse intervals | **FAILED** | IPI‑permutation surrogate performed essentially the same |
0666 | | Influence propagates specifically through the six‑crystal chain topology | **FAILED** | Shuffled‑edge control was nearly identical |
0667 | | Local 6×6 topology produces measurable organised signalling | **FAILED** | Real and shuffled boards were nearly identical |
0668 | | The channel carries semantics | **UNTESTED** | No semantic task was introduced |
0669 | | The crystals coordinate toward a shared objective | **UNTESTED** | No shared objective was introduced |
0670 | | Channel capacity has been measured | **UNTESTED** | No information‑theoretic capacity experiment was run |
0671 | | Digital Crystal life has been established | **UNTESTED** | This experiment addresses only primitive causal transmission |
0672 | 
0673 | ---
0674 | 
0675 | ## The Bounded Claim
0676 | 
0677 | The strongest claim supported by this chapter is:
0678 | 
0679 | > Within Digital Crystal v1, changing one received bit while holding receiver state, stochastic state and external forcing fixed can alter the receiver’s subsequent morphology. Real sender‑generated pulse timing produces stronger receiver relationships than shuffled or rate‑matched random timing, but it does not outperform count‑matched same‑class sender replay or an inter‑pulse‑interval‑preserving surrogate. This supports primitive causal transmission, not sender‑specific signalling.
0680 | 
0681 | That boundary matters.  
0682 | If we had stopped after the first positive result, we might have written “the crystals communicate.”  
0683 | After controls, the claim is much better:
0684 | 
0685 | > **The crystal can hear a pulse. It cannot yet tell who spoke.**
0686 | 
0687 | ---
0688 | 
0689 | ## The Next Question
0690 | 
0691 | Not: can we send more bits?  
0692 | But: what would make one pulse stream carry something specific about its source?
0693 | 
0694 | A channel that merely perturbs another process is not enough.  
0695 | To become signalling, something about the sender must survive transmission in a way the receiver can detect.
0696 | 
0697 | The pulse exists.  
0698 | The channel exists.  
0699 | The sender does not yet exist **in the signal**.
0700 | 
0701 | **Next, we ask how does the crystal respond to perturbation.**
```
