```markdown
0001 | +++
0002 | title = "17: How Does the Crystal Respond to Perturbation"
0003 | date = "2026-08-13T12:38:00+01:00"
0004 | draft = false
0005 | description = "A Digital Crystal has a causal past. Chapter 17 asks whether that past remains legible, and discovers that even the way randomness is held fixed changes the answer."
0006 | categories = ["Programming", "Artificial Life"]
0007 | tags = ["Digital Life", "Digital Crystal", "Causality", "Stochastic Systems", "Counterfactuals", "Memory", "Experiments"]
0008 | series = ["Digital Life From First Principles"]
0009 | +++
0010 | 
0011 | The crystal could hear a pulse.
0012 | 
0013 | That much we already knew.
0014 | 
0015 | In the previous experiment, a single received bit was inserted into the continuation of a Digital Crystal. Everything else was held as closely as possible to the same conditions.  
0016 | The resulting crystal changed.
0017 | 
0018 | Not metaphorically.  
0019 | Not because a classifier happened to prefer one label.  
0020 | The intervention altered the subsequent growth of the receiver.
0021 | 
0022 | That gave us one of the weakest but most useful forms of causality available to an experimental system:
0023 | 
0024 | > Change one thing. Hold the rest fixed. Observe a different future.
0025 | 
0026 | It was tempting to move immediately from there to communication.  
0027 | Could one crystal send a pattern to another?  
0028 | Could the receiver distinguish one temporal pattern from another?  
0029 | Could a sequence of pulses survive inside the crystal long enough to become information?
0030 | 
0031 | Those were attractive questions.  
0032 | They were also premature.
0033 | 
0034 | Before asking whether the crystal could preserve a message, we first needed to understand something simpler.
0035 | 
0036 | **What does a perturbation actually do to it?**
0037 | 
0038 | ---
0039 | 
0040 | ## A Past Is Not Yet a Memory
0041 | 
0042 | Suppose we tap a glass.  
0043 | The vibration changes the glass.  
0044 | For a short time, its future is different because we touched it.  
0045 | That does not mean the glass remembers being tapped.
0046 | 
0047 | The distinction matters.  
0048 | A system can be causally dependent on its history without containing any useful representation of that history.
0049 | 
0050 | A dropped stone depends on the height from which it fell.  
0051 | A cooling pan depends on its earlier temperature.  
0052 | A crack in a crystal depends on the force that produced it.
0053 | 
0054 | History is everywhere.  
0055 | Memory is a much stronger claim.
0056 | 
0057 | Our Digital Crystal already had a past. Chapter 15 established that its exact continuation depended on more than its visible morphology. A complete checkpoint could reproduce its future, while morphology alone could not.  
0058 | Chapter 16 then showed that an incoming pulse could change that future.
0059 | 
0060 | The next question was therefore not:
0061 | 
0062 | > Does the crystal have memory?
0063 | 
0064 | It was:
0065 | 
0066 | > **What happens to the consequence of a perturbation after it occurs?**
0067 | 
0068 | That is a smaller question.  
0069 | And that made it testable.
0070 | 
0071 | ```mermaid
0072 | flowchart LR
0073 |     subgraph Causal Dependence
0074 |     A[Perturbation] --> B[Causal Consequence]
0075 |     B --> C[Altered Future State]
0076 |     end
0077 |     subgraph Memory
0078 |     D[Perturbation] --> E[Persistent Internal Change]
0079 |     E --> F[Distinguishable State]
0080 |     F --> G[Future Behaviour Altered]
0081 |     end
0082 |     C -.- H[No requirement of legibility]
0083 |     G -.- I[Requires recoverable trace]
0084 | ```
0085 | 
0086 | ---
0087 | 
0088 | ## One Pulse, Two Futures
0089 | 
0090 | We began with a simple intervention.  
0091 | Grow a crystal to a checkpoint.  
0092 | Clone the checkpoint.  
0093 | Give one continuation a pulse.  
0094 | Give the other no pulse.
0095 | 
0096 | Then compare them after one step, two steps, four steps and beyond.
0097 | 
0098 | The original result looked dramatic.  
0099 | Immediately after the pulse, the two crystals differed only slightly.  
0100 | A few steps later, they were very different.
0101 | 
0102 | By roughly ten to sixteen updates after the intervention, the pulse branch was approaching the level of divergence we saw when two crystals were simply given independently seeded random continuations.
0103 | 
0104 | It looked as though a tiny event had triggered a rapidly expanding cascade.  
0105 | That seemed interesting.  
0106 | Perhaps the crystal amplified tiny events.  
0107 | Perhaps one pulse threw it onto a different developmental trajectory.  
0108 | Perhaps this was a primitive form of historical persistence.
0109 | 
0110 | But there was another possibility.  
0111 | The crystal itself might not have been doing all of the amplification.
0112 | 
0113 | **Our random-number generator might have been doing it for us.**
0114 | 
0115 | ---
0116 | 
0117 | ## The Random Numbers Were Part of the Experiment
0118 | 
0119 | The original Digital Crystal uses a conventional pseudorandom-number stream.  
0120 | At each growth step it constructs a frontier of possible cells.  
0121 | The frontier is sorted.  
0122 | Then each candidate consumes the next random value:
0123 | 
0124 | ```text
0125 | candidate 1 → random number 1
0126 | candidate 2 → random number 2
0127 | candidate 3 → random number 3
0128 | ...
0129 | ```
0130 | 
0131 | This is perfectly reproducible.  
0132 | If two crystal states are identical, the same cells encounter the same random values.
0133 | 
0134 | But now perturb one branch.  
0135 | A single extra attachment changes its frontier.  
0136 | That changes which cells appear in the sorted list.
0137 | 
0138 | Now random number 27 may be applied to a different cell in each branch.  
0139 | After the next difference, the streams misalign again.
0140 | 
0141 | Soon the two runs are no longer asking:
0142 | 
0143 | > What happens to the same cell under the same random opportunity?
0144 | 
0145 | They are asking:
0146 | 
0147 | > What happens to two increasingly different frontiers while each consumes the same sequential stream in a different way?
0148 | 
0149 | The distinction is subtle.  
0150 | It is also enormous.
0151 | 
0152 | We had treated identical initial random-number states as though they created identical random worlds.  
0153 | They did not.  
0154 | They created identical **streams**.
0155 | 
0156 | Once the two branches consumed those streams differently, the correspondence between their stochastic opportunities collapsed.  
0157 | That meant our apparent perturbation amplification could partly be an artifact of how we coupled the two counterfactual worlds.
0158 | 
0159 | ---
0160 | 
0161 | ## Holding Randomness Still
0162 | 
0163 | To test this, we built a second experimental runner.  
0164 | We did not replace the frozen Digital Crystal. The original sequential‑RNG model remained unchanged.
0165 | 
0166 | Instead, we introduced a different way of pairing randomness between two counterfactual runs.  
0167 | Each possible random event received its random value from:
0168 | 
0169 | ```text
0170 | seed
0171 | +
0172 | absolute step
0173 | +
0174 | cell coordinate
0175 | ```
0176 | 
0177 | Conceptually:
0178 | 
0179 | [
0180 | U(\text{seed}, \text{step}, q, r)
0181 | ]
0182 | 
0183 | A cell at coordinate `(q,r)` therefore sees the same random value at the same step in both branches.  
0184 | If the cell exists in one branch but not the other, only that opportunity differs.  
0185 | A different frontier somewhere else no longer shifts every subsequent random draw.
0186 | 
0187 | This is a common‑random‑number coupling.  
0188 | It does not make the system deterministic.  
0189 | It makes the comparison more precise.
0190 | 
0191 | Before relying on it, however, we had to check that we had not quietly created a different crystal.  
0192 | The confirmatory preflight compared the original sequential‑RNG implementation with the cell‑keyed runner.  
0193 | Both reproduced exactly when rerun.
0194 | 
0195 | Across 96 runs per implementation, the omnibus morphology comparison found no evidence of a gross distributional discrepancy (`p ≈ 0.922`).  
0196 | We also predeclared practical compatibility margins for population, maximum radius, attachment rate and covariance anisotropy.  
0197 | All four checks passed.
0198 | 
0199 | This did not prove that the two stochastic processes were mathematically identical. That was not the claim.  
0200 | It gave us enough evidence to use the keyed system as a counterfactual coupling without pretending that it had become the canonical substrate.
0201 | 
0202 | Then we repeated the pulse experiment.
0203 | 
0204 | ---
0205 | 
0206 | ## The Divergence Collapsed
0207 | 
0208 | The result was immediate.
0209 | 
0210 | Under the original sequential RNG, a pulse branch rapidly approached the spread produced by independent stochastic continuations.  
0211 | Under cell‑keyed common random numbers, it did not.
0212 | 
0213 | The intervention still caused a real difference.  
0214 | But the difference remained much smaller.
0215 | 
0216 | In the exploratory run, by elapsed step 20 the sequentially coupled pulse branch had reached about 87% of the symmetric‑difference scale of independently reseeded continuations.  
0217 | Under common random numbers, it remained at only about 11%.
0218 | 
0219 | ```mermaid
0220 | flowchart TD
0221 |     subgraph Sequential RNG
0222 |     A1[Pulse branch] --> B1[Frontier changes]
0223 |     B1 --> C1[Stream misaligns]
0224 |     C1 --> D1[Large apparent divergence\<br/>~87% of independent scale]
0225 |     end
0226 |     subgraph Common Random Numbers
0227 |     A2[Pulse branch] --> B2[Frontier changes]
0228 |     B2 --> C2[Same cell sees same draw]
0229 |     C2 --> D2[Small residual divergence\<br/>~11% of independent scale]
0230 |     end
0231 |     D1 -.-> E[Much of the cascade was\<br/>coupling artifact]
0232 |     D2 -.-> F[True causal effect is\<br/>substantially smaller]
0233 | ```
0234 | 
0235 | The pulse had not stopped mattering.  
0236 | What disappeared was most of the apparent explosion of divergence.
0237 | 
0238 | This changed the interpretation of the earlier experiment.  
0239 | We could no longer say:
0240 | 
0241 | > The crystal rapidly forgets which branch it is on.
0242 | 
0243 | What we had actually established was:
0244 | 
0245 | > **Pathwise divergence depends strongly on how randomness is coupled between counterfactual branches.**
0246 | 
0247 | That is not a minor statistical detail.  
0248 | It changes what a counterfactual trajectory means in a stochastic computational system.
0249 | 
0250 | Two treatment distributions may be identical regardless of how we pair their randomness.  
0251 | But the apparent distance between one particular treated world and one particular untreated world can depend enormously on that pairing.
0252 | 
0253 | The intervention was real.  
0254 | The size of its pathwise aftermath was not coupling‑invariant.
0255 | 
0256 | ---
0257 | 
0258 | ## The Additivity Surprise
0259 | 
0260 | This correction also forced us to revisit another apparent result.
0261 | 
0262 | Earlier experiments had suggested that four pulses interacted nonlinearly.  
0263 | We measured the average response to each isolated pulse.  
0264 | Then we added those responses together.
0265 | 
0266 | The predicted response to the four‑pulse sequence differed substantially from the observed response.  
0267 | The relative error looked large.  
0268 | That could have meant that the crystal integrated its input history in some nonlinear way.
0269 | 
0270 | But after fixing the randomness coupling, we added another control.  
0271 | We estimated the size of a mean feature difference that could appear even when comparing two finite samples from the same no‑pulse population.  
0272 | That gave us a measurement‑noise floor.
0273 | 
0274 | At the first matched observation, this floor was approximately `0.045`.  
0275 | The superposition residuals were only around `0.007`.
0276 | 
0277 | The discrepancy that had previously looked mechanistically interesting was substantially smaller than ordinary uncertainty in estimating a population mean at this sample size.
0278 | 
0279 | The actual and predicted response vectors were also well aligned.  
0280 | The earlier claim therefore did not survive the better control.
0281 | 
0282 | We did **not** establish nonlinear integration.  
0283 | Instead, the more defensible statement became:
0284 | 
0285 | > Within the resolution of this experiment, the average multi‑pulse response remained compatible with the sum of the measured isolated‑pulse responses.
0286 | 
0287 | That was a failure of an interesting hypothesis.  
0288 | It was also progress.  
0289 | The control removed something we had been in danger of inventing.
0290 | 
0291 | ---
0292 | 
0293 | ## Now Ask About History
0294 | 
0295 | With the perturbation dynamics better understood, we could finally return to the question that had motivated the chapter.
0296 | 
0297 | Can two different temporal histories leave distinguishable present states?
0298 | 
0299 | The obvious comparison would be weak.  
0300 | Consider:
0301 | 
0302 | ```text
0303 | 11110000
0304 | ```
0305 | 
0306 | and:
0307 | 
0308 | ```text
0309 | 10010010
0310 | ```
0311 | 
0312 | Even if both contain the same number of pulses, their final pulses occur at different times.  
0313 | A classifier could distinguish the crystals simply because one had been perturbed more recently.  
0314 | That would be recency detection.  
0315 | Not temporal‑history retention.
0316 | 
0317 | So we constructed two shorter sequences:
0318 | 
0319 | ```text
0320 | A = 11100001
0321 | B = 10001101
0322 | ```
0323 | 
0324 | Their pulse positions were:
0325 | 
0326 | ```text
0327 | A = {0, 1, 2, 7}
0328 | B = {0, 4, 5, 7}
0329 | ```
0330 | 
0331 | They had the same number of pulses.  
0332 | The same first pulse.  
0333 | The same last pulse.  
0334 | Only the interior arrangement differed.
0335 | 
0336 | The first measurement occurred immediately after the final pulse.  
0337 | Further measurements followed one, two and four steps later.  
0338 | This placed the experiment inside the coherent response window rather than waiting until the stochastic trajectories had largely decorrelated.
0339 | 
0340 | The final confirmation used 48 independently generated receiver checkpoints.  
0341 | The counterfactual coupling was frozen in advance.  
0342 | The codewords were frozen.  
0343 | The primary endpoint was frozen at step 8.  
0344 | The primary measurement was frozen as a regularized paired multivariate statistic applied to a nine‑feature angular morphology subspace.  
0345 | Secondary endpoints and the larger 24‑feature measurement were allowed to be recorded.  
0346 | They were not allowed to rescue the primary experiment.
0347 | 
0348 | That rule mattered.  
0349 | Without it, every negative result could become another invitation to search.
0350 | 
0351 | ---
0352 | 
0353 | ## Different Futures
0354 | 
0355 | The two pulse histories did not produce identical crystals.
0356 | 
0357 | Immediately after the complete sequence, their average normalized symmetric difference was approximately `0.053`, with a bootstrap interval of approximately `[0.048, 0.059]`.
0358 | 
0359 | So changing the interior timing changed particular futures.  
0360 | That was real.
0361 | 
0362 | But particular difference was not the claim we were testing.  
0363 | The question was whether one temporal arrangement left a **consistent morphological signature** across the population.
0364 | 
0365 | The answer was no.
0366 | 
0367 | The frozen primary angular test returned `p = 0.7366`.  
0368 | Its observed statistic was not hovering just below some significance threshold.  
0369 | It was below the average statistic generated by the permutation null.
0370 | 
0371 | The secondary 24‑feature test was even less suggestive: `p = 0.9320`.
0372 | 
0373 | The later observations did not reveal a hidden delayed effect.  
0374 | The angular test remained unconvincing at every measured secondary endpoint.  
0375 | There was no plausible story in which we had simply measured one step too early.
0376 | 
0377 | The predeclared experiment had failed.  
0378 | Not the software.  
0379 | Not the preflight.  
0380 | Not the random‑number coupling.  
0381 | The hypothesis.
0382 | 
0383 | ---
0384 | 
0385 | ## What Failed?
0386 | 
0387 | Precision matters especially when a result is negative.
0388 | 
0389 | We did **not** establish that temporal arrangement can never matter.  
0390 | We did not test every sequence, every timescale, every observable, every crystal rule.
0391 | 
0392 | We also did not establish that the two histories had no causal consequences.  
0393 | They plainly produced different particular states.
0394 | 
0395 | What failed was this specific claim:
0396 | 
0397 | > **Under the frozen Digital Crystal protocol, changing the interior timing of four pulses while holding pulse count, onset and offset fixed did not produce a reproducible population‑level morphology signature detectable by the predeclared angular measurement at the primary endpoint.**
0398 | 
0399 | The formal status was therefore: **FAILED**.  
0400 | Not untested. Not inconclusive. Failed within the declared experimental scope.
0401 | 
0402 | That distinction is one of the reasons for doing the experiment this way.  
0403 | A negative result becomes scientifically useful only when we have made it difficult to escape.
0404 | 
0405 | ---
0406 | 
0407 | ## Did the Crystal Forget?
0408 | 
0409 | There is a tempting sentence:
0410 | 
0411 | > The crystal forgot the sequence.
0412 | 
0413 | We cannot quite say that.  
0414 | Forgetting assumes there was something equivalent to memory in the first place.
0415 | 
0416 | What we can say is stranger.  
0417 | The past mattered. Different histories produced different particular futures.  
0418 | But we could not reliably recover which history occurred from the morphology we measured.
0419 | 
0420 | So:
0421 | 
0422 | > **A history can contribute causally to the present without remaining legible in the present.**
0423 | 
0424 | ```mermaid
0425 | flowchart LR
0426 |     A[History A\<br/>11100001] --> C[Particular future A]
0427 |     B[History B\<br/>10001101] --> D[Particular future B]
0428 |     C -.-> E[Futures are causally different]
0429 |     D -.-> E
0430 |     E --> F{Legible difference\<br/>in morphology?}
0431 |     F -->|No| G[Causal consequences present\<br/>but not recoverable]
0432 | ```
0433 | 
0434 | That distinction turns out to be central.  
0435 | The crystal has history. But history is not automatically state.  
0436 | It has causal consequences. But causal consequences are not automatically recoverable information.  
0437 | It can be changed. But being changed is not the same as remembering what changed it.
0438 | 
0439 | ---
0440 | 
0441 | ## Why This Matters for Digital Life
0442 | 
0443 | At first glance, this looks like a dead end.  
0444 | We want digital life. We built a primitive growing process. We perturbed it.  
0445 | We asked whether its present retained something distinguishable about that past.  
0446 | And the confirmatory experiment failed.
0447 | 
0448 | But this is exactly the kind of failure we need.
0449 | 
0450 | The goal of this project is not to assemble a creature by giving classes biological names.  
0451 | We are trying to discover which mechanisms become necessary.
0452 | 
0453 | A living organism carries its history forward in many ways.  
0454 | Damage changes later behaviour.  
0455 | Chemical concentrations alter future reactions.  
0456 | Development constrains later development.  
0457 | Past environments alter regulatory state.  
0458 | Mutations persist into descendants.
0459 | 
0460 | None of those mechanisms are present simply because “history happened.”  
0461 | They require physical state that survives long enough to matter.
0462 | 
0463 | Our crystal has exposed the same distinction in a much simpler setting.  
0464 | The growth process is history‑dependent.  
0465 | But the morphology we have built does not reliably expose the internal temporal arrangement of recent perturbations.
0466 | 
0467 | That tells us something concrete about the next design.  
0468 | We should not add a variable called `memory` and declare the problem solved.
0469 | 
0470 | We should ask:
0471 | 
0472 | > **What is the smallest additional mechanism by which a past event can alter a future event in a way that remains distinguishable?**
0473 | 
0474 | That mechanism might be local.  
0475 | Perhaps a recently attached cell changes the probability of neighbouring attachment for several steps.  
0476 | Perhaps cells can occupy more than one persistent internal state.  
0477 | Perhaps exposure changes a local threshold.  
0478 | Perhaps an event leaves a slowly decaying field.  
0479 | Perhaps the growth material itself becomes modified.
0480 | 
0481 | We do not yet know.  
0482 | That is the experiment.
0483 | 
0484 | ---
0485 | 
0486 | ## Not a Memory Register
0487 | 
0488 | The easiest implementation would be:
0489 | 
0490 | ```python
0491 | state.last_signal = signal
0492 | ```
0493 | 
0494 | That would work.  
0495 | It would also teach us almost nothing.
0496 | 
0497 | The challenge is not to store information in Python.  
0498 | Computers already do that extremely well.
0499 | 
0500 | The challenge is to discover the equivalent of a **material consequence** inside the digital substrate.  
0501 | We want a mechanism where history persists because the system’s own dynamics make it persist.  
0502 | Not because an external programmer created a history variable.
0503 | 
0504 | The distinction is the same one we have followed throughout this project.  
0505 | Do not build an animal.  
0506 | Find the digital aerodynamics.
0507 | 
0508 | ---
0509 | 
0510 | ## What Survived the Hypothesis?
0511 | 
0512 | The confirmatory history-signature hypothesis failed.
0513 | 
0514 | The two matched pulse histories:
0515 | 
0516 | ```text
0517 | A = 11100001
0518 | B = 10001101
0519 | ```
0520 | 
0521 | produced different particular futures.
0522 | 
0523 | Immediately after the sequence, their average normalized symmetric difference was about:
0524 | 
0525 | ```text
0526 | 0.053
0527 | ```
0528 | 
0529 | So temporal arrangement had a real causal consequence.
0530 | 
0531 | But the frozen population-level test asked something stronger:
0532 | 
0533 | > Does one temporal arrangement leave a reproducible morphological signature that can be distinguished across independently generated receiver checkpoints?
0534 | 
0535 | The answer was no.
0536 | 
0537 | The primary angular statistic returned:
0538 | 
0539 | ```text
0540 | p = 0.7366
0541 | ```
0542 | 
0543 | and the secondary 24-feature statistic returned:
0544 | 
0545 | ```text
0546 | p = 0.9320
0547 | ```
0548 | 
0549 | So the chapter leaves us with a crucial separation:
0550 | 
0551 | ```text
0552 | DIFFERENT HISTORY
0553 | → DIFFERENT PARTICULAR FUTURE
0554 | SUPPORTED
0555 | 
0556 | DIFFERENT HISTORY
0557 | → STABLE POPULATION-LEVEL SIGNATURE
0558 | FAILED
0559 | ```
0560 | 
0561 | The causal effect survived.
0562 | 
0563 | The readable signature did not.
0564 | 
0565 | ### Phenomenon record
0566 | 
0567 | **Phenomenon:** Causal history without recoverable signature
0568 | 
0569 | **Status:** **SUPPORTED**
0570 | 
0571 | **Current bounded description:**
0572 | 
0573 | > Different recent pulse histories can causally produce different particular Digital Crystal futures while failing to produce a reproducible population-level morphology signature under the frozen measurement protocol.
0574 | 
0575 | This gives us an important substrate distinction:
0576 | 
0577 | ```text
0578 | CAUSAL DIFFERENCE
0579 | ≠
0580 | SYSTEMATIC SIGNATURE
0581 | ```
0582 | 
0583 | and:
0584 | 
0585 | ```text
0586 | PAST CONTRIBUTED TO PRESENT
0587 | ≠
0588 | PAST IS LEGIBLE IN PRESENT
0589 | ```
0590 | 
0591 | That extends the Lossy-History Principle from Chapters 14 and 16.
0592 | 
0593 | Chapter 14 showed that coarse forcing family survives more readily than exact temporal order.
0594 | 
0595 | Chapter 16 showed that coarse pulse-stream structure matters more than sender identity or exact interval chronology.
0596 | 
0597 | Chapter 17 now shows that even when two temporal arrangements causally alter particular futures, those differences need not organize themselves into a stable readable signature across a population.
0598 | 
0599 | The hierarchy is becoming clearer:
0600 | 
0601 | ```text
0602 | causal consequence
0603 |         cheap
0604 | 
0605 | persistent consequence
0606 |         harder
0607 | 
0608 | systematic signature
0609 |         harder still
0610 | 
0611 | recoverable information
0612 |         harder still
0613 | ```
0614 | 
0615 | ### A second phenomenon: counterfactual coupling matters
0616 | 
0617 | Chapter 17 also exposed a different and extremely important effect.
0618 | 
0619 | Under sequential RNG coupling, a tiny perturbation caused the two branches to consume the shared random stream differently.
0620 | 
0621 | That made:
0622 | 
0623 | ```text
0624 | same random stream
0625 | ```
0626 | 
0627 | look like:
0628 | 
0629 | ```text
0630 | same random world
0631 | ```
0632 | 
0633 | when those are not equivalent.
0634 | 
0635 | Once a perturbation altered the frontier, random draws became reassigned to different cells and large apparent divergence followed.
0636 | 
0637 | With cell-keyed common random numbers, the same cell at the same step received the same random opportunity in both branches.
0638 | 
0639 | The apparent cascade then collapsed dramatically:
0640 | 
0641 | ```text
0642 | sequential RNG
0643 | ~87% of independent-divergence scale
0644 | 
0645 | cell-keyed CRN
0646 | ~11% of independent-divergence scale
0647 | ```
0648 | 
0649 | The intervention remained causal.
0650 | 
0651 | What changed was the measured pathwise aftermath.
0652 | 
0653 | So:
0654 | 
0655 | ```text
0656 | CAUSAL EFFECT
0657 | ≠
0658 | COUPLING-INVARIANT PATHWISE DIVERGENCE
0659 | ```
0660 | 
0661 | ### Phenomenon record
0662 | 
0663 | **Phenomenon:** Coupling-dependent counterfactual divergence
0664 | 
0665 | **Status:** **SUPPORTED**
0666 | 
0667 | **Current bounded description:**
0668 | 
0669 | > In a stochastic Digital Crystal, the measured pathwise distance between treated and untreated counterfactual branches depends strongly on how stochastic opportunities are coupled between those branches.
0670 | 
0671 | This is not a detail about one random-number generator.
0672 | 
0673 | It is a general experimental warning for stochastic computational systems:
0674 | 
0675 | > **A counterfactual comparison is partly defined by how shared randomness is aligned across possible worlds.**
0676 | 
0677 | That means future experiments must distinguish:
0678 | 
0679 | ```text
0680 | MARGINAL EFFECT
0681 | average difference between treatment distributions
0682 | 
0683 | PAIRED EFFECT
0684 | difference under a declared coupling
0685 | 
0686 | PATHWISE DIVERGENCE
0687 | distance between particular paired trajectories
0688 | ```
0689 | 
0690 | Those are different scientific objects.
0691 | 
0692 | ### The failed nonlinearity result belongs here too
0693 | 
0694 | An apparent multi-pulse nonlinearity also disappeared after introducing a measurement-noise floor.
0695 | 
0696 | The superposition residual was about:
0697 | 
0698 | ```text
0699 | 0.007
0700 | ```
0701 | 
0702 | while the finite-sample measurement floor was about:
0703 | 
0704 | ```text
0705 | 0.045
0706 | ```
0707 | 
0708 | So the putative interaction was smaller than ordinary uncertainty in estimating the mean response.
0709 | 
0710 | Again:
0711 | 
0712 | ```text
0713 | OBSERVED DISCREPANCY
0714 | ≠
0715 | RESOLVED MECHANISTIC NONLINEARITY
0716 | ```
0717 | 
0718 | The better bounded conclusion is that the measured multi-pulse response remained compatible with the sum of isolated-pulse responses at the resolution of this experiment.
0719 | 
0720 | ### What this phenomenon does not establish
0721 | 
0722 | The surviving phenomena do **not** establish:
0723 | 
0724 | - memory,
0725 | - learning,
0726 | - temporal decoding,
0727 | - nonlinear integration,
0728 | - universal perturbation amplification,
0729 | - or a coupling-independent measure of causal distance.
0730 | 
0731 | They establish something more useful:
0732 | 
0733 | > **A causal past is cheap. A persistent, systematic and recoverable consequence of that past is a separate property that must be demonstrated.**
0734 | 
0735 | And they add a methodological rule:
0736 | 
0737 | > **In stochastic systems, counterfactual path distance is meaningful only relative to an explicit stochastic coupling.**
0738 | 
0739 | Both should now be tracked independently of the failed temporal-signature hypothesis.
0740 | 
0741 | ---
0742 | 
0743 | ## What Chapter 17 Gave Us
0744 | 
0745 | The experiment began with a simple question:
0746 | 
0747 | > How does the crystal respond to perturbation?
0748 | 
0749 | It ended by separating several things that initially looked like one phenomenon.
0750 | 
0751 | - A pulse can causally change a future.
0752 | - A stochastic counterfactual can look dramatically different depending on how randomness is coupled.
0753 | - An apparent failure of additivity can disappear once measurement noise is controlled.
0754 | - Two different histories can produce different particular trajectories without producing a stable population‑level signature.
0755 | - And history dependence does not automatically give us a usable past.
0756 | 
0757 | Those are not failures of the project.  
0758 | They are constraints on any digital life we eventually claim to have built.
0759 | 
0760 | The evidence now supports a sharper progression:
0761 | 
0762 | ```mermaid
0763 | flowchart TD
0764 |     A[PERTURBATION] --> B[CAUSAL CONSEQUENCE]
0765 |     B --> C[PERSISTENT INTERNAL CONSEQUENCE]
0766 |     C --> D[DISTINGUISHABLE STATE]
0767 |     D --> E[USE OF THAT STATE]
0768 |     E --> F[ONLY THEN: MEMORY?]
0769 |     style A fill:#ccffcc,stroke:#333
0770 |     style B fill:#ccffcc,stroke:#333
0771 |     style C fill:#fff5cc,stroke:#333
0772 |     style D fill:#ffe0cc,stroke:#333
0773 |     style E fill:#ffcccc,stroke:#333
0774 |     style F fill:#ffcccc,stroke:#333
0775 | ```
0776 | 
0777 | We have reached the second step.  
0778 | The third remains open.
0779 | 
0780 | ---
0781 | 
0782 | ## The Boundary
0783 | 
0784 | The Digital Crystal reacts.  
0785 | It is changed by what happens to it.  
0786 | Its future depends on its past.
0787 | 
0788 | But within the experiment we just completed, the exact internal arrangement of that past did not become a reliably readable property of its later morphology.
0789 | 
0790 | That is the boundary of the current system.  
0791 | We should not push language past it.
0792 | 
0793 | Whatever the crystal has, we have not earned the word memory.  
0794 | What it has given us is something more useful:
0795 | 
0796 | **a reason to build the next mechanism.**
0797 | 
0798 | The next question is no longer:
0799 | 
0800 | > Can we decode yesterday from today’s crystal?
0801 | 
0802 | It is:
0803 | 
0804 | > **What is the smallest piece of digital matter that can be changed by experience, keep that change, and allow the retained change to alter what happens next?**
0805 | 
0806 | That would not yet be life.  
0807 | But it would be one mechanism closer.
```

```markdown
0001 | +++
0002 | title = "18: Can Experience Change the Material?"
0003 | date = "2026-08-12T16:40:00+01:00"
0004 | draft = false
0005 | description = "A pulse can leave a permanent mark in a Digital Crystal. Chapter 18 asks the harder question: when does a persistent mark remain causally usable?"
0006 | categories = ["Programming", "Artificial Life"]
0007 | tags = ["Digital Life", "Digital Crystal", "Causality", "Material State", "Path Dependence", "Experiments"]
0008 | series = ["Digital Life From First Principles"]
0009 | +++
0010 | 
0011 | The crystal had a past.
0012 | 
0013 | But we had learned to be careful with that sentence.
0014 | 
0015 | In the previous chapter, a perturbation changed the crystal's future. Under a carefully controlled counterfactual comparison, the consequence of a pulse could remain locally visible for several updates.
0016 | 
0017 | That was causality.
0018 | 
0019 | It was not memory.
0020 | 
0021 | The distinction left us with a smaller question.
0022 | 
0023 | Not:
0024 | 
0025 | > **Can the crystal remember?**
0026 | 
0027 | But:
0028 | 
0029 | > **Can experience change the material?**
0030 | 
0031 | That sounds almost trivial.
0032 | 
0033 | Of course software can change a variable.
0034 | 
0035 | We could create:
0036 | 
0037 | ```text
0038 | memory = 1
0039 | ```
0040 | 
0041 | after a pulse and declare victory.
0042 | 
0043 | But then the answer would have been written into the architecture by us.
0044 | 
0045 | The experiment would tell us nothing.
0046 | 
0047 | We wanted something weaker and more difficult:
0048 | 
0049 | > **What is the smallest local change produced by experience that can persist and alter what the crystal does later?**
0050 | 
0051 | So we added almost nothing.
0052 | 
0053 | ---
0054 | 
0055 | ## One More Kind of Cell
0056 | 
0057 | Until now, the Digital Crystal needed only two material conditions:
0058 | 
0059 | ```text
0060 | empty
0061 | occupied
0062 | ```
0063 | 
0064 | For this experiment we allowed an occupied cell to have one additional local state:
0065 | 
0066 | ```text
0067 | empty
0068 | occupied-normal
0069 | occupied-modified
0070 | ```
0071 | 
0072 | A pulse could modify some cells near the active boundary.
0073 | 
0074 | That was all.
0075 | 
0076 | There was no history list.
0077 | 
0078 | No global memory register.
0079 | 
0080 | No timestamp saying when the pulse happened.
0081 | 
0082 | No stored copy of the signal.
0083 | 
0084 | No target morphology.
0085 | 
0086 | No learned weight.
0087 | 
0088 | No module called `memory`.
0089 | 
0090 | The modification simply changed one local fact about the material.
0091 | 
0092 | A modified occupied neighbour could slightly increase the attachment probability of a nearby frontier cell.
0093 | 
0094 | The proposed mechanism was therefore:
0095 | 
0096 | ```text
0097 | experience
0098 | ↓
0099 | local material change
0100 | ↓
0101 | change persists
0102 | ↓
0103 | later growth encounters changed material
0104 | ↓
0105 | future attachment probability changes
0106 | ```
0107 | 
0108 | If even this failed, that would be useful.
0109 | 
0110 | If it succeeded, we would still have to ask exactly what had succeeded.
0111 | 
0112 | ---
0113 | 
0114 | ## The First Result Looked Like Success
0115 | 
0116 | The pulse worked.
0117 | 
0118 | It wrote modified cells into the crystal.
0119 | 
0120 | Those cells stayed modified.
0121 | 
0122 | After the pulse disappeared, the state remained.
0123 | 
0124 | We had created a persistent consequence of experience.
0125 | 
0126 | It was tempting to stop there.
0127 | 
0128 | The crystal had been changed by its past.
0129 | 
0130 | The change remained physically present.
0131 | 
0132 | Surely that was the beginning of memory.
0133 | 
0134 | Then we performed the obvious ablation.
0135 | 
0136 | Take an experienced crystal.
0137 | 
0138 | At some later checkpoint, erase only the modified labels while leaving the visible occupied geometry unchanged.
0139 | 
0140 | Then continue both versions under the same future conditions.
0141 | 
0142 | ```text
0143 | experienced + labels retained
0144 |                 \
0145 |                  → continue
0146 | 
0147 | experienced + labels erased
0148 |                 /
0149 | ```
0150 | 
0151 | The result was brutal.
0152 | 
0153 | The two futures were identical.
0154 | 
0155 | Not approximately identical.
0156 | 
0157 | For the late ablation we tested, the retained material state made no detectable difference at all.
0158 | 
0159 | The state was still there.
0160 | 
0161 | But the dynamics could no longer reach it.
0162 | 
0163 | That gave us the first important result of the chapter:
0164 | 
0165 | > **A persistent state does not automatically become a usable state.**
0166 | 
0167 | Or more compactly:
0168 | 
0169 | ```text
0170 | PERSISTENCE
0171 | ≠
0172 | CAUSAL ACCESSIBILITY
0173 | ```
0174 | 
0175 | The crystal had not forgotten because the modified cells disappeared.
0176 | 
0177 | They had not disappeared.
0178 | 
0179 | They had become irrelevant.
0180 | 
0181 | ---
0182 | 
0183 | ## The Moving Aperture
0184 | 
0185 | The reason was geometric.
0186 | 
0187 | The Digital Crystal grows outward.
0188 | 
0189 | New cells attach around the current boundary.
0190 | 
0191 | Cells that were once on that boundary are gradually surrounded by newer material.
0192 | 
0193 | A modified cell may therefore remain encoded forever while moving farther and farther away from every location where a new construction decision is being made.
0194 | 
0195 | Imagine painting a mark onto wet concrete while a wall is being built.
0196 | 
0197 | The mark remains in the wall.
0198 | 
0199 | But once ten new layers have been built over it, the bricklayer no longer encounters it.
0200 | 
0201 | The information did not decay.
0202 | 
0203 | **Access did.**
0204 | 
0205 | So we stopped asking only how many modified cells survived.
0206 | 
0207 | We measured where they were.
0208 | 
0209 | At each update we asked:
0210 | 
0211 | ```text
0212 | How many modified cells remain?
0213 | How many are still on the boundary?
0214 | How many current frontier sites have a modified neighbour?
0215 | What fraction of active construction can still encounter modified material?
0216 | ```
0217 | 
0218 | Immediately after the experience, the modified material was exposed.
0219 | 
0220 | A few updates later, exposure collapsed.
0221 | 
0222 | By the later observation points, most or all of the modified state had been buried.
0223 | 
0224 | The apparent mystery from the first experiment disappeared.
0225 | 
0226 | The state had persisted exactly as designed.
0227 | 
0228 | It simply no longer occupied the crystal's **causal aperture**.
0229 | 
0230 | The active frontier is not merely the edge of a shape.
0231 | 
0232 | It is the part of the material from which the present can still influence what gets constructed next.
0233 | 
0234 | Inside the aperture:
0235 | 
0236 | ```text
0237 | state
0238 | → probability
0239 | → construction
0240 | ```
0241 | 
0242 | Outside it:
0243 | 
0244 | ```text
0245 | state
0246 | → nothing currently reads it
0247 | ```
0248 | 
0249 | The Digital Crystal had abundant storage.
0250 | 
0251 | What it lacked was access.
0252 | 
0253 | ---
0254 | 
0255 | ## Persistence Was the Wrong Scarcity
0256 | 
0257 | This is a very digital result.
0258 | 
0259 | We often assume that memory is primarily a storage problem.
0260 | 
0261 | Bits decay.
0262 | 
0263 | Biological tissue degrades.
0264 | 
0265 | A physical record has to survive long enough to be useful.
0266 | 
0267 | But our material state could survive indefinitely.
0268 | 
0269 | That did not help.
0270 | 
0271 | The scarce resource was not persistence.
0272 | 
0273 | It was **causal contact**.
0274 | 
0275 | ```text
0276 | storage can remain
0277 | while
0278 | causal access disappears
0279 | ```
0280 | 
0281 | That changes the design question.
0282 | 
0283 | If a past event is to matter later, it is not enough to preserve its trace.
0284 | 
0285 | The trace has to remain somewhere the current dynamics can use.
0286 | 
0287 | So our next intervention was obvious.
0288 | 
0289 | What if the modified state could move outward with the growing crystal?
0290 | 
0291 | ---
0292 | 
0293 | ## Propagation Is Not Enough Either
0294 | 
0295 | We allowed newly attached cells to acquire the modified state when they grew beside modified material.
0296 | 
0297 | Now the mark could spread through subsequent construction.
0298 | 
0299 | Again, the first result looked encouraging.
0300 | 
0301 | The number of modified cells increased.
0302 | 
0303 | The state persisted for longer.
0304 | 
0305 | But the same failure returned.
0306 | 
0307 | Most of the propagated material was still eventually buried.
0308 | 
0309 | We had improved the transport of the state without solving the access problem.
0310 | 
0311 | Another distinction appeared:
0312 | 
0313 | ```text
0314 | STATE PERSISTS
0315 | ≠
0316 | STATE PROPAGATES
0317 | ≠
0318 | STATE REMAINS ACCESSIBLE
0319 | ```
0320 | 
0321 | Propagation was not maintenance.
0322 | 
0323 | A process can copy a historical state repeatedly and still copy it into places where it stops mattering.
0324 | 
0325 | This was becoming less like a storage problem and more like a spatial transport problem.
0326 | 
0327 | Where the state went mattered at least as much as whether it was copied.
0328 | 
0329 | Before changing the transport rule, however, we needed to establish one more thing.
0330 | 
0331 | Perhaps the entire local material mechanism was too weak to matter even when exposed.
0332 | 
0333 | So we audited it directly.
0334 | 
0335 | ---
0336 | 
0337 | ## Close the Causal Chain
0338 | 
0339 | For every candidate attachment site at the frontier, we can compute its attachment probability with the material effect and without it.
0340 | 
0341 | That gives a local probability difference:
0342 | 
0343 | ```text
0344 | Δp
0345 | ```
0346 | 
0347 | But a changed probability is not the same as a changed event.
0348 | 
0349 | If the attachment probability changes from:
0350 | 
0351 | ```text
0352 | 0.510 → 0.515
0353 | ```
0354 | 
0355 | and the random draw is:
0356 | 
0357 | ```text
0358 | 0.900
0359 | ```
0360 | 
0361 | nothing changes.
0362 | 
0363 | The cell remains empty in both worlds.
0364 | 
0365 | If the same probability change meets a draw of:
0366 | 
0367 | ```text
0368 | 0.512
0369 | ```
0370 | 
0371 | then the counterfactual branches disagree:
0372 | 
0373 | ```text
0374 | without modified neighbour: no attachment
0375 | with modified neighbour: attachment
0376 | ```
0377 | 
0378 | That is a realized causal flip.
0379 | 
0380 | The whole local chain is therefore:
0381 | 
0382 | ```text
0383 | MODIFIED STATE PRESENT
0384 | ↓
0385 | STATE REACHES ACTIVE FRONTIER
0386 | ↓
0387 | LOCAL ATTACHMENT PROBABILITY CHANGES
0388 | ↓
0389 | RANDOM DRAW CROSSES DECISION BOUNDARY
0390 | ↓
0391 | ACTUAL CONSTRUCTION EVENT CHANGES
0392 | ```
0393 | 
0394 | We measured every level.
0395 | 
0396 | When enough modified material remained adjacent to the frontier, the gain mechanism was not merely decorative.
0397 | 
0398 | It changed attachment probabilities.
0399 | 
0400 | And some of those changes crossed the stochastic decision boundary and changed which cells were actually built.
0401 | 
0402 | That closed an important causal gap.
0403 | 
0404 | The problem was not that the state lacked causal power.
0405 | 
0406 | The problem was keeping it where that power could still be exercised.
0407 | 
0408 | ---
0409 | 
0410 | ## Where the Past Lives
0411 | 
0412 | So we tried changing only the placement of propagated state.
0413 | 
0414 | Suppose a newly attached cell is eligible to acquire the modified condition.
0415 | 
0416 | We can prefer cells that will be relatively buried.
0417 | 
0418 | Or choose among eligible cells without a surface preference.
0419 | 
0420 | Or prefer cells with greater outward exposure.
0421 | 
0422 | That gives three policies:
0423 | 
0424 | ```text
0425 | INTERIOR-BIASED
0426 | RANDOM
0427 | SURFACE-BIASED
0428 | ```
0429 | 
0430 | The surface policy produced an immediately striking result.
0431 | 
0432 | Modified state remained near the active construction region for much longer.
0433 | 
0434 | It continued to generate more frontier exposure, more probability leverage and more realized construction differences.
0435 | 
0436 | For a moment, this looked like the result we wanted.
0437 | 
0438 | Then we found the confound.
0439 | 
0440 | Surface placement did not merely place the same amount of state better.
0441 | 
0442 | By keeping more modified state near active construction, it created more opportunities for later cells to become modified.
0443 | 
0444 | That meant:
0445 | 
0446 | ```text
0447 | surface placement
0448 | ↓
0449 | more accessible modified material
0450 | ↓
0451 | more eligible propagation opportunities
0452 | ↓
0453 | more actual propagation
0454 | ↓
0455 | still more accessible material
0456 | ```
0457 | 
0458 | The surface branch was not only placing copies differently.
0459 | 
0460 | It was making **more copies**.
0461 | 
0462 | So we had not yet learned whether placement mattered.
0463 | 
0464 | Perhaps quantity alone explained everything.
0465 | 
0466 | This is exactly why controls matter.
0467 | 
0468 | The most exciting run in the experiment was not yet evidence for the claim we wanted.
0469 | 
0470 | ---
0471 | 
0472 | ## Hold the Amount Fixed
0473 | 
0474 | We rebuilt the comparison.
0475 | 
0476 | At every propagation step, all three branches were forced to transmit exactly the same number of modified cells.
0477 | 
0478 | The controller looked across the branches, found a shared feasible copy budget, and applied that same budget everywhere.
0479 | 
0480 | ```text
0481 | same checkpoint
0482 | same environment
0483 | same amount copied
0484 | same number of propagation events
0485 | different placement only
0486 | ```
0487 | 
0488 | Now the intervention was clean:
0489 | 
0490 | > **Where does the same amount of historical state go?**
0491 | 
0492 | The first exact-budget experiment used a late endpoint.
0493 | 
0494 | It failed.
0495 | 
0496 | At the predeclared late measurement, the predicted ordering was not established.
0497 | 
0498 | We did not change that result.
0499 | 
0500 | It remains a failed experiment.
0501 | 
0502 | But the trajectories revealed something useful.
0503 | 
0504 | Earlier in the run, the measurements showed a clear candidate ordering:
0505 | 
0506 | ```text
0507 | INTERIOR
0508 | <
0509 | RANDOM
0510 | <
0511 | SURFACE
0512 | ```
0513 | 
0514 | The late snapshot appeared to be asking the wrong scientific question.
0515 | 
0516 | Not because we disliked the answer.
0517 | 
0518 | Because all three branches were approaching the point where the state had lost contact with the frontier.
0519 | 
0520 | A snapshot taken after the mechanism is mostly exhausted tells us little about how long it remained active on the way there.
0521 | 
0522 | That observation generated a new hypothesis:
0523 | 
0524 | > **Placement may control causal lifetime rather than one particular late state.**
0525 | 
0526 | The next experiment was built around that hypothesis.
0527 | 
0528 | ---
0529 | 
0530 | ## Measure the Lifetime, Not One Frame
0531 | 
0532 | We kept the exact matched-copy controller.
0533 | 
0534 | Nothing about the material mechanism changed.
0535 | 
0536 | The only change was the outcome definition.
0537 | 
0538 | Instead of selecting one late frame, we froze an observation window:
0539 | 
0540 | ```text
0541 | t = 5 ... 18
0542 | ```
0543 | 
0544 | and integrated what happened through that window.
0545 | 
0546 | The primary quantities included:
0547 | 
0548 | ```text
0549 | frontier accessibility over time
0550 | probability leverage over time
0551 | realized causal attachment flips over time
0552 | ```
0553 | 
0554 | with loss of frontier contact as a supporting lifetime measure.
0555 | 
0556 | The run used a new seed and a new population of crystals.
0557 | 
0558 | The window and metrics were fixed before examining the V7 results.
0559 | 
0560 | And the result was unusually clean.
0561 | 
0562 | All three central integrated measurements followed the same ordering:
0563 | 
0564 | ```text
0565 | INTERIOR
0566 | <
0567 | RANDOM
0568 | <
0569 | SURFACE
0570 | ```
0571 | 
0572 | The mean integrated frontier-access fraction was approximately:
0573 | 
0574 | ```text
0575 | interior     0.515
0576 | random       0.847
0577 | surface      1.293
0578 | ```
0579 | 
0580 | Integrated local probability leverage was approximately:
0581 | 
0582 | ```text
0583 | interior      3.87
0584 | random        7.33
0585 | surface      12.26
0586 | ```
0587 | 
0588 | And the mean number of realized causal attachment flips over the window was approximately:
0589 | 
0590 | ```text
0591 | interior      4.06
0592 | random        7.52
0593 | surface      12.39
0594 | ```
0595 | 
0596 | Crucially, the amount of propagated state remained exactly matched.
0597 | 
0598 | Each policy averaged the same cumulative number of transmissions:
0599 | 
0600 | ```text
0601 | 27.1875
0602 | ```
0603 | 
0604 | The experiment was no longer comparing more state with less state.
0605 | 
0606 | It was comparing where an equal amount of state had been placed.
0607 | 
0608 | That earned the strongest claim in this chapter:
0609 | 
0610 | > **With propagated-state quantity held constant, spatial placement changes how long and how strongly that state remains causally available to subsequent growth.**
0611 | 
0612 | The crystal did not need more history.
0613 | 
0614 | It needed history in the right place.
0615 | 
0616 | ---
0617 | 
0618 | ## A Strong Result Is Also a Place to Stop
0619 | 
0620 | We did not stop immediately.
0621 | 
0622 | There was another mechanism hiding inside the earlier surface-biased experiment.
0623 | 
0624 | If remaining accessible creates more future opportunities to propagate, perhaps accessibility could participate in maintaining itself.
0625 | 
0626 | That possibility deserved one direct test.
0627 | 
0628 | So we compared a natural, state-dependent propagation schedule against an externally frozen schedule.
0629 | 
0630 | The natural branch produced stronger integrated accessibility, probability leverage and realized causal flips.
0631 | 
0632 | But the predeclared mechanism required it to demonstrate a reliable increase in total transmissions.
0633 | 
0634 | It did not.
0635 | 
0636 | The experiment failed.
0637 | 
0638 | We then asked whether temporal arrangement might explain the difference.
0639 | 
0640 | We replayed the same transmission-budget values in three ways:
0641 | 
0642 | ```text
0643 | ALIGNED
0644 | SHUFFLED
0645 | SHIFTED
0646 | ```
0647 | 
0648 | The total requested amount was held fixed.
0649 | 
0650 | Aligned timing changed some downstream quantities, particularly probability leverage.
0651 | 
0652 | But it did not reliably improve gross accessibility.
0653 | 
0654 | The broad timing claim failed.
0655 | 
0656 | One final experiment normalized causal effects by contact and by transmission.
0657 | 
0658 | Again, one narrow quantity looked interesting.
0659 | 
0660 | Again, the broad predeclared claim failed.
0661 | 
0662 | At this point the scientific problem had changed.
0663 | 
0664 | We were no longer discovering the basic material mechanism.
0665 | 
0666 | We were subdividing an effect we already understood:
0667 | 
0668 | ```text
0669 | accessibility
0670 | placement
0671 | timing
0672 | leverage
0673 | realized flips
0674 | efficiency
0675 | ```
0676 | 
0677 | Each distinction was real.
0678 | 
0679 | But a real distinction is not automatically a new property.
0680 | 
0681 | Three consecutive failed broad hypotheses were telling us something.
0682 | 
0683 | Not that the chapter had failed.
0684 | 
0685 | That the chapter had finished.
0686 | 
0687 | ---
0688 | 
0689 | ## What the Negative Results Protect Us From
0690 | 
0691 | There is a dangerous way to tell this story.
0692 | 
0693 | We could take every experiment that failed and retain whichever secondary metric happened to remain significant.
0694 | 
0695 | Then the narrative would become:
0696 | 
0697 | ```text
0698 | feedback failed
0699 | → perhaps timing
0700 | 
0701 | timing failed
0702 | → perhaps efficiency
0703 | 
0704 | efficiency failed
0705 | → perhaps another normalization
0706 | ```
0707 | 
0708 | That process can continue forever.
0709 | 
0710 | A sufficiently complicated experiment always contains another denominator, another endpoint, another subset or another interpretation.
0711 | 
0712 | That is not the evidence ladder we started this book with.
0713 | 
0714 | So the failed claims remain failed.
0715 | 
0716 | ```text
0717 | V8:
0718 | endogenous opportunity feedback
0719 | did not establish its predeclared transmission-amplification claim
0720 | 
0721 | V9:
0722 | temporal alignment
0723 | did not establish a broad accessibility advantage
0724 | 
0725 | V10:
0726 | temporal alignment
0727 | did not establish a robust general causal-efficiency advantage
0728 | ```
0729 | 
0730 | There are interesting mechanistic observations inside those failures.
0731 | 
0732 | They may matter later.
0733 | 
0734 | They are not promotions.
0735 | 
0736 | This distinction is part of the result.
0737 | 
0738 | ---
0739 | 
0740 | ## What Did We Actually Build?
0741 | 
0742 | Strip away the words **experience** and **memory**.
0743 | 
0744 | The mechanism is simple.
0745 | 
0746 | A past event can write a persistent local state.
0747 | 
0748 | That state changes later construction probabilities while it remains spatially connected to active construction.
0749 | 
0750 | Propagation can carry the state forward.
0751 | 
0752 | Spatial placement controls how long it remains usable.
0753 | 
0754 | Once it leaves the active construction region, it can persist forever without affecting anything.
0755 | 
0756 | A useful description is:
0757 | 
0758 | > **state-dependent construction with a bounded causal window**
0759 | 
0760 | Or more mechanically:
0761 | 
0762 | > **a persistent local construction bias whose causal availability depends on its position relative to a moving growth frontier**
0763 | 
0764 | The phrase I like most, though, is even simpler:
0765 | 
0766 | > **Storage is cheap. Access is scarce.**
0767 | 
0768 | The modified state itself is not difficult to preserve.
0769 | 
0770 | The computational problem is maintaining contact between history and the part of the system that is still making decisions.
0771 | 
0772 | That is a very different picture from the biological intuition that stored state is precious because matter decays.
0773 | 
0774 | In this digital substrate, the bit can survive perfectly.
0775 | 
0776 | The read path disappears.
0777 | 
0778 | ---
0779 | 
0780 | ## This Is Still Not Memory
0781 | 
0782 | We now have:
0783 | 
0784 | ```text
0785 | experience
0786 | ↓
0787 | persistent state
0788 | ↓
0789 | causal accessibility
0790 | ↓
0791 | later probability change
0792 | ↓
0793 | sometimes:
0794 | later construction change
0795 | ```
0796 | 
0797 | That is considerably more than we had at the start of the chapter.
0798 | 
0799 | But there are things we have not demonstrated.
0800 | 
0801 | The crystal cannot yet distinguish two kinds of experience.
0802 | 
0803 | It has not shown that:
0804 | 
0805 | ```text
0806 | history A
0807 | ```
0808 | 
0809 | and:
0810 | 
0811 | ```text
0812 | history B
0813 | ```
0814 | 
0815 | leave meaningfully different retained states.
0816 | 
0817 | It has not shown that a later common challenge produces a different response depending on which history occurred.
0818 | 
0819 | It has not demonstrated rewriting.
0820 | 
0821 | It has not demonstrated controlled erasure.
0822 | 
0823 | It has not demonstrated selective retention.
0824 | 
0825 | It has not demonstrated reconstruction of a lost historical state.
0826 | 
0827 | And it has not demonstrated that the state is used as a representation of anything.
0828 | 
0829 | So we still do not earn:
0830 | 
0831 | ```text
0832 | memory
0833 | learning
0834 | adaptation
0835 | homeostasis
0836 | self-maintenance
0837 | ```
0838 | 
0839 | That is not disappointing.
0840 | 
0841 | The purpose of the experiment was never to manufacture those words.
0842 | 
0843 | It was to discover what survived after we tried to remove the assumptions hidden inside them.
0844 | 
0845 | ---
0846 | 
0847 | ## What Survived the Hypothesis?
0848 | 
0849 | Several attractive interpretations failed in this chapter.
0850 | 
0851 | A persistent mark did not automatically remain useful.
0852 | 
0853 | Propagation did not automatically preserve access.
0854 | 
0855 | Surface-biased propagation initially looked stronger, but the first comparison was confounded because the surface branch produced more copies.
0856 | 
0857 | A late exact-budget endpoint then failed.
0858 | 
0859 | Three later broad hypotheses about feedback, timing and efficiency also failed.
0860 | 
0861 | Those failures stay failed.
0862 | 
0863 | But underneath them, one phenomenon became much clearer.
0864 | 
0865 | ### The surviving observation
0866 | 
0867 | The modified material mattered when the active growth process could still reach it.
0868 | 
0869 | When modified cells were buried behind later growth, they could remain present indefinitely while having no detectable effect on future construction.
0870 | 
0871 | When the same amount of modified state was placed nearer the active frontier, it remained causally available for longer and produced larger integrated effects.
0872 | 
0873 | With cumulative propagation quantity held exactly equal, the frozen V7 experiment measured:
0874 | 
0875 | ```text
0876 | integrated frontier access
0877 | 
0878 | interior    0.515
0879 | random      0.847
0880 | surface     1.293
0881 | ```
0882 | 
0883 | ```text
0884 | integrated probability leverage
0885 | 
0886 | interior     3.87
0887 | random       7.33
0888 | surface     12.26
0889 | ```
0890 | 
0891 | ```text
0892 | realized causal attachment flips
0893 | 
0894 | interior     4.06
0895 | random       7.52
0896 | surface     12.39
0897 | ```
0898 | 
0899 | The total transmitted quantity was identical:
0900 | 
0901 | ```text
0902 | 27.1875
0903 | ```
0904 | 
0905 | So the effect cannot be explained simply by one policy creating more modified material.
0906 | 
0907 | The chapter therefore leaves us with:
0908 | 
0909 | ```text
0910 | PERSISTENT STATE
0911 | SUPPORTED
0912 | 
0913 | PERSISTENT STATE IS AUTOMATICALLY USABLE
0914 | FAILED
0915 | 
0916 | SPATIAL PLACEMENT CONTROLS CAUSAL AVAILABILITY
0917 | SUPPORTED
0918 | ```
0919 | 
0920 | ### Phenomenon record
0921 | 
0922 | **Phenomenon:** Interface-mediated causal access
0923 | 
0924 | **Status:** **SUPPORTED**
0925 | 
0926 | **Current bounded description:**
0927 | 
0928 | > A persistent local state in Digital Crystal v1 can alter later construction while it remains coupled to the active growth interface, and with state quantity held constant, spatial placement strongly changes the duration and magnitude of that causal availability.
0929 | 
0930 | This is the strongest evidence so far for what we can now call the **Interface Principle**:
0931 | 
0932 | > **In the Digital Crystal, stored state becomes causally relevant primarily when it remains coupled to the dynamically active construction interface.**
0933 | 
0934 | The important object is therefore not just:
0935 | 
0936 | ```text
0937 | modified material
0938 | ```
0939 | 
0940 | but:
0941 | 
0942 | ```text
0943 | modified material
0944 |         ∩
0945 | active construction opportunity
0946 | ```
0947 | 
0948 | The bulk can preserve state.
0949 | 
0950 | The interface determines whether that state can still participate in the future.
0951 | 
0952 | ### Storage and access are different resources
0953 | 
0954 | Chapter 18 exposes a distinctly digital asymmetry.
0955 | 
0956 | The stored material state can survive indefinitely.
0957 | 
0958 | Nothing forces it to decay.
0959 | 
0960 | Yet its causal usefulness can vanish rapidly.
0961 | 
0962 | So:
0963 | 
0964 | ```text
0965 | STORAGE
0966 | can remain abundant
0967 | 
0968 | while
0969 | 
0970 | CAUSAL ACCESS
0971 | becomes scarce
0972 | ```
0973 | 
0974 | This gives us one of the cleanest substrate-level statements in the project:
0975 | 
0976 | > **Storage is cheap. Access is scarce.**
0977 | 
0978 | That is not merely a metaphor.
0979 | 
0980 | It is an operational result.
0981 | 
0982 | The bit can remain present while the current dynamics lose every route by which that bit can influence construction.
0983 | 
0984 | ### The active frontier is more than geometry
0985 | 
0986 | The active frontier is not important merely because it is visually on the outside.
0987 | 
0988 | It is where future construction decisions are currently being made.
0989 | 
0990 | That makes it a causal interface:
0991 | 
0992 | ```text
0993 | current state
0994 | ↓
0995 | frontier exposure
0996 | ↓
0997 | local probability change
0998 | ↓
0999 | stochastic decision boundary
1000 | ↓
1001 | realized construction difference
1002 | ```
1003 | 
1004 | The chapter closes this chain experimentally.
1005 | 
1006 | Modified material changes local attachment probability.
1007 | 
1008 | Some of those probability shifts cross the shared stochastic decision boundary.
1009 | 
1010 | Actual attachment events then differ.
1011 | 
1012 | So the frontier is where persistent historical state becomes operational.
1013 | 
1014 | ### Connection to earlier chapters
1015 | 
1016 | This phenomenon clarifies several earlier results.
1017 | 
1018 | Chapter 14 showed that morphology can preserve coarse information about forcing while losing exact temporal order.
1019 | 
1020 | Chapter 15 showed that visible morphology is not the same thing as executable continuation state.
1021 | 
1022 | Chapter 17 showed that a causal consequence can exist without becoming a stable readable signature.
1023 | 
1024 | Chapter 18 now adds:
1025 | 
1026 | ```text
1027 | even persistent state
1028 | does not matter
1029 | unless the active process can reach it
1030 | ```
1031 | 
1032 | That means the project-wide history hierarchy is no longer only about what survives.
1033 | 
1034 | It is also about what remains **accessible**:
1035 | 
1036 | ```text
1037 | PAST EVENT
1038 | ↓
1039 | TRACE
1040 | ↓
1041 | PERSISTENT TRACE
1042 | ↓
1043 | ACCESSIBLE TRACE
1044 | ↓
1045 | CAUSAL LEVERAGE
1046 | ↓
1047 | REALIZED FUTURE DIFFERENCE
1048 | ```
1049 | 
1050 | Every arrow is a separate property.
1051 | 
1052 | ### A possible moving-interface interpretation
1053 | 
1054 | There is also a broader observation worth recording carefully.
1055 | 
1056 | The region of causal activity moves outward as the crystal grows.
1057 | 
1058 | A modified state may remain fixed in the lattice while the active construction interface moves past it.
1059 | 
1060 | So the causal phenomenon is not static.
1061 | 
1062 | It has a moving spatial support.
1063 | 
1064 | That is consistent with a broader **moving-interface** or **propagating-field** hypothesis.
1065 | 
1066 | But Chapter 18 did not measure:
1067 | 
1068 | ```text
1069 | phase propagation
1070 | lag-distance relation
1071 | propagation velocity
1072 | dispersion
1073 | wave equation
1074 | ```
1075 | 
1076 | So this is not evidence for a literal wave.
1077 | 
1078 | The correct status is:
1079 | 
1080 | ```text
1081 | MOVING CAUSAL INTERFACE
1082 | OBSERVED
1083 | 
1084 | WAVE-LIKE PROPAGATION
1085 | OPEN HYPOTHESIS
1086 | ```
1087 | 
1088 | ### The negative results matter
1089 | 
1090 | V8, V9 and V10 do not disappear into this broader interpretation.
1091 | 
1092 | They remain negative constraints.
1093 | 
1094 | The chapter did **not** establish:
1095 | 
1096 | ```text
1097 | self-maintaining accessibility
1098 | general timing advantage
1099 | general causal-efficiency advantage
1100 | ```
1101 | 
1102 | Those failures narrow the Interface Principle.
1103 | 
1104 | The evidence supports:
1105 | 
1106 | > **placement controls access**
1107 | 
1108 | not:
1109 | 
1110 | > **the system actively regulates that access**
1111 | 
1112 | That distinction is essential.
1113 | 
1114 | ### What this phenomenon does not establish
1115 | 
1116 | The surviving phenomenon does **not** establish:
1117 | 
1118 | - memory,
1119 | - learning,
1120 | - adaptation,
1121 | - homeostasis,
1122 | - self-maintenance,
1123 | - active regulation,
1124 | - a wave mechanism,
1125 | - or life.
1126 | 
1127 | It establishes something narrower and more fundamental:
1128 | 
1129 | > **A history-induced material state can persist indefinitely yet become causally inert when it falls out of contact with the active construction interface. Spatial placement determines how long the same amount of historical state remains usable.**
1130 | 
1131 | This phenomenon now belongs in the project-wide phenomenon record independently of the failed memory, feedback, timing and efficiency hypotheses.
1132 | 
1133 | ---
1134 | 
1135 | ## The Result of Chapter 18
1136 | 
1137 | We can now say something we could not say before.
1138 | 
1139 | > **An external event can produce a persistent local material change in a Digital Crystal. That change can bias subsequent construction and alter realized growth events while it remains causally accessible. With the amount of propagated material held constant, spatial placement strongly changes the duration and magnitude of that causal availability.**
1140 | 
1141 | That is the bounded result.
1142 | 
1143 | Around it we also discovered several separations:
1144 | 
1145 | ```text
1146 | PERSISTENCE
1147 | ≠
1148 | CAUSAL ACCESSIBILITY
1149 | 
1150 | PROPAGATION
1151 | ≠
1152 | SURFACE MAINTENANCE
1153 | 
1154 | ACCESSIBILITY
1155 | ≠
1156 | PROBABILITY LEVERAGE
1157 | 
1158 | PROBABILITY LEVERAGE
1159 | ≠
1160 | REALIZED STOCHASTIC CONSEQUENCE
1161 | ```
1162 | 
1163 | Those distinctions matter because they tell us what not to collapse into one convenient word.
1164 | 
1165 | A causal past is cheap.
1166 | 
1167 | A persistent trace is cheap.
1168 | 
1169 | Even permanent storage can be cheap.
1170 | 
1171 | What becomes interesting is when the current process can still reach that trace and when reaching it changes what happens next.
1172 | 
1173 | ---
1174 | 
1175 | ## What Comes Next
1176 | 
1177 | There is one obvious way to continue.
1178 | 
1179 | Not by adding another timing metric.
1180 | 
1181 | Not by making the current state survive three updates longer.
1182 | 
1183 | Not by finding a better normalization.
1184 | 
1185 | We need a new property.
1186 | 
1187 | Until now, the crystal has only been able to answer one historical question:
1188 | 
1189 | ```text
1190 | was this material modified?
1191 | ```
1192 | 
1193 | The next question is qualitatively different:
1194 | 
1195 | ```text
1196 | WHAT happened?
1197 | ```
1198 | 
1199 | Suppose two different experiences can leave two different material states.
1200 | 
1201 | Then stop the experiences.
1202 | 
1203 | Let the crystal continue.
1204 | 
1205 | Later, give both crystals exactly the same challenge.
1206 | 
1207 | Now ask:
1208 | 
1209 | ```text
1210 | history A ─┐
1211 |            ├──→ same later condition ──→ same response?
1212 | history B ─┘
1213 | ```
1214 | 
1215 | If the later response depends on which history occurred, we will have crossed a new boundary.
1216 | 
1217 | Not memory.
1218 | 
1219 | Not yet.
1220 | 
1221 | But for the first time, the identity of the past rather than merely the presence of a persistent mark would have become a causal variable in the future.
1222 | 
1223 | That is no longer Chapter 18.
1224 | 
1225 | Chapter 18 has done its job.
1226 | 
1227 | The crystal can carry a mark from its past.
1228 | 
1229 | Now we have to find out whether it can tell **which past** it had.
```
