```markdown
0001 | +++
0002 | title = "19: Can the Crystal Tell Two Pasts Apart?"
0003 | date = "2026-08-12T18:28:00+01:00"
0004 | draft = false
0005 | description = "Two different experiences can leave different material traces in the Digital Crystal. Chapter 19 asks whether those differences actually matter when the future arrives."
0006 | categories = ["Programming", "Artificial Life"]
0007 | tags = ["Digital Life", "Digital Crystal", "Causality", "Material State", "History", "Experiments"]
0008 | series = ["Digital Life From First Principles"]
0009 | +++
0010 | 
0011 | The crystal could carry a mark from its past.
0012 | 
0013 | Chapter 18 established that much.
0014 | 
0015 | A local event could change the material.
0016 | 
0017 | The change could persist.
0018 | 
0019 | It could propagate.
0020 | 
0021 | And, while it remained in contact with the moving growth frontier, it could alter later construction.
0022 | 
0023 | But that still left a much harder question.
0024 | 
0025 | Not:
0026 | 
0027 | > **Did something happen before?**
0028 | 
0029 | But:
0030 | 
0031 | > **What happened before?**
0032 | 
0033 | That distinction sounds small.
0034 | 
0035 | It is not.
0036 | 
0037 | A system that only records:
0038 | 
0039 | ```text
0040 | something happened
0041 | ```
0042 | 
0043 | is very different from one whose future depends on:
0044 | 
0045 | ```text
0046 | which thing happened
0047 | ```
0048 | 
0049 | So Chapter 19 began with a new target.
0050 | 
0051 | > **Can two different prior experiences leave different retained material states that alter the crystal's response to the same later challenge?**
0052 | 
0053 | If the answer was yes, we would have crossed a genuinely new boundary.
0054 | 
0055 | If the answer was no, that would matter too.
0056 | 
0057 | ---
0058 | 
0059 | ## The Stronger Question
0060 | 
0061 | Chapter 18 ended with a local material state:
0062 | 
0063 | ```text
0064 | NORMAL
0065 | MODIFIED
0066 | ```
0067 | 
0068 | A pulse could change some cells from normal to modified.
0069 | 
0070 | Modified material could remain causally active while it stayed near the active construction region.
0071 | 
0072 | But one binary distinction only tells us:
0073 | 
0074 | ```text
0075 | not changed
0076 | changed
0077 | ```
0078 | 
0079 | It does not tell us whether the material can distinguish:
0080 | 
0081 | ```text
0082 | experience A
0083 | ```
0084 | 
0085 | from:
0086 | 
0087 | ```text
0088 | experience B
0089 | ```
0090 | 
0091 | That is the question here.
0092 | 
0093 | The experiment must therefore separate four different claims:
0094 | 
0095 | ```text
0096 | different experiences occur
0097 | ↓
0098 | different traces are written
0099 | ↓
0100 | different traces remain distinguishable
0101 | ↓
0102 | different traces alter later response
0103 | ```
0104 | 
0105 | We had evidence only for the first two in a very weak sense.
0106 | 
0107 | The rest had to be earned.
0108 | 
0109 | ---
0110 | 
0111 | ## First Attempt: Give the Histories Different Labels
0112 | 
0113 | The most direct design was obvious.
0114 | 
0115 | Add three material states:
0116 | 
0117 | ```text
0118 | NORMAL
0119 | HISTORY_A
0120 | HISTORY_B
0121 | ```
0122 | 
0123 | Then arrange two otherwise identical histories.
0124 | 
0125 | One writes `HISTORY_A`.
0126 | 
0127 | The other writes `HISTORY_B`.
0128 | 
0129 | Critically, the labels are written to exactly the same positions.
0130 | 
0131 | Before the later challenge, the two crystals therefore have:
0132 | 
0133 | ```text
0134 | same occupied cells
0135 | same visible morphology
0136 | same label locations
0137 | same propagation count
0138 | same environment
0139 | same random-number coupling
0140 | ```
0141 | 
0142 | Only the identity of the retained local state differs.
0143 | 
0144 | That is a strong control.
0145 | 
0146 | If the two futures later diverge, geometry cannot explain it.
0147 | 
0148 | But we still need a way for the later challenge to read the difference.
0149 | 
0150 | So the first mechanism used a symmetric local rule:
0151 | 
0152 | ```text
0153 | HISTORY_A neighbour
0154 | → small positive challenge bias
0155 | 
0156 | HISTORY_B neighbour
0157 | → small negative challenge bias
0158 | ```
0159 | 
0160 | Outside the challenge, both labels were inert.
0161 | 
0162 | That gave us a clean causal test:
0163 | 
0164 | ```text
0165 | (A challenge - A no-challenge)
0166 | -
0167 | (B challenge - B no-challenge)
0168 | ```
0169 | 
0170 | If the result was positive, history identity had altered the response.
0171 | 
0172 | But before running, we added something we had been missing in earlier chapters.
0173 | 
0174 | A smallest effect worth interpreting.
0175 | 
0176 | ---
0177 | 
0178 | ## Significance Was No Longer Enough
0179 | 
0180 | The book had accumulated too many experiments where `p < 0.05` risked carrying more epistemic weight than it deserved.
0181 | 
0182 | So this time we froze two magnitude requirements.
0183 | 
0184 | The A-vs-B interaction had to be at least:
0185 | 
0186 | ```text
0187 | 1% of pre-challenge crystal population
0188 | ```
0189 | 
0190 | and at least:
0191 | 
0192 | ```text
0193 | 0.5 seed-noise standard deviations
0194 | ```
0195 | 
0196 | It also had to clear the directional statistical test.
0197 | 
0198 | So the rule was:
0199 | 
0200 | ```text
0201 | statistically detectable
0202 | AND
0203 | large enough to matter
0204 | ```
0205 | 
0206 | not merely:
0207 | 
0208 | ```text
0209 | statistically detectable
0210 | ```
0211 | 
0212 | That distinction would become important almost immediately.
0213 | 
0214 | ---
0215 | 
0216 | ## The First Result Was Tempting
0217 | 
0218 | The V1 controls passed perfectly.
0219 | 
0220 | Before challenge:
0221 | 
0222 | ```text
0223 | A geometry = B geometry
0224 | A label locations = B label locations
0225 | ```
0226 | 
0227 | Without the challenge:
0228 | 
0229 | ```text
0230 | A future = B future
0231 | ```
0232 | 
0233 | Erase the retained history labels immediately before challenge:
0234 | 
0235 | ```text
0236 | A future = B future
0237 | ```
0238 | 
0239 | So if a difference appeared in the retained-state challenge condition, the labels themselves were responsible.
0240 | 
0241 | And a difference did appear.
0242 | 
0243 | The normalized interaction was:
0244 | 
0245 | ```text
0246 | 0.00440
0247 | ```
0248 | 
0249 | or about:
0250 | 
0251 | ```text
0252 | 0.44% of pre-challenge population
0253 | ```
0254 | 
0255 | The bootstrap interval was entirely positive:
0256 | 
0257 | ```text
0258 | 0.00331 ... 0.00548
0259 | ```
0260 | 
0261 | The directional randomization test produced:
0262 | 
0263 | ```text
0264 | p ≈ 0.00025
0265 | ```
0266 | 
0267 | By conventional significance logic, this looked convincing.
0268 | 
0269 | But the effect was smaller than the threshold we had committed to.
0270 | 
0271 | We had required:
0272 | 
0273 | ```text
0274 | 1.00%
0275 | ```
0276 | 
0277 | We got:
0278 | 
0279 | ```text
0280 | 0.44%
0281 | ```
0282 | 
0283 | And against the seed-noise scale:
0284 | 
0285 | ```text
0286 | observed effect     0.383 SD
0287 | required            0.500 SD
0288 | ```
0289 | 
0290 | So the formal result was:
0291 | 
0292 | ```text
0293 | FAILED
0294 | ```
0295 | 
0296 | That was exactly why we had introduced a magnitude gate.
0297 | 
0298 | A result can be statistically clear and scientifically too small.
0299 | 
0300 | ---
0301 | 
0302 | ## The Effect Happened Almost Immediately
0303 | 
0304 | There was another interesting pattern in V1.
0305 | 
0306 | The challenge-step attachment counts looked like this:
0307 | 
0308 | ```text
0309 |              A          B
0310 | 
0311 | step 1      76.79      71.13
0312 | 
0313 | step 2      71.51      72.34
0314 | 
0315 | step 3      74.81      75.50
0316 | 
0317 | step 4      78.00      78.22
0318 | ```
0319 | 
0320 | Most of the difference occurred immediately.
0321 | 
0322 | Then it disappeared.
0323 | 
0324 | That suggested something like:
0325 | 
0326 | ```text
0327 | challenge encounters retained state
0328 | ↓
0329 | short local response
0330 | ↓
0331 | later stochastic construction washes out the difference
0332 | ```
0333 | 
0334 | It would have been easy to promote the first response step after seeing it.
0335 | 
0336 | We did not.
0337 | 
0338 | The primary endpoint was the frozen four-step interaction.
0339 | 
0340 | The first-step pattern remained a diagnostic observation.
0341 | 
0342 | No more.
0343 | 
0344 | ---
0345 | 
0346 | ## The First Design Had a Deeper Problem
0347 | 
0348 | V1 had also smuggled too much meaning into the mechanism.
0349 | 
0350 | We had created:
0351 | 
0352 | ```text
0353 | HISTORY_A
0354 | HISTORY_B
0355 | ```
0356 | 
0357 | and then explicitly told the challenge how to interpret them.
0358 | 
0359 | In other words:
0360 | 
0361 | ```text
0362 | if A:
0363 |     increase probability
0364 | 
0365 | if B:
0366 |     decrease probability
0367 | ```
0368 | 
0369 | That proved something about retained symbolic labels.
0370 | 
0371 | But our broader question was stronger.
0372 | 
0373 | We wanted to know whether:
0374 | 
0375 | > **different experiences could create different material consequences which ordinary later dynamics would respond to differently**
0376 | 
0377 | not merely whether an explicit A/B decoder could read two labels.
0378 | 
0379 | So instead of tuning V1, we redesigned the mechanism.
0380 | 
0381 | ---
0382 | 
0383 | ## Second Attempt: Remove the Symbols
0384 | 
0385 | V2 returned to one altered material state:
0386 | 
0387 | ```text
0388 | NORMAL
0389 | MODIFIED
0390 | ```
0391 | 
0392 | There was no:
0393 | 
0394 | ```text
0395 | HISTORY_A
0396 | HISTORY_B
0397 | ```
0398 | 
0399 | anywhere in the substrate.
0400 | 
0401 | The two experiences differed only in where they wrote the same modified state.
0402 | 
0403 | Experience A wrote toward one directional region of the boundary.
0404 | 
0405 | Experience B wrote toward another.
0406 | 
0407 | The initial number of modified cells was identical.
0408 | 
0409 | Then both histories continued under the same material physics.
0410 | 
0411 | Modified state propagated with the same Chapter 18 mechanism.
0412 | 
0413 | And at every propagation step we forced the two histories to copy exactly the same number of modified cells.
0414 | 
0415 | So the two histories differed in spatial organization, not in how much historical material they were allowed to create.
0416 | 
0417 | Conceptually:
0418 | 
0419 | ```text
0420 | experience A
0421 | ↓
0422 | same material state
0423 | ↓
0424 | spatial organization A
0425 | 
0426 | experience B
0427 | ↓
0428 | same material state
0429 | ↓
0430 | spatial organization B
0431 | ```
0432 | 
0433 | Then both received one identical later challenge.
0434 | 
0435 | No symbolic decoder.
0436 | 
0437 | No A/B-specific rule.
0438 | 
0439 | ---
0440 | 
0441 | ## But Geometry Had Become a Confound Again
0442 | 
0443 | There was one complication.
0444 | 
0445 | Because modified material affects growth, the two spatial histories might themselves create slightly different crystal geometries before the challenge.
0446 | 
0447 | That means a simple A-vs-B challenge difference would not isolate retained material.
0448 | 
0449 | Perhaps the geometry alone caused the later difference.
0450 | 
0451 | So we added a stronger control.
0452 | 
0453 | At the exact pre-challenge checkpoint, clone each history.
0454 | 
0455 | Then erase only its modified-state labels.
0456 | 
0457 | Keep the occupied geometry unchanged.
0458 | 
0459 | Now we have:
0460 | 
0461 | ```text
0462 | A retained
0463 | A erased
0464 | 
0465 | B retained
0466 | B erased
0467 | ```
0468 | 
0469 | For each history:
0470 | 
0471 | ```text
0472 | challenge
0473 | vs
0474 | no challenge
0475 | ```
0476 | 
0477 | The primary quantity becomes:
0478 | 
0479 | ```text
0480 | retained interaction
0481 | -
0482 | erased interaction
0483 | ```
0484 | 
0485 | or more explicitly:
0486 | 
0487 | ```text
0488 | [(A challenge - A no-challenge)
0489 |  -
0490 |  (B challenge - B no-challenge)]
0491 | 
0492 | MINUS
0493 | 
0494 | [(A-erased challenge - A-erased no-challenge)
0495 |  -
0496 |  (B-erased challenge - B-erased no-challenge)]
0497 | ```
0498 | 
0499 | That is a demanding test.
0500 | 
0501 | It asks:
0502 | 
0503 | > **Does retained material organization itself contribute to a history-dependent challenge response beyond whatever geometry the history already created?**
0504 | 
0505 | That is the experiment we actually wanted.
0506 | 
0507 | ---
0508 | 
0509 | ## The Histories Really Did Remain Different
0510 | 
0511 | The mechanism audit produced something important.
0512 | 
0513 | Both histories began with the same amount of written material:
0514 | 
0515 | ```text
0516 | mean initial write count ≈ 19.6 cells
0517 | ```
0518 | 
0519 | Both propagated exactly the same number of copies:
0520 | 
0521 | ```text
0522 | A mean cumulative propagation = 78.5
0523 | B mean cumulative propagation = 78.5
0524 | ```
0525 | 
0526 | Both ended with the same average amount of modified material:
0527 | 
0528 | ```text
0529 | A ≈ 98.1
0530 | B ≈ 98.1
0531 | ```
0532 | 
0533 | So quantity was not the difference.
0534 | 
0535 | But spatial organization remained different.
0536 | 
0537 | A secondary angular diagnostic tracked the mean orientation of the modified material.
0538 | 
0539 | Across the retention period:
0540 | 
0541 | ```text
0542 |           A angle       B angle
0543 | 
0544 | t1       -0.063         +0.331
0545 | t2       -0.073         +0.329
0546 | t4       -0.097         +0.318
0547 | t6       -0.107         +0.330
0548 | t8       -0.119         +0.326
0549 | t10      -0.132         +0.330
0550 | ```
0551 | 
0552 | The traces did not simply collapse into one indistinguishable distribution.
0553 | 
0554 | The past remained spatially different.
0555 | 
0556 | That was already useful.
0557 | 
0558 | But we still needed to know whether those differences mattered.
0559 | 
0560 | ---
0561 | 
0562 | ## The Histories Were Still in the Causal Aperture
0563 | 
0564 | Chapter 18 had taught us not to confuse persistence with accessibility.
0565 | 
0566 | So V2 also tracked how much historical material remained in contact with the active growth frontier.
0567 | 
0568 | The contact fractions declined over time, but did not disappear.
0569 | 
0570 | At the end of the retention window:
0571 | 
0572 | ```text
0573 | A contact fraction ≈ 0.215
0574 | B contact fraction ≈ 0.219
0575 | ```
0576 | 
0577 | Roughly one fifth of the active frontier was still in contact with modified material.
0578 | 
0579 | So when the challenge arrived, the history had not simply been buried beyond reach.
0580 | 
0581 | That matters.
0582 | 
0583 | If the final result failed, the obvious explanation could not be:
0584 | 
0585 | ```text
0586 | the history was gone
0587 | ```
0588 | 
0589 | It was still there.
0590 | 
0591 | It was still accessible.
0592 | 
0593 | The real question was whether accessibility became readout.
0594 | 
0595 | ---
0596 | 
0597 | ## Then the Challenge Arrived
0598 | 
0599 | The common challenge was applied.
0600 | 
0601 | The mean attachment trajectories were:
0602 | 
0603 | ```text
0604 |           A       B
0605 | 
0606 | step 1   77.02   77.25
0607 | step 2   73.29   73.76
0608 | step 3   75.56   76.19
0609 | step 4   80.47   81.15
0610 | ```
0611 | 
0612 | They were almost on top of each other.
0613 | 
0614 | The retained-history interaction was already near zero:
0615 | 
0616 | ```text
0617 | -0.000354
0618 | ```
0619 | 
0620 | The geometry-only erased interaction was:
0621 | 
0622 | ```text
0623 | -0.000786
0624 | ```
0625 | 
0626 | Subtract one from the other and the primary material-mediated effect was:
0627 | 
0628 | ```text
0629 | 0.000431
0630 | ```
0631 | 
0632 | or:
0633 | 
0634 | ```text
0635 | 0.043% of crystal population
0636 | ```
0637 | 
0638 | The confidence interval crossed zero:
0639 | 
0640 | ```text
0641 | -0.000380 ... +0.001235
0642 | ```
0643 | 
0644 | The directional test gave:
0645 | 
0646 | ```text
0647 | p ≈ 0.163
0648 | ```
0649 | 
0650 | Against seed noise:
0651 | 
0652 | ```text
0653 | effect ≈ 0.033 SD
0654 | ```
0655 | 
0656 | The predeclared requirement was:
0657 | 
0658 | ```text
0659 | 0.500 SD
0660 | ```
0661 | 
0662 | This was not a near miss.
0663 | 
0664 | It was essentially null at the scale we cared about.
0665 | 
0666 | Formal status:
0667 | 
0668 | ```text
0669 | FAILED
0670 | ```
0671 | 
0672 | ---
0673 | 
0674 | ## A Different Kind of Failure
0675 | 
0676 | V1 and V2 failed for different reasons.
0677 | 
0678 | V1:
0679 | 
0680 | ```text
0681 | explicit symbolic readout
0682 | ↓
0683 | detectable response
0684 | ↓
0685 | too small to clear scientific threshold
0686 | ```
0687 | 
0688 | V2:
0689 | 
0690 | ```text
0691 | non-symbolic spatial history
0692 | ↓
0693 | different traces remain
0694 | ↓
0695 | traces remain causally accessible
0696 | ↓
0697 | no meaningful common-challenge readout
0698 | ```
0699 | 
0700 | V2 is the stronger result.
0701 | 
0702 | It tells us something that V1 could not.
0703 | 
0704 | The problem was not merely that the historical traces disappeared.
0705 | 
0706 | They did not.
0707 | 
0708 | The problem was not merely that A and B became spatially identical.
0709 | 
0710 | They did not.
0711 | 
0712 | And the problem was not unequal historical material quantity.
0713 | 
0714 | That was exactly controlled.
0715 | 
0716 | What failed was the next step:
0717 | 
0718 | ```text
0719 | different accessible material history
0720 | ↓
0721 | different later response
0722 | ```
0723 | 
0724 | Under this protocol, that arrow did not hold.
0725 | 
0726 | ---
0727 | 
0728 | ## Distinguishable Is Not Readable
0729 | 
0730 | This gives us the central distinction of Chapter 19:
0731 | 
0732 | ```text
0733 | PERSISTENT TRACE
0734 | ≠
0735 | ACCESSIBLE TRACE
0736 | ≠
0737 | DISTINGUISHABLE TRACE
0738 | ≠
0739 | READABLE TRACE
0740 | ```
0741 | 
0742 | Chapter 18 had already separated the first two.
0743 | 
0744 | Chapter 19 adds the next separation.
0745 | 
0746 | The crystal can contain two different spatial consequences of two different pasts.
0747 | 
0748 | Those consequences can remain measurably different.
0749 | 
0750 | They can remain in contact with active construction.
0751 | 
0752 | And still:
0753 | 
0754 | > **the later dynamics may not care which trace is present.**
0755 | 
0756 | That is a stronger negative than simply losing the history.
0757 | 
0758 | A stored distinction is not automatically a functional distinction.
0759 | 
0760 | ---
0761 | 
0762 | ## The Detector Problem
0763 | 
0764 | There is another way to think about this.
0765 | 
0766 | Suppose two waves travel through a medium with different phases.
0767 | 
0768 | A detector sensitive only to total amplitude may respond identically to both.
0769 | 
0770 | The histories differ.
0771 | 
0772 | The detector is simply insensitive to the degree of freedom in which they differ.
0773 | 
0774 | We should be very careful here.
0775 | 
0776 | We have not shown that the crystal contains waves.
0777 | 
0778 | We have not shown phase.
0779 | 
0780 | We have not shown a propagating field.
0781 | 
0782 | But the analogy exposes a real experimental issue.
0783 | 
0784 | Our common challenge is one particular probe.
0785 | 
0786 | A negative result means:
0787 | 
0788 | > **this probe did not extract a scientifically meaningful history-dependent response**
0789 | 
0790 | It does not prove:
0791 | 
0792 | > **no possible later interaction could ever distinguish the histories**
0793 | 
0794 | Those are very different claims.
0795 | 
0796 | But that does not mean we should now try twenty different probes until one succeeds.
0797 | 
0798 | That would turn the experimental program into a search for significance.
0799 | 
0800 | So we stop this mechanism family here.
0801 | 
0802 | ---
0803 | 
0804 | ## Remember the Flocking Lesson
0805 | 
0806 | Earlier in the book, Outlier gave us another seductive visual impression.
0807 | 
0808 | It looked like flocking.
0809 | 
0810 | Nearby structures moved with striking local coherence.
0811 | 
0812 | At first, causal family relationships even appeared to explain some of it.
0813 | 
0814 | Then better controls removed the family effect.
0815 | 
0816 | The surviving result was narrower:
0817 | 
0818 | ```text
0819 | local geometry
0820 | +
0821 | local cellular dynamics
0822 | +
0823 | spatial organization
0824 | ↓
0825 | coherent motion
0826 | ```
0827 | 
0828 | Not flocking.
0829 | 
0830 | That episode taught us something important.
0831 | 
0832 | > **Interesting visual structure is where a question begins, not where a claim ends.**
0833 | 
0834 | The same rule applies here.
0835 | 
0836 | The directional material patterns persist.
0837 | 
0838 | They look like different histories moving through the crystal.
0839 | 
0840 | That is interesting.
0841 | 
0842 | But we do not promote:
0843 | 
0844 | ```text
0845 | wave
0846 | memory
0847 | stored message
0848 | representation
0849 | ```
0850 | 
0851 | from that appearance.
0852 | 
0853 | We retain only what the experiment earned.
0854 | 
0855 | ---
0856 | 
0857 | ## What Did Chapter 19 Actually Establish?
0858 | 
0859 | The cleanest result is not positive or negative in the ordinary sense.
0860 | 
0861 | It is a boundary.
0862 | 
0863 | We can say:
0864 | 
0865 | > **Two different directional experiences can leave persistent, spatially distinguishable organizations of the same altered material while that material remains substantially in contact with the active growth frontier. Under the frozen common-challenge protocol, those differences did not produce a scientifically meaningful difference in later response beyond a geometry-preserving erasure control.**
0866 | 
0867 | That is the technical statement.
0868 | 
0869 | In simpler language:
0870 | 
0871 | > **The crystal can carry different traces of different pasts. We have not shown that its later dynamics can use the difference.**
0872 | 
0873 | That is enough.
0874 | 
0875 | ---
0876 | 
0877 | ## Why We Are Not Building V3
0878 | 
0879 | There are many tempting next moves.
0880 | 
0881 | Change the angles.
0882 | 
0883 | Increase the material gain.
0884 | 
0885 | Move the challenge earlier.
0886 | 
0887 | Move it later.
0888 | 
0889 | Use only the first challenge step.
0890 | 
0891 | Try a different challenge shape.
0892 | 
0893 | Measure a different denominator.
0894 | 
0895 | Increase the number of groups.
0896 | 
0897 | Every one of those could produce another experiment.
0898 | 
0899 | That is exactly why we should not do them.
0900 | 
0901 | The primary result was not ambiguous.
0902 | 
0903 | It was tiny.
0904 | 
0905 | The confidence interval included zero.
0906 | 
0907 | The effect was only a few hundredths of one seed-noise standard deviation.
0908 | 
0909 | So another small tuning step would not cross a conceptual boundary.
0910 | 
0911 | It would merely search the neighbourhood of a failed mechanism.
0912 | 
0913 | We already know where that leads.
0914 | 
0915 | The book does not need V3.
0916 | 
0917 | It needs a new question.
0918 | 
0919 | ---
0920 | 
0921 | ## The New Ladder
0922 | 
0923 | At this point our historical-material ladder looks like this:
0924 | 
0925 | ```text
0926 | EXTERNAL EVENT
0927 | ↓
0928 | LOCAL MATERIAL CHANGE
0929 | ↓
0930 | PERSISTENT TRACE
0931 | ↓
0932 | CAUSAL ACCESS
0933 | ↓
0934 | DISTINGUISHABLE MATERIAL ORGANIZATION
0935 | ↓
0936 | READOUT INTO LATER RESPONSE
0937 | ```
0938 | 
0939 | The experiments support the ladder only up to:
0940 | 
0941 | ```text
0942 | DISTINGUISHABLE MATERIAL ORGANIZATION
0943 | ```
0944 | 
0945 | The final arrow remains unearned.
0946 | 
0947 | That is a useful place to stop.
0948 | 
0949 | ---
0950 | 
0951 | ## What Survived the Hypothesis?
0952 | 
0953 | The chapter asked whether two different pasts could become functionally distinguishable later.
0954 | 
0955 | That stronger claim failed.
0956 | 
0957 | But the failure was unusually informative because several lower rungs of the mechanism clearly survived.
0958 | 
0959 | ### What survived before readout
0960 | 
0961 | In V2, both histories wrote the same amount of modified material.
0962 | 
0963 | They propagated the same number of copies.
0964 | 
0965 | They ended with the same average quantity of altered material.
0966 | 
0967 | Yet their spatial organizations remained measurably different.
0968 | 
0969 | At the same time, roughly one fifth of the active frontier still remained in contact with modified material at the end of the retention window.
0970 | 
0971 | So the histories were not:
0972 | 
0973 | ```text
0974 | gone
0975 | ```
0976 | 
0977 | and they were not:
0978 | 
0979 | ```text
0980 | buried beyond causal reach
0981 | ```
0982 | 
0983 | They were still:
0984 | 
0985 | ```text
0986 | persistent
0987 | spatially distinguishable
0988 | causally accessible
0989 | ```
0990 | 
0991 | Then the common challenge arrived.
0992 | 
0993 | The material-mediated history effect was:
0994 | 
0995 | ```text
0996 | 0.000431
0997 | ```
0998 | 
0999 | or about:
1000 | 
1001 | ```text
1002 | 0.043% of crystal population
1003 | ```
1004 | 
1005 | with confidence interval:
1006 | 
1007 | ```text
1008 | -0.000380 ... +0.001235
1009 | ```
1010 | 
1011 | and:
1012 | 
1013 | ```text
1014 | p ≈ 0.163
1015 | ```
1016 | 
1017 | Against seed noise, the effect was only:
1018 | 
1019 | ```text
1020 | 0.033 SD
1021 | ```
1022 | 
1023 | versus the frozen requirement:
1024 | 
1025 | ```text
1026 | 0.500 SD
1027 | ```
1028 | 
1029 | So the final readout arrow failed decisively.
1030 | 
1031 | ### Phenomenon record
1032 | 
1033 | **Phenomenon:** Distinguishable history without functional readout
1034 | 
1035 | **Status:** **SUPPORTED**
1036 | 
1037 | **Current bounded description:**
1038 | 
1039 | > Two different prior experiences can leave persistent, spatially distinguishable organizations of the same altered material while that material remains substantially coupled to the active growth interface, yet those differences need not produce a scientifically meaningful difference under a common later challenge.
1040 | 
1041 | This is stronger than merely saying:
1042 | 
1043 | ```text
1044 | history was lost
1045 | ```
1046 | 
1047 | The history was not lost.
1048 | 
1049 | The distinction was still physically present.
1050 | 
1051 | The distinction was still accessible.
1052 | 
1053 | What failed was:
1054 | 
1055 | ```text
1056 | DIFFERENT ACCESSIBLE TRACE
1057 | ↓
1058 | DIFFERENT LATER RESPONSE
1059 | ```
1060 | 
1061 | So Chapter 19 adds another rung to the project-wide history hierarchy:
1062 | 
1063 | ```text
1064 | PERSISTENT TRACE
1065 | ≠
1066 | ACCESSIBLE TRACE
1067 | ≠
1068 | DISTINGUISHABLE TRACE
1069 | ≠
1070 | READABLE TRACE
1071 | ```
1072 | 
1073 | ### This extends the Lossy-History Principle
1074 | 
1075 | Chapter 14 showed that broad source statistics survive more readily than exact temporal order.
1076 | 
1077 | Chapter 16 showed that coarse pulse-stream structure survives more readily than sender identity or exact interval chronology.
1078 | 
1079 | Chapter 17 showed that different histories can produce different particular futures without producing a stable population-level signature.
1080 | 
1081 | Chapter 18 showed that even persistent historical state matters only while it remains coupled to active construction.
1082 | 
1083 | Chapter 19 now shows something still stronger:
1084 | 
1085 | > **Even a persistent, accessible and measurably different historical trace can remain functionally inert with respect to a later probe.**
1086 | 
1087 | So the Lossy-History Principle is no longer only about loss of chronology.
1088 | 
1089 | It now has several possible failure points:
1090 | 
1091 | ```text
1092 | history may fail to persist
1093 | 
1094 | or
1095 | 
1096 | history may persist but lose access
1097 | 
1098 | or
1099 | 
1100 | history may remain accessible but lose distinction
1101 | 
1102 | or
1103 | 
1104 | history may remain distinct but fail readout
1105 | ```
1106 | 
1107 | Those are different mechanisms.
1108 | 
1109 | ### The detector problem is real
1110 | 
1111 | The chapter's wave analogy is useful only as an analogy.
1112 | 
1113 | Two states can differ in a degree of freedom that the chosen probe does not measure.
1114 | 
1115 | So a failed challenge means:
1116 | 
1117 | ```text
1118 | THIS PROBE
1119 | did not extract
1120 | a meaningful history-dependent response
1121 | ```
1122 | 
1123 | It does **not** establish:
1124 | 
1125 | ```text
1126 | NO POSSIBLE FUTURE INTERACTION
1127 | could ever distinguish the traces
1128 | ```
1129 | 
1130 | But that does not justify searching indefinitely for a probe that works.
1131 | 
1132 | The mechanism family was frozen and tested.
1133 | 
1134 | The primary effect was tiny.
1135 | 
1136 | So the correct action remains:
1137 | 
1138 | ```text
1139 | NO V3
1140 | ```
1141 | 
1142 | That is a strength of the result.
1143 | 
1144 | ### Connection to the Interface Principle
1145 | 
1146 | Chapter 19 also sharpens the Interface Principle.
1147 | 
1148 | Chapter 18 showed:
1149 | 
1150 | ```text
1151 | state must remain accessible to the active interface
1152 | ```
1153 | 
1154 | Chapter 19 shows:
1155 | 
1156 | ```text
1157 | accessibility is necessary
1158 | but not sufficient
1159 | ```
1160 | 
1161 | The active interface can still encounter a historical difference without the later dynamics producing a meaningful differential response.
1162 | 
1163 | So the revised relationship is:
1164 | 
1165 | ```text
1166 | PERSISTENCE
1167 | ↓
1168 | ACCESSIBILITY
1169 | ↓
1170 | DISTINGUISHABILITY
1171 | ↓
1172 | READOUT
1173 | ```
1174 | 
1175 | and each arrow must be earned separately.
1176 | 
1177 | This prevents us from collapsing:
1178 | 
1179 | ```text
1180 | accessible
1181 | ```
1182 | 
1183 | into:
1184 | 
1185 | ```text
1186 | usable
1187 | ```
1188 | 
1189 | ### Open propagating-field question
1190 | 
1191 | The persistent directional material organizations also resemble a moving spatial phenomenon.
1192 | 
1193 | That resemblance is worth preserving as an observation.
1194 | 
1195 | But the chapter does not establish:
1196 | 
1197 | ```text
1198 | wave
1199 | phase
1200 | propagation velocity
1201 | dispersion
1202 | ```
1203 | 
1204 | So the correct status remains:
1205 | 
1206 | ```text
1207 | DIRECTIONAL SPATIAL TRACE
1208 | MEASURED
1209 | 
1210 | PROPAGATING-FIELD INTERPRETATION
1211 | OPEN
1212 | ```
1213 | 
1214 | This belongs with the Chapter 13 and Chapter 18 observations for a later spatiotemporal audit.
1215 | 
1216 | ### What this phenomenon does not establish
1217 | 
1218 | The surviving phenomenon does **not** establish:
1219 | 
1220 | - memory,
1221 | - representation,
1222 | - semantic history,
1223 | - learning,
1224 | - adaptation,
1225 | - a wave mechanism,
1226 | - a general-purpose history decoder,
1227 | - or life.
1228 | 
1229 | It establishes something narrower:
1230 | 
1231 | > **The Digital Crystal can retain different, accessible material organizations corresponding to different pasts without those differences becoming functionally readable by the tested later dynamics.**
1232 | 
1233 | That phenomenon should now be tracked independently of the failed readout hypothesis.
1234 | 
1235 | ---
1236 | 
1237 | ## The Result of Chapter 19
1238 | 
1239 | The two experiments together give us one bounded conclusion.
1240 | 
1241 | V1 showed that an explicit symbolic material decoder can produce a small, reproducible history-dependent response, but the effect did not clear the predeclared scientific magnitude threshold.
1242 | 
1243 | V2 removed the symbolic decoder and allowed two experiences to create different spatial organizations of the same material state.
1244 | 
1245 | Those organizations remained measurably distinct.
1246 | 
1247 | They remained causally accessible.
1248 | 
1249 | But they did not produce a meaningful difference under the common later challenge.
1250 | 
1251 | So:
1252 | 
1253 | > **Different pasts can leave different traces without those traces becoming functionally readable by later dynamics.**
1254 | 
1255 | That is the result.
1256 | 
1257 | And another distinction joins the growing list:
1258 | 
1259 | ```text
1260 | DIFFERENT HISTORY
1261 | ≠
1262 | DIFFERENT TRACE
1263 | ≠
1264 | READABLE HISTORY
1265 | ```
1266 | 
1267 | We now know that storing history is easier than using it.
1268 | 
1269 | Again.
1270 | 
1271 | But this time the reason is not simply that the trace was buried.
1272 | 
1273 | Even an accessible difference can be inert with respect to the future probe.
1274 | 
1275 | ---
1276 | 
1277 | ## What Comes Next
1278 | 
1279 | For nine chapters, the Digital Crystal has lived inside a strange world.
1280 | 
1281 | Once material appears, it never disappears.
1282 | 
1283 | The frontier only moves outward.
1284 | 
1285 | Old cells become buried forever.
1286 | 
1287 | A trace can fall out of the causal aperture because the crystal builds over it, but nothing ever exposes it again.
1288 | 
1289 | That assumption has shaped almost every result we have obtained.
1290 | 
1291 | So rather than inventing another history reader, we are going to remove the assumption.
1292 | 
1293 | For the first time, occupied material will be allowed to vanish.
1294 | 
1295 | Nothing else.
1296 | 
1297 | No repair mechanism.
1298 | 
1299 | No energy.
1300 | 
1301 | No maintenance controller.
1302 | 
1303 | Just:
1304 | 
1305 | ```text
1306 | occupied
1307 | ↓
1308 | empty
1309 | ```
1310 | 
1311 | with some small probability.
1312 | 
1313 | That one change will make the frontier non-monotone.
1314 | 
1315 | Buried material may become exposed.
1316 | 
1317 | Construction will have to compete with loss.
1318 | 
1319 | The crystal may acquire a finite sustainable scale.
1320 | 
1321 | And properties that were impossible to distinguish under irreversible growth may finally separate.
1322 | 
1323 | Chapter 19 ends with an inaccessible word still hanging in front of us:
1324 | 
1325 | ```text
1326 | memory?
1327 | ```
1328 | 
1329 | We have not earned it.
1330 | 
1331 | Good.
1332 | 
1333 | The next experiment is not going to chase the word.
1334 | 
1335 | It is going to change the world.
```

```markdown
0001 | +++
0002 | title = "20: What Happens When the Crystal Can Lose Material?"
0003 | date = "2026-08-12T18:49:00+01:00"
0004 | draft = false
0005 | description = "The Digital Crystal had always lived in a world where occupied material lasted forever. Chapter 20 removes that assumption and discovers that loss creates new construction opportunity."
0006 | categories = ["Programming", "Artificial Life"]
0007 | tags = ["Digital Life", "Digital Crystal", "Causality", "Material Loss", "Reoccupation", "Experiments"]
0008 | series = ["Digital Life From First Principles"]
0009 | +++
0010 | 
0011 | Until now, the crystal had lived in an impossible world.
0012 | 
0013 | Once a cell became occupied, it stayed occupied.
0014 | 
0015 | Forever.
0016 | 
0017 | The frontier moved outward.
0018 | 
0019 | Old material accumulated behind it.
0020 | 
0021 | Nothing eroded.
0022 | 
0023 | Nothing vanished.
0024 | 
0025 | Nothing exposed the interior again.
0026 | 
0027 | That assumption had quietly shaped almost every result since Chapter 11.
0028 | 
0029 | So Chapter 20 removed it.
0030 | 
0031 | We added one rule:
0032 | 
0033 | ```text
0034 | occupied
0035 | ↓
0036 | empty
0037 | ```
0038 | 
0039 | with some small probability.
0040 | 
0041 | Nothing else.
0042 | 
0043 | No repair mechanism.
0044 | 
0045 | No resource system.
0046 | 
0047 | No maintenance controller.
0048 | 
0049 | No energy variable.
0050 | 
0051 | No metabolism.
0052 | 
0053 | No target morphology.
0054 | 
0055 | Just loss.
0056 | 
0057 | Then we asked what broke.
0058 | 
0059 | ---
0060 | 
0061 | ## The Obvious Prediction
0062 | 
0063 | The first prediction seemed almost embarrassingly simple.
0064 | 
0065 | Suppose the crystal has an effective radius \(r\).
0066 | 
0067 | If new construction happens mainly around the outside boundary, then construction opportunity should scale roughly like perimeter:
0068 | 
0069 | ```text
0070 | construction ~ r
0071 | ```
0072 | 
0073 | But if every occupied cell can disappear, then total expected loss should scale roughly like occupied area:
0074 | 
0075 | ```text
0076 | loss ~ r²
0077 | ```
0078 | 
0079 | That gives a natural prediction.
0080 | 
0081 | At small size:
0082 | 
0083 | ```text
0084 | construction > loss
0085 | ```
0086 | 
0087 | At larger size:
0088 | 
0089 | ```text
0090 | loss > construction
0091 | ```
0092 | 
0093 | So there ought to be some scale where the two balance.
0094 | 
0095 | Perhaps the crystal would stop expanding.
0096 | 
0097 | Perhaps it would hover around a finite size.
0098 | 
0099 | Perhaps above some loss rate it would collapse.
0100 | 
0101 | It was exactly the kind of candidate scaling law we wanted.
0102 | 
0103 | And because it sounded so plausible, we wrote the failure condition before running the experiment.
0104 | 
0105 | A finite dynamic regime had to satisfy all of these:
0106 | 
0107 | ```text
0108 | late population slope approximately zero
0109 | 
0110 | population still substantially above zero
0111 | 
0112 | world far from simulation capacity
0113 | 
0114 | population meaningfully smaller than decay-off baseline
0115 | ```
0116 | 
0117 | A plateau caused by extinction would not count.
0118 | 
0119 | A plateau caused by reaching the edge of the world would not count.
0120 | 
0121 | We wanted a genuine balance between construction and loss.
0122 | 
0123 | ---
0124 | 
0125 | ## First Establish the Baseline
0126 | 
0127 | With no loss, the crystal still behaved exactly as expected.
0128 | 
0129 | The decay-off baseline had a late normalized population slope around:
0130 | 
0131 | ```text
0132 | 0.037 per update
0133 | ```
0134 | 
0135 | and late net growth around:
0136 | 
0137 | ```text
0138 | 154 cells per update
0139 | ```
0140 | 
0141 | The crystal was nowhere near world capacity.
0142 | 
0143 | So we had a clean expanding reference.
0144 | 
0145 | Then we turned on loss.
0146 | 
0147 | The sweep was:
0148 | 
0149 | ```text
0150 | δ = 0.00
0151 | δ = 0.02
0152 | δ = 0.04
0153 | δ = 0.06
0154 | δ = 0.08
0155 | δ = 0.12
0156 | δ = 0.16
0157 | ```
0158 | 
0159 | The prediction was straightforward:
0160 | 
0161 | ```text
0162 | higher δ
0163 | ↓
0164 | more material removed
0165 | ↓
0166 | growth slows
0167 | ↓
0168 | eventually slope approaches zero
0169 | ```
0170 | 
0171 | That is not what happened.
0172 | 
0173 | ---
0174 | 
0175 | ## Loss Did Not Stop the Expansion
0176 | 
0177 | The late normalized population slopes stayed remarkably similar.
0178 | 
0179 | Across the entire sweep they remained close to:
0180 | 
0181 | ```text
0182 | 0.036 – 0.038
0183 | ```
0184 | 
0185 | Even at the highest tested loss rate, the crystal was still expanding.
0186 | 
0187 | The late mean population did fall.
0188 | 
0189 | At `δ = 0.16`, it was roughly one third smaller than the decay-off baseline.
0190 | 
0191 | But the slope did not flatten.
0192 | 
0193 | No non-zero loss rate met the predeclared finite-regime condition.
0194 | 
0195 | So the primary result was:
0196 | 
0197 | ```text
0198 | FAILED
0199 | ```
0200 | 
0201 | The obvious perimeter-versus-area argument had not survived contact with the substrate.
0202 | 
0203 | That failure was already useful.
0204 | 
0205 | But the reason was much more interesting.
0206 | 
0207 | ---
0208 | 
0209 | ## Look at the Gross Construction Rate
0210 | 
0211 | At first glance, increasing loss should simply subtract material from the crystal.
0212 | 
0213 | If the crystal ordinarily adds around 150 cells per update, then adding loss ought to produce something like:
0214 | 
0215 | ```text
0216 | +150 construction
0217 | -80 loss
0218 | = +70 net
0219 | ```
0220 | 
0221 | Then perhaps:
0222 | 
0223 | ```text
0224 | +150 construction
0225 | -150 loss
0226 | = 0 net
0227 | ```
0228 | 
0229 | But the construction rate itself did not remain fixed.
0230 | 
0231 | It exploded.
0232 | 
0233 | Late averages looked approximately like this:
0234 | 
0235 | ```text
0236 | δ       attachments       losses       net growth
0237 | 
0238 | 0.00       152               0            +152
0239 | 0.02       227              81            +146
0240 | 0.04       299             158            +141
0241 | 0.06       358             227            +131
0242 | 0.08       430             303            +127
0243 | 0.12       530             420            +110
0244 | 0.16       632             531            +101
0245 | ```
0246 | 
0247 | At `δ = 0.16`, the crystal was losing more than five hundred cells per update.
0248 | 
0249 | But it was also attaching more than six hundred.
0250 | 
0251 | Loss had made the crystal build faster.
0252 | 
0253 | That was not in the prediction.
0254 | 
0255 | ---
0256 | 
0257 | ## The Missing Variable Was Frontier
0258 | 
0259 | The original scaling argument treated the crystal as if it had one meaningful boundary.
0260 | 
0261 | Something like:
0262 | 
0263 | ```text
0264 | solid interior
0265 | +
0266 | outer perimeter
0267 | ```
0268 | 
0269 | But random loss changes the geometry.
0270 | 
0271 | Every time an occupied site disappears, it can create a new empty location surrounded by occupied material.
0272 | 
0273 | That location becomes part of the set that ordinary growth can potentially fill.
0274 | 
0275 | So:
0276 | 
0277 | ```text
0278 | loss
0279 | ↓
0280 | new empty site
0281 | ↓
0282 | new local boundary
0283 | ↓
0284 | new attachment opportunity
0285 | ```
0286 | 
0287 | The loss process was manufacturing frontier.
0288 | 
0289 | And the boundary measurements showed it dramatically.
0290 | 
0291 | Approximate late mean boundary counts were:
0292 | 
0293 | ```text
0294 | δ=0.00      372
0295 | δ=0.02      807
0296 | δ=0.04     1167
0297 | δ=0.06     1430
0298 | δ=0.08     1695
0299 | δ=0.12     1940
0300 | δ=0.16     2068
0301 | ```
0302 | 
0303 | So our original statement:
0304 | 
0305 | ```text
0306 | construction opportunity ~ outer perimeter
0307 | ```
0308 | 
0309 | was simply the wrong geometry.
0310 | 
0311 | Once material can disappear, the crystal develops internal construction surfaces.
0312 | 
0313 | The active boundary is no longer only outside.
0314 | 
0315 | That destroys the naive scaling law.
0316 | 
0317 | ---
0318 | 
0319 | ## Where You Lose Material Matters
0320 | 
0321 | V1 contained another test.
0322 | 
0323 | Suppose the total number of removed cells is held exactly equal.
0324 | 
0325 | Does it matter whether they are removed from the surface or the interior?
0326 | 
0327 | We synchronized the loss count.
0328 | 
0329 | Every update:
0330 | 
0331 | ```text
0332 | surface branch removes K cells
0333 | interior branch removes K cells
0334 | ```
0335 | 
0336 | The total removal budget was identical.
0337 | 
0338 | The only difference was placement.
0339 | 
0340 | The result was strong.
0341 | 
0342 | Late population was about:
0343 | 
0344 | ```text
0345 | 11.1%
0346 | ```
0347 | 
0348 | higher under interior-biased loss than under surface-biased loss.
0349 | 
0350 | That cleared the predeclared 10% effect threshold.
0351 | 
0352 | And the hole counts were dramatically different:
0353 | 
0354 | ```text
0355 | surface loss      ~2.7 late holes
0356 | interior loss    ~29.8 late holes
0357 | ```
0358 | 
0359 | So equal loss did not have equal consequences.
0360 | 
0361 | Where the missing material appeared mattered.
0362 | 
0363 | At this point we had a plausible explanation.
0364 | 
0365 | Perhaps interior loss creates high-neighbour-count vacancies that ordinary growth rapidly fills.
0366 | 
0367 | But that was still only an interpretation.
0368 | 
0369 | We needed to measure it directly.
0370 | 
0371 | ---
0372 | 
0373 | ## V2: Separate New Construction from Reoccupation
0374 | 
0375 | Until loss existed, every attachment meant the same thing.
0376 | 
0377 | A cell had never been occupied before.
0378 | 
0379 | Then it became occupied.
0380 | 
0381 | So:
0382 | 
0383 | ```text
0384 | attachment = new construction
0385 | ```
0386 | 
0387 | But after material loss, that equivalence breaks.
0388 | 
0389 | A cell can now follow this path:
0390 | 
0391 | ```text
0392 | occupied
0393 | ↓
0394 | lost
0395 | ↓
0396 | empty
0397 | ↓
0398 | occupied again
0399 | ```
0400 | 
0401 | So V2 introduced an observer-only occupancy ledger.
0402 | 
0403 | It did not change the crystal.
0404 | 
0405 | It merely remembered whether each lattice position had ever been occupied before.
0406 | 
0407 | Now every attachment could be classified as one of two things:
0408 | 
0409 | ```text
0410 | FIRST OCCUPATION
0411 | a site is occupied for the first time ever
0412 | 
0413 | REOCCUPATION
0414 | a previously occupied site was lost
0415 | and later becomes occupied again
0416 | ```
0417 | 
0418 | This distinction immediately changes how we should read the V1 numbers.
0419 | 
0420 | `632 attachments per update` does not mean:
0421 | 
0422 | ```text
0423 | 632 new places added
0424 | ```
0425 | 
0426 | It means:
0427 | 
0428 | ```text
0429 | first occupations
0430 | +
0431 | reoccupations
0432 | ```
0433 | 
0434 | The crystal may be doing enormous internal turnover while net population grows much more slowly.
0435 | 
0436 | ---
0437 | 
0438 | ## Verify the Null First
0439 | 
0440 | With no material loss, reoccupation must be impossible.
0441 | 
0442 | Nothing ever becomes empty again.
0443 | 
0444 | So V2 first ran a structural null.
0445 | 
0446 | Across 96 no-loss runs:
0447 | 
0448 | ```text
0449 | reoccupation count = 0
0450 | ```
0451 | 
0452 | exactly.
0453 | 
0454 | All attachments were first occupations.
0455 | 
0456 | Good.
0457 | 
0458 | Now we could trust the ledger.
0459 | 
0460 | ---
0461 | 
0462 | ## The Exact-Count Loss Experiment
0463 | 
0464 | We returned to the matched surface-versus-interior design.
0465 | 
0466 | Again:
0467 | 
0468 | ```text
0469 | surface loss count = interior loss count
0470 | ```
0471 | 
0472 | on every update.
0473 | 
0474 | The mean cumulative loss count in each branch was about:
0475 | 
0476 | ```text
0477 | 890 cells
0478 | ```
0479 | 
0480 | Then we asked:
0481 | 
0482 | > What fraction of those losses later became reoccupations?
0483 | 
0484 | The answer was much larger than expected.
0485 | 
0486 | ---
0487 | 
0488 | ## Almost Everything Came Back
0489 | 
0490 | For surface-biased loss:
0491 | 
0492 | ```text
0493 | reoccupation per loss ≈ 0.936
0494 | ```
0495 | 
0496 | For interior-biased loss:
0497 | 
0498 | ```text
0499 | reoccupation per loss ≈ 0.956
0500 | ```
0501 | 
0502 | Another way to measure the same phenomenon is to ask what fraction of unique lost sites were ever reoccupied.
0503 | 
0504 | Again:
0505 | 
0506 | ```text
0507 | surface     ≈ 93.6%
0508 | interior    ≈ 95.7%
0509 | ```
0510 | 
0511 | That is the central mechanistic result of Chapter 20.
0512 | 
0513 | Under these conditions, most removed sites did not remain empty.
0514 | 
0515 | They came back.
0516 | 
0517 | And usually quickly.
0518 | 
0519 | Mean reoccupation delay:
0520 | 
0521 | ```text
0522 | surface     ≈ 1.56 updates
0523 | interior    ≈ 1.09 updates
0524 | ```
0525 | 
0526 | So a typical lost site was often empty for only one or two steps.
0527 | 
0528 | ---
0529 | 
0530 | ## But the Primary V2 Hypothesis Still Failed
0531 | 
0532 | V2 had not merely predicted that reoccupation would occur.
0533 | 
0534 | It made a stronger claim.
0535 | 
0536 | We predicted that interior loss would produce a much larger reoccupation rate than surface loss.
0537 | 
0538 | The predeclared minimum difference was:
0539 | 
0540 | ```text
0541 | 0.15 additional reoccupations per loss
0542 | ```
0543 | 
0544 | The observed difference was only:
0545 | 
0546 | ```text
0547 | 0.0198
0548 | ```
0549 | 
0550 | It was statistically clear.
0551 | 
0552 | The interval was narrow.
0553 | 
0554 | The directional test was extremely small.
0555 | 
0556 | But it was nowhere near the effect size we had said would matter.
0557 | 
0558 | So the primary V2 result was:
0559 | 
0560 | ```text
0561 | FAILED
0562 | ```
0563 | 
0564 | Again.
0565 | 
0566 | And again the failure corrected the interpretation.
0567 | 
0568 | Interior loss was not special in the way we expected.
0569 | 
0570 | The much larger phenomenon was shared by both conditions.
0571 | 
0572 | ---
0573 | 
0574 | ## Almost Every Loss Creates Frontier
0575 | 
0576 | V2 also measured how much new frontier was created per removed cell.
0577 | 
0578 | The numbers were:
0579 | 
0580 | ```text
0581 | surface     ~0.995 new frontier sites per loss
0582 | interior    ~1.000 new frontier sites per loss
0583 | ```
0584 | 
0585 | That is almost one-for-one.
0586 | 
0587 | So the more general mechanism is:
0588 | 
0589 | ```text
0590 | LOCAL MATERIAL LOSS
0591 | ↓
0592 | NEW EMPTY LOCATION
0593 | ↓
0594 | NEW ATTACHMENT OPPORTUNITY
0595 | ```
0596 | 
0597 | not:
0598 | 
0599 | ```text
0600 | INTERIOR LOSS ONLY
0601 | ↓
0602 | SPECIAL ATTACHMENT OPPORTUNITY
0603 | ```
0604 | 
0605 | Interior sites were somewhat more rapidly and completely reoccupied.
0606 | 
0607 | But that was a second-order difference.
0608 | 
0609 | The major effect was universal.
0610 | 
0611 | Removing a cell almost always creates a site ordinary growth can see again.
0612 | 
0613 | ---
0614 | 
0615 | ## Reoccupation Is Not Repair
0616 | 
0617 | This is where the language matters.
0618 | 
0619 | It is tempting to say:
0620 | 
0621 | > The crystal repairs itself.
0622 | 
0623 | We have not earned that.
0624 | 
0625 | There is no:
0626 | 
0627 | ```text
0628 | damage detector
0629 | ```
0630 | 
0631 | No:
0632 | 
0633 | ```text
0634 | repair objective
0635 | ```
0636 | 
0637 | No:
0638 | 
0639 | ```text
0640 | desired target morphology
0641 | ```
0642 | 
0643 | No:
0644 | 
0645 | ```text
0646 | special reconstruction pathway
0647 | ```
0648 | 
0649 | The exact same local growth rule runs whether the empty site is:
0650 | 
0651 | ```text
0652 | new territory
0653 | ```
0654 | 
0655 | or:
0656 | 
0657 | ```text
0658 | a place that used to exist
0659 | ```
0660 | 
0661 | The crystal does not know the difference.
0662 | 
0663 | The observer does.
0664 | 
0665 | So the bounded claim is:
0666 | 
0667 | > **Material removal creates attachment opportunities that the ordinary Digital Crystal growth rule rapidly reuses.**
0668 | 
0669 | That is enough.
0670 | 
0671 | And it is more interesting than calling it repair too early.
0672 | 
0673 | ---
0674 | 
0675 | ## Population Was Hiding Turnover
0676 | 
0677 | The biggest conceptual change in this chapter is not about loss.
0678 | 
0679 | It is about measurement.
0680 | 
0681 | A population graph shows:
0682 | 
0683 | ```text
0684 | occupied cells at time t
0685 | ```
0686 | 
0687 | But that is only a net stock.
0688 | 
0689 | Once material can disappear and reappear, the same population can conceal very different underlying traffic.
0690 | 
0691 | Imagine two systems.
0692 | 
0693 | System A:
0694 | 
0695 | ```text
0696 | +100 new cells
0697 | 0 lost
0698 | ```
0699 | 
0700 | System B:
0701 | 
0702 | ```text
0703 | +600 attachments
0704 | -500 losses
0705 | ```
0706 | 
0707 | Both end the update at:
0708 | 
0709 | ```text
0710 | +100 net
0711 | ```
0712 | 
0713 | A population curve treats them as equivalent.
0714 | 
0715 | They are not.
0716 | 
0717 | System B is undergoing enormous internal turnover.
0718 | 
0719 | So another distinction joins our evidence ladder:
0720 | 
0721 | ```text
0722 | NET POPULATION CHANGE
0723 | ≠
0724 | GROSS CONSTRUCTION ACTIVITY
0725 | ```
0726 | 
0727 | and:
0728 | 
0729 | ```text
0730 | ATTACHMENT
0731 | ≠
0732 | FIRST OCCUPATION
0733 | ```
0734 | 
0735 | Those distinctions did not exist before material could disappear.
0736 | 
0737 | Now they are essential.
0738 | 
0739 | ---
0740 | 
0741 | ## The Hole Paradox
0742 | 
0743 | There was one result that initially looked contradictory.
0744 | 
0745 | Interior-biased loss produced many more visible holes:
0746 | 
0747 | ```text
0748 | surface     ~3.2
0749 | interior    ~37.6
0750 | ```
0751 | 
0752 | Yet interior lost sites were reoccupied slightly more often and much faster.
0753 | 
0754 | How can a process with faster refill have more holes?
0755 | 
0756 | Because a snapshot counts how many holes exist now.
0757 | 
0758 | It does not tell us how long each hole survives.
0759 | 
0760 | Imagine:
0761 | 
0762 | ```text
0763 | lose A
0764 | lose B
0765 | refill A
0766 | lose C
0767 | refill B
0768 | lose D
0769 | refill C
0770 | ...
0771 | ```
0772 | 
0773 | A snapshot can show many holes even if individual holes are short-lived.
0774 | 
0775 | The process may be generating vacancies faster than any one vacancy persists.
0776 | 
0777 | Again:
0778 | 
0779 | > **State is not dynamics.**
0780 | 
0781 | A single picture of the crystal cannot reveal how much material is cycling underneath it.
0782 | 
0783 | We keep rediscovering this lesson.
0784 | 
0785 | ---
0786 | 
0787 | ## What Happened to the Scaling Law?
0788 | 
0789 | We began Chapter 20 with a candidate law:
0790 | 
0791 | ```text
0792 | growth ~ boundary
0793 | loss ~ occupied area
0794 | ```
0795 | 
0796 | therefore:
0797 | 
0798 | ```text
0799 | loss eventually wins
0800 | ```
0801 | 
0802 | The experiment rejected that reasoning under this substrate.
0803 | 
0804 | Why?
0805 | 
0806 | Because loss is not an external subtraction from a fixed geometry.
0807 | 
0808 | Loss changes the geometry that determines construction opportunity.
0809 | 
0810 | The real relationship is closer to:
0811 | 
0812 | ```text
0813 | loss
0814 | ↓
0815 | changes active boundary
0816 | ↓
0817 | changes future construction rate
0818 | ```
0819 | 
0820 | So loss and construction are dynamically coupled.
0821 | 
0822 | That means the original scaling argument treated:
0823 | 
0824 | ```text
0825 | construction rate
0826 | ```
0827 | 
0828 | as independent of:
0829 | 
0830 | ```text
0831 | loss rate
0832 | ```
0833 | 
0834 | when it is not.
0835 | 
0836 | That was the mistake.
0837 | 
0838 | And finding that mistake is more valuable than confirming the original prediction would have been.
0839 | 
0840 | ---
0841 | 
0842 | ## A Different Kind of Compensation
0843 | 
0844 | There is another tempting word here:
0845 | 
0846 | ```text
0847 | maintenance
0848 | ```
0849 | 
0850 | We should not use it yet.
0851 | 
0852 | But something important has appeared.
0853 | 
0854 | A perturbation that removes material automatically creates conditions under which the ordinary growth process tends to replace much of that material.
0855 | 
0856 | Conceptually:
0857 | 
0858 | ```text
0859 | loss
0860 | ↓
0861 | creates opportunity for the mechanism
0862 | that counteracts loss
0863 | ```
0864 | 
0865 | That is a form of **structural compensation**.
0866 | 
0867 | Not because the crystal senses damage.
0868 | 
0869 | Not because it allocates resources.
0870 | 
0871 | Not because it prefers preservation.
0872 | 
0873 | But because the geometry of removal feeds directly into the geometry of construction.
0874 | 
0875 | That is a substrate-level feedback.
0876 | 
0877 | The word `feedback` is justified here only in the mechanistic sense:
0878 | 
0879 | ```text
0880 | loss changes state
0881 | state changes future construction opportunities
0882 | construction changes state again
0883 | ```
0884 | 
0885 | We do not need to make it biological.
0886 | 
0887 | ---
0888 | 
0889 | ## What Survived the Hypothesis?
0890 | 
0891 | Both primary hypotheses in this chapter failed.
0892 | 
0893 | The first predicted a finite near-stationary regime in which loss would eventually balance growth.
0894 | 
0895 | That did not happen.
0896 | 
0897 | The second predicted that interior-biased loss would produce a scientifically large reoccupation advantage over surface-biased loss.
0898 | 
0899 | That also did not happen.
0900 | 
0901 | Those failures remain failures.
0902 | 
0903 | But underneath them, the experiment exposed a much broader mechanism.
0904 | 
0905 | ### Loss changed the geometry of opportunity
0906 | 
0907 | The original scaling argument treated growth and loss almost as independent processes:
0908 | 
0909 | ```text
0910 | growth
0911 | ~ outer boundary
0912 | 
0913 | loss
0914 | ~ occupied area
0915 | ```
0916 | 
0917 | That picture failed because material loss changes the very geometry that determines where growth can occur.
0918 | 
0919 | A removed cell becomes:
0920 | 
0921 | ```text
0922 | empty
0923 | ```
0924 | 
0925 | and if occupied neighbours remain around it, that location becomes an ordinary attachment opportunity.
0926 | 
0927 | So:
0928 | 
0929 | ```text
0930 | LOSS
0931 | ↓
0932 | NEW EMPTY SITE
0933 | ↓
0934 | NEW LOCAL FRONTIER
0935 | ↓
0936 | NEW ATTACHMENT OPPORTUNITY
0937 | ```
0938 | 
0939 | The experiment measured this almost one-for-one:
0940 | 
0941 | ```text
0942 | surface loss
0943 | ~0.995 new frontier sites per loss
0944 | 
0945 | interior loss
0946 | ~1.000 new frontier sites per loss
0947 | ```
0948 | 
0949 | The crystal did not merely suffer loss.
0950 | 
0951 | Loss manufactured frontier.
0952 | 
0953 | ### Phenomenon record
0954 | 
0955 | **Phenomenon:** Loss-generated construction opportunity
0956 | 
0957 | **Status:** **SUPPORTED**
0958 | 
0959 | **Current bounded description:**
0960 | 
0961 | > Under the tested Digital Crystal loss conditions, removing occupied material almost always creates a new local frontier opportunity that the ordinary frozen growth rule can potentially reuse.
0962 | 
0963 | This extends the Interface Principle from Chapter 18.
0964 | 
0965 | Chapter 18 showed that historical state matters when it remains coupled to the active construction interface.
0966 | 
0967 | Chapter 20 shows that the interface itself can be **created by loss**.
0968 | 
0969 | So the active interface is not just something the crystal carries outward.
0970 | 
0971 | It can also be regenerated internally whenever material disappears.
0972 | 
0973 | ### Reoccupation is the dominant shared phenomenon
0974 | 
0975 | Under exact-count matched loss:
0976 | 
0977 | ```text
0978 | surface unique lost sites reoccupied
0979 | ≈ 93.6%
0980 | 
0981 | interior unique lost sites reoccupied
0982 | ≈ 95.7%
0983 | ```
0984 | 
0985 | and mean reoccupation delay was approximately:
0986 | 
0987 | ```text
0988 | surface
0989 | 1.56 updates
0990 | 
0991 | interior
0992 | 1.09 updates
0993 | ```
0994 | 
0995 | The predeclared interior-minus-surface difference failed because the shared effect was already close to saturation.
0996 | 
0997 | The stronger surviving observation is therefore:
0998 | 
0999 | > **Sparse material loss is usually converted into a new attachment opportunity that ordinary growth rapidly reuses.**
1000 | 
1001 | This is not repair.
1002 | 
1003 | No damage detector exists.
1004 | 
1005 | No target state is reconstructed.
1006 | 
1007 | No special pathway distinguishes:
1008 | 
1009 | ```text
1010 | new territory
1011 | ```
1012 | 
1013 | from:
1014 | 
1015 | ```text
1016 | territory that used to be occupied
1017 | ```
1018 | 
1019 | The observer knows a site is being reoccupied.
1020 | 
1021 | The crystal does not.
1022 | 
1023 | So:
1024 | 
1025 | ```text
1026 | REOCCUPATION
1027 | ≠
1028 | REPAIR
1029 | ```
1030 | 
1031 | ### Phenomenon record
1032 | 
1033 | **Phenomenon:** Rapid reuse of lost material
1034 | 
1035 | **Status:** **SUPPORTED**
1036 | 
1037 | **Current bounded description:**
1038 | 
1039 | > Under the tested exact-count conditions, more than 93% of lost sites were subsequently reoccupied, typically within one or two updates, through the same local growth rule used for ordinary expansion.
1040 | 
1041 | This is a substrate-level consequence of geometry, not evidence of biological maintenance.
1042 | 
1043 | ### The Flux Principle
1044 | 
1045 | Chapter 20 also exposes a second major phenomenon.
1046 | 
1047 | Before loss existed:
1048 | 
1049 | ```text
1050 | attachment
1051 | =
1052 | first occupation
1053 | ```
1054 | 
1055 | After loss exists:
1056 | 
1057 | ```text
1058 | attachment
1059 | =
1060 | first occupation
1061 | +
1062 | reoccupation
1063 | ```
1064 | 
1065 | That means population becomes an incomplete description of the process.
1066 | 
1067 | A crystal can have modest net growth while undergoing enormous gross material traffic.
1068 | 
1069 | For example:
1070 | 
1071 | ```text
1072 | +632 attachments
1073 | -531 losses
1074 | ----------------
1075 | +101 net
1076 | ```
1077 | 
1078 | A population curve sees:
1079 | 
1080 | ```text
1081 | +101
1082 | ```
1083 | 
1084 | but the process actually executed:
1085 | 
1086 | ```text
1087 | 1163 gross material events
1088 | ```
1089 | 
1090 | So the chapter establishes:
1091 | 
1092 | ```text
1093 | NET POPULATION CHANGE
1094 | ≠
1095 | GROSS MATERIAL TURNOVER
1096 | ```
1097 | 
1098 | This gives us the **Flux Principle**:
1099 | 
1100 | > **Static population or morphology can conceal large ongoing construction, loss and reoccupation flows.**
1101 | 
1102 | The active process may be much more dynamic than its net state suggests.
1103 | 
1104 | ### State is not dynamics
1105 | 
1106 | The hole paradox reinforces the same point.
1107 | 
1108 | Interior-biased loss can show more holes in a snapshot while individual lost sites are also being reoccupied faster.
1109 | 
1110 | That is possible because:
1111 | 
1112 | ```text
1113 | number of holes now
1114 | ```
1115 | 
1116 | is not the same thing as:
1117 | 
1118 | ```text
1119 | lifetime of each hole
1120 | ```
1121 | 
1122 | A system can create and close vacancies rapidly while maintaining many vacancies at any one instant.
1123 | 
1124 | So again:
1125 | 
1126 | ```text
1127 | SNAPSHOT
1128 | ≠
1129 | PROCESS
1130 | ```
1131 | 
1132 | This is the Flux Principle in another form.
1133 | 
1134 | ### Structural compensation, not maintenance
1135 | 
1136 | The chapter also reveals a bounded feedback loop:
1137 | 
1138 | ```text
1139 | loss
1140 | ↓
1141 | changes local geometry
1142 | ↓
1143 | creates attachment opportunity
1144 | ↓
1145 | ordinary growth reoccupies sites
1146 | ↓
1147 | changes local geometry again
1148 | ```
1149 | 
1150 | That is legitimate mechanistic feedback.
1151 | 
1152 | But it is not evidence for:
1153 | 
1154 | ```text
1155 | damage sensing
1156 | goal-directed preservation
1157 | repair objective
1158 | self-maintenance
1159 | ```
1160 | 
1161 | The correct term is **structural compensation**.
1162 | 
1163 | The system counteracts some loss because the geometry of loss creates opportunities for the same process that fills empty sites.
1164 | 
1165 | ### Connection to earlier phenomena
1166 | 
1167 | The cross-chapter picture is now becoming much tighter.
1168 | 
1169 | Chapter 18:
1170 | 
1171 | ```text
1172 | causal relevance
1173 | lives at active interface
1174 | ```
1175 | 
1176 | Chapter 19:
1177 | 
1178 | ```text
1179 | accessible difference
1180 | does not guarantee readout
1181 | ```
1182 | 
1183 | Chapter 20:
1184 | 
1185 | ```text
1186 | loss creates interface
1187 | ```
1188 | 
1189 | So the interface is no longer merely:
1190 | 
1191 | ```text
1192 | the outer boundary of a growing object
1193 | ```
1194 | 
1195 | It is better understood as:
1196 | 
1197 | > **the dynamically generated set of locations where the process currently has an opportunity to change state.**
1198 | 
1199 | That definition will matter later.
1200 | 
1201 | ### What this phenomenon does not establish
1202 | 
1203 | The surviving phenomena do **not** establish:
1204 | 
1205 | - repair,
1206 | - homeostasis,
1207 | - self-maintenance,
1208 | - energy use,
1209 | - metabolism,
1210 | - a finite sustainable size,
1211 | - or life.
1212 | 
1213 | They establish something narrower and more fundamental:
1214 | 
1215 | > **Material loss dynamically generates new construction opportunity, and ordinary Digital Crystal growth rapidly reuses most lost sites. At the same time, large gross turnover can remain hidden beneath relatively modest net population change.**
1216 | 
1217 | Those phenomena should now be tracked independently of the failed finite-regime and interior-superiority hypotheses.
1218 | 
1219 | ---
1220 | 
1221 | ## What Chapter 20 Established
1222 | 
1223 | The primary V1 hypothesis failed.
1224 | 
1225 | The primary V2 hypothesis failed.
1226 | 
1227 | That sounds disastrous if experiments are supposed to confirm what we hoped.
1228 | 
1229 | It is exactly what we wanted from this book.
1230 | 
1231 | V1:
1232 | 
1233 | > Background loss did **not** produce the predicted finite near-stationary regime across the tested sweep.
1234 | 
1235 | V2:
1236 | 
1237 | > Interior loss did **not** increase reoccupation over surface loss by the predeclared scientifically meaningful amount.
1238 | 
1239 | But together they exposed a stronger mechanism:
1240 | 
1241 | > **Material loss creates new attachment opportunity, and under the tested exact-count conditions more than 93% of lost sites were subsequently reoccupied, typically within one or two updates, by the ordinary frozen growth rule.**
1242 | 
1243 | That statement is measured.
1244 | 
1245 | It does not require the failed hypotheses to become true.
1246 | 
1247 | ---
1248 | 
1249 | ## The New Ladder
1250 | 
1251 | The crystal now has a new causal chain:
1252 | 
1253 | ```text
1254 | MATERIAL PRESENT
1255 | ↓
1256 | MATERIAL LOST
1257 | ↓
1258 | EMPTY SITE
1259 | ↓
1260 | NEW FRONTIER
1261 | ↓
1262 | ORDINARY ATTACHMENT
1263 | ↓
1264 | REOCCUPATION
1265 | ```
1266 | 
1267 | And we can distinguish:
1268 | 
1269 | ```text
1270 | LOSS
1271 | ≠
1272 | PERMANENT LOSS
1273 | 
1274 | ATTACHMENT
1275 | ≠
1276 | NEW CONSTRUCTION
1277 | 
1278 | REOCCUPATION
1279 | ≠
1280 | REPAIR
1281 | 
1282 | NET GROWTH
1283 | ≠
1284 | GROSS MATERIAL TURNOVER
1285 | ```
1286 | 
1287 | Those are the results of Chapter 20.
1288 | 
1289 | ---
1290 | 
1291 | ## Why We Stop Here
1292 | 
1293 | We could continue.
1294 | 
1295 | Measure exact hole lifetime distributions.
1296 | 
1297 | Sweep the placement fraction.
1298 | 
1299 | Try different loss schedules.
1300 | 
1301 | Separate one-neighbour from six-neighbour vacancies.
1302 | 
1303 | Increase the number of groups.
1304 | 
1305 | But none of those would cross a new conceptual boundary.
1306 | 
1307 | We know the essential mechanism now.
1308 | 
1309 | Loss makes reusable frontier.
1310 | 
1311 | Ordinary growth reuses it.
1312 | 
1313 | The crystal can therefore undergo large material turnover without net population collapse.
1314 | 
1315 | That is enough.
1316 | 
1317 | No V3.
1318 | 
1319 | ---
1320 | 
1321 | ## The Question We Have Now Earned
1322 | 
1323 | There is still something artificial about this world.
1324 | 
1325 | The crystal currently gets unlimited opportunities to act.
1326 | 
1327 | Every update, the growth rule can evaluate every eligible frontier site.
1328 | 
1329 | It does not have to choose between:
1330 | 
1331 | ```text
1332 | expanding outward
1333 | ```
1334 | 
1335 | and:
1336 | 
1337 | ```text
1338 | reoccupying lost material
1339 | ```
1340 | 
1341 | It can attempt both everywhere.
1342 | 
1343 | So rebuilding what vanished is almost free.
1344 | 
1345 | That is why Chapter 20 should not end with:
1346 | 
1347 | ```text
1348 | repair
1349 | ```
1350 | 
1351 | It should end with a constraint.
1352 | 
1353 | Suppose the crystal can perform only:
1354 | 
1355 | ```text
1356 | B
1357 | ```
1358 | 
1359 | construction updates per step.
1360 | 
1361 | Now every action has an opportunity cost.
1362 | 
1363 | For the first time:
1364 | 
1365 | ```text
1366 | OUTWARD CONSTRUCTION
1367 |         competes with
1368 | REOCCUPATION
1369 | ```
1370 | 
1371 | And then a genuinely new question appears:
1372 | 
1373 | > **When computational action is limited, what gets built and what gets preserved?**
1374 | 
1375 | That is no longer a question about loss alone.
1376 | 
1377 | It is a question about allocation under scarcity.
1378 | 
1379 | The crystal did not learn to repair itself.
1380 | 
1381 | We merely discovered that, while computation was unlimited, rebuilding what disappeared was almost free.
1382 | 
1383 | Next, we remove that luxury.
```
