```markdown
0001 | +++
0002 | title = "21: What Does It Cost to Stay?"
0003 | date = "2026-08-12T19:26:00+01:00"
0004 | draft = false
0005 | description = "Once material can disappear, the Digital Crystal can rebuild what is lost. Chapter 21 asks what happens when computation itself becomes scarce."
0006 | categories = ["Programming", "Artificial Life"]
0007 | tags = ["Digital Life", "Digital Crystal", "Scarcity", "Turnover", "Computation", "Experiments"]
0008 | series = ["Digital Life From First Principles"]
0009 | +++
0010 | 
0011 | Chapter 20 ended with an uncomfortable fact.
0012 | 
0013 | The crystal could lose material.
0014 | 
0015 | And yet most of that material came back.
0016 | 
0017 | Not because we gave it a repair mechanism.
0018 | 
0019 | Not because it knew what had been damaged.
0020 | 
0021 | Not because it protected an intended shape.
0022 | 
0023 | The ordinary growth rule simply saw the empty sites created by loss and treated them as new attachment opportunities.
0024 | 
0025 | Under the tested exact-count conditions, more than ninety-three percent of lost sites were eventually reoccupied, usually within one or two updates.
0026 | 
0027 | That sounded impressive.
0028 | 
0029 | But there was a hidden luxury.
0030 | 
0031 | The crystal could examine essentially every available construction opportunity.
0032 | 
0033 | Every update, every eligible frontier site could receive an attachment evaluation.
0034 | 
0035 | So the crystal never really had to choose between:
0036 | 
0037 | ```text
0038 | expanding into new territory
0039 | ```
0040 | 
0041 | and:
0042 | 
0043 | ```text
0044 | reoccupying material that had disappeared
0045 | ```
0046 | 
0047 | It could attempt both.
0048 | 
0049 | Everywhere.
0050 | 
0051 | At once.
0052 | 
0053 | Rebuilding was cheap because computation was unlimited.
0054 | 
0055 | Chapter 21 removes that luxury.
0056 | 
0057 | ---
0058 | 
0059 | ## One New Constraint
0060 | 
0061 | We changed exactly one thing.
0062 | 
0063 | Instead of evaluating every eligible frontier site, the crystal could evaluate at most:
0064 | 
0065 | ```text
0066 | B
0067 | ```
0068 | 
0069 | sites per update.
0070 | 
0071 | An unevaluated site simply received no attachment draw on that step.
0072 | 
0073 | The underlying attachment probability remained unchanged.
0074 | 
0075 | The loss rule remained unchanged.
0076 | 
0077 | The crystal gained no new state.
0078 | 
0079 | No energy.
0080 | 
0081 | No resource counter.
0082 | 
0083 | No maintenance controller.
0084 | 
0085 | No memory of damage.
0086 | 
0087 | No ability to identify whether a frontier position had ever been occupied before.
0088 | 
0089 | Just:
0090 | 
0091 | ```text
0092 | many possible construction opportunities
0093 | ↓
0094 | only B can be evaluated
0095 | ```
0096 | 
0097 | For the first time, computation itself was scarce.
0098 | 
0099 | ---
0100 | 
0101 | ## Scarcity Creates Allocation
0102 | 
0103 | The new constraint introduces something the earlier crystal never experienced.
0104 | 
0105 | Competition.
0106 | 
0107 | Suppose one update contains:
0108 | 
0109 | ```text
0110 | 500 possible frontier sites
0111 | ```
0112 | 
0113 | but:
0114 | 
0115 | ```text
0116 | B = 128
0117 | ```
0118 | 
0119 | Only 128 can even be considered.
0120 | 
0121 | Now the order in which sites are selected matters.
0122 | 
0123 | That does not mean the crystal is choosing.
0124 | 
0125 | It does not mean it has priorities.
0126 | 
0127 | But different local scheduling rules can produce different material futures.
0128 | 
0129 | So V1 began with three deliberately simple policies.
0130 | 
0131 | ```text
0132 | HIGH SUPPORT
0133 | evaluate sites with more occupied neighbours first
0134 | 
0135 | NEUTRAL
0136 | evaluate frontier sites in keyed random order
0137 | 
0138 | LOW SUPPORT
0139 | evaluate sites with fewer occupied neighbours first
0140 | ```
0141 | 
0142 | The policies were allowed to see only current geometry.
0143 | 
0144 | They were not allowed to inspect occupancy history.
0145 | 
0146 | They did not know whether a site was:
0147 | 
0148 | ```text
0149 | new territory
0150 | ```
0151 | 
0152 | or:
0153 | 
0154 | ```text
0155 | a previously occupied site waiting to be reoccupied
0156 | ```
0157 | 
0158 | That distinction remained observer-only.
0159 | 
0160 | So if high-support scheduling happened to favour reoccupation, it would be an emergent consequence of local geometry rather than an explicit repair instruction.
0161 | 
0162 | ---
0163 | 
0164 | ## The First Question
0165 | 
0166 | V1 asked:
0167 | 
0168 | > **At the same finite computational budget, can local scheduling change the tradeoff between reusing lost material and occupying new sites?**
0169 | 
0170 | We froze:
0171 | 
0172 | ```text
0173 | loss rate δ = 0.08
0174 | budget B = 256
0175 | ```
0176 | 
0177 | and required both sides of the predicted tradeoff to clear predeclared magnitude thresholds.
0178 | 
0179 | High-support scheduling had to produce a meaningful increase in:
0180 | 
0181 | ```text
0182 | reoccupation / loss
0183 | ```
0184 | 
0185 | while low-support scheduling had to produce a meaningful increase in:
0186 | 
0187 | ```text
0188 | first occupations / 1000 evaluations
0189 | ```
0190 | 
0191 | Both were required.
0192 | 
0193 | That mattered.
0194 | 
0195 | Because significance alone was no longer enough.
0196 | 
0197 | ---
0198 | 
0199 | ## Budget Changed Scale Immediately
0200 | 
0201 | Before looking at the policies, the neutral budget sweep already showed something striking.
0202 | 
0203 | Approximate late populations were:
0204 | 
0205 | ```text
0206 | B = 64        ~381
0207 | B = 128       ~829
0208 | B = 256      ~1717
0209 | B = 512      ~3092
0210 | B = 1024     ~3513
0211 | unlimited    ~3462
0212 | ```
0213 | 
0214 | The amount of computational opportunity strongly constrained how large the lossy crystal became.
0215 | 
0216 | At severe scarcity, late net change approached zero.
0217 | 
0218 | At larger budgets, the crystal expanded rapidly.
0219 | 
0220 | So:
0221 | 
0222 | ```text
0223 | COMPUTATIONAL OPPORTUNITY
0224 | ↓
0225 | PROCESS SCALE
0226 | ```
0227 | 
0228 | was already real.
0229 | 
0230 | That was a new property.
0231 | 
0232 | Not metabolism.
0233 | 
0234 | Not energy.
0235 | 
0236 | But the crystal could no longer be described without reference to the amount of computation available to it.
0237 | 
0238 | ---
0239 | 
0240 | ## Same Budget, Different Futures
0241 | 
0242 | Then we held the budget fixed.
0243 | 
0244 | At:
0245 | 
0246 | ```text
0247 | B = 256
0248 | ```
0249 | 
0250 | the three local scheduling rules produced very different results.
0251 | 
0252 | Approximate late behaviour:
0253 | 
0254 | ```text
0255 | HIGH SUPPORT
0256 | 
0257 | late population                  ~1923
0258 | reoccupation / loss               0.959
0259 | first occupations / 1000 evals    188
0260 | late net growth                   +24.3
0261 | ```
0262 | 
0263 | ```text
0264 | NEUTRAL
0265 | 
0266 | late population                  ~1723
0267 | reoccupation / loss               0.844
0268 | first occupations / 1000 evals    212
0269 | late net growth                   +10.0
0270 | ```
0271 | 
0272 | ```text
0273 | LOW SUPPORT
0274 | 
0275 | late population                  ~1131
0276 | reoccupation / loss               0.534
0277 | first occupations / 1000 evals    249
0278 | late net growth                    -1.5
0279 | ```
0280 | 
0281 | The differences were large.
0282 | 
0283 | High-support scheduling strongly favoured reoccupation and produced the largest persistent population.
0284 | 
0285 | Low-support scheduling favoured first occupation and left much less material behind.
0286 | 
0287 | So finite computation had created a real allocation tension:
0288 | 
0289 | ```text
0290 | REUSE LOST MATERIAL
0291 |         versus
0292 | OCCUPY NEW MATERIAL
0293 | ```
0294 | 
0295 | The crystal still did not know which was which.
0296 | 
0297 | Geometry created the bias.
0298 | 
0299 | ---
0300 | 
0301 | ## But V1 Still Failed
0302 | 
0303 | The high-support reoccupation advantage easily cleared its threshold.
0304 | 
0305 | Observed:
0306 | 
0307 | ```text
0308 | ~0.425
0309 | ```
0310 | 
0311 | Required:
0312 | 
0313 | ```text
0314 | 0.150
0315 | ```
0316 | 
0317 | The low-support first-occupation advantage was also statistically clear.
0318 | 
0319 | Observed:
0320 | 
0321 | ```text
0322 | ~61.6 first occupations per 1000 evaluations
0323 | ```
0324 | 
0325 | But the predeclared requirement was:
0326 | 
0327 | ```text
0328 | 100
0329 | ```
0330 | 
0331 | So the formal two-arm tradeoff failed.
0332 | 
0333 | ```text
0334 | V1 STATUS: FAILED
0335 | ```
0336 | 
0337 | That failure matters.
0338 | 
0339 | We had predicted a stronger exploration-side effect than the experiment produced.
0340 | 
0341 | We do not get to reduce the threshold afterward.
0342 | 
0343 | But V1 still established several measured facts.
0344 | 
0345 | Finite computational budget strongly constrained scale.
0346 | 
0347 | Local scheduling strongly changed persistence.
0348 | 
0349 | And scarce evaluations were not neutral with respect to what kind of construction occurred.
0350 | 
0351 | That was enough to motivate a new question.
0352 | 
0353 | ---
0354 | 
0355 | ## Was There a Budget Where the Crystal Simply Stayed?
0356 | 
0357 | The neutral sweep contained one especially interesting region.
0358 | 
0359 | At low budgets, net growth approached zero.
0360 | 
0361 | That suggested a new possibility.
0362 | 
0363 | Perhaps Chapter 20 had failed to produce finite scale because material loss alone was not enough.
0364 | 
0365 | But perhaps:
0366 | 
0367 | ```text
0368 | LOSS
0369 | +
0370 | FINITE COMPUTATIONAL OPPORTUNITY
0371 | ```
0372 | 
0373 | could create something much closer to dynamic persistence.
0374 | 
0375 | So V2 dropped the scheduling-policy comparison entirely.
0376 | 
0377 | Only neutral scheduling remained.
0378 | 
0379 | The new question was:
0380 | 
0381 | > **Is there a finite budget at which the population becomes approximately stationary while material turnover continues?**
0382 | 
0383 | This was not allowed to succeed by freezing.
0384 | 
0385 | It was not allowed to succeed by dying.
0386 | 
0387 | It was not allowed to succeed by reaching the simulation boundary.
0388 | 
0389 | A qualifying regime required all of the following:
0390 | 
0391 | ```text
0392 | population remains nonzero
0393 | 
0394 | late population slope approximately zero
0395 | 
0396 | loss continues
0397 | 
0398 | reoccupation continues
0399 | 
0400 | first occupation continues
0401 | 
0402 | gross turnover remains substantial
0403 | 
0404 | world capacity is not binding
0405 | ```
0406 | 
0407 | We froze a candidate family before the fresh run:
0408 | 
0409 | ```text
0410 | B = 48, 64, 80, 96, 128
0411 | ```
0412 | 
0413 | No new candidate could be added afterward.
0414 | 
0415 | ---
0416 | 
0417 | ## V2 Came Very Close
0418 | 
0419 | The V2 result was peculiar.
0420 | 
0421 | Almost every gate passed for every budget.
0422 | 
0423 | Population survived.
0424 | 
0425 | Capacity was nowhere near binding.
0426 | 
0427 | Loss continued.
0428 | 
0429 | Reoccupation continued.
0430 | 
0431 | First occupation continued.
0432 | 
0433 | Gross turnover remained substantial.
0434 | 
0435 | Late net growth stayed small.
0436 | 
0437 | The only gate that failed was:
0438 | 
0439 | ```text
0440 | late normalized population slope
0441 | ```
0442 | 
0443 | The approximate slopes were:
0444 | 
0445 | ```text
0446 | B=48     -0.00319
0447 | B=64     -0.00271
0448 | B=80     -0.00252
0449 | B=96     -0.00268
0450 | B=128    -0.00280
0451 | ```
0452 | 
0453 | The frozen threshold was:
0454 | 
0455 | ```text
0456 | |slope| <= 0.00250
0457 | ```
0458 | 
0459 | At `B=80`, the mean missed by only a tiny amount.
0460 | 
0461 | But it missed.
0462 | 
0463 | So:
0464 | 
0465 | ```text
0466 | V2 STATUS: FAILED
0467 | ```
0468 | 
0469 | No candidate met every predeclared condition.
0470 | 
0471 | We did not search around `B=80`.
0472 | 
0473 | We did not try `78`, `79`, `81`, `82`.
0474 | 
0475 | That would have turned the experiment into threshold hunting.
0476 | 
0477 | Instead we asked why all five budgets looked so similar in proportional terms.
0478 | 
0479 | ---
0480 | 
0481 | ## The Strange Stability Underneath the Failure
0482 | 
0483 | The absolute scales were very different.
0484 | 
0485 | The approximate late populations increased from:
0486 | 
0487 | ```text
0488 | ~260 at B=48
0489 | ```
0490 | 
0491 | to:
0492 | 
0493 | ```text
0494 | ~800 at B=128
0495 | ```
0496 | 
0497 | Yet their normalized decline rates were surprisingly similar.
0498 | 
0499 | And something else was even more stable.
0500 | 
0501 | Gross turnover as a fraction of population remained close to:
0502 | 
0503 | ```text
0504 | 0.17 per update
0505 | ```
0506 | 
0507 | across the entire budget family.
0508 | 
0509 | That suggested we might have been asking the wrong kind of stability question.
0510 | 
0511 | We had been looking for:
0512 | 
0513 | ```text
0514 | stable body size
0515 | ```
0516 | 
0517 | But perhaps the substrate was offering:
0518 | 
0519 | ```text
0520 | stable process traffic
0521 | ```
0522 | 
0523 | instead.
0524 | 
0525 | That distinction became V3.
0526 | 
0527 | ---
0528 | 
0529 | ## Stable Size Is Not Stable Process
0530 | 
0531 | V3 abandoned the search for a flat population curve.
0532 | 
0533 | It did not add more budgets.
0534 | 
0535 | It did not alter the V2 slope criterion.
0536 | 
0537 | It asked a genuinely different question:
0538 | 
0539 | > **Can normalized construction and loss flows remain stable even while absolute population drifts?**
0540 | 
0541 | For each update, we measured:
0542 | 
0543 | ```text
0544 | loss / population
0545 | 
0546 | attachments / population
0547 | 
0548 | reoccupation / population
0549 | 
0550 | first occupation / population
0551 | 
0552 | gross turnover / population
0553 | ```
0554 | 
0555 | Then we changed not only computational budget, but starting size.
0556 | 
0557 | The frozen start conditions were:
0558 | 
0559 | ```text
0560 | SMALL      warmup = 8
0561 | 
0562 | MEDIUM     warmup = 14
0563 | 
0564 | LARGE      warmup = 20
0565 | ```
0566 | 
0567 | Crossed with:
0568 | 
0569 | ```text
0570 | B = 48, 64, 80, 96, 128
0571 | ```
0572 | 
0573 | If the process really had a substrate-native statistical regime, it should not depend strongly on whether we started from a smaller or larger crystal.
0574 | 
0575 | ---
0576 | 
0577 | ## What Stayed Stable
0578 | 
0579 | The most striking result was gross turnover.
0580 | 
0581 | Mean late gross turnover fractions across budgets were approximately:
0582 | 
0583 | ```text
0584 | B=48     0.17229
0585 | B=64     0.17150
0586 | B=80     0.17066
0587 | B=96     0.17147
0588 | B=128    0.17132
0589 | ```
0590 | 
0591 | The coefficient of variation across those budget means was only:
0592 | 
0593 | ```text
0594 | 0.0030
0595 | ```
0596 | 
0597 | about three tenths of one percent.
0598 | 
0599 | That is remarkably small.
0600 | 
0601 | And the temporal drift of gross turnover was also tiny.
0602 | 
0603 | The worst absolute late slope across the budget family was around:
0604 | 
0605 | ```text
0606 | 0.00024
0607 | ```
0608 | 
0609 | against a frozen tolerance of:
0610 | 
0611 | ```text
0612 | 0.0025
0613 | ```
0614 | 
0615 | So the gross material traffic was not only similar across budgets.
0616 | 
0617 | It was also approximately flat within the late observation window.
0618 | 
0619 | Meanwhile:
0620 | 
0621 | ```text
0622 | no runs collapsed
0623 | 
0624 | all population gates passed
0625 | 
0626 | all activity gates passed
0627 | 
0628 | all capacity gates passed
0629 | ```
0630 | 
0631 | The crystal continued to undergo substantial material traffic.
0632 | 
0633 | ---
0634 | 
0635 | ## Starting Size Hardly Mattered to Turnover
0636 | 
0637 | Take `B=48`.
0638 | 
0639 | Gross turnover by starting condition was roughly:
0640 | 
0641 | ```text
0642 | small     0.17330
0643 | medium    0.17267
0644 | large     0.17089
0645 | ```
0646 | 
0647 | Loss fraction was also tightly clustered.
0648 | 
0649 | Attachment fraction was tightly clustered.
0650 | 
0651 | Reoccupation fraction was tightly clustered.
0652 | 
0653 | Those metrics easily cleared the predeclared start-size invariance gate.
0654 | 
0655 | The same general pattern persisted across the other budgets.
0656 | 
0657 | At `B=96`, for example, loss, attachment, reoccupation and gross turnover all showed very low sensitivity to starting size.
0658 | 
0659 | Something in the process was genuinely stable.
0660 | 
0661 | But not everything.
0662 | 
0663 | ---
0664 | 
0665 | ## Expansion Was the Weak Point
0666 | 
0667 | The metric that broke the full V3 claim was:
0668 | 
0669 | ```text
0670 | first occupation / population
0671 | ```
0672 | 
0673 | At `B=48`:
0674 | 
0675 | ```text
0676 | small     0.01806
0677 | medium    0.01689
0678 | large     0.01356
0679 | ```
0680 | 
0681 | The coefficient of variation was:
0682 | 
0683 | ```text
0684 | 0.118
0685 | ```
0686 | 
0687 | The frozen maximum was:
0688 | 
0689 | ```text
0690 | 0.100
0691 | ```
0692 | 
0693 | So that gate failed.
0694 | 
0695 | At larger budgets, the start-size dependence weakened:
0696 | 
0697 | ```text
0698 | B=64     CV ~0.092
0699 | B=80     CV ~0.077
0700 | B=96     CV ~0.074
0701 | B=128    CV ~0.063
0702 | ```
0703 | 
0704 | Those passed.
0705 | 
0706 | But V3 required every process metric at every budget to clear the threshold.
0707 | 
0708 | So:
0709 | 
0710 | ```text
0711 | V3 STATUS: FAILED
0712 | ```
0713 | 
0714 | Again.
0715 | 
0716 | Three experiments.
0717 | 
0718 | Three formal failures.
0719 | 
0720 | And yet by now the pattern underneath those failures was much clearer than any single positive result would have been.
0721 | 
0722 | ---
0723 | 
0724 | ## Two Processes Were Hiding Inside One
0725 | 
0726 | The data suggest a useful decomposition.
0727 | 
0728 | One set of flows appears remarkably stable:
0729 | 
0730 | ```text
0731 | LOSS
0732 | 
0733 | ATTACHMENT
0734 | 
0735 | REOCCUPATION
0736 | 
0737 | GROSS MATERIAL TURNOVER
0738 | ```
0739 | 
0740 | Another remains more sensitive to history and scale:
0741 | 
0742 | ```text
0743 | FIRST OCCUPATION
0744 | ```
0745 | 
0746 | That suggests a distinction between:
0747 | 
0748 | ```text
0749 | CONTINUATION
0750 | ```
0751 | 
0752 | and:
0753 | 
0754 | ```text
0755 | EXPANSION
0756 | ```
0757 | 
0758 | The crystal's ongoing material traffic is highly stable across:
0759 | 
0760 | ```text
0761 | different starting sizes
0762 | 
0763 | different computational budgets
0764 | 
0765 | different absolute population scales
0766 | ```
0767 | 
0768 | But occupation of never-before-used territory is more contingent.
0769 | 
0770 | Especially under severe scarcity.
0771 | 
0772 | That is a much more interesting result than a fixed-size crystal would have been.
0773 | 
0774 | ---
0775 | 
0776 | ## Staying and Growing Are Not the Same Problem
0777 | 
0778 | Earlier versions implicitly treated all attachments as equivalent.
0779 | 
0780 | Chapter 20 already broke that assumption by separating:
0781 | 
0782 | ```text
0783 | first occupation
0784 | ```
0785 | 
0786 | from:
0787 | 
0788 | ```text
0789 | reoccupation
0790 | ```
0791 | 
0792 | Chapter 21 now shows why the distinction matters.
0793 | 
0794 | Under scarcity, reoccupation can remain relatively stable while first occupation changes much more strongly.
0795 | 
0796 | So:
0797 | 
0798 | ```text
0799 | STAYING
0800 | ≠
0801 | GROWING
0802 | ```
0803 | 
0804 | Not biologically.
0805 | 
0806 | Operationally.
0807 | 
0808 | The dynamics required to keep material traffic going are not identical to the dynamics required to expand into new territory.
0809 | 
0810 | And because the scheduling policy never knows whether a site is old or new, this distinction emerges from geometry and history rather than from a hard-coded biological category.
0811 | 
0812 | ---
0813 | 
0814 | ## What Does It Cost to Stay?
0815 | 
0816 | We can finally answer the chapter title.
0817 | 
0818 | Not with an energy unit.
0819 | 
0820 | Not with calories.
0821 | 
0822 | Not with ATP.
0823 | 
0824 | Not with a made-up digital metabolism.
0825 | 
0826 | In this substrate, the relevant cost is:
0827 | 
0828 | ```text
0829 | evaluation opportunity
0830 | ```
0831 | 
0832 | The crystal can only continue constructing where computation is actually spent.
0833 | 
0834 | Reduce the number of evaluated frontier sites and the scale of the process falls dramatically.
0835 | 
0836 | Change how those evaluations are allocated and the balance between reoccupation and first occupation changes.
0837 | 
0838 | So the bounded claim is not:
0839 | 
0840 | > The crystal has metabolism.
0841 | 
0842 | It is:
0843 | 
0844 | > **Continued material turnover in the lossy Digital Crystal depends strongly on finite computational opportunity, and the allocation of that opportunity changes whether construction tends toward reuse of lost material or occupation of new sites.**
0845 | 
0846 | That is a real computational constraint.
0847 | 
0848 | And it required no biological metaphor to define.
0849 | 
0850 | ---
0851 | 
0852 | ## Three Failures, One Better Model
0853 | 
0854 | Chapter 21 can be summarized as a sequence of rejected simplifications.
0855 | 
0856 | ### V1
0857 | 
0858 | We predicted a strong two-sided allocation tradeoff.
0859 | 
0860 | High-support scheduling strongly increased reoccupation.
0861 | 
0862 | Low-support scheduling increased first occupation, but not enough to clear the frozen magnitude threshold.
0863 | 
0864 | ```text
0865 | FAILED
0866 | ```
0867 | 
0868 | ### V2
0869 | 
0870 | We predicted that some finite budget would produce approximately stationary population while turnover continued.
0871 | 
0872 | Every tested budget retained substantial turnover.
0873 | 
0874 | But none cleared the frozen population-slope criterion.
0875 | 
0876 | ```text
0877 | FAILED
0878 | ```
0879 | 
0880 | ### V3
0881 | 
0882 | We predicted that the entire normalized process vector would be invariant across starting sizes and budgets.
0883 | 
0884 | Most of it was.
0885 | 
0886 | First occupation under the harshest scarcity retained too much dependence on starting condition.
0887 | 
0888 | ```text
0889 | FAILED
0890 | ```
0891 | 
0892 | The failures progressively removed bad simplifications.
0893 | 
0894 | What remained was more precise.
0895 | 
0896 | ---
0897 | 
0898 | ## What Survived the Hypothesis?
0899 | 
0900 | Chapter 21 contained three formal hypotheses.
0901 | 
0902 | All three failed.
0903 | 
0904 | V1 did not establish the full two-sided allocation tradeoff at the predeclared magnitudes.
0905 | 
0906 | V2 did not establish a finite approximately stationary population regime.
0907 | 
0908 | V3 did not establish invariance of the complete normalized process vector across every tested starting size and budget.
0909 | 
0910 | Those failures remain failures.
0911 | 
0912 | But beneath them, several process-level regularities survived.
0913 | 
0914 | ### Scarce computation changed the scale of the process
0915 | 
0916 | The first result was immediate.
0917 | 
0918 | As computational evaluation budget fell, the size of the lossy crystal fell with it.
0919 | 
0920 | Approximate late populations under neutral scheduling ranged from:
0921 | 
0922 | ```text
0923 | B = 64
0924 | ~381
0925 | ```
0926 | 
0927 | to:
0928 | 
0929 | ```text
0930 | B = 1024
0931 | ~3513
0932 | ```
0933 | 
0934 | while the unlimited condition was of similar large scale.
0935 | 
0936 | So:
0937 | 
0938 | ```text
0939 | AVAILABLE COMPUTATIONAL OPPORTUNITY
0940 | ↓
0941 | PROCESS SCALE
0942 | ```
0943 | 
0944 | is not metaphorical.
0945 | 
0946 | It is measured.
0947 | 
0948 | The crystal can only attempt construction where evaluation opportunity is actually spent.
0949 | 
0950 | This gives us a new substrate-level constraint:
0951 | 
0952 | > **Finite computation limits the scale at which the material process can continue.**
0953 | 
0954 | That is not energy.
0955 | 
0956 | It is not metabolism.
0957 | 
0958 | It is simply computational scarcity.
0959 | 
0960 | ### Scheduling changed what the same budget produced
0961 | 
0962 | At the same budget, local scheduling changed the balance between reoccupation and first occupation.
0963 | 
0964 | High-support scheduling produced much greater reoccupation.
0965 | 
0966 | Low-support scheduling produced more first occupation.
0967 | 
0968 | The full V1 tradeoff failed because the exploration-side magnitude did not clear its frozen threshold.
0969 | 
0970 | But the measured allocation effect remains real.
0971 | 
0972 | So:
0973 | 
0974 | ```text
0975 | SAME COMPUTATIONAL BUDGET
0976 | ≠
0977 | SAME MATERIAL FUTURE
0978 | ```
0979 | 
0980 | and:
0981 | 
0982 | ```text
0983 | WHERE COMPUTATION IS SPENT
0984 | MATTERS
0985 | ```
0986 | 
0987 | The crystal does not know whether it is servicing:
0988 | 
0989 | ```text
0990 | old territory
0991 | ```
0992 | 
0993 | or:
0994 | 
0995 | ```text
0996 | new territory
0997 | ```
0998 | 
0999 | Yet geometry makes different scheduling rules allocate evaluation opportunity differently across those categories.
1000 | 
1001 | ### The Flux Principle became much stronger
1002 | 
1003 | V2 failed to find a truly stationary population.
1004 | 
1005 | But it exposed something more interesting.
1006 | 
1007 | Across the tested budget family, gross turnover remained close to:
1008 | 
1009 | ```text
1010 | 0.17 per update
1011 | ```
1012 | 
1013 | as a fraction of population.
1014 | 
1015 | V3 then measured this more carefully.
1016 | 
1017 | Late gross-turnover fractions were approximately:
1018 | 
1019 | ```text
1020 | B=48     0.17229
1021 | B=64     0.17150
1022 | B=80     0.17066
1023 | B=96     0.17147
1024 | B=128    0.17132
1025 | ```
1026 | 
1027 | The coefficient of variation across those budget means was only:
1028 | 
1029 | ```text
1030 | 0.0030
1031 | ```
1032 | 
1033 | and temporal drift in late gross turnover was also very small.
1034 | 
1035 | That gives us the strongest version yet of the **Flux Principle**:
1036 | 
1037 | > **The Digital Crystal can exhibit highly stable normalized material traffic even while absolute population and spatial scale remain different or drifting.**
1038 | 
1039 | So:
1040 | 
1041 | ```text
1042 | STABLE SIZE
1043 | ≠
1044 | STABLE PROCESS
1045 | ```
1046 | 
1047 | The process can be statistically stable in flow without being stationary in body size.
1048 | 
1049 | ### Phenomenon record
1050 | 
1051 | **Phenomenon:** Stable normalized material flux
1052 | 
1053 | **Status:** **MEASURED**
1054 | 
1055 | **Current bounded description:**
1056 | 
1057 | > Across the tested budgets and starting sizes, normalized loss, attachment, reoccupation and gross material turnover were highly stable, while first occupation retained greater sensitivity to starting condition under severe computational scarcity.
1058 | 
1059 | This is narrower than calling the system homeostatic.
1060 | 
1061 | But it is also more precise.
1062 | 
1063 | The stable object may be:
1064 | 
1065 | ```text
1066 | process traffic
1067 | ```
1068 | 
1069 | rather than:
1070 | 
1071 | ```text
1072 | body size
1073 | ```
1074 | 
1075 | ### Continuation and expansion separated
1076 | 
1077 | The metric that broke V3 was:
1078 | 
1079 | ```text
1080 | first occupation / population
1081 | ```
1082 | 
1083 | especially at the harshest budget.
1084 | 
1085 | Meanwhile:
1086 | 
1087 | ```text
1088 | loss
1089 | attachment
1090 | reoccupation
1091 | gross turnover
1092 | ```
1093 | 
1094 | remained much more stable.
1095 | 
1096 | That exposes another structural decomposition:
1097 | 
1098 | ```text
1099 | CONTINUATION
1100 | =
1101 | ongoing loss / attachment / reuse traffic
1102 | 
1103 | EXPANSION
1104 | =
1105 | occupation of never-before-used territory
1106 | ```
1107 | 
1108 | The two are not the same process.
1109 | 
1110 | Under severe scarcity, continuation remains comparatively regular while expansion remains more dependent on starting history and scale.
1111 | 
1112 | So:
1113 | 
1114 | ```text
1115 | STAYING
1116 | ≠
1117 | GROWING
1118 | ```
1119 | 
1120 | and:
1121 | 
1122 | ```text
1123 | CONTINUATION
1124 | ≠
1125 | EXPANSION
1126 | ```
1127 | 
1128 | These are operational distinctions, not biological ones.
1129 | 
1130 | ### Phenomenon record
1131 | 
1132 | **Phenomenon:** Continuation–expansion separation
1133 | 
1134 | **Status:** **SUPPORTED**
1135 | 
1136 | **Current bounded description:**
1137 | 
1138 | > Under finite computational opportunity, the Digital Crystal's ongoing reuse and turnover dynamics are more stable than its expansion into never-before-occupied territory.
1139 | 
1140 | This result could become important later because it tells us not to treat every attachment as one undifferentiated activity.
1141 | 
1142 | Chapter 20 already separated:
1143 | 
1144 | ```text
1145 | first occupation
1146 | ```
1147 | 
1148 | from:
1149 | 
1150 | ```text
1151 | reoccupation
1152 | ```
1153 | 
1154 | Chapter 21 shows that those categories respond differently to scarcity.
1155 | 
1156 | ### A caution about the apparent turnover invariant
1157 | 
1158 | The striking stability around:
1159 | 
1160 | ```text
1161 | ~0.171
1162 | ```
1163 | 
1164 | should not yet be promoted into a deep emergent law.
1165 | 
1166 | The loss rate is frozen at:
1167 | 
1168 | ```text
1169 | δ = 0.08
1170 | ```
1171 | 
1172 | and rapid reoccupation is already known from Chapter 20.
1173 | 
1174 | So part of the observed gross-turnover stability may arise mechanically from:
1175 | 
1176 | ```text
1177 | expected losses
1178 | +
1179 | rapid replacement
1180 | ```
1181 | 
1182 | rather than from a new self-organized invariant.
1183 | 
1184 | That means the phenomenon is real:
1185 | 
1186 | ```text
1187 | stable normalized turnover
1188 | ```
1189 | 
1190 | but the explanation remains open.
1191 | 
1192 | A future audit should compare:
1193 | 
1194 | ```text
1195 | OBSERVED TURNOVER
1196 | -
1197 | MECHANICALLY EXPECTED TURNOVER
1198 | ```
1199 | 
1200 | before claiming a deeper substrate law.
1201 | 
1202 | This does not weaken the chapter.
1203 | 
1204 | It tells us exactly what remains to be explained.
1205 | 
1206 | ### Connection to the Interface Principle
1207 | 
1208 | Chapter 20 showed that loss creates new frontier.
1209 | 
1210 | Chapter 21 now shows that finite computation determines which frontier opportunities can actually be serviced.
1211 | 
1212 | So the interface story becomes:
1213 | 
1214 | ```text
1215 | LOSS
1216 | ↓
1217 | CREATES FRONTIER
1218 | 
1219 | FRONTIER
1220 | ↓
1221 | CREATES OPPORTUNITY
1222 | 
1223 | FINITE BUDGET
1224 | ↓
1225 | SELECTS WHICH OPPORTUNITIES ARE EVALUATED
1226 | 
1227 | SCHEDULING
1228 | ↓
1229 | CHANGES WHICH MATERIAL FUTURE OCCURS
1230 | ```
1231 | 
1232 | That is a much stronger substrate picture than simply saying:
1233 | 
1234 | ```text
1235 | the crystal has a boundary
1236 | ```
1237 | 
1238 | The relevant object is increasingly:
1239 | 
1240 | > **the dynamically generated set of construction opportunities together with the finite computation available to act on them.**
1241 | 
1242 | ### What this phenomenon does not establish
1243 | 
1244 | The surviving phenomena do **not** establish:
1245 | 
1246 | - metabolism,
1247 | - homeostasis,
1248 | - self-maintenance,
1249 | - energy use,
1250 | - resource sensing,
1251 | - active prioritization,
1252 | - a fixed sustainable body size,
1253 | - or life.
1254 | 
1255 | They establish something narrower:
1256 | 
1257 | > **Finite computational opportunity constrains the scale and allocation of Digital Crystal construction. Under those constraints, gross normalized turnover can remain strikingly stable while expansion remains more sensitive to history and scarcity.**
1258 | 
1259 | That belongs in the project-wide phenomenon record independently of the three failed primary hypotheses.
1260 | 
1261 | ---
1262 | 
1263 | ## What We Actually Earned
1264 | 
1265 | The strongest description of the Chapter 21 system is:
1266 | 
1267 | > **A lossy Digital Crystal under finite computational opportunity exhibits persistent material turnover whose gross normalized traffic is strikingly stable across budget, population scale and starting condition, while expansion into never-before-occupied territory remains more sensitive to history and scarcity.**
1268 | 
1269 | That statement is much narrower than:
1270 | 
1271 | ```text
1272 | homeostasis
1273 | ```
1274 | 
1275 | or:
1276 | 
1277 | ```text
1278 | metabolism
1279 | ```
1280 | 
1281 | or:
1282 | 
1283 | ```text
1284 | self-maintenance
1285 | ```
1286 | 
1287 | And that is exactly why it is useful.
1288 | 
1289 | The mechanism exists without those words.
1290 | 
1291 | ---
1292 | 
1293 | ## Another Ladder
1294 | 
1295 | The project now has another separation:
1296 | 
1297 | ```text
1298 | MATERIAL EXISTS
1299 | ↓
1300 | MATERIAL TURNS OVER
1301 | ↓
1302 | TURNOVER CONTINUES UNDER SCARCITY
1303 | ↓
1304 | NORMALIZED TURNOVER CAN BE STABLE
1305 | ↓
1306 | EXPANSION REMAINS HISTORY-SENSITIVE
1307 | ```
1308 | 
1309 | And another set of distinctions:
1310 | 
1311 | ```text
1312 | STABLE SIZE
1313 | ≠
1314 | STABLE PROCESS
1315 | 
1316 | NET GROWTH
1317 | ≠
1318 | GROSS TURNOVER
1319 | 
1320 | REOCCUPATION
1321 | ≠
1322 | FIRST OCCUPATION
1323 | 
1324 | CONTINUATION
1325 | ≠
1326 | EXPANSION
1327 | ```
1328 | 
1329 | We should keep all four.
1330 | 
1331 | They will matter later.
1332 | 
1333 | ---
1334 | 
1335 | ## Why We Stop Here
1336 | 
1337 | It would be easy to run V4.
1338 | 
1339 | We could relax the first-occupation CV threshold.
1340 | 
1341 | Remove `B=48`.
1342 | 
1343 | Change the starting sizes.
1344 | 
1345 | Wait longer.
1346 | 
1347 | Use a different late window.
1348 | 
1349 | None of those would ask a genuinely new question.
1350 | 
1351 | They would turn Chapter 21 into a search for a passing definition.
1352 | 
1353 | So we stop.
1354 | 
1355 | The chapter has already done its job.
1356 | 
1357 | It showed us that the Digital Crystal under loss and scarcity is not well described by a single scalar such as:
1358 | 
1359 | ```text
1360 | population
1361 | ```
1362 | 
1363 | The more stable object may be the flow itself.
1364 | 
1365 | And now a harder problem appears.
1366 | 
1367 | ---
1368 | 
1369 | ## Is There Actually a Thing Here?
1370 | 
1371 | Throughout Chapters 20 and 21 we have increasingly spoken about:
1372 | 
1373 | ```text
1374 | the process
1375 | ```
1376 | 
1377 | But what exactly is that process?
1378 | 
1379 | Is it one coherent entity?
1380 | 
1381 | Or are we looking at a large stochastic field whose local statistics happen to be stable?
1382 | 
1383 | A connected shape is not enough.
1384 | 
1385 | A stable turnover rate is not enough.
1386 | 
1387 | A persistent region is not enough.
1388 | 
1389 | If there is something individual-like here, we should be able to earn that claim experimentally.
1390 | 
1391 | So the next question is not:
1392 | 
1393 | > How does the crystal maintain itself?
1394 | 
1395 | We have not earned that.
1396 | 
1397 | It is:
1398 | 
1399 | > **Does this continuing process occupy a causally coherent region, or are we still looking at a field with no natural individual?**
1400 | 
1401 | That is the next boundary.
1402 | 
1403 | The crystal can grow.
1404 | 
1405 | It can be perturbed.
1406 | 
1407 | It can carry material history.
1408 | 
1409 | It can lose material.
1410 | 
1411 | It can reoccupy what vanished.
1412 | 
1413 | It can continue under computational scarcity.
1414 | 
1415 | Its gross material traffic can remain strikingly stable even while its size drifts.
1416 | 
1417 | But none of that tells us whether there is actually **one thing** there.
1418 | 
1419 | Chapter 22 will try to find out.
```

```markdown
0001 | +++
0002 | title = "22: Is the Crystal a Thing or a Flow?"
0003 | date = "2026-08-12T21:47:00+01:00"
0004 | draft = false
0005 | description = "After finding stable turnover, causal locality and dynamically generated interfaces, Chapter 22 asks whether the Digital Crystal is best understood as a bounded thing or as a coherent process in space and time."
0006 | categories = ["Programming", "Artificial Life"]
0007 | tags = ["Digital Life", "Digital Crystal", "Causality", "Process", "Interfaces", "Flux", "Experiments"]
0008 | series = ["Digital Life From First Principles"]
0009 | +++
0010 | 
0011 | Chapter 21 ended with a question that sounded obvious.
0012 | 
0013 | Is there actually one thing here?
0014 | 
0015 | For most of the book, we had spoken naturally about:
0016 | 
0017 | ```text
0018 | the crystal
0019 | ```
0020 | 
0021 | as though the noun had already been earned.
0022 | 
0023 | There was a connected occupied structure.
0024 | 
0025 | It grew.
0026 | 
0027 | It could be perturbed.
0028 | 
0029 | It could carry historical material.
0030 | 
0031 | It could lose material.
0032 | 
0033 | Lost sites could be reoccupied.
0034 | 
0035 | Under finite computational opportunity, large amounts of material could turn over while the process continued.
0036 | 
0037 | And in Chapter 21, normalized gross turnover became strikingly stable even while absolute population and scale changed.
0038 | 
0039 | That made the question harder, not easier.
0040 | 
0041 | A stable process is not automatically an individual.
0042 | 
0043 | A connected shape is not automatically an individual.
0044 | 
0045 | A region that persists is not automatically an individual.
0046 | 
0047 | And a system with local causal relationships is not automatically an individual.
0048 | 
0049 | So Chapter 22 began with a very cautious question:
0050 | 
0051 | > **Does the continuing Digital Crystal occupy a causally coherent region that is meaningfully different from the surrounding field?**
0052 | 
0053 | That was already weaker than asking whether the crystal was an organism.
0054 | 
0055 | It was weaker than asking whether it had a self.
0056 | 
0057 | It was weaker than asking whether it was autonomous.
0058 | 
0059 | But after the experiments in this chapter, even that framing needs revision.
0060 | 
0061 | The most interesting object may not be a bounded region of occupied material at all.
0062 | 
0063 | It may be the process moving through it.
0064 | 
0065 | ---
0066 | 
0067 | ## The Temptation to Draw a Boundary
0068 | 
0069 | We have a strong habit of looking for boundaries.
0070 | 
0071 | If something is alive, we expect an inside and an outside.
0072 | 
0073 | A bacterium has a membrane.
0074 | 
0075 | A cell has a boundary.
0076 | 
0077 | An animal has skin.
0078 | 
0079 | Even a machine usually has a chassis.
0080 | 
0081 | So when the Digital Crystal began to exhibit:
0082 | 
0083 | ```text
0084 | persistent turnover
0085 | local causal effects
0086 | history-dependent material
0087 | loss
0088 | reoccupation
0089 | scarce computation
0090 | ```
0091 | 
0092 | the natural question was:
0093 | 
0094 | ```text
0095 | where does the thing end?
0096 | ```
0097 | 
0098 | But that question already contains a hypothesis.
0099 | 
0100 | It assumes there is a privileged spatial boundary to discover.
0101 | 
0102 | The crystal may not work that way.
0103 | 
0104 | Chapter 18 had already shown that causally useful historical state lives near the active construction frontier.
0105 | 
0106 | Chapter 20 showed that loss can create new frontier inside previously occupied material.
0107 | 
0108 | Chapter 21 showed that finite computation determines which frontier opportunities are actually serviced.
0109 | 
0110 | So the active region is not a fixed shell.
0111 | 
0112 | It is dynamically generated.
0113 | 
0114 | A site can be:
0115 | 
0116 | ```text
0117 | inert bulk
0118 | ```
0119 | 
0120 | at one moment and become:
0121 | 
0122 | ```text
0123 | active construction opportunity
0124 | ```
0125 | 
0126 | after nearby material disappears.
0127 | 
0128 | That should have made us suspicious of any definition based only on a centered radius.
0129 | 
0130 | But we tried one anyway.
0131 | 
0132 | ---
0133 | 
0134 | ## V1: Look for Predictive Coherence
0135 | 
0136 | The first Chapter 22 experiment asked a deliberately modest question.
0137 | 
0138 | Not:
0139 | 
0140 | > Is this region an individual?
0141 | 
0142 | But:
0143 | 
0144 | > **Does any predeclared spatial scale contain predictive information about its own future beyond what is already available from the surrounding lattice?**
0145 | 
0146 | The substrate was frozen:
0147 | 
0148 | ```text
0149 | Digital Crystal v1
0150 | loss rate δ = 0.08
0151 | neutral evaluation budget B = 96
0152 | ```
0153 | 
0154 | We tested five candidate scales:
0155 | 
0156 | ```text
0157 | R / R_eff
0158 | 
0159 | 0.30
0160 | 0.45
0161 | 0.60
0162 | 0.75
0163 | 0.90
0164 | ```
0165 | 
0166 | At each measurement point, we divided the system into:
0167 | 
0168 | ```text
0169 | S_t
0170 | candidate region
0171 | 
0172 | E_t
0173 | surrounding active environment
0174 | ```
0175 | 
0176 | and asked whether adding the current candidate-region state improved prediction of that region's later state.
0177 | 
0178 | The state representation was process-oriented.
0179 | 
0180 | It included measurements such as:
0181 | 
0182 | ```text
0183 | population
0184 | frontier density
0185 | recent attachment
0186 | loss
0187 | reoccupation
0188 | first occupation
0189 | gross turnover
0190 | angular process structure
0191 | ```
0192 | 
0193 | rather than occupancy alone.
0194 | 
0195 | The central quantity was:
0196 | 
0197 | ```text
0198 | Δ_self
0199 | =
0200 | prediction using S_t + E_t
0201 | -
0202 | prediction using E_t alone
0203 | ```
0204 | 
0205 | Then we compared that with an observer-only spatial null.
0206 | 
0207 | The hope was simple.
0208 | 
0209 | If one scale contained unusually strong information about its own future, perhaps we had found a candidate process boundary.
0210 | 
0211 | ---
0212 | 
0213 | ## At First, V1 Looked Promising
0214 | 
0215 | The excess predictive-coherence values were:
0216 | 
0217 | ```text
0218 | R = 0.30    0.1691
0219 | R = 0.45    0.0447
0220 | R = 0.60    0.0611
0221 | R = 0.75    0.1666
0222 | R = 0.90    0.2906
0223 | ```
0224 | 
0225 | The largest value occurred at:
0226 | 
0227 | ```text
0228 | 0.90 R_eff
0229 | ```
0230 | 
0231 | and it was far above the frozen minimum effect of:
0232 | 
0233 | ```text
0234 | 0.02
0235 | ```
0236 | 
0237 | Looking only at the observed statistic, this seemed spectacular.
0238 | 
0239 | A large outer region appeared to contain substantial additional information about its own future.
0240 | 
0241 | But we had predeclared a family-level permutation test for exactly this reason.
0242 | 
0243 | We were allowed to look across all five frozen scales.
0244 | 
0245 | So the null had to preserve that search.
0246 | 
0247 | The observed family maximum was:
0248 | 
0249 | ```text
0250 | 0.2906
0251 | ```
0252 | 
0253 | The permutation-null mean was approximately:
0254 | 
0255 | ```text
0256 | 0.2569
0257 | ```
0258 | 
0259 | and its 95th percentile was approximately:
0260 | 
0261 | ```text
0262 | 0.2947
0263 | ```
0264 | 
0265 | The one-sided permutation result was:
0266 | 
0267 | ```text
0268 | p ≈ 0.0849
0269 | ```
0270 | 
0271 | So the formal result was:
0272 | 
0273 | ```text
0274 | FAILED
0275 | ```
0276 | 
0277 | The observed maximum did not beat the frozen family-level null at the required threshold.
0278 | 
0279 | We did not tune the radius.
0280 | 
0281 | We did not add more scales.
0282 | 
0283 | We did not increase decoder complexity.
0284 | 
0285 | The prediction had failed.
0286 | 
0287 | ---
0288 | 
0289 | ## A Large Statistic Can Still Be a Weak Boundary
0290 | 
0291 | This result is useful because the raw number looked so convincing.
0292 | 
0293 | A value near:
0294 | 
0295 | ```text
0296 | 0.29 excess R²
0297 | ```
0298 | 
0299 | feels large.
0300 | 
0301 | But the null also generated large apparent predictive coherence.
0302 | 
0303 | That tells us something important.
0304 | 
0305 | The Digital Crystal contains strong shared process structure.
0306 | 
0307 | Large regions of the system can predict later large-region state simply because they share:
0308 | 
0309 | ```text
0310 | growth phase
0311 | population scale
0312 | frontier geometry
0313 | turnover regime
0314 | global stochastic context
0315 | ```
0316 | 
0317 | Predictability alone does not isolate a thing.
0318 | 
0319 | It may only reveal that the field is structured.
0320 | 
0321 | So:
0322 | 
0323 | ```text
0324 | PREDICTIVE COHERENCE
0325 | ≠
0326 | INDIVIDUATION
0327 | ```
0328 | 
0329 | and even:
0330 | 
0331 | ```text
0332 | LARGE PREDICTIVE COHERENCE
0333 | ≠
0334 | PRIVILEGED BOUNDARY
0335 | ```
0336 | 
0337 | That was already enough to weaken the original framing.
0338 | 
0339 | But there was another problem.
0340 | 
0341 | ---
0342 | 
0343 | ## The V1 Audit
0344 | 
0345 | After running V1, we examined the experimental geometry more carefully.
0346 | 
0347 | Several issues made the test less clean than we wanted.
0348 | 
0349 | First, the candidate system was defined as a centered region.
0350 | 
0351 | But the earlier chapters increasingly suggest that the causally active object lives near dynamically generated interfaces rather than at the geometric center.
0352 | 
0353 | Second, the observer-null environment was not exactly the same geometry as the real annular environment.
0354 | 
0355 | That makes the real-versus-null comparison harder to interpret.
0356 | 
0357 | Third, the construction of the measurement support used future extent in part of the bookkeeping.
0358 | 
0359 | Even if that did not create the entire result, future-dependent geometry should not be allowed into a clean present-state predictor.
0360 | 
0361 | And fourth, the scrambled region was generated independently at different times, so the null did not preserve a stable temporal identity for the candidate region.
0362 | 
0363 | These are not reasons to rescue V1.
0364 | 
0365 | They are reasons to record it as:
0366 | 
0367 | ```text
0368 | PRIMARY PREDICTIVE HYPOTHESIS
0369 | FAILED
0370 | 
0371 | PROTOCOL
0372 | ALSO NOT CLEAN ENOUGH
0373 | TO SUPPORT A STRONG BOUNDARY CLAIM
0374 | ```
0375 | 
0376 | That distinction matters.
0377 | 
0378 | A failed experiment can still be informative while also teaching us how not to formulate the next one.
0379 | 
0380 | ---
0381 | 
0382 | ## V2: Stop Predicting and Perturb the Boundary
0383 | 
0384 | If predictive coherence was too indirect, perhaps causality could give us a stronger test.
0385 | 
0386 | So V2 asked:
0387 | 
0388 | > **Does the V1 carry-forward boundary at 0.90 R_eff localize causal consequences more strongly than an ordinary interior pseudo-boundary at 0.60 R_eff?**
0389 | 
0390 | The test was intervention-based.
0391 | 
0392 | At a checkpoint, we removed exactly:
0393 | 
0394 | ```text
0395 | K = 16
0396 | ```
0397 | 
0398 | occupied cells either just inside or just outside a boundary.
0399 | 
0400 | The intervention cells were matched on:
0401 | 
0402 | ```text
0403 | occupied-neighbour count
0404 | absolute distance from boundary
0405 | exact intervention count
0406 | ```
0407 | 
0408 | and the future evolution used the cell-keyed common-random-number coupling developed in Chapter 17.
0409 | 
0410 | For each boundary we measured whether:
0411 | 
0412 | ```text
0413 | inside perturbation
0414 | affected inner region more
0415 | 
0416 | and
0417 | 
0418 | outside perturbation
0419 | affected outer region more
0420 | ```
0421 | 
0422 | The localization score was:
0423 | 
0424 | ```text
0425 | (inside → inner - outside → inner)
0426 | +
0427 | (outside → outer - inside → outer)
0428 | ```
0429 | 
0430 | Then we compared:
0431 | 
0432 | ```text
0433 | candidate boundary
0434 | 0.90 R_eff
0435 | ```
0436 | 
0437 | with:
0438 | 
0439 | ```text
0440 | interior pseudo-boundary
0441 | 0.60 R_eff
0442 | ```
0443 | 
0444 | If the outer candidate boundary was privileged, its causal localization should be stronger.
0445 | 
0446 | ---
0447 | 
0448 | ## V2 Had a Serious Practical Limitation
0449 | 
0450 | The experiment requested:
0451 | 
0452 | ```text
0453 | 96 groups
0454 | ```
0455 | 
0456 | but only:
0457 | 
0458 | ```text
0459 | 25
0460 | ```
0461 | 
0462 | produced usable matched interventions.
0463 | 
0464 | That is a usable fraction of approximately:
0465 | 
0466 | ```text
0467 | 0.260
0468 | ```
0469 | 
0470 | Seventy-one groups were skipped because the frozen matching conditions could not be satisfied.
0471 | 
0472 | That is a real limitation.
0473 | 
0474 | It means the confirmatory sample is much smaller than intended.
0475 | 
0476 | But it does not rescue the hypothesis.
0477 | 
0478 | Because the direction of the result was not merely weak.
0479 | 
0480 | It was opposite.
0481 | 
0482 | ---
0483 | 
0484 | ## The Candidate Boundary Was Not Special
0485 | 
0486 | The candidate localization mean was approximately:
0487 | 
0488 | ```text
0489 | 0.03772
0490 | ```
0491 | 
0492 | The interior pseudo-boundary localization mean was approximately:
0493 | 
0494 | ```text
0495 | 0.04497
0496 | ```
0497 | 
0498 | So:
0499 | 
0500 | ```text
0501 | candidate - control
0502 | =
0503 | -0.00724
0504 | ```
0505 | 
0506 | with a 95% interval of approximately:
0507 | 
0508 | ```text
0509 | [-0.01399, -0.00020]
0510 | ```
0511 | 
0512 | The one-sided test for candidate superiority gave:
0513 | 
0514 | ```text
0515 | p ≈ 0.9693
0516 | ```
0517 | 
0518 | The candidate boundary was not more causally privileged.
0519 | 
0520 | The interior pseudo-boundary localized causal effects slightly more strongly under this protocol.
0521 | 
0522 | So:
0523 | 
0524 | ```text
0525 | PRIVILEGED OUTER CAUSAL BOUNDARY
0526 | FAILED
0527 | ```
0528 | 
0529 | Again.
0530 | 
0531 | That is a much stronger negative result than V1.
0532 | 
0533 | V1 could be criticized as an observational prediction problem.
0534 | 
0535 | V2 directly intervened.
0536 | 
0537 | And the boundary still did not win.
0538 | 
0539 | ---
0540 | 
0541 | ## But Something Else Was Clearly There
0542 | 
0543 | Now look at the component measurements.
0544 | 
0545 | For the candidate boundary:
0546 | 
0547 | ```text
0548 | inside perturbation → inner response     0.02709
0549 | outside perturbation → inner response    0.00896
0550 | 
0551 | inside perturbation → outer response     0.00508
0552 | outside perturbation → outer response    0.02467
0553 | ```
0554 | 
0555 | For the interior control boundary:
0556 | 
0557 | ```text
0558 | inside perturbation → inner response     0.02869
0559 | outside perturbation → inner response    0.00359
0560 | 
0561 | inside perturbation → outer response     0.00545
0562 | outside perturbation → outer response    0.02530
0563 | ```
0564 | 
0565 | That is extremely informative.
0566 | 
0567 | At **both** boundaries:
0568 | 
0569 | ```text
0570 | inside perturbations
0571 | affected the inside more
0572 | 
0573 | and
0574 | 
0575 | outside perturbations
0576 | affected the outside more
0577 | ```
0578 | 
0579 | So although the outer candidate boundary was not privileged, causal influence was spatially localized.
0580 | 
0581 | The failed boundary hypothesis exposed a different phenomenon.
0582 | 
0583 | ---
0584 | 
0585 | ## What Survived the Hypothesis?
0586 | 
0587 | The strongest interpretation failed.
0588 | 
0589 | We did not find:
0590 | 
0591 | ```text
0592 | A SPECIAL OUTER BOUNDARY
0593 | THAT LOCALIZES CAUSAL EFFECTS
0594 | MORE STRONGLY THAN
0595 | AN INTERIOR PSEUDO-BOUNDARY
0596 | ```
0597 | 
0598 | But we did find:
0599 | 
0600 | ```text
0601 | SPATIAL CAUSAL LOCALITY
0602 | ```
0603 | 
0604 | Perturbations preferentially affected nearby regions on the same side of either tested boundary.
0605 | 
0606 | That gives us a clean phenomenon record.
0607 | 
0608 | ### Phenomenon record
0609 | 
0610 | **Phenomenon:** Spatial causal locality
0611 | 
0612 | **Status:** **MEASURED**
0613 | 
0614 | **Current bounded description:**
0615 | 
0616 | > Under the frozen Chapter 22 V2 intervention, local material removal produced spatially localized causal consequences across both the outer candidate boundary and the interior pseudo-boundary, but the outer candidate boundary was not preferentially privileged.
0617 | 
0618 | This is not individuality.
0619 | 
0620 | It is not causal closure.
0621 | 
0622 | It is not autonomy.
0623 | 
0624 | It is a local property of the field.
0625 | 
0626 | And that changes how we should understand the Digital Crystal.
0627 | 
0628 | ---
0629 | 
0630 | ## The Backward Audit Changes Chapter 22
0631 | 
0632 | At this point we went back through the previous chapters.
0633 | 
0634 | That changed the interpretation of Chapter 22 more than another radius sweep would have.
0635 | 
0636 | Several phenomena that had appeared separately now line up.
0637 | 
0638 | Chapter 18 showed:
0639 | 
0640 | ```text
0641 | persistent historical state
0642 | matters while coupled to
0643 | active construction interface
0644 | ```
0645 | 
0646 | Chapter 20 showed:
0647 | 
0648 | ```text
0649 | loss
0650 | creates new frontier
0651 | ```
0652 | 
0653 | Chapter 21 showed:
0654 | 
0655 | ```text
0656 | finite computation
0657 | selects which frontier opportunities
0658 | are serviced
0659 | ```
0660 | 
0661 | And Chapter 22 now shows:
0662 | 
0663 | ```text
0664 | causal consequences
0665 | are spatially local
0666 | ```
0667 | 
0668 | Put those together and the important object begins to look less like:
0669 | 
0670 | ```text
0671 | a centered occupied body
0672 | ```
0673 | 
0674 | and more like:
0675 | 
0676 | ```text
0677 | a dynamically generated field
0678 | of active construction opportunities
0679 | ```
0680 | 
0681 | That is a major change.
0682 | 
0683 | ---
0684 | 
0685 | ## The Interface Principle
0686 | 
0687 | The previous chapters now support a recurring principle.
0688 | 
0689 | > **In the Digital Crystal, causal relevance is concentrated around dynamically active interfaces where state changes can influence future construction.**
0690 | 
0691 | The interface is not merely the visual outside edge.
0692 | 
0693 | Chapter 20 destroyed that simplification.
0694 | 
0695 | Loss can create new empty sites inside the existing structure.
0696 | 
0697 | Those sites become active local boundaries.
0698 | 
0699 | So the interface is better described as:
0700 | 
0701 | > **the dynamically generated set of locations where the process currently has an opportunity to change material state.**
0702 | 
0703 | That definition does not care whether the opportunity is:
0704 | 
0705 | ```text
0706 | outside the old crystal
0707 | ```
0708 | 
0709 | or:
0710 | 
0711 | ```text
0712 | inside a vacancy created by loss
0713 | ```
0714 | 
0715 | It cares whether the process can act there.
0716 | 
0717 | ---
0718 | 
0719 | ## The Flux Principle
0720 | 
0721 | Chapter 20 also taught us that a snapshot can hide enormous activity.
0722 | 
0723 | A population might change by only:
0724 | 
0725 | ```text
0726 | +100
0727 | ```
0728 | 
0729 | while the same update contains:
0730 | 
0731 | ```text
0732 | +600 attachments
0733 | -500 losses
0734 | ```
0735 | 
0736 | Chapter 21 then showed that normalized gross material traffic can remain extremely stable even while:
0737 | 
0738 | ```text
0739 | population differs
0740 | scale differs
0741 | first occupation differs
0742 | absolute size drifts
0743 | ```
0744 | 
0745 | That gives us another principle:
0746 | 
0747 | > **The dynamically stable object may be the flow of construction and loss rather than the stock of occupied material.**
0748 | 
0749 | This matters enormously for Chapter 22.
0750 | 
0751 | If we define the system using:
0752 | 
0753 | ```text
0754 | occupied cells inside radius R
0755 | ```
0756 | 
0757 | we may be measuring accumulated residue rather than the active process.
0758 | 
0759 | The bulk is what remains.
0760 | 
0761 | The flux is what is happening.
0762 | 
0763 | ---
0764 | 
0765 | ## The Lossy-History Principle
0766 | 
0767 | The history experiments also point in the same direction.
0768 | 
0769 | Across Chapters 14, 16, 17 and 19, the Digital Crystal repeatedly preserved coarse process structure more easily than fine identity.
0770 | 
0771 | We saw:
0772 | 
0773 | ```text
0774 | source family
0775 | recoverable
0776 | 
0777 | exact temporal ordering
0778 | not recoverable
0779 | ```
0780 | 
0781 | Then:
0782 | 
0783 | ```text
0784 | coarse pulse timing
0785 | matters
0786 | 
0787 | sender identity
0788 | not recovered
0789 | ```
0790 | 
0791 | Then:
0792 | 
0793 | ```text
0794 | different histories
0795 | cause different particular futures
0796 | 
0797 | stable history signature
0798 | not recovered
0799 | ```
0800 | 
0801 | Then:
0802 | 
0803 | ```text
0804 | different traces
0805 | remain persistent
0806 | remain accessible
0807 | remain distinguishable
0808 | 
0809 | yet
0810 | common-challenge readout fails
0811 | ```
0812 | 
0813 | The process does not behave like an event tape.
0814 | 
0815 | It integrates history in a lossy way.
0816 | 
0817 | That suggests that looking for a persistent object with a clean internal record may again be the wrong abstraction.
0818 | 
0819 | ---
0820 | 
0821 | ## Maybe the Crystal Is a Process First
0822 | 
0823 | The accumulating picture is now:
0824 | 
0825 | ```text
0826 | LOCAL RULE
0827 | ↓
0828 | ATTACHMENT / LOSS EVENTS
0829 | ↓
0830 | DYNAMICALLY GENERATED INTERFACES
0831 | ↓
0832 | FINITE COMPUTATIONAL SELECTION
0833 | ↓
0834 | MATERIAL TURNOVER
0835 | ↓
0836 | NEW INTERFACES
0837 | ↓
0838 | MORE EVENTS
0839 | ```
0840 | 
0841 | The occupied crystal is produced by that process.
0842 | 
0843 | But it may not be the best representation of the process.
0844 | 
0845 | A useful analogy is a flame.
0846 | 
0847 | A flame is not identical to a fixed collection of molecules.
0848 | 
0849 | Material enters.
0850 | 
0851 | Material leaves.
0852 | 
0853 | The shape can persist while the constituents change.
0854 | 
0855 | But we should be careful.
0856 | 
0857 | The Digital Crystal is not a flame.
0858 | 
0859 | It has no chemistry.
0860 | 
0861 | No combustion.
0862 | 
0863 | No energy claim.
0864 | 
0865 | The analogy is useful only for one distinction:
0866 | 
0867 | ```text
0868 | persistent process
0869 | ≠
0870 | persistent material identity
0871 | ```
0872 | 
0873 | That is exactly the distinction Chapter 22 now forces us to consider.
0874 | 
0875 | ---
0876 | 
0877 | ## A Thing or a Flow?
0878 | 
0879 | The title question can now be answered only partially.
0880 | 
0881 | Is the crystal a thing?
0882 | 
0883 | We have not established that.
0884 | 
0885 | Is it a flow?
0886 | 
0887 | We have strong evidence that flow is an important part of the correct description.
0888 | 
0889 | But even that can be overclaimed.
0890 | 
0891 | A stable statistical field is not automatically a coherent process.
0892 | 
0893 | A fluid can have stable local statistics without containing a natural individual.
0894 | 
0895 | A reaction-diffusion system can have persistent patterns without those patterns being autonomous entities.
0896 | 
0897 | So the new question is not:
0898 | 
0899 | ```text
0900 | IS THIS AN INDIVIDUAL?
0901 | ```
0902 | 
0903 | It is:
0904 | 
0905 | > **Can the causally active process itself be localized and tracked through space-time more successfully than a static occupied region can?**
0906 | 
0907 | That is a different experiment.
0908 | 
0909 | And it is the one Chapter 22 has now earned.
0910 | 
0911 | ---
0912 | 
0913 | ## The Moving Causal Interface
0914 | 
0915 | Chapter 18 gave us another clue.
0916 | 
0917 | Historical material became irrelevant when the growth frontier moved away from it.
0918 | 
0919 | The material could remain forever.
0920 | 
0921 | Its causal availability moved.
0922 | 
0923 | That means the active locus of the process itself has spatial motion.
0924 | 
0925 | Not cell motion.
0926 | 
0927 | The cells are fixed to the lattice.
0928 | 
0929 | What moves is:
0930 | 
0931 | ```text
0932 | where causal construction is happening
0933 | ```
0934 | 
0935 | Chapter 20 then made this more complicated.
0936 | 
0937 | Loss creates new local active regions.
0938 | 
0939 | So the active process can:
0940 | 
0941 | ```text
0942 | move outward
0943 | appear internally
0944 | split across multiple interfaces
0945 | collapse locally
0946 | reappear after loss
0947 | ```
0948 | 
0949 | That begins to look like a spatiotemporal field.
0950 | 
0951 | Again, we must not call it a wave.
0952 | 
0953 | We have not measured:
0954 | 
0955 | ```text
0956 | phase
0957 | lag-distance relation
0958 | propagation velocity
0959 | dispersion
0960 | wave equation
0961 | ```
0962 | 
0963 | But we can state:
0964 | 
0965 | ```text
0966 | ACTIVE CAUSAL SUPPORT
0967 | CHANGES POSITION THROUGH TIME
0968 | ```
0969 | 
0970 | That is already enough to motivate a new representation.
0971 | 
0972 | ---
0973 | 
0974 | ## The Propagating-Field Hypothesis Remains Open
0975 | 
0976 | Chapter 13 gave us strong short-range coherent motion after removing global radial expansion.
0977 | 
0978 | Chapter 18 exposed a moving causal aperture.
0979 | 
0980 | Chapter 19 produced persistent directional material traces.
0981 | 
0982 | Chapter 22 now gives us spatial causal locality.
0983 | 
0984 | Those observations may eventually connect.
0985 | 
0986 | A broader hypothesis is:
0987 | 
0988 | > **Some of the apparent object-like or coordinated behavior may arise from a spatially propagating dynamical field rather than from independent bounded objects.**
0989 | 
0990 | For now:
0991 | 
0992 | ```text
0993 | PROPAGATING-FIELD HYPOTHESIS
0994 | OPEN
0995 | ```
0996 | 
0997 | It should remain open until we measure something literal.
0998 | 
0999 | For example:
1000 | 
1001 | ```text
1002 | activity at position x,t
1003 | ↓
1004 | predictable lag
1005 | activity at position x+d,t+τ
1006 | ```
1007 | 
1008 | If the lag peak changes systematically with distance, then a propagation velocity becomes measurable.
1009 | 
1010 | If not, the field interpretation should weaken.
1011 | 
1012 | No metaphor should substitute for that test.
1013 | 
1014 | ---
1015 | 
1016 | ## What Chapter 22 Did Not Establish
1017 | 
1018 | We should be explicit.
1019 | 
1020 | Chapter 22 did **not** establish:
1021 | 
1022 | ```text
1023 | individuality
1024 | autonomy
1025 | causal closure
1026 | self
1027 | organism
1028 | agency
1029 | life
1030 | ```
1031 | 
1032 | It also did not establish:
1033 | 
1034 | ```text
1035 | a privileged outer boundary
1036 | a unique predictive scale
1037 | a wave
1038 | a coherent moving entity
1039 | ```
1040 | 
1041 | And V2 had an important protocol limitation:
1042 | 
1043 | ```text
1044 | 25 usable groups
1045 | out of 96 requested
1046 | ```
1047 | 
1048 | That means we should not generalize its magnitude carelessly.
1049 | 
1050 | But the direction of the boundary comparison does not support the original hypothesis.
1051 | 
1052 | The correct bounded result remains negative.
1053 | 
1054 | ---
1055 | 
1056 | ## Evidence Ledger
1057 | 
1058 | | Claim | Status | Evidence / limitation |
1059 | |---|---|---|
1060 | | One predeclared spatial scale shows excess predictive coherence large enough to beat the full frozen family null | **FAILED** | family maximum `0.2906`, permutation `p ≈ 0.0849` |
1061 | | `0.90 R_eff` is a privileged predictive boundary | **NOT ESTABLISHED** | V1 failed family test; post-run audit also identified protocol weaknesses |
1062 | | `0.90 R_eff` preferentially localizes causal effects relative to `0.60 R_eff` pseudo-boundary | **FAILED** | candidate-minus-control `≈ -0.00724`; one-sided `p ≈ 0.9693` |
1063 | | Causal effects are spatially localized across the tested boundaries | **MEASURED** | same-side intervention responses exceed opposite-side responses at both candidate and control boundaries |
1064 | | Spatial causal locality implies individuality | **NOT ESTABLISHED** | locality also occurs at interior pseudo-boundary |
1065 | | Active frontier/interface is repeatedly causally important across the Digital Crystal experiments | **SUPPORTED ACROSS CHAPTERS** | Chapters 18, 20 and 21 provide independent mechanisms |
1066 | | Stable normalized flux defines a natural individual | **UNTESTED** | flow stability is not individuation |
1067 | | The active process propagates as a wave | **UNTESTED / OPEN** | no phase, velocity or dispersion measurement |
1068 | | The Digital Crystal is alive | **NOT CLAIMED** | evidence insufficient |
1069 | 
1070 | ---
1071 | 
1072 | ## What Survived the Hypothesis?
1073 | 
1074 | The most important result of Chapter 22 is not a new boundary.
1075 | 
1076 | It is the collapse of the assumption that a boundary is necessarily the right object.
1077 | 
1078 | We tried:
1079 | 
1080 | ```text
1081 | PREDICTIVE REGION
1082 | ```
1083 | 
1084 | and failed.
1085 | 
1086 | We tried:
1087 | 
1088 | ```text
1089 | PRIVILEGED CAUSAL BOUNDARY
1090 | ```
1091 | 
1092 | and failed.
1093 | 
1094 | What survived was:
1095 | 
1096 | ```text
1097 | SPATIAL CAUSAL LOCALITY
1098 | ```
1099 | 
1100 | combined with the cross-chapter observation that:
1101 | 
1102 | ```text
1103 | causal relevance repeatedly follows
1104 | dynamically generated interfaces
1105 | ```
1106 | 
1107 | and:
1108 | 
1109 | ```text
1110 | stable process flux
1111 | can survive without stable size
1112 | ```
1113 | 
1114 | So the current phenomenon record becomes:
1115 | 
1116 | ### Phenomenon record
1117 | 
1118 | **Phenomenon:** Spatial causal locality
1119 | 
1120 | **Status:** **MEASURED**
1121 | 
1122 | > Local perturbations have stronger consequences in nearby regions of the Digital Crystal, but the tested outer candidate boundary is not more privileged than an ordinary interior pseudo-boundary.
1123 | 
1124 | ### Cross-chapter phenomenon
1125 | 
1126 | **Phenomenon:** Interface-mediated process organization
1127 | 
1128 | **Status:** **SUPPORTED / PROVISIONAL AS A GENERAL PRINCIPLE**
1129 | 
1130 | > Across the tested Digital Crystal mechanisms, causal activity repeatedly concentrates around dynamically generated construction interfaces: historical state matters while coupled to them, material loss creates them, and finite computation determines which of their opportunities are serviced.
1131 | 
1132 | ### Cross-chapter phenomenon
1133 | 
1134 | **Phenomenon:** Flux-dominated process description
1135 | 
1136 | **Status:** **MEASURED / OPEN INTERPRETATION**
1137 | 
1138 | > Gross construction, loss and reoccupation flows can remain highly structured and comparatively stable even when occupied population and spatial scale do not, suggesting that process traffic may be a more informative state variable than static material stock.
1139 | 
1140 | None of these establish an individual.
1141 | 
1142 | Together they tell us what to measure next.
1143 | 
1144 | ---
1145 | 
1146 | ## The New Experimental Object
1147 | 
1148 | The next representation should not begin with:
1149 | 
1150 | ```text
1151 | all cells inside radius R
1152 | ```
1153 | 
1154 | Instead we should construct something like:
1155 | 
1156 | ```text
1157 | ACTIVE PROCESS FIELD
1158 | ```
1159 | 
1160 | where each local region records quantities such as:
1161 | 
1162 | ```text
1163 | attachment opportunity
1164 | actual attachment
1165 | loss
1166 | reoccupation
1167 | frontier creation
1168 | evaluation allocation
1169 | recent causal influence
1170 | ```
1171 | 
1172 | Then ask whether the active field at one time predicts or causes the active field at later times through a coherent spatiotemporal path.
1173 | 
1174 | Conceptually:
1175 | 
1176 | ```text
1177 | ACTIVE REGION at t
1178 | ↓
1179 | ACTIVE REGION at t+1
1180 | ↓
1181 | ACTIVE REGION at t+2
1182 | ↓
1183 | ACTIVE REGION at t+3
1184 | ```
1185 | 
1186 | The scientific question becomes:
1187 | 
1188 | > **Does active causal organization persist through space-time as a coherent process even when the material participating in it changes?**
1189 | 
1190 | That is much closer to the phenomena the previous chapters actually exposed.
1191 | 
1192 | And it avoids pretending we already know what the individual is.
1193 | 
1194 | ---
1195 | 
1196 | ## A Better Boundary Question
1197 | 
1198 | If a coherent active process is eventually found, boundaries can return later.
1199 | 
1200 | But then the boundary should be discovered from the process.
1201 | 
1202 | Not imposed as:
1203 | 
1204 | ```text
1205 | radius = 0.90
1206 | ```
1207 | 
1208 | A future boundary might be defined by where:
1209 | 
1210 | ```text
1211 | predictive influence falls
1212 | causal influence falls
1213 | event flux separates
1214 | local future becomes conditionally independent
1215 | ```
1216 | 
1217 | or some combination of those.
1218 | 
1219 | The important reversal is:
1220 | 
1221 | ```text
1222 | OLD APPROACH
1223 | 
1224 | choose region
1225 | ↓
1226 | ask whether it behaves like a thing
1227 | ```
1228 | 
1229 | becomes:
1230 | 
1231 | ```text
1232 | NEW APPROACH
1233 | 
1234 | measure causal process
1235 | ↓
1236 | find coherent organization
1237 | ↓
1238 | only then ask whether a boundary emerges
1239 | ```
1240 | 
1241 | That is a much more defensible route.
1242 | 
1243 | ---
1244 | 
1245 | ## Where Chapter 22 Stops
1246 | 
1247 | It would be easy to build V3 immediately.
1248 | 
1249 | Choose a new interface thickness.
1250 | 
1251 | Choose a new activity threshold.
1252 | 
1253 | Choose a new field representation.
1254 | 
1255 | Run another experiment.
1256 | 
1257 | We should not do that yet.
1258 | 
1259 | The backfill changed the ontology of the problem.
1260 | 
1261 | Before another confirmatory run, the next experiment needs a genuinely new frozen object:
1262 | 
1263 | ```text
1264 | not occupied body
1265 | 
1266 | not centered radius
1267 | 
1268 | not hand-chosen boundary
1269 | 
1270 | but
1271 | 
1272 | spatiotemporal active process
1273 | ```
1274 | 
1275 | That design deserves to be specified separately.
1276 | 
1277 | So Chapter 22 ends with two failed boundary hypotheses and one much better question.
1278 | 
1279 | The crystal may not be a thing with a process inside it.
1280 | 
1281 | The process may be the thing we have been trying to see.
1282 | 
1283 | Not an organism.
1284 | 
1285 | Not yet an individual.
1286 | 
1287 | Not even necessarily one coherent entity.
1288 | 
1289 | But finally, perhaps, the right experimental object.
1290 | 
1291 | ```text
1292 | MATERIAL
1293 | ↓
1294 | INTERFACE
1295 | ↓
1296 | EVENTS
1297 | ↓
1298 | FLUX
1299 | ↓
1300 | CAUSAL LOCALITY
1301 | ↓
1302 | SPATIOTEMPORAL PROCESS?
1303 | ```
1304 | 
1305 | That final question mark stays.
1306 | 
1307 | It has earned the right to.
```
