0010 | 
0011 | ## Appendix 01: Phenomenon Ledger
0012 | 
0013 | This appendix records something different from the chapter-by-chapter claim ledger.
0014 | 
0015 | The claim ledger asks:
0016 | 
0017 | ```text
0018 | What did we predict?
0019 | Did the predeclared experiment pass?
0020 | What claim did the evidence earn?
0021 | ```
0022 | 
0023 | The phenomenon ledger asks:
0024 | 
0025 | ```text
0026 | What did the experiment reveal anyway?
0027 | Did the same pattern appear again elsewhere?
0028 | Is a substrate-level regularity beginning to emerge?
0029 | ```
0030 | 
0031 | Those are not the same questions.
0032 | 
0033 | A primary hypothesis can fail while exposing a different phenomenon.
0034 | 
0035 | That does not rescue the failed hypothesis.
0036 | 
0037 | It means the experiment taught us something else.
0038 | 
0039 | The purpose of this appendix is to preserve those surviving observations across the entire book so that recurring mechanisms are not lost when a chapter moves on.
0040 | 
0041 | The standard is the same one used throughout the project:
0042 | 
0043 | ```text
0044 | PROPERTY
0045 | ↓
0046 | MECHANISM
0047 | ↓
0048 | IMPLEMENTATION
0049 | ↓
0050 | OBSERVATION
0051 | ↓
0052 | MEASUREMENT
0053 | ↓
0054 | CONTROLLED EXPERIMENT
0055 | ↓
0056 | BOUNDED CLAIM
0057 | ```
0058 | 
0059 | Nothing in this ledger becomes a biological property merely because it resembles one.
0060 | 
0061 | Nothing becomes a law merely because it recurs twice.
0062 | 
0063 | Nothing becomes evidence for life merely because it is interesting.
0064 | 
0065 | Each phenomenon remains bounded by the experiments that produced it.
0066 | 
0067 | ---
0068 | 
0069 | # P001 — Interface Principle
0070 | 
0071 | **Status:** **SUPPORTED AS A RECURRING PHENOMENON**
0072 | 
0073 | **First clear appearance:** Chapter 18
0074 | 
0075 | **Strongest supporting chapters:** Chapters 18, 20 and 21
0076 | 
0077 | **Related chapters:** Chapter 19 and Chapter 22
0078 | 
0079 | ---
0080 | 
0081 | ## Short Description
0082 | 
0083 | > **In the Digital Crystal, causal relevance is concentrated around dynamically active construction interfaces: stored state matters while coupled to them, material loss can create them, and finite computation determines which of their opportunities are serviced.**
0084 | 
0085 | The important object is not simply:
0086 | 
0087 | ```text
0088 | occupied material
0089 | ```
0090 | 
0091 | and not simply:
0092 | 
0093 | ```text
0094 | the outer edge of the crystal
0095 | ```
0096 | 
0097 | It is the dynamically generated set of locations at which the current process can still change what happens next.
0098 | 
0099 | ---
0100 | 
0101 | ## Why This Phenomenon Was Not Obvious
0102 | 
0103 | The early Digital Crystal experiments treated the structure largely as accumulated material.
0104 | 
0105 | Once a site became occupied, it remained occupied.
0106 | 
0107 | Growth moved outward.
0108 | 
0109 | That made the visible outer boundary look like the obvious active region.
0110 | 
0111 | But later experiments broke that picture.
0112 | 
0113 | A historical state could remain physically present while becoming causally irrelevant.
0114 | 
0115 | Material loss could create new active boundaries inside old material.
0116 | 
0117 | A finite computational budget could leave some frontier opportunities unevaluated.
0118 | 
0119 | The experiments therefore began separating:
0120 | 
0121 | ```text
0122 | MATERIAL EXISTS
0123 | ```
0124 | 
0125 | from:
0126 | 
0127 | ```text
0128 | MATERIAL CAN STILL PARTICIPATE
0129 | IN FUTURE CONSTRUCTION
0130 | ```
0131 | 
0132 | That distinction is the foundation of the Interface Principle.
0133 | 
0134 | ---
0135 | 
0136 | ## Chapter 18 — Persistent State Was Not Enough
0137 | 
0138 | Chapter 18 introduced a local modified material state.
0139 | 
0140 | An experience could write the state.
0141 | 
0142 | The state could persist.
0143 | 
0144 | But when the modified cells were later buried behind new construction, removing the labels produced no detectable difference in future growth.
0145 | 
0146 | The state still existed.
0147 | 
0148 | The process could no longer reach it.
0149 | 
0150 | This produced the first major separation:
0151 | 
0152 | ```text
0153 | PERSISTENCE
0154 | ≠
0155 | CAUSAL ACCESSIBILITY
0156 | ```
0157 | 
0158 | The experiment then measured the active construction region directly.
0159 | 
0160 | Modified material mattered when it remained adjacent to frontier sites where attachment decisions were still being made.
0161 | 
0162 | When it left that region, its causal effect disappeared even though the stored state remained intact.
0163 | 
0164 | This suggested that the active frontier was not merely geometric.
0165 | 
0166 | It was a **causal aperture**.
0167 | 
0168 | ---
0169 | 
0170 | ## Chapter 18 — Placement Changed Causal Lifetime
0171 | 
0172 | The stronger Chapter 18 experiment held the amount of propagated historical material constant.
0173 | 
0174 | Only spatial placement changed.
0175 | 
0176 | Three policies were compared:
0177 | 
0178 | ```text
0179 | INTERIOR-BIASED
0180 | RANDOM
0181 | SURFACE-BIASED
0182 | ```
0183 | 
0184 | With copy quantity controlled exactly, surface-biased placement produced substantially greater:
0185 | 
0186 | ```text
0187 | integrated frontier access
0188 | integrated probability leverage
0189 | realized causal attachment flips
0190 | ```
0191 | 
0192 | The important result was therefore not:
0193 | 
0194 | ```text
0195 | more stored state
0196 | → more effect
0197 | ```
0198 | 
0199 | It was:
0200 | 
0201 | ```text
0202 | same amount of state
0203 | +
0204 | different spatial placement
0205 | →
0206 | different causal lifetime
0207 | ```
0208 | 
0209 | The strongest bounded result from that chapter was:
0210 | 
0211 | > A persistent local state can alter later construction while it remains causally accessible, and with state quantity held constant, spatial placement strongly changes the duration and magnitude of that causal availability.
0212 | 
0213 | This is the clearest experimental foundation for the Interface Principle.
0214 | 
0215 | ---
0216 | 
0217 | ## Chapter 20 — Loss Created New Interface
0218 | 
0219 | Chapter 20 removed the assumption that occupied material lasts forever.
0220 | 
0221 | A cell could become empty again.
0222 | 
0223 | The initial prediction treated construction opportunity as though it scaled mainly with the crystal's outer perimeter while loss scaled with occupied area.
0224 | 
0225 | That prediction failed.
0226 | 
0227 | The reason was geometric.
0228 | 
0229 | A lost occupied site often became a new local attachment opportunity.
0230 | 
0231 | The observed chain was:
0232 | 
0233 | ```text
0234 | MATERIAL LOSS
0235 | ↓
0236 | EMPTY SITE
0237 | ↓
0238 | NEW LOCAL FRONTIER
0239 | ↓
0240 | NEW ATTACHMENT OPPORTUNITY
0241 | ```
0242 | 
0243 | Measured frontier creation was approximately:
0244 | 
0245 | ```text
0246 | surface loss
0247 | ~0.995 new frontier sites per loss
0248 | 
0249 | interior loss
0250 | ~1.000 new frontier sites per loss
0251 | ```
0252 | 
0253 | The interface therefore was not limited to the outside of the object.
0254 | 
0255 | Loss could create new active interface inside previously occupied material.
0256 | 
0257 | That substantially changed the interpretation of the crystal's geometry.
0258 | 
0259 | ---
0260 | 
0261 | ## Chapter 20 — Reoccupation Was Not Repair
0262 | 
0263 | More than ninety-three percent of lost sites were subsequently reoccupied under the tested exact-count conditions.
0264 | 
0265 | Most returned within one or two updates.
0266 | 
0267 | This looked superficially like repair.
0268 | 
0269 | But there was no:
0270 | 
0271 | ```text
0272 | damage detector
0273 | repair objective
0274 | target morphology
0275 | special reconstruction pathway
0276 | ```
0277 | 
0278 | The same ordinary growth rule acted on newly empty sites.
0279 | 
0280 | So the correct interpretation was:
0281 | 
0282 | ```text
0283 | LOSS
0284 | →
0285 | CREATES INTERFACE
0286 | 
0287 | INTERFACE
0288 | →
0289 | CREATES ORDINARY CONSTRUCTION OPPORTUNITY
0290 | ```
0291 | 
0292 | The system did not repair damage because it recognized damage.
0293 | 
0294 | Its geometry made loss available to the same mechanism that already fills eligible empty sites.
0295 | 
0296 | This is a strong substrate-level extension of the Interface Principle.
0297 | 
0298 | ---
0299 | 
0300 | ## Chapter 21 — Computation Selected the Interface
0301 | 
0302 | Chapter 21 added finite computational opportunity.
0303 | 
0304 | The crystal could no longer evaluate every eligible frontier site.
0305 | 
0306 | At most:
0307 | 
0308 | ```text
0309 | B
0310 | ```
0311 | 
0312 | frontier candidates could be evaluated per update.
0313 | 
0314 | This produced another separation.
0315 | 
0316 | The interface could contain many possible actions.
0317 | 
0318 | But only some could actually be serviced.
0319 | 
0320 | So:
0321 | 
0322 | ```text
0323 | FRONTIER OPPORTUNITY
0324 | ≠
0325 | EVALUATED OPPORTUNITY
0326 | ```
0327 | 
0328 | Different local scheduling policies produced different material futures even at the same total budget.
0329 | 
0330 | High-support scheduling strongly favoured reoccupation.
0331 | 
0332 | Low-support scheduling favoured first occupation more strongly.
0333 | 
0334 | The crystal did not know whether a site was:
0335 | 
0336 | ```text
0337 | old territory
0338 | ```
0339 | 
0340 | or:
0341 | 
0342 | ```text
0343 | new territory
0344 | ```
0345 | 
0346 | The distinction emerged from geometry.
0347 | 
0348 | The result extended the Interface Principle again:
0349 | 
0350 | > The active interface is not merely where change is possible. Under computational scarcity, it is also where finite evaluation opportunity must be allocated.
0351 | 
0352 | ---
0353 | 
0354 | ## Chapter 19 — Accessibility Was Necessary but Not Sufficient
0355 | 
0356 | Chapter 19 provides an important limitation.
0357 | 
0358 | Two different historical traces remained:
0359 | 
0360 | ```text
0361 | persistent
0362 | spatially distinguishable
0363 | causally accessible
0364 | ```
0365 | 
0366 | and still failed to produce a scientifically meaningful difference under the common later challenge.
0367 | 
0368 | So:
0369 | 
0370 | ```text
0371 | CAUSAL ACCESS
0372 | ≠
0373 | FUNCTIONAL READOUT
0374 | ```
0375 | 
0376 | This prevents the Interface Principle from becoming too strong.
0377 | 
0378 | Being coupled to the active interface is not enough to guarantee that a difference will matter.
0379 | 
0380 | It only means the current process has an opportunity to encounter it.
0381 | 
0382 | The later dynamics must still be sensitive to the particular degree of freedom in which the states differ.
0383 | 
0384 | ---
0385 | 
0386 | ## Chapter 22 — The Boundary Was Not Privileged
0387 | 
0388 | Chapter 22 tried to identify a privileged spatial boundary.
0389 | 
0390 | That failed.
0391 | 
0392 | The outer candidate boundary did not localize causal effects more strongly than an interior pseudo-boundary.
0393 | 
0394 | But local perturbations still produced stronger effects on nearby regions.
0395 | 
0396 | So Chapter 22 added:
0397 | 
0398 | ```text
0399 | SPATIAL CAUSAL LOCALITY
0400 | ```
0401 | 
0402 | without adding:
0403 | 
0404 | ```text
0405 | PRIVILEGED OUTER BOUNDARY
0406 | ```
0407 | 
0408 | This is consistent with the Interface Principle.
0409 | 
0410 | The causally active structure may be distributed across dynamically generated local interfaces rather than enclosed by one permanent boundary.
0411 | 
0412 | ---
0413 | 
0414 | ## Current Mechanistic Picture
0415 | 
0416 | The recurring mechanism can now be written as:
0417 | 
0418 | ```text
0419 | CURRENT MATERIAL STATE
0420 | ↓
0421 | DYNAMICALLY GENERATED INTERFACE
0422 | ↓
0423 | LOCAL CONSTRUCTION OPPORTUNITIES
0424 | ↓
0425 | FINITE EVALUATION / STOCHASTIC DECISION
0426 | ↓
0427 | ATTACHMENT OR LOSS
0428 | ↓
0429 | NEW MATERIAL STATE
0430 | ↓
0431 | NEW INTERFACE
0432 | ```
0433 | 
0434 | The process continually regenerates the conditions under which its next changes can occur.
0435 | 
0436 | The interface is therefore not a static object.
0437 | 
0438 | It is produced by the dynamics.
0439 | 
0440 | ---
0441 | 
0442 | ## Current Bounded Principle
0443 | 
0444 | The strongest cross-chapter statement currently justified is:
0445 | 
0446 | > **Across the tested Digital Crystal mechanisms, causal influence repeatedly depends on dynamically generated construction interfaces. Historical material exerts influence while coupled to active construction, loss creates new active interfaces, and finite computational opportunity determines which interface events can be evaluated.**
0447 | 
0448 | This is the current **Interface Principle**.
0449 | 
0450 | It is a recurring empirical pattern.
0451 | 
0452 | It is not yet a universal law of digital systems.
0453 | 
0454 | ---
0455 | 
0456 | ## What Would We Expect If the Principle Is Real?
0457 | 
0458 | If the Interface Principle continues to hold, future experiments should repeatedly find that measures based on active construction opportunity explain more causal behavior than measures based only on accumulated occupancy.
0459 | 
0460 | For example:
0461 | 
0462 | ```text
0463 | ACTIVE INTERFACE STATE
0464 | ```
0465 | 
0466 | should often be more informative about immediate future changes than:
0467 | 
0468 | ```text
0469 | INTERIOR BULK STATE
0470 | ```
0471 | 
0472 | after controlling for trivial geometry.
0473 | 
0474 | The relevant process may therefore be better represented by:
0475 | 
0476 | ```text
0477 | where change can happen
0478 | ```
0479 | 
0480 | than by:
0481 | 
0482 | ```text
0483 | where material currently exists
0484 | ```
0485 | 
0486 | This is a prediction for future work.
0487 | 
0488 | It has not yet been established generally.
0489 | 
0490 | ---
0491 | 
0492 | ## What Would Weaken or Falsify It?
0493 | 
0494 | The Interface Principle would weaken substantially if future experiments showed that:
0495 | 
0496 | ```text
0497 | deep interior state
0498 | systematically controls future construction
0499 | without interface mediation
0500 | ```
0501 | 
0502 | or that:
0503 | 
0504 | ```text
0505 | active-interface measurements
0506 | provide no additional causal or predictive information
0507 | beyond static occupancy and geometry
0508 | ```
0509 | 
0510 | or that:
0511 | 
0512 | ```text
0513 | apparent interface effects disappear
0514 | under stronger matched controls
0515 | ```
0516 | 
0517 | A stronger version would also fail if the apparent recurrence across Chapters 18–22 turned out to be an artifact of the particular frozen Digital Crystal growth rule rather than a more general computational phenomenon.
0518 | 
0519 | The principle should therefore be treated as specific to the tested substrate until replicated elsewhere.
0520 | 
0521 | ---
0522 | 
0523 | ## What This Does Not Establish
0524 | 
0525 | The Interface Principle does **not** establish:
0526 | 
0527 | ```text
0528 | membrane
0529 | organism
0530 | self
0531 | individual
0532 | autonomy
0533 | homeostasis
0534 | repair
0535 | metabolism
0536 | agency
0537 | life
0538 | ```
0539 | 
0540 | It does not establish that the Digital Crystal possesses a privileged boundary.
0541 | 
0542 | It does not establish that the active interface is one coherent entity.
0543 | 
0544 | It does not establish that the interface propagates as a wave.
0545 | 
0546 | It does not establish that every Digital Crystal phenomenon is controlled by the interface.
0547 | 
0548 | It establishes something smaller:
0549 | 
0550 | > **Where the Digital Crystal can currently act matters more than the mere persistence of material that can no longer participate in that action.**
0551 | 
0552 | ---
0553 | 
0554 | ## Relationship to Other Phenomena
0555 | 
0556 | The Interface Principle currently interacts with several other phenomena that will receive their own entries in this appendix.
0557 | 
0558 | ```text
0559 | P002
0560 | LOSSY-HISTORY PRINCIPLE
0561 | 
0562 | history can survive in coarse form
0563 | without remaining exactly recoverable
0564 | ```
0565 | 
0566 | ```text
0567 | P003
0568 | FLUX PRINCIPLE
0569 | 
0570 | static material stock can conceal
0571 | large construction / loss / reoccupation traffic
0572 | ```
0573 | 
0574 | ```text
0575 | P004
0576 | SPATIAL CAUSAL LOCALITY
0577 | 
0578 | local interventions preferentially affect
0579 | nearby future dynamics
0580 | without establishing a privileged body boundary
0581 | ```
0582 | 
0583 | ```text
0584 | H001
0585 | PROPAGATING-FIELD HYPOTHESIS
0586 | 
0587 | active causal organization may move
0588 | through space-time in a measurable way
0589 | ```
0590 | 
0591 | The relationships between these phenomena may eventually become more important than any one of them individually.
0592 | 
0593 | ---
0594 | 
0595 | ## Current Status
0596 | 
0597 | ```text
0598 | PHENOMENON:
0599 | Interface Principle
0600 | 
0601 | STATUS:
0602 | SUPPORTED AS A RECURRING PHENOMENON
0603 | 
0604 | STRONGEST EVIDENCE:
0605 | Chapter 18 exact-quantity placement experiment
0606 | 
0607 | MECHANISTIC EXTENSIONS:
0608 | Chapter 20 loss-generated frontier
0609 | Chapter 21 finite evaluation allocation
0610 | 
0611 | IMPORTANT LIMIT:
0612 | Chapter 19 shows accessibility does not guarantee readout
0613 | 
0614 | BOUNDARY RESULT:
0615 | Chapter 22 found locality but no privileged outer boundary
0616 | 
0617 | GENERALITY:
0618 | UNTESTED OUTSIDE THE DIGITAL CRYSTAL SUBSTRATE
0619 | ```
0620 | 
0621 | For now, that is where the evidence stops.
```

```markdown
0001 | # Narrative Method
0002 | 
0003 | Digital Life is not organized as a conventional textbook progression.
0004 | 
0005 | Do not default to:
0006 | 
0007 | ```text
0008 | concept
0009 | → explanation
0010 | → implementation
0011 | → exercise
0012 | → next concept
0013 | ```
0014 | 
0015 | The preferred rhythm is investigative:
0016 | 
0017 | ```text
0018 | FRONTIER
0019 |     ↓
0020 | MYSTERY
0021 |     ↓
0022 | REDUCTION
0023 |     ↓
0024 | BUILD
0025 |     ↓
0026 | EXPERIMENT
0027 |     ↓
0028 | MEASUREMENT
0029 |     ↓
0030 | RETURN TO THE ORIGINAL CLAIM
0031 | ```
0032 | 
0033 | The book should regularly alternate between:
0034 | 
0035 | * **Spectacle** — show a striking system before explaining it.
0036 | * **Reduction** — strip a complicated phenomenon down to the smallest mechanism that could produce it.
0037 | * **Build** — implement the mechanism explicitly.
0038 | * **Experiment** — change one variable and observe the result.
0039 | * **Autopsy** — interrogate claims such as “healing,” “reproduction,” “memory,” or “evolution.”
0040 | * **Failure** — show systems optimizing the wrong thing or failing under perturbation.
0041 | * **Search** — allow computation to discover candidates rather than selecting only famous examples.
0042 | * **Frontier** — connect the mechanism to contemporary artificial-life research.
0043 | 
0044 | The reader should repeatedly encounter something surprising first and then be asked:
0045 | 
0046 | > **How can this happen?**
0047 | 
0048 | The book then earns the explanation.
0049 | 
0050 | ---
0051 | 
0052 | # Opening Arc
0053 | 
0054 | ## Part I — Something Is Moving
0055 | 
0056 | The first Part establishes the central mystery of Digital Life.
0057 | 
0058 | ### 00 — What Would Digital Life Mean?
0059 | 
0060 | Establish the anti-cargo-cult rule.
0061 | 
0062 | Do not define life.
0063 | 
0064 | Establish that every life-like property must be demonstrated and measured.
0065 | 
0066 | ### 01 — Look at This Thing
0067 | 
0068 | Lead with a striking contemporary artificial-life system such as Lenia or Flow-Lenia.
0069 | 
0070 | Show the behaviour before fully explaining the mechanism.
0071 | 
0072 | Ask:
0073 | 
0074 | > What are we actually looking at?
0075 | 
0076 | Reveal progressively that the apparent creature is produced by local dynamics rather than an explicitly programmed creature controller.
0077 | 
0078 | ### 02 — Remove Almost Everything
0079 | 
0080 | Reduce the complicated system aggressively.
0081 | 
0082 | Move from:
0083 | 
0084 | ```text
0085 | continuous
0086 | 2D
0087 | rich neighborhood
0088 | many parameters
0089 | organism-like pattern
0090 | ```
0091 | 
0092 | toward:
0093 | 
0094 | ```text
0095 | binary
0096 | 1D
0097 | three-cell neighborhood
0098 | tiny lookup rule
0099 | single active cell
0100 | ```
0101 | 
0102 | Build the smallest useful cellular automaton.
0103 | 
0104 | The chapter's purpose is experimental reduction, not merely introduction to CA.
0105 | 
0106 | ### 03 — The First Surprise
0107 | 
0108 | Use Rule 30.
0109 | 
0110 | Demonstrate that removing machinery does not necessarily remove complicated behaviour.
0111 | 
0112 | Central result:
0113 | 
0114 | ```text
0115 | simple mechanism
0116 | ≠
0117 | simple trajectory
0118 | ```
0119 | 
0120 | ### 04 — When Does a Pattern Become a Thing?
0121 | 
0122 | Use Conway's Game of Life and the glider.
0123 | 
0124 | Investigate pattern identity.
0125 | 
0126 | The cells composing the glider continually change, but the pattern persists and moves.
0127 | 
0128 | Introduce:
0129 | 
0130 | ```text
0131 | material identity
0132 | vs
0133 | pattern identity
0134 | ```
0135 | 
0136 | Ask whether persistent information structures can meaningfully be treated as entities.
0137 | 
0138 | Do not answer philosophically before measuring their properties.
0139 | 
0140 | ### 05 — Kill It
0141 | 
0142 | Perturb structures deliberately.
0143 | 
0144 | Distinguish:
0145 | 
0146 | ```text
0147 | persistence
0148 | ≠
0149 | robustness
0150 | ≠
0151 | regeneration
0152 | ```
0153 | 
0154 | Compare systems that merely survive, systems that change under damage, and systems capable of restoring structure.
0155 | 
0156 | This establishes perturbation as one of the book's primary experimental methods.
0157 | 
0158 | ### 06 — Can It Make Another One?
0159 | 
0160 | Investigate digital self-reproduction.
0161 | 
0162 | Use historical and contemporary self-replicating cellular systems where appropriate.
0163 | 
0164 | Distinguish copying, reproduction, heredity, and useful inheritance.
0165 | 
0166 | Do not claim life merely because a pattern produces another pattern.
0167 | 
0168 | ### 07 — Evolution Without Life?
0169 | 
0170 | Introduce:
0171 | 
0172 | ```text
0173 | variation
0174 | +
0175 | inheritance
0176 | +
0177 | selection
0178 | ```
0179 | 
0180 | Ask whether this is enough to call a system evolutionary.
0181 | 
0182 | Investigate fitness, novelty, adaptation, open-endedness, and cumulative improvement.
0183 | 
0184 | Introduce the problem that endless novelty alone may be a weak target.
0185 | 
0186 | ### 08 — Now Prove It
0187 | 
0188 | Turn against the previous chapters.
0189 | 
0190 | The reader has now seen:
0191 | 
0192 | ```text
0193 | emergence
0194 | persistent patterns
0195 | entity-like behaviour
0196 | damage
0197 | replication
0198 | evolutionary dynamics
0199 | ```
0200 | 
0201 | Now ask:
0202 | 
0203 | > **How much of that did we actually demonstrate?**
0204 | 
0205 | This chapter opens the measurement section.
0206 | 
0207 | The governing transition is:
0208 | 
0209 | ```text
0210 | LOOK
0211 | 
0212 | becomes
0213 | 
0214 | MEASURE
0215 | ```
0216 | 
0217 | From this point onward, every interesting visual phenomenon becomes a candidate hypothesis rather than a conclusion.
```
